# Introduction: The Language of the Canon

Pāli is the language in which the Theravāda canon has come down to us. That much is agreed. Almost everything else that is commonly said about it is inference, not record: where the language came from, whose everyday speech it once was, how close it stands to the words the Buddha himself used.

The distinction matters from the first page, because the two kinds of statement look alike once they are set in print. A statement about the grammar can be checked by anyone who has the texts: it says that a particular ending does a particular job, and the texts either bear it out or they do not. A statement about the language's history cannot be checked in that way. It is reconstructed from inscriptions, from comparison with related languages and from the internal evidence of the texts themselves, and competent scholars reconstruct it differently. This book states the first kind as fact, because it is fact, and marks the second kind as inference wherever it has occasion to mention it. The grammar is settled. Where the language came from is not.

## What is settled

Pāli is a Middle Indo-Aryan language. It descends, along with the other Prakrits, from the same Old Indo-Aryan stock as Sanskrit, but it does not descend from the Sanskrit we have.

In its sounds and its endings it belongs with the languages of *western* India, not with those of the east, and this can be shown from evidence that is dated, localised, and has nothing to do with Buddhism. The emperor Aśoka had his edicts cut in stone in the third century BCE at both ends of his empire, and the same imperial text comes out differently at each end: at Girnar in the west the king is *rāja*, at Kālsī in the east he is *lāja*. Set Pāli beside those two columns and it goes with the western one nearly throughout. Appendix C sets the columns out.

Scattered through it, however, are forms that do not fit that pattern and belong with the eastern column instead. The clearest of them needs no grammar at all to see: the canon writes *nibbāna* where Sanskrit writes *nirvāṇa*, and the difference in the final consonant is an eastern trait, not a western one. There are not many of them, and Appendix C lists what they are. What they are evidence *of* is the harder question, and Appendix C takes that up too.

The word *pāḷi* is not, in the texts, the name of a language. It means **the text**: the canonical wording, as against the commentary written to explain it. A commentator who writes *pārājikapāḷiyaṁ* means "in the text of the Pārājikas", not "in the Pārājikas' language". The name we use is a shortening of *pāḷibhāsā*, "the language of the text", a phrase neither the canon nor the commentaries ever use, and which turns up only in the sub-commentaries and later. Using *Pāli* as the name of a particular language appears to be no older than the eighteenth century.[^intro-pali-name] The tradition's name for the language is *māgadhabhāsā*, "the Magadha language". The canon's own word, in the one passage where it needs one, is *ariyaka*, "Aryan", set against *milakkha*, a speaker of something else.

And it was nobody's mother tongue. Von Hinüber puts it without qualification: Pāli "has never been a spoken language neither in Magadha nor elsewhere",[^intro-never-spoken] and the reason is the western character just described. A language with the features of a region hundreds of miles from where the events it narrates took place was not the daily speech of either region. Whatever the Buddha spoke, he did not speak Pāli in the form in which we have it.

## What is not

Almost everything else: where the language came from, how far the westward recasting altered it, and what the Buddha's own speech was like. These are inferences. Some of them are good ones, and none is checkable in the way a declension is checkable.

The dispute has one famous text at its centre. Two brahmin monks complain to the Buddha that the word of the Buddha is being spoiled, and propose a remedy; he refuses the remedy, and rules:

- Anujānāmi, bhikkhave, **sakāya niruttiyā** buddhavacanaṁ pariyāpuṇitun ti. (Kd 15 — Vin ii 139)
- *anujānāmi* I allow · *bhikkhave* monks (voc. pl.) · *sakāya* one's own (fem. inst. sg., agreeing with *niruttiyā*) · *niruttiyā* (in) expression · *buddhavacanaṁ* the word of the Buddha · *pariyāpuṇituṁ* to learn thoroughly, to master
- I allow you, monks, to learn the word of the Buddha **in its own expression**.

Read *sakāya niruttiyā* as "each in his own dialect" and the ruling licenses translating the canon into whatever a listener speaks. Read it as "in its own expression" and it forbids that very thing, and requires the Buddha's wording to be kept. The commentary takes the second; the grammar permits both; and a century and a half of philology has not closed the question. Appendix C sets that argument out: what the tradition claims and where, what the Aśokan inscriptions show, what the two disputed words can and cannot be made to mean, and which scholars currently hold what. If you want only the grammar, you can pass it by.

## The closest surviving witness

If Pāli is not the Buddha's own speech, the question is what it is worth, and the answer is a comparison, not a claim. *Closest* needs something to be closest to.

Pāli is neither the only surviving witness to what the early Buddhist texts said nor the oldest. Fragments survive in Gāndhārī, the language of the north-western kingdom of Gandhāra, dated as early as the first century BCE: they are the oldest Buddhist manuscripts in existence, and roughly a thousand years older than the oldest Pāli manuscript we possess.[^intro-gandhari] Other schools' versions of the same discourses survive in Sanskrit and in the mixed language conventionally called Buddhist Hybrid Sanskrit. The Chinese and Tibetan canons preserve, in translation, a great deal of Indian material whose originals are lost. Against that field, any claim resting on the age of the surviving copy fails at once.

Its claim rests on two other things. It carries **one school's canon entire**, neither fragments nor a selection, in a single consistent language. And that language stayed Middle Indo-Aryan. The other Indian recensions were progressively reworked in the direction of Sanskrit, the prestige language of learned India, and the reworking reached the wording itself; the Theravāda tradition, though it shows traces of the same pull, never carried it through. So a Pāli page stands nearer in its actual forms to what an Indian speaker of the Buddha's era would have said than the corresponding Sanskrit page does, whatever the dates on the two manuscripts. Age of witness and age of language are different measurements, and only the second favours Pāli.

Between the Buddha's speech and the Pāli of the printed editions there stand a period of oral transmission, a recasting of the language westward, and an editorial tradition that reached its present shape centuries later. Each of those is a distance, and none of them is small. But no surviving body of Buddhist text stands closer. Pāli is the closest surviving witness to the language the Buddha spoke. Not the thing itself, and near enough to be listened to.

## What follows for this book

The grammar taught here is the grammar of the received Theravāda canon as the printed editions actually have it. It is not a reconstruction of an earlier stage of the language, and it does not tidy the text into what a historical linguist might expect the text to have been. Three consequences follow, and they will show on almost every page.

Where the editions disagree, this book says so. The canon survives in several printed recensions, among them the Burmese Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana, the Sinhalese Buddha Jayanti and the Pali Text Society's roman-script edition, each prepared by scholars working from manuscripts that do not always agree with one another. Where such an editor has had to choose between two spellings, the edition records the rejected alternatives in its **apparatus**: the register of variant readings printed under or beside the running text, with a mark against each to show which manuscript tradition it came from. A grammar that reads only the running text will present one editor's choice as though it were the language. This book goes to the apparatus, and where the choice is a real one it is reported, not settled in silence.

Forms the grammarians derive but the texts never use are marked as such. The Pāli grammatical tradition completes its paradigms by rule, so a full table will contain cells that no surviving text ever fills. Those cells belong there, because they show how the system works. But you are entitled to know which forms have been read somewhere and which have only been calculated, and the two are not distinguished by looking at them.

Forms that look irregular because they are eastern are explained as eastern, not filed under exceptions. An exception is something a reader has to memorise. A survival from an earlier version of the text is something a reader can understand, and understanding it costs a sentence.

That is a narrower object than "the Buddha's language". It is also the one a reader can check, and it is enough for the purpose. Someone who learns this system can open the Saṁyutta at any page and follow the argument in the words it was made in, which is in the end the only reason to learn it.

[^intro-pali-name]: Oberlies §1, p. 1, on *pāli* as a designation "which originally meant 'text' and whose use as the name of a particular language seems not to antedate the 18th century", citing von Hinüber 1994: 76–90. For the shortening itself, Norman, *A Philological Approach to Buddhism*, p. 53: "Pāli is an abbreviation of pāli-bhāsā, which means the language of the pāli, i.e. the texts, or the canon." The distribution of *pāḷibhāsā* across the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana is a count made over that edition; Appendix C gives it, with the eleven places that print it.

[^intro-never-spoken]: von Hinüber, *A Handbook of Pāli Literature*, §7 (p. 5), whose next sentence gives the ground: "it is possible to infer from linguistic peculiarities of this language that it has been created as some kind of *lingua franca* presumably used in a large area at a time considerably later than the Buddha."

[^intro-gandhari]: Levman, *Pāli and Buddhism* (2023), Introduction, p. 11: the Gāndhārī fragments "have been dated to as early as the first century BCE, making them the oldest Buddhist manuscripts in existence (for comparison, the oldest Pali manuscript is dated to approx. the ninth century CE)."

**Principal sources.** Norman, *A Philological Approach to Buddhism*, lecture IV; von Hinüber §7 and Oberlies §1; the Samantapāsādikā and its ṭīkās, read directly. The argument behind this introduction, with its apparatus and its dissenters, is **Appendix C**.

# How to Use This Book

This is a reference grammar of the Pāli of the Theravāda canon, and it is also a course. Two kinds of reader are expected, and they want opposite things. One is beginning, and wants to be taken through the language in an order that builds: sounds, then the noun phrase, then the verb, then how a sentence is put together. The other already reads Pāli, has met a form on the page, and wants one place where that form is set out with its variants and with some indication of whether the texts actually use it. A book arranged for the first reader is tedious for the second, and a book arranged for the second is unusable by the first. This one is arranged for the first and indexed for the second, and the sections below say how.

One promise runs underneath all of it. Every Pāli sentence in this book is canonical, and every one is cited to the text it stands in. None has been composed to make a point come out neatly. That constraint has a cost, because a real sentence rarely isolates one grammatical feature as cleanly as an invented one would, and it is why the examples reward a second reading: a sentence parsed here is a sentence of the Buddha's word that you have read, not a demonstration of one.

## Two volumes

The grammar and the exercises are separate books: this volume carries the explanation, the paradigms and the worked examples, and the companion volume, *Pāli: A Workbook*, carries the drills and their keys.

The worked examples are here and not there on purpose, beside the rule each one illustrates, because a reader can check a rule that is shown and cannot check one merely stated. The workbook holds the drilling, and it will be of no use until the chapter beside it has been read.

The two volumes are numbered alike. The exercises for Chapter 6 are "Exercises: Numerals" in the workbook, and their key is "Answers: Numerals" at the back of it. No cross-reference in either book gives a page or a number, only a name, because names survive repagination.

The workbook also carries four **cumulative reviews**, gathered after the exercise sets, not interleaved with them. These are a different instrument from the chapter drills: each one tests what only becomes visible when several chapters are in place, and no item in them can be answered out of a single chapter. R1 covers Part I, R2 Part II, R3 Part III, and R4 the whole of Parts IV to VII.

## How the grammar is arranged

Eighteen chapters in seven parts, in the order a reader can actually build on:

Part I, sounds and script, covers the alphabet, the pronunciation and the rules of junction. It comes first because sandhi disguises words, and if you cannot undo a junction you will look up forms that are not there.

Part II, the noun phrase (nouns, gender and adjectives, pronouns, numerals), and **Part III, the verb**, are the two halves of the morphology. Between them they account for most of what a page of Pāli is made of.

Part IV, the non-finite forms, takes the participles, absolutives, infinitives and indeclinables. With these Pāli does what English does with subordinate clauses, and here a reader begins to stop translating word by word.

**Part V, word formation**, covers compounds and the prefixes and suffixes. **Part VI, syntax**, returns to the cases and asks what they do in a sentence, not what their endings are, and then takes the clause and the relative construction. **Part VII** is a single chapter on metre.

The order is a teaching order and not a strict dependency chain, and it strains in places. Compounds cannot be discussed without nouns, so Chapter 13 assumes Part II throughout. Case syntax revisits declensions first met in Chapter 3, twelve chapters later, on purpose: the endings are learnable early and what the cases *mean* is not.

Four appendices follow. **Appendix A** gathers the paradigms in full. Chapters carry only the working subset a reader needs at that point, and the complete tables, with every attested variant, are here. **Appendix B** is a graded reading pathway: which texts to read first, and in what order, once the grammar is in place. **Appendix C** is the argument behind the introduction: where Pāli came from, what the tradition claims, and why the famous Vinaya ruling on language will not settle it. **Appendix D** is the bibliography, which names every grammar, dictionary and coursebook the synthesis draws on.

## How to read a page

A handful of typographic conventions run through both volumes, and they mean one thing each.

*Italic* marks a cited Pāli form, anywhere: in prose, in a footnote, in a paradigm cell. **Bold** has two uses that never collide in practice: it marks an English grammatical term at the point where it is being defined, and it marks whichever element a worked example is drawing attention to. Where an example needs to point at three things at once, the third is ***bold italic***.

Parentheses mark a rare form. In a paradigm, *-o (-e)* means that the nominative singular is *-o* and that *-e* also occurs but is rare, dialectal, poetic or post-canonical. This is Oberlies's convention, not a new one, and it frees italic to mean a single consistent thing everywhere. Appendix A's notation paragraph sets out the two further uses its source makes of the same brackets, which are not rarity markings and are reproduced as he printed them.

A **boxed passage**, set off as a block quotation, means one of two things: real variation in the language itself, such as a manuscript spelling or a *ṭ* where another edition prints *t*, or a point on which reputable grammars give different and defensible analyses. It never means an argument. Ordinary variation between textbooks is settled silently and stated as fact, which is what any grammar does; the box is reserved for the places where the disagreement is real. A box is always headed by a bold line saying what it is; a block quotation with no such heading is a passage quoted at length, and carries no claim about disagreement at all.

**Footnotes carry the working.** A contested dictionary sense, an etymology, a note that a form is found only in the commentaries and not in the canon: these go below instead of interrupting the argument.

A citation names a text, and it carries a page. MN 10 — M i 56 means the Satipaṭṭhāna Sutta, standing on page 56 of the first Pali Text Society volume of the Majjhima. The citation promises the page, and the page finds the passage in a book a reader can hold open. Where a text has no volumes the page is marked as one, Dhp p. 19, so that it cannot be read as a second verse. The Vinaya is cited in Brahmāli's own style, Kd 15 — Vin ii 139, because that is what a reader of his translation will recognise. A few citations carry no page at all. In those the passage could not be placed on one page beyond doubt, and a page that might be wrong is worse than no page. One consequence follows, and it should be said: where a long text is quoted twice from the same page, or from a text that carries no page, the two citations read alike. The Maṅgalasutta is short and is cited eleven times as *Kp 5*.

The niggahīta is written *ṁ* throughout. Other books print *ṃ* or *ŋ* for the same sound, and the editions themselves differ; Chapter 1 sets out what the notations are and why this one was chosen.

### The three-layer example

Every substantial example is given three ways, following Bhikkhu Bodhi's practice in *Reading the Buddha's Discourses in Pāli*: the Pāli as the edition prints it, with its citation; then a word-by-word gloss; then an idiomatic rendering. The middle layer is literal on purpose and is not English. It lets a reader see what each Pāli word contributes, and it will often be ugly for that reason. Bold in the first layer and bold in the third mark the same element, so that the eye can find in the translation what it has just been shown in the original.

In print the middle layer is set **interlinear**: each Pāli word stands above its English, and under the English, where the word inflects, sits a short tag in small capitals. The tags use one spelling throughout. M, f and n are the genders; nom, acc, ins, dat, abl, gen, loc and voc the cases; sg and pl the numbers. So the eye reads down a column for one word and across a row for the sentence.

Where an example is about agreement, a small upright letter after a Pāli word links it to the other words that refer to the same thing and agree with it. In the Vinaya's ordination sentence in Chapter 4, *chāyā*<sub>a</sub> 'the shadow' is linked to *metabbā*<sub>a</sub> 'is to be measured', and *saṅgīti*<sub>d</sub> 'the recitation' to its own *ācikkhitabbā*<sub>d</sub>. Words that share a letter agree in gender, number and case, with one exception: a relative and its correlative, which Chapter 17 sets out, refer to one thing and agree in gender and number while differing in case. Read the letters first, and the structure of the sentence is visible before a word of it has been translated.

The recommendation is to cover the second and third layers and try the Pāli first. Reading a gloss you have not attempted teaches a fact; reading it after an attempt teaches the reason the fact is needed.

## Two ways through

**For the reader learning the language:** work forward, one chapter at a time, and do the drills for a chapter before moving to the next. Take each cumulative review when its part is finished, instead of saving them all for the end. They are diagnostic, and a diagnosis is useful only while there is still time to act on it. When Part IV is done, start Appendix B's reading pathway in parallel with the remaining chapters. The reading-volume evidence below warns against waiting for the grammar to be finished before reading anything.

**For the reader consulting:** Appendix A is the fastest route to a form, and each chapter's paradigm tables are the same material in the subset that chapter teaches. The **Principal sources** line that closes every chapter names the one to three works that chapter leans on most, and what each was leant on for, so that a claim can be traced to the grammar it came from without reading the chapter around it.

## Why the wording matters

Learning a classical language by its morphology is slow, and it is fair to ask what the exactness buys. The canon asks the same question and answers it, in a passage about how the teaching is lost. The first of four causes of the decline of the true Dhamma is bad memorisation, neither persecution nor heresy. The words and phrases in bold:

- Idha, bhikkhave, bhikkhū **duggahitaṁ** suttantaṁ pariyāpuṇanti **dunnikkhittehi padabyañjanehi**. Dunnikkhittassa, bhikkhave, padabyañjanassa **atthopi dunnayo** hoti. (AN 4.160 — A ii 147)
- *idha* here · *bhikkhave* monks (voc. pl.) · *bhikkhū* monks (nom. pl.) · *duggahitaṁ* badly grasped, poorly learned · *suttantaṁ* discourse · *pariyāpuṇanti* they learn thoroughly, they master · *dunnikkhittehi* badly set down (inst. pl.) · *padabyañjanehi* with words and phrases · *dunnikkhittassa* of what is badly set down (gen. sg.) · *padabyañjanassa* of the words and phrases · *attho* the meaning · *pi* too · *dunnayo* hard to draw out · *hoti* is
- Here, monks, the monks master a discourse **badly grasped**, **with its words and phrases badly set down**. And of words and phrases badly set down, **the meaning too is hard to draw out**.

The commentary reads the second sentence in the narrowest and most practical way available: when the wording has been set down out of order, the explanation cannot be drawn out of it and stated.[^dunnaya] That is a claim about transmission, not scholarship, and it makes a grammar of this language more than a pedantic exercise. The meaning is carried in the wording, in a language where one vowel of an ending decides who is doing what to whom, and if you cannot see the endings you cannot find out that they are wrong.

The modern evidence points the same way, which is less obvious than it sounds, because a generation of popular advice about language learning says the opposite. Norris and Ortega's meta-analysis of forty-nine studies found that focused instruction produces large gains, that explicit teaching outperforms implicit, and that the effects held at delayed testing.[^explicit] Those studies are of modern languages learned partly for conversation, and no comparable meta-analysis of classical-language teaching exists, so carrying the finding across to Pāli is an inference, not a result. It seems a safe one. Every condition under which explicit instruction pays best is present here at full strength: adult literate learners, a heavily inflected morphology, and a goal that is reading, not speech. A grammar-forward text is not a concession to how these things have always been done.

## How to study a classical language

The canon also describes the method, and it is more specific than one might expect. A monk masters the teaching, and then four things are said of what he has mastered. The four stages are in bold:

- Tassa te dhammā **sotānugatā** honti, **vacasā paricitā**, **manasānupekkhitā**, **diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā**. (AN 4.191 — A ii 185)
- *tassa* of him, for him (gen. sg.) · *te* those · *dhammā* teachings · *sotānugatā* followed by ear, listened to · *honti* are · *vacasā* by speech, verbally · *paricitā* rehearsed, made familiar · *manasānupekkhitā* mentally reconsidered, gone over in the mind · *diṭṭhiyā* by view · *suppaṭividdhā* well penetrated, thoroughly understood
- His teachings are **followed by ear**, **rehearsed aloud**, **gone over in the mind**, **well penetrated by view**.

Four stages, and they are four operations, not four intensities of one. Hearing is exposure. *Vacasā paricita*, made familiar by speech, is recitation; *paricita* is literally "piled up", built by accumulation. *Manasānupekkhita*, gone over in the mind, is review without the text in front of one. And *diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdha*, penetrated by view, is understanding, which the sequence puts fourth and not first.

The formula recurs across the canon, and it describes *sajjhāya*, "recitation, rehearsal": the tradition's technique for holding a text without writing, and the technique that preserved this canon through the centuries before it was written down. It was more than a devotional flourish laid on top of study. For most of this literature's history it *was* study.

That matters here because the modern evidence points at the same two things. Of the ten study techniques Dunlosky and colleagues reviewed in 2013, two came out rated high utility, and they were practice testing and distributed practice: testing yourself, and spreading the sessions out. A meta-analysis of the same ten techniques across 242 studies and 169,179 participants ranked those two first again.[^spacing] Recitation from memory is both at once, which is my own observation and not theirs: the recall is the test, and daily recitation sustained over years is distribution in an extreme form. I would not press the parallel further than that. The tradition was not doing cognitive psychology, and that its practice turns out to be well designed is a happy accident, not an argument.

A reference grammar teaches the system; only volume of reading builds a reader. This is the standing complaint about grammar-first training in every classical language, and it is fair. Two meta-analyses of extensive reading, one of 34 studies and one of 71 samples and nearly six thousand learners, find medium to large gains in comprehension, reading rate and vocabulary, and the second reports that adults gain more from it than children do.[^volume] So the grammar is not the destination, and finishing it is not the point at which reading begins.

There is a threshold to respect, though, and so the pathway in Appendix B is graded, not a list of good suttas. Nation's work on vocabulary coverage puts unassisted comprehension of ordinary written text at about 98% of the running words known; at 95% a reader meets an unknown word every twenty, which is decoding, not reading.[^coverage] Sutta Pāli is far more formulaic and repetitive than ordinary prose, so the effective threshold is almost certainly lower here, and by how much nobody has measured. The practical consequence stands either way. Reading text far above your coverage does not build fluency, and reading text a little above it does.

What follows for a reader of this book is concrete enough:

- **Recite the Pāli aloud.** Not silently. Vowel length and the doubled consonants are half the morphology, and they are invisible until they are pronounced.
- **Test before you look.** Cover the gloss, cover the answer key, and attempt the form first. The attempt teaches, and a failed attempt teaches more than a fluent read-through.
- **Space the repetitions.** A paradigm met on four separate days will hold; the same total time in one sitting will not.
- **Start reading before you feel ready, and read within your range.** Appendix B exists for both halves of that, and beginning it at the end of Part IV, not at the end of the book, turns the grammar into reading.
- **Do the cumulative reviews on time.** They are the only place in either volume where two chapters are made to interfere with each other, and the real errors appear there.

One more finding bears on why anyone finishes. In the largest meta-analysis of second-language motivation, a learner's vision of the reader they want to become correlated strongly with intended effort and only weakly with measured achievement.[^motivation] Read that carefully: motivation does not multiply ability. It predicts whether someone keeps turning up, which over an undertaking measured in years is most of what there is to predict. For a reader whose reason for learning Pāli is to meet the Buddha's words without an intermediary, that reason will still be there in year three.

And one caution, which the canon supplies too. The Buddha describes a monk who masters the discourses, spends his days on them, neglects seclusion and does not commit himself to inner calm; the verdict on him is *pariyattibahulo, no dhammavihārī*: "one keen on learning, not one who lives by the Dhamma" (AN 5.73 — A iii 86). The same is said in the same terms of one who spends the day teaching, and of one who spends it reciting.[^sajjhayabahulo] Grammar is a means. It is a good means, it is not the point, and the tradition that produced these texts said so first.

## What this book owes

The chapters began as **Bhikkhu Sunyo's course handouts**, the lessons of a Pāli course, still marked draft, which map onto the first fourteen chapters here and supplied their structure, much of their sequencing and a good deal of their explanation. Little of his wording survives the redrafting, and the debt is to something else: the decision about what a beginner needs to meet first, which is the hardest part of a grammar to get right and the part a reader never notices when it has been done well.

The paradigm tables in Appendix A are **Bhikkhu Ñāṇatusita's**, transcribed and verified cell by cell, and used under his permission for non-commercial distribution. The Pāli text is the **Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana** edition of the Vipassana Research Institute, with the Buddha Jayanti and Pali Text Society editions consulted wherever a spelling is in question. The **Digital Pāli Dictionary** stands behind every gloss. Appendix D names the rest.

None of them is responsible for what has been made of their work here.

[^dunnaya]: The Aṅguttara commentary glosses *dunnikkhittassa* as "badly set down, placed out of order" (*duṭṭhu nikkhittassa uppaṭipāṭiyā ṭhapitassa*) and *atthopi dunnayo hoti* as "the explanation cannot be drawn out and stated" (*aṭṭhakathā nīharitvā kathetuṁ na sakkā hoti*): Manorathapūraṇī, *Catukkanipāta-aṭṭhakathā*, on the Sugatavinaya Sutta (Mp III 137). PED, s.v. *naya*, gives *dunnaya* as "wrongly inferred, hard to be understood", citing this idiom; DPD carries the headword without a gloss.
[^explicit]: Norris, J. M. & Ortega, L. (2000). "Effectiveness of L2 Instruction: A Research Synthesis and Quantitative Meta-Analysis", *Language Learning* 50(3), 417–528. Forty-nine unique sample studies; focused instruction produced large target-oriented gains, explicit instruction outperformed implicit, and the effects held at delayed post-test.
[^spacing]: Dunlosky, J., Rawson, K. A., Marsh, E. J., Nathan, M. J. & Willingham, D. T. (2013). "Improving Students' Learning With Effective Learning Techniques", *Psychological Science in the Public Interest* 14(1), 4–58: practice testing and distributed practice the only two of ten rated high utility, with rereading and highlighting rated low. Donoghue, G. M. & Hattie, J. A. C. (2021). "A Meta-Analysis of Ten Learning Techniques", *Frontiers in Education* 6:581216: 242 studies, 1,619 effects, 169,179 participants, with distributed practice and practice testing again the most effective. Their own caution carries here: most of the studies measured factual or surface outcomes, so the ranking transfers to a paradigm more safely than to an interpretation.
[^volume]: Nakanishi, T. (2015). "A Meta-Analysis of Extensive Reading Research", *TESOL Quarterly* 49(1), 6–37 — 34 studies, 3,942 participants, *d* = 0.71 pre–post and *d* = 0.46 against controls. Jeon, E.-Y. & Day, R. R. (2016). "The Effectiveness of ER on Reading Proficiency: A Meta-Analysis", *Reading in a Foreign Language* 28(2), 246–265 — 71 samples from 49 studies, 5,919 participants, with a higher effect for adults than for children and adolescents.
[^coverage]: Nation, I. S. P. (2006). "How Large a Vocabulary Is Needed for Reading and Listening?", *Canadian Modern Language Review* 63(1), 59–82. The figure is for general written English, and the vocabulary size it implies there, 8,000 to 9,000 word families, cannot be set beside a count of Pāli surface forms, because the two are counting different things. Appendix B computes the curve over the four Nikāyas, 956,218 running words: half of everything the Buddha is recorded as saying is five hundred word-forms, and 98% of it is 36,578. Resolved to dictionary headwords and decomposed, that last figure comes to roughly eight thousand distinct words, the same order as the English one. The appendix carries the working and the error rate.
[^motivation]: Al-Hoorie, A. H. (2018). "The L2 Motivational Self System: A Meta-Analysis", *Studies in Second Language Learning and Teaching* 8(4), 721–754 — 39 samples, 32,078 learners. The "ideal L2 self" correlated with intended effort at *r* = .61 and with objective achievement at *r* = .20.
[^sajjhayabahulo]: AN 5.73 — A iii 87 (*paññattibahulo*, "one keen on describing") and AN 5.73 — A iii 87 (*sajjhāyabahulo*, "one keen on recitation"). The sutta's final term is *dhammavihārī*, one who lives by the Dhamma, which is what all four fall short of.

**Principal sources.** AN 4.191 — A ii 185 and AN 4.160 — A ii 147 for the canon's account of how the texts were learned and why the wording had to be exact, with AN 5.73 — A iii 86 for the caution that goes with it; the meta-analytic literature on instruction, extensive reading, retrieval and spacing (Norris and Ortega, Nakanishi, Jeon and Day, Nation, Dunlosky and colleagues, Donoghue and Hattie, Al-Hoorie) for what is actually known about how adults learn a language, each cited where it is used; Bodhi, *Reading the Buddha's Discourses in Pāli*, for the three-layer example this book presents its Pāli in.

## Script and Pronunciation

Pāli is a language that was spoken for centuries before anyone wrote it down, and it has no script of its own. That fact governs everything in this chapter. The alphabet below is a set of borrowings, not a Pāli invention; the spellings in the editions are the grammarians', not the Buddha's; and the pronunciation this chapter teaches is a reconstruction, better attested at some points than at others.

None of that makes the sounds unimportant. They matter more here than in a written language, because the texts were composed to be heard and held in memory, and a great deal of their structure (the pairing, the repetition, the metre of the verses) is audible. A reader who never says the words aloud reads half the text.

### The language before the script

The early discourses were carried orally for some centuries before being committed to writing, and when they were written the scribes used whatever script was to hand. Manuscripts survive in Sinhala, Burmese, Thai and Khmer letters, all of them descended from the old Indian Brāhmī, and a few in Brāhmī itself. None of these is *the* Pāli script; each is a local script pressed into service.

What they have in common is that they are not alphabets in the European sense. Each consonant sign carries an inherent *a*, so the sign transliterated *k* is read *ka* unless something marks otherwise, and so the tradition names the letters *ka*, *kha*, *ga*, not *k*, *kh*, *g*. The technical term for such a system is an **abugida**.[^abugida]

[^abugida]: Thānuttamo p. 14, correcting Gornall (2014: 511), who calls Pāli's writing a syllabary; the correction is Ānandajoti's, from a personal communication Thānuttamo reports. The distinction matters because a syllabary has an unanalysable sign per syllable, while an abugida builds the syllable out of a consonant sign and a vowel mark, as the *saṁyoga* rules below presuppose.

In the nineteenth century European scholars began transcribing Pāli into the Roman alphabet, and with the new letters came something the manuscripts had never had: spaces between words, capitals and full stops. That is a larger change than it sounds. A manuscript line runs unbroken, and dividing it is already an act of interpretation: the reader must decide where one word ends and the next begins, and in a language full of sandhi that is the hard part.

The homage that opens most recitations will serve to show it. Written the way a manuscript writes it, in Roman letters and without the division, it reads:

*namotassabhagavatoarahatosammāsambuddhassa*

And as the editions print it:

- Namo tassa bhagavato arahato sammāsambuddhassa. (MN 91 — M ii 140)
- *namo* homage · *tassa* to him · *bhagavato* to the Blessed One · *arahato* to the perfected one · *sammāsambuddhassa* to the fully awakened one
- Homage to him, the Blessed One, the perfected one, the fully awakened one.

Nothing has been added but the spaces, and the spaces are the whole difficulty. This book gives Pāli in Roman letters throughout, with the divisions the editors supply; the chapter on sandhi is about what happens at the joins those spaces conceal.

Roman is only one of the alphabets this sentence lives in, and it is the newest. Here is the same line, at the same place in the same edition, as it is set in Sinhala, in Thai and in Myanmar:[^script-sample]

| | |
|---|---|
| Sinhala | නමො තස්‌ස භගවතො අරහතො සම්‌මාසම්‌බුද්‌ධස්‌ස |
| Thai | ฯ นโม ตสฺส ภควโต อรหโต สมฺมาสมฺพุทฺธสฺสฯ |
| Myanmar | ။ နမော တဿ ဘဂဝတော အရဟတော သမ္မာသမ္ဗုဒ္ဓဿ။ |
| Roman | Namo tassa bhagavato arahato sammāsambuddhassa |

[^script-sample]: All four are the homage that opens the Majjhimapaṇṇāsa, the middle fifty suttas of the Majjhima Nikāya, in the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana of the Vipassana Research Institute. The Institute publishes that one edition in fifteen scripts, so the four are the same text at the same place, and the Roman line is the text this book quotes from throughout. The Thai *paiyannoi* (ฯ) opening and closing the line is that tradition's punctuation, not part of the Pāli.

Read the rows against each other and the abugida shows itself. The *-mo* of *namo* is one consonant sign with a vowel mark on it in all three Asian scripts and two separate letters only in Roman. In Thai the mark for *o* is written to the left of the consonant it is pronounced after, which shows as clearly as anything could that these are not alphabets running left to right one sound at a time. The clusters of *sammāsambuddhassa* behave the same way: each is a single stacked or joined shape, where Roman writes two characters and trusts the reader to know it is one sound.

Of the four, only Roman is an alphabet, and its apparatus of macrons, dots and digraphs is the price of making an alphabet serve as an abugida. Hold on to that, because the next section is a list of forty-one letters, and the list is a European convenience, not something the tradition would recognise as the structure of its own writing.

One thing has to be said before the list, because everything after it depends on the answer. The orthography of the printed editions is not a transcript of ancient speech. Oberlies §2 puts it flatly: our texts follow the rules of the Pāli grammarians of the twelfth century, and that orthography is "historical and not phonetical": it records how a word was understood to be built, not how it was said. So a spelling in this book is evidence about a word's structure and only indirectly about its sound. Where this chapter reconstructs a pronunciation, the reconstruction matters, and where the sources disagree about one, this chapter says so instead of choosing.

### The alphabet

The Pali Text Society's dictionary sets out its alphabet in this order, and it is the order this book follows:[^ped-alphabet]

*a ā i ī u ū e o ṁ — k kh g gh ṅ — c ch j jh ñ — ṭ ṭh ḍ ḍh ṇ — t th d dh n — p ph b bh m — y r l ḷ v s h*

[^ped-alphabet]: Rhys Davids and Stede, *PTS Pāli–English Dictionary*, front matter: "Pāli alphabet used in this dictionary". Two notes there: the original print uses **ŋ** for the niggahīta, and it "is not distinguished in the original print" from *n*; see the box on *ṅ* below.

Forty-one letters, then. That count is the most often given, and on it the tradition is least united.

Eight of the forty-one are vowels (*a, ā, i, ī, u, ū, e, o*), and on this point alone every source agrees.[^vowel-inventory] The remainder divide into five groups of five, each group sharing a place in the mouth, plus seven that belong to no group, plus the niggahīta.

[^vowel-inventory]: Oberlies §3.1, p. 17, citing Saddanīti IV 1.1.2; Perniola §1, p. 1; Thānuttamo p. 15. Oberlies adds what Pāli has *lost* against Old Indo-Aryan: the vowels *ṛ*, *ṝ* and *ḷ*, and the diphthongs *ai* and *au*. The lost vowels reappear in Pāli as *a*, *i* or *u* (Sanskrit *kṛta* is Pāli *kata*, *śṛgāla* is *sigāla*, *pṛcchati* is *pucchati*: Perniola §1b, p. 2), so a Sanskritist meets many Pāli words as familiar forms with an unexpected vowel.

The tradition calls the five groups the *vaggā*, and names each after its first member: the *ka*-group, the *ca*-group, the *ṭa*-group, the *ta*-group and the *pa*-group. The seven that belong to none of them (*y, r, l, v, s, h, ḷ*) it calls simply *avaggā*, ungrouped.

Because the Roman alphabet has only twenty-six letters, the extra sounds are written with diacritics (the dots and bars) and with digraphs, pairs of Roman characters standing for a single Pāli sound. Every combination with *h* listed in the alphabet above is a digraph; every other combination with *h* is two letters. So *dukkha* has five letters and not six, because *kh* is one; but *amhi* has four, because *mh* is an *m* followed by an *h*, not a digraph. The tradition makes the same point from the other end: the aspirates are not conjunct consonants, because each is a single sound.[^samyoga]

[^samyoga]: Thānuttamo p. 14, citing Kaccāyana 2–3 and 6–7 and Ānandajoti (2004: 16). A *saṁyoga* is a cluster of consonants with no vowel between them, and in the traditional analysis it includes geminates: *kkh*, *dda*, *ss* as well as *ndh*, *mba*.

Two rules govern which consonants may form such a cluster, and between them they account for most of the clusters a reader will meet. Only the first and second members of a group may combine, and only the third and fourth, and only in that order: *k* with *kh* but never *kh* with *k*. The nasal that closes each group may attach to any member of its own group, including itself.[^conjunct-rules] That is the general fact behind the distribution of *ṅ* below.

[^conjunct-rules]: Thānuttamo p. 15, after Ñāṇadhaja and Rūpasiddhi. He records one exception, on Yindee's authority: *ṅ* alone cannot geminate with itself, so no Pāli word contains *ṅṅ*.

> **Variants of *ṅ***
>
> The letter *ṅ* occurs only before *k*, *kh*, *g* or *gh*, and those four are never preceded by *n*. The distribution is therefore predictable in both directions, and some works exploit that by writing simply *n* and leaving the reader to infer the *ṅ* from what follows: *sangha* for *saṅgha*. The PTS dictionary does this, and says so in its own front matter.
>
> The reason is the conjunct rule above: a group nasal attaches to members of its own group, so a nasal before a guttural can only be the guttural nasal. Leaving it unwritten loses nothing and gains nothing. This book writes *ṅ*.

A small difference of letters makes a different word, and the differences that trip an English speaker are those English does not use: the length of a vowel, the doubling of a consonant, the retroflex against the dental, the aspirate against the plain.

| | | | |
|---|---|---|---|
| *dhamma* teaching, thing | *dhama* blower | *dāma* bond | *damma* tameable |
| *aṭṭha* eight | *attha* meaning | *atta* self | *atha* then |

These are given in their dictionary forms, which is how a reader will meet them in a glossary; in a text they will be inflected.[^minimal-pairs]

[^minimal-pairs]: In the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana, *dhama* appears only in the commentaries (2 in the aṭṭhakathā, 4 in the grammatical literature) and *damma* is thin (1 in the root texts). Both are kept: the pair teaches a contrast between real words, and a word that survives only in the commentaries is still a word the reader will meet. *Dāma* does not occur as a bare stem anywhere: it is a noun the canon uses in oblique cases, and *dāmena* 'with a rope' has nine occurrences in the root texts; DPD's own illustration is *ekena dāmena vā yottena vā*, 'bound by a single rope or tether'.

> **Variant spellings**
>
> Several words are spelled more than one way, and the variation runs between editions of the canon and sometimes within a single manuscript. The commonest kinds are these.
>
> **Retroflex against dental**, especially *ṭ* for *t* and *ḷ* for *l*. The clearest case is *anelagala* 'clearly enunciated', an epithet of good speech, which has two *l*s and therefore four possible spellings, and all four are printed. Counted across the three romanised editions:
>
> | | Buddha Jayanti | Pali Text Society | Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana | …and in its own footnotes |
> |---|---|---|---|---|
> | *anelagal-* | 41 | 10 | 40 | — |
> | *anelagaḷ-* | 2 | 23 | 24 | 3 |
> | *aneḷagal-* | 8 | 9 | — | 3 |
> | *aneḷagaḷ-* | 2 | — | — | 1 |
>
> No edition is consistent with itself and the three do not agree with one another. Look at the last column: the two spellings the Burmese text never prints are both recorded in its own apparatus, attributed there to the Sinhalese and PTS readings. Every editor knew the alternatives and each chose one.[^ela-apparatus] That the variation belongs to the letter and not to this one word is shown by *aneḷamūga* 'intelligent', where it runs the other way: *aneḷamūgo* 20 times against *anelamūgā* twice.[^ela-etymology]
>
> **Long against short vowels**: *cātumāsaṁ* beside *catumāsaṁ*, both canonical.
>
> **Single against double consonants**, especially after *e* and *o*: *upekhā* beside *upekkhā* 'equanimity'. The pair shows what kind of thing this box is describing:
>
> | | Buddha Jayanti | Pali Text Society | Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana |
> |---|---|---|---|
> | *upekkhā* | 565 | 106 | 5,246 |
> | *upekhā* | 540 | 460 | 4 |
>
> The Burmese editors chose the double consonant and the European editors the single one; the Sri Lankan text is divided almost evenly against itself. The variation is real, and it is between editions, so to check one edition and find nothing is to learn nothing.
>
> **The alternation of *ava* with *o*, and of *aya* with *e***: *avakāso* beside *okāso*, *sayana* beside *sena*. These are the residue of a sound change, not scribal at all; see "The vowels" below.
>
> None of these is more correct than the others.

[^ela-apparatus]: The dashes are not zeros. The Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana prints *aneḷagalāya* nowhere in its running text, and carries it in its own apparatus at the line quoted below. The text there reads *anelagalāya*, and the note beside it gives *aneḷagalāya* for the Sinhalese and Pali Text Society editions and *anelagaḷāya* for some manuscripts. Every editor knew about the other spellings and each chose one. The Pali Text Society prints *aneḷagalāya* at DN 4 — D i 114 and records the other two in a note of its own; the Buddha Jayanti prints *anelagalāya*. Three editions, three choices, one word.

[^ela-etymology]: DPD derives *anelagala* as *na* > *an* + *eḷā* + *gala*, literally 'free from dripping saliva', so the retroflex *ḷ* is the etymological form and the *l* spelling is the innovation, even though the *l* spelling is the commoner one in this edition. The same word appears at Dhp-style *n'elā* in DPD's illustration of *porī*.

The word can be seen in place here, in a brahmin's description of the Buddha's speech:

- …poriyā vācāya samannāgato vissaṭṭhāya anelagalāya atthassa viññāpaniyā… (DN 4 — D i 114)
- *poriyā* refined · *vācāya* with speech · *samannāgato* endowed · *vissaṭṭhāya* distinct · *anelagalāya* clearly enunciated · *atthassa* of the meaning · *viññāpaniyā* conveying
- …endowed with refined speech, distinct and clearly enunciated, conveying the meaning…

Every term in that list is about *sound*, which is a fair indication of how much the tradition cared about it. The sentence in the edition begins *Bhavañhi soṇadaṇḍo*, 'For you, sir Soṇadaṇḍa', and that first word is *bhavaṁ* + *hi*, with the niggahīta assimilated to *ñ* before the *h*. The rule behind it is below.

### How the sounds are made

The early grammarians analysed each sound by three parameters: its **place** (*ṭhāna*), the point in the mouth where it is formed; its **instrument** (*karaṇa*), the part of the tongue or lip that forms it; and its **mode** (*payatana*), the action of the breath and the voice meanwhile.[^three-parameters] Modern descriptions usually collapse the first two, and for most sounds nothing is lost. For three of them something is.

[^three-parameters]: Thānuttamo p. 17, after Rūpasiddhi and Ñāṇadhaja. The table below keeps place across the rows and mode across the columns, in the ordinary way, and names the instrument only for the three sounds where it changes the answer.

The Pāli letters by place and mode of pronunciation.

| place | vowels: short | vowels: long | voiceless plain | voiceless aspirate | voiced plain | voiced aspirate | nasal | other |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| guttural (*kaṇṭhaja*) | *a* | *ā* | *k* | *kh* | *g* | *gh* | *ṅ* | *h* |
| palatal (*tāluja*) | *i* | *ī* | *c* | *ch* | *j* | *jh* | *ñ* | *y* |
| retroflex (*muddhaja*) | | | *ṭ* | *ṭh* | *ḍ* | *ḍh* | *ṇ* | *r*, *ḷ* |
| dental (*dantaja*) | | | *t* | *th* | *d* | *dh* | *n* | *l*, *s* |
| labial (*oṭṭhaja*) | *u* | *ū* | *p* | *ph* | *b* | *bh* | *m* | |
| guttural-palatal (*kaṇṭhatāluja*) | *e* | | | | | | | |
| guttural-labial (*kaṇṭhoṭṭhaja*) | *o* | | | | | | | |
| dental-labial (*dantoṭṭhaja*) | | | | | | | | *v* |
| nasal (*nāsikaṭṭhāna*) | | | | | | *ṁ* | | |

The five principal places run from the back of the mouth forward. The **gutturals**, or velars, are made at the back, near the throat. The **palatals** are made at the hard palate, the middle of the roof of the mouth. The **retroflexes** (the tradition calls them *muddhaja*, 'head-born', and older grammars call them cerebrals) are made with the tip of the tongue curled back towards the palate. The **dentals** are made with the tongue against the back of the front teeth. The **labials** are made at the lips.

Three sounds have two places, not one, and the tradition names each of them. *E* is guttural-palatal and *o* is guttural-labial, a statement of what they are, not a curiosity of classification: *e* is the merger of *a* with *i*, and *o* the merger of *a* with *u*, so each keeps both of its parents' places. *V* is dental-labial, made with the lower lip against the upper teeth. Filing *v* with the labials, as most modern tables do, is close enough for a first approximation, but the grammarians say otherwise.

The niggahīta has its own place, the nose, and it is discussed on its own below.

#### The vowels

Of the eight vowels, *ā*, *ī* and *ū* are long and *a*, *i* and *u* are short: twice the duration and half of it. The tradition measures this in *mattā*, and defines the unit concretely: one *mattā* is the time it takes to pronounce a short vowel, which it glosses as the time it takes to blink once. A long vowel is two.[^matta] A consonant on its own is half a *mattā*.

[^matta]: Thānuttamo pp. 15–16, citing Kaccāyana 4, Rūpasiddhi and Ñāṇadhaja, and the commentary to the Parivāra (Sp VII 1399, which he cites by the page of the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana edition). He records one refinement before the metre chapter: in verse a short vowel may be given a *mattā* and a half. Oberlies §3.2, p. 17, uses the same unit under its Sanskrit name, *mora*.

*E* and *o* are the awkward pair. They are long by nature, and shorten when a cluster of consonants follows: long in *vane* 'in the forest' and *putto* 'a son', where nothing follows; long in *kāmesu* and *odanaṁ*, where a single consonant follows; but short in *mettā* 'loving-kindness' and *bhāseyya* 'he should speak', where a doubled consonant follows and the vowel has to give up its length to make room. Perniola states the same rule from the syllable's side: *e* and *o* are long at the end of a syllable, as in *e-so* and *do-so*, and short when they are closed by a consonant, as in *met-tā* and *pot-thakaṁ*.

That much is settled. Where the rule stops is not.

> **How many letters, and how long is *e*?**
>
> Grammars differ, and the difference is old. It depends on two questions, and answering them differently gives a different count of the alphabet.
>
> *Is *ḷh* a letter?* It is written as two characters even in the Asian scripts, but it stands for a single sound. Perniola counts it among his liquids and so reaches **42 letters**: eight vowels, thirty-three consonants, and the niggahīta counted separately. The PTS dictionary's list and Kaccāyana's both omit it and reach **41**.
>
> *Are short *e* and short *o* the same letters as long *e* and long *o*?* Kaccāyana holds that *e* and *o* are simply long. Buddhappiya, in the *Padarūpasiddhi* and within Kaccāyana's own tradition, holds that they shorten before geminates but not before conjuncts formed with a group nasal, so *bhāseyya* has a short *e*, but *meṇḍo* 'ram' and *soṇḍo* 'drunkard' have long ones, because *ṇḍ* is a group nasal with a stop of its own class and not a geminate. Moggallāna rejects the compromise: a sound cannot be long and then, in certain places, short, so short *e* and short *o* must be different letters altogether. Counting them separately gives him **43**.[^moggallana]
>
> This book follows the majority and counts forty-one, and teaches the rule as Perniola and Buddhappiya give it. A different count in another grammar is not an error.

[^moggallana]: Thānuttamo p. 16, the only source consulted that states all three positions and attributes each. He names the class at p. 15 instead of leaving it as "other clusters": "Exceptions for *e* and *o*: occurrences before conjuncts with end-group nasals are long".  Gornall (2014: 519) for Moggallāna's argument. Perniola's count is at his p. 1, the PTS list in the dictionary's front matter, Kaccāyana's at Kacc 5. *Meṇḍo* and *soṇḍo* are both canonical, 4 and 2 occurrences respectively in the root texts.

Two of the vowels are historically compounds, and the fact is still visible. *E* arises from *a* plus *i* and *o* from *a* plus *u*, so *upa* + *ikkhati* gives *upekkhati*, and the stock phrase for a well-provisioned place, *satiṇakaṭṭhodakaṁ* 'with grass, wood and water', has *kaṭṭha* + *udaka* joined as *-kaṭṭhodaka-*. The same merger, running in reverse, produces the *ava* ~ *o* and *aya* ~ *e* variants in the box above: *avakāso* and *okāso* are the same word at two stages of one change. Sanskrit spells the older stage (*maitrī* for *mettā*, *auṣadha* for *osadha* 'medicine'), so the correspondence is easiest to see from that side.[^gliding]

[^gliding]: Perniola §1b, p. 2, calls *e* and *o* diphthongs and derives them from *a-i* / *ā-i* and *a-u* / *ā-u*; the tradition's *kaṇṭhatāluja* and *kaṇṭhoṭṭhaja* say the same thing in the language of articulation. Both are now usually pronounced as pure vowels, not glides, and it is likely that both pronunciations existed.

#### The consonants

Twenty-five of the thirty-three consonants sit in the five groups, and within each group the five members differ by mode alone. The **voiced** are made with the vocal cords vibrating and the **voiceless** without; the **aspirates** are made with a distinct pulse of breath after the closure and the **non-aspirates** without; the **nasals** are made with the breath escaping through the nose, not the mouth. Read across a row of the table and the five columns are: voiceless plain, voiceless aspirate, voiced plain, voiced aspirate, nasal.

The seven ungrouped consonants are *y*, *r*, *l*, *v*, *s*, *h* and *ḷ*. *Y* and *v* are semivowels, and the label means something: they are what *i* and *u* become when a vowel follows, a fact the next chapter uses constantly. *S* is the only sibilant; Pāli has lost Sanskrit's *ś* and *ṣ*. *H* is doubly classified: on its own it is a guttural, and when it appears as the second element of a digraph, or after *y*, *r*, *l* or *v*, the tradition calls it *uraja*, 'chest-born', and treats it as the mark of aspiration, not as a consonant in its own right.

Two cross-cutting distinctions matter for the chapter on sandhi and are best met here. Perniola groups the first two columns of each row together with *s* as **hard** and the rest as **soft**, which is voicing under another name; and he ranks the consonants by strength (mutes, then the sibilant, then the nasals, then *l*, *v*, *y*, *r*), because when two consonants collide the stronger survives.

No Pāli word ends in a consonant. Every word ends in a vowel or in the niggahīta. That is the fact that makes external sandhi tractable: there are only so many ways two words can meet.

### The niggahīta

The niggahīta is written *ṁ* in this book, *ṃ* in many others, and *ŋ* in the older ones. It is one sound, not three, and the three symbols are three transliteration conventions: *ṁ* is the ISO 15919 form, *ṃ* the IAST form used by the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana edition and by the later Pali Text Society volumes, and *ŋ* the form the original PTS dictionary prints.[^niggahita-symbols] The choice requires only consistency, and this book is consistent.

[^niggahita-symbols]: Thānuttamo pp. 23–24 sets out the schemes, including the ASCII conventions (ITRANS `M`, `N`, `.m`; Velthuis `.m`) that a reader will meet in older electronic texts. The PTS dictionary's front matter states its own use of ŋ. In the Asian scripts the niggahīta is a diacritic, not a letter at all, usually a dot or a small circle written above or beside the syllable.

Its name is a description. *Niggahīta* is the past participle of *niggaṇhāti*, 'to press down, to restrain', and the tradition explains that what is restrained is the organ of articulation: the mouth is not fully opened, so the breath escapes partly through the nose.[^niggahita-etymology] It never stands alone, and it never follows a long vowel: it is founded on, and takes up, the three short vowels *a*, *i* and *u*.

[^niggahita-etymology]: Thānuttamo p. 24, quoting the *Padarūpasiddhi*: the letter is called *niggahīta* because the *karaṇa* is restrained (*niggahīta*) by an unopened mouth (*mukhenāvivaṭena*), and because it rests on (*nissāya*) the short vowels *a*, *i*, *u*. The traditional grammarians list it among the **consonants** (Kacc 2–3, 6), not as a third category beside vowels and consonants.

Before a consonant, the niggahīta is regularly replaced in writing by the nasal of that consonant's own group: the homorganic nasal, made at the same place as the sound that follows.[^homorganic]

| following sound | niggahīta becomes | example |
|---|---|---|
| *k, kh, g, gh* | *ṅ* | *saṁ* + *kappa* → *saṅkappa* |
| *c, ch, j, jh* | *ñ* | *taṁ* + *ca* → *tañca* |
| *ṭ, ṭh, ḍ, ḍh* | *ṇ* | *saṁ* + *ṭhāna* → *saṇṭhāna* |
| *t, th, d, dh* | *n* | *saṁ* + *nisīdati* → *sannisīdati* |
| *p, ph, b, bh* | *m* | *ahaṁ* + *api* → *ahampi* |

[^homorganic]: Thānuttamo pp. 23–24, citing Kaccāyana 31, 466 and 537. The table covers the five groups; *h*, which belongs to none of them, takes the palatal nasal: *evaṁ* + *hi* gives *evañhi*, 329 occurrences in the root texts, and *bhavaṁ* + *hi* gives the *bhavañhi* that opens the example above (DN 4 — D i 114).

That much is orthography, and it is not always applied: editions often leave the niggahīta written and let the reader supply the nasal. What the niggahīta actually *sounded* like is a different question, and a harder one.

> **Grammars differ: what the niggahīta was**
>
> Two accounts are current and neither can be dismissed.
>
> On the first, the niggahīta is a **nasalised vowel**: *aṁ*, *iṁ* and *uṁ* are the vowels of French *genre*, *vin* and *un*, and the symbol marks nasality, not a segment of its own. Thānuttamo takes this view, with Collins, Geiger, Deokar and Levman behind him, and adds an argument from the neighbours: nasalised vowels are native to the Dravidian and Munda languages spoken in the region, so the feature would not have been foreign.
>
> On the second, the two things are distinct and the scripts cannot show it. Oberlies distinguishes a vowel followed by an *anusvāra*, which is a pure nasal sound after a vowel that is **not** itself nasalised, from a truly nasalised vowel, the tradition's *sānunāsika*. The first should belong to long syllables and the second to short ones; but no script of the Pāli tradition has a sign for the second, so the printed editions confuse them.
>
> One point is not in dispute, and a reader can check it: a syllable closed by a niggahīta is **heavy**. That is a fact about the metre of the verses, not a reconstruction, and the next section builds on it.

The living traditions have settled the question differently from one another and from both accounts above: Sri Lanka and Thailand pronounce a final niggahīta as *ng*, Burma as *m*. Neither is likely to be the ancient value, and neither is wrong to use. The point is made rather sharply in the Vinaya commentary's directions for giving the refuges: *buddhaṁ saraṇaṁ gacchāmi* may be given as one connected utterance with the ends nasalised, or broken up with the *m* fully sounded, and both are valid.[^tisarana]

[^tisarana]: Thānuttamo p. 25, quoting the commentary on how the refuges are to be given (Sp V 969). He supplies the tradition's contrast term a page earlier, at p. 24, from a different commentarial passage (the Parivāra commentary on *pattakallaṁ* against *pattakallā*, Sp VII 1400), where *vimutta* 'released' names a vowel spoken with the mouth open and no nasalisation, the direct opposite of *niggahīta*, 'restrained'. That a commentary on monastic procedure should turn on the distinction is the best evidence there is that it was audible.

### Syllables and their weight

Dividing a word into syllables is needed for pronunciation, and needed again for the verses. The working rules are three.

A break falls **between two adjacent consonants**: *at·tā*, *duk·khaṁ*, *am·hi*. A break falls **before a single consonant**: *a·ku·sa·lo*, *bha·ga·vā*. And a digraph is a single consonant, so *adhi* divides *a·dhi* and not *ad·hi*, while *amhi* (where *mh* is two letters) divides *am·hi* and not *a·mhi*. The same goes for *ḷh*: *daḷha* divides *da·ḷha*.

Every syllable is then either **light** or **heavy**. A syllable is light if it ends in a short vowel, and heavy in every other case: if it ends in a long vowel, or in a consonant, or in the niggahīta. A heavy syllable takes about twice as long to say as a light one, and it is this alternation, not stress, that gives the chanting its rhythm.

Behind the definition is a law with consequences. A syllable may not hold more than two *mattā*. A short vowel is one, a long vowel two, and a vowel closed by a consonant is two as well, so a long vowel in a closed syllable would make three, and Pāli does not allow it.[^mora-law] The pressure has to escape somewhere, and it escapes in three regular ways, all of which a reader will meet constantly:

[^mora-law]: Oberlies §3.2, pp. 17–18, who states it as the 'law of *mora*'; Thānuttamo p. 15 gives the same law in the tradition's terms and notes that it is a strong tendency, not an exceptionless rule: *svākkhāto* 'well proclaimed', a long vowel before a cluster, occurs 136 times in the root texts.

A long vowel in an open syllable may shorten while the following consonant doubles, the total staying at two: *dīyati* beside *diyyati* 'is given', *dāṭhā* beside *daṭṭhā* 'fang'. A short vowel in a closed syllable may lengthen while the cluster simplifies, which is the same trade run backwards: *kattabbaṁ* beside *kātabbaṁ* 'to be done', *kattuṁ* beside *kātuṁ* 'to do'. And a nasal may be exchanged for vowel length, since a short vowel plus a nasal comes to the same two *mattā* as a long vowel alone, and so Sanskrit *viṁśati* 'twenty' is Pāli *vīsati*, and Sanskrit *siṁha* 'lion' is Pāli *sīha*.[^three-changes]

[^three-changes]: Perniola §3, pp. 4–5, who derives the first two from the two-*mattā* ceiling and alone among the sources read for this chapter presents them as one phenomenon, not as separate lists; his own examples for the third are not attested in the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana and are replaced here with Oberlies's. Oberlies treats the same material historically, under *metathesis quantitatum* (§3.3), compensatory lengthening (§3.4), where *kātuṁ* < *kartum* is his, and the replacement of a long vowel by *-Vṁ-* (§3.5), where *vīsati* < *viṁśati* and *sīha* < *siṁha* are his, together with the intra-Pāli pair *mahisa* ~ *mahiṁso* 'buffalo'. Of that last pair only *mahiṁso* is canonical in this edition, so it is left in the footnote.

None of this has to be memorised. It has to be recognised, so that a form met in a text is seen as the same word in a different guise, not as a new one.

> **Where the rules do not settle it**
>
> Some clusters may be divided either way, and no simple rule decides: *a·brah·ma* or *ab·rah·ma*, and likewise with *by*, *vy*, *dv*, *nh* and *tv*. In prose it makes no difference to the sense. In verse the metre decides, and the division exists for the metre; that is the business of the chapter on metre.

The recollection of the Buddha will show the weights in place. The syllable dots are added here; heavy syllables are in bold, light ones plain.

i·ti·pi **so** bha·ga·**vā** a·ra·**haṁ** **sam·mā·sam·bud·dho** **vij·jā**·ca·ra·ṇa·**sam·pan·no** su·ga·**to** **lo**·ka·vi·**dū** a·**nut**·ta·**ro** pu·ri·sa·**dam**·ma·**sā**·ra·thi **sat·thā** **de**·va·ma·**nus·sā·naṁ** **bud·dho** bha·ga·**vā**. (AN 6.10 — A iii 285)

"Such is the Blessed One: perfected, fully awakened, accomplished in knowledge and conduct, holy, knower of the world, supreme guide for those fit to be trained, teacher of gods and humans, awakened, blessed."

Two features stand out there. The heavy syllables cluster at the ends of the epithets and in their compounded cores, so the line moves in bursts, not evenly, and that makes it possible to chant. And *de-* in *devamanussānaṁ* is heavy, though only a single *v* follows it: the *e* is long because the syllable is open, and taking it as short produces a limp where the line has none.

Two last practical points. Double consonants are pronounced double, as in English *unnecessary*, *not to*, *say yes*, not as the single consonants they look like to an English eye. And stress is not a feature of Pāli in the way it is of English. There is no consensus on where an ancient speaker put it, and probably the knowledge is simply lost; syllable length carries the timing of the line, as stress does in English. Give every syllable its proper length and you will be understood, whatever you do about stress.

### What the chapter is for

Almost everything above is a description of sound, in a book about a language nobody speaks and a canon most people now meet on a page. It is fair to ask why it comes first.

The answer is that in Pāli the sound *is* the morphology. Vowel length distinguishes a nominative plural from an accusative singular; a doubled consonant is the difference between an active verb and a passive one; the niggahīta is the ending of half the cases in the language. An English reader's eye slides over all three, because none of them is meaningful in English, and to miss *dhammā* against *dhammaṁ* is to miss more than a nicety of pronunciation. It is to miss the case.

This is also the one chapter whose subject the tradition itself treated as a discipline. These texts were held for centuries by people who knew them as sound, and the *sajjhāya* that carried them was exact about quantity because the meaning depended on it. Reading aloud is therefore not a devotional addition to the study of this grammar. It is the fastest way to notice that you have read an ending wrong.

**Exercises for this chapter are in the workbook**, under "Exercises: Script and Pronunciation", with worked answers at the back of that volume.

**Principal sources.** Thānuttamo for the indigenous account of the letters and their articulation, and for the disagreements inside the tradition; Perniola chapters I–II for the description of the sound system and for what follows from syllable weight; Oberlies §§2–3 for the orthography of the received texts and for the law of *mora*.

## Sandhi

*Sandhi* is what happens where two sounds meet. The word means 'junction', and the phenomenon is not peculiar to Pāli: English does it constantly, in *isn't* and *I'll* and in the *beauty* that becomes *beauti-* before *-ful*. In Pāli it becomes a problem, not a curiosity, because Pāli writes the result and not the parts. Meet *esevanto* without knowing that three words are inside it, and there is no way to look any of them up.

So this chapter is less about a set of rules than about taking a written form apart. A reader does that dozens of times on any page of the canon, and no other skill so reliably separates those who can work through a sutta from those who cannot.

Two things have to be said before any of the patterns. The joins fall into four kinds, and the four can be worked out instead of memorised. And very often nothing happens at all. A reader is least prepared for that outcome, because a chapter full of rules trains the expectation that a rule must have applied.

### Junction inside a word and between words

The tradition divides sandhi in two, and the division is the right place to start because the two behave differently.

***Akkharasandhi***, 'junction of letters', happens **inside a single word**: where a prefix meets a root, where a root meets a suffix, and at every join in a compound.[^akkhara-pada] Every compound has at least one:

- *ariya* + *sacca* → *ariyasacca* 'noble truth'
- *na* + *ariya* → *anariya* 'ignoble'
- *saṁ* + *yutta* → *saññutta* 'connected'

[^akkhara-pada]: Thānuttamo p. 26, who gives the pair *akkharasandhi* / *padasandhi* after Gair and Karunatillake and Nhu Lien. Perniola divides the same material between two chapters instead of by two names (his ch. II §5 handles the internal joins as part of word-building, his ch. XVII the external ones). That comes to the same distinction, and it shows that internal sandhi belongs to morphology. Most of it is treated in this book under compounds and word formation; this chapter keeps what a reader needs in order to undo a join.

***Padasandhi***, 'junction of words', happens **between two separate words** that are habitually spoken together, and the editions write them joined:

- *na* + *atthi* → *natthi* 'there is not'
- *seyyathā* + *pi* → *seyyathāpi* 'just as'
- *tassa* + *iti* → *tassāti* 'of him, thus'

Perniola makes a point here that is easy to miss. In Sanskrit, external sandhi is the general rule of the sentence: words are joined as a matter of course. In Pāli they are not. Only a much narrower set is joined (adverbs, prepositions, pronouns, numerals, the forms of the verb *as* 'to be', and whatever else habit has run together), because a speaker pronounces those without a break.[^external-restricted] Pāli external sandhi is a fact about particular words, not a rule about sentences.

[^external-restricted]: Perniola §230, p. 285: "In building up a sentence in Sanskrit the words are joined together according to the rules of external sandhi. In Pali the words are kept separate. But as in a sentence the adverbs, the prepositions, the pronouns, the numerals, the forms of the verb *as* — are usually pronounced together with a preceding or a following word, they undergo some changes." He adds that longer words habitually used together are joined too, giving *uṭṭhāya āsanā* → *uṭṭhāyāsanā* 'having risen from the seat'.

Because there is no single scheme for showing where a join is, editors mark them variously or not at all, with an apostrophe, a hyphen, or a circumflex over the surviving vowel:

- *na* + *atthi* written *n'atthi*
- *puna* + *eti* written *puna-r-eti*
- *na* + *ahaṁ* written *nâhaṁ*

None of these is standard, and a reader will meet editions that use one, another or none. Recognising a join is therefore a matter of experience, not typography, and that is the reason this chapter exists.

### When nothing happens

Two vowels meeting do not have to combine. The tradition gives the case a name of its own, ***pakatisandhi***, 'natural junction', where the structural pattern is simply retained, and lists the environments in which it is the expected outcome.[^pakati] Oberlies arrives at the same place from the other direction: a hiatus, he says, is "generally permitted (including in compounds)", and the interesting question is what *bridges* one when a bridge appears.

[^pakati]: Thānuttamo p. 32, with Kaccāyana 23 and 24. His seven environments: a vowel before a consonant; a vowel before a vowel; *i* and *u* before a vowel-initial verb, and before any verb; a vowel before a vocative; a final long vowel not followed by *iti* and not in a compound; and a vowel before a particle beginning with anything other than *a*, *i* or *e*. Oberlies §25, p. 124, opens with the general permission.

The most famous line in the Dhammapada is an instance, and shows how ordinary the case is:

- Manopubbaṅgamā dhammā, manoseṭṭhā manomayā; (Dhp 1 — Dhp p. 1)
- *manopubbaṅgamā* < *mano* mind + *pubbaṅgama* going before · *dhammā* mental states · *manoseṭṭhā* < *mano* + *seṭṭha* chief · *manomayā* < *mano* + *maya* made of
- Mind precedes mental states; mind is their chief, they are made by mind.[^dhamma-gloss]

[^dhamma-gloss]: *Dhammā* here is the contested word, and the gloss above is DPD's, which files this verse under the sense 'mental states' specifically. The Dhammapada commentary reads it narrowly, as the three immaterial aggregates (feeling, perception and formations) with mind as their forerunner (Dhp-a I 22), and Bodhi reads it more widely still: translating the prose parallel at AN 1.56 he has 'qualities', and takes the statement, "in line with Dhp 1 and 2", to be ethical, not psychological: one decides to act before one acts. Nothing in this chapter turns on the choice; it is flagged because a grammar has no business settling it in passing.

Three compounds in six words, and in every one the *-o* of *mano* stands unchanged in front of the following consonant. No rule applies here, and none did.

The practical consequence is the mistake this chapter most wants to prevent, and it is best stated flatly. Meeting two vowels side by side is not evidence that a rule has failed to apply. In *ko imaṁ*, *atha kho āyasmā* and *kassapa etaṁ* the vowels sit next to one another and stay there. Learn the patterns below as things that *may* happen, not as things that must.

### The four junctions

Between two words there are four possible joins, and the count is not arbitrary. A Pāli word may **begin** with either a vowel or a consonant, but it may **end** only in a vowel or in the niggahīta; no Pāli word ends in a consonant. Four combinations, therefore, and no fifth:[^four-cases]

| | followed by a vowel | followed by a consonant |
|---|---|---|
| **ends in a vowel** | *tena* + *upasaṅkami* → *tenupasaṅkami* | *na* + *pajānāti* → *nappajānāti* |
| **ends in the niggahīta** | *ahaṁ* + *api* → *ahampi* | *taṁ* + *ca* → *tañca* |

[^four-cases]: Perniola §230, p. 285, who derives the enumeration this way, alone among the sources consulted. Thānuttamo's four traditional types, p. 26, map onto it without being derived from it: *sarasandhi* is the first cell, *byañjanasandhi* (which he defines as a final vowel meeting an initial consonant) is the second, and *niggahītasandhi* covers both of the third row. His fourth type is *pakatisandhi*, the outcome treated above, which has no cell because it is what happens in any of them.

#### A vowel meets a vowel

Any of four things can happen, and the first two account for most of what a reader will meet.

**One vowel is elided.** Usually the first goes, and the surviving vowel may lengthen if its syllable will take it:[^elision-kacc]

- *tena* + *upasaṅkami* → *tenupasaṅkami* 'he approached by that [way]'
- *cakkhu* + *indriyaṁ* → *cakkhundriyaṁ* 'the faculty of the eye'
- *so* + *ahaṁ* → *sohaṁ* 'so I'
- *puriso* + *asi* → *purisosi* 'you are a man'

[^elision-kacc]: Kaccāyana 12 and 13 (Thānuttamo p. 28). Which of the two vowels goes is not fixed by rule; Perniola §231(b), p. 286, offers the working guide that the vowel of the more important word is retained. *Cakkhundriyaṁ* has 1,262 occurrences in the root texts, so the elision is not a rarity.

**Two vowels contract into one.** Identical vowels give the corresponding long vowel; and a following *iti* regularly lengthens the vowel before it and loses its own *i*:

- *na* + *ahaṁ* → *nāhaṁ* 'not I'
- *tatra* + *ayaṁ* → *tatrāyaṁ* 'there, this'
- *passati* + *iti* → *passatīti* 'thus he sees'
- *sato* + *eva* → *satova* 'mindful indeed'

**Where *a* meets *i* the result is *e*, and where *a* meets *u* it is *o***, which is the same merger that produced those two vowels in the first place:[^ao-merger]

- *upa* + *ikkhati* → *upekkhati* 'he looks on'
- *kaṭṭha* + *udaka* → *-kaṭṭhodaka-*, as in *satiṇakaṭṭhodakaṁ* 'with grass, wood and water'

[^ao-merger]: Kaccāyana 14 (Thānuttamo p. 28); Perniola §6(b), p. 8. The chapter on script and pronunciation gives the historical side: *e* is guttural-palatal because it is *a* plus *i*, and *o* guttural-labial because it is *a* plus *u*.

**A sonant vowel becomes its semivowel.** *I* becomes *y* and *u* becomes *v* when a different vowel follows; the tradition heads this separately as ***ādeso***, 'substitution', and it is not the same thing as a consonant appearing from nowhere, though it looks like one on the page:[^adeso]

- *vi* + *ākaroti* → *byākaroti* 'he explains'
- *su* + *akkhāto* → *svākkhāto* 'well proclaimed'
- *yo* + *ayaṁ* → *yvāyaṁ* 'this [one] who'
- *so* + *eva* → *sveva* 'that very one'

[^adeso]: Kaccāyana 21 and 17 (Thānuttamo pp. 29–30); Perniola §8, pp. 9–10, who derives it from the division of the vowels into pure (*a*, *ā*), sonant (*i*, *ī*, *u*, *ū*) and diphthong (*e*, *o*). A sonant vowel can serve as either a vowel or a consonant, and before another vowel it takes the consonantal role. Where the vowel is *e* or *o* the diphthong is first resolved into its parts, so *so* + *eva* passes through *sa-u* + *eva* to *sveva*. On *byākaroti*: Perniola and most grammars print *vyākaroti*, the Sanskritising spelling, which the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana carries once, in the grammatical literature. The canon has *byākaroti*, 122 times in the root texts, and *byākāsi* 88 times. Pāli turns this initial *v* into *b*, and the *vy-* spelling is imported from Sanskrit, not read off a Pāli text.

#### A vowel meets a consonant

The commonest case is that a consonant simplified in Pāli reappears doubled when a vowel comes before it. Sanskrit *kṣ* is Pāli *kkh*, reduced to *kh* at the start of a word, but put a word in front and the double consonant comes back:[^doubling]

- *na* + *khamati* → *nakkhamati* 'he does not approve'
- *na* + *pajānāti* → *nappajānāti* 'he does not understand'
- *api* + *sudaṁ* → *apissudaṁ* 'moreover'
- *puna* + *puna* → *punappuna* 'again and again'
