[^doubling]: Perniola §232, p. 287, who explains the doubling historically: *na khamati* is *na* + *kṣamate*, and the *kkh* that the word lost at its beginning survives in the middle of the joined form. *Punappuna* he derives through *punar puna*, the older *-r* being what the doubling replaces.

A long vowel may also **shorten before a consonant**, particularly in the particles: *vā* becomes *va* where the metre or the phrase wants it lighter.

#### The niggahīta meets a vowel

The niggahīta may stand, may become *m*, may be lost, or may be replaced by *d*.

- *evaṁ* + *eva* → *evameva* 'just so'
- *pāsādaṁ* + *āruyha* → *pāsādamāruyha* 'having climbed the palace'
- *labheyyaṁ* + *ahaṁ* → *labheyyāhaṁ* 'might I obtain'
- *etaṁ* + *avoca* → *etadavoca* 'said this'
- *yaṁ* + *eva* → *yadeva* 'whatever'

The last two are the interesting pair. The *d* is inherited, not inserted: *etad* and *yad* are what these pronouns looked like in Old Indic, and before a vowel the old final consonant surfaces again.[^etad] The same happens with *sammā*, *bahu* and *sakiṁ* before *eva*: *sammadeva*, *bahudeva*, *sakideva*.

[^etad]: Perniola §231(d), p. 287: "The pronouns *ya*, *ta*, followed by a word beginning with a vowel, retain the Sanskrit neuter forms." He lists the *-d* of *sammad eva*, *bahud eva*, *sakid eva* with them.

Where the following word begins with a consonant after all, because the niggahīta was written across a join that the pronunciation closed, the niggahīta takes the nasal of that consonant's own group, as it does inside a word:

- *ahaṁ* + *api* → *ahampi* 'I too'
- *dutiyaṁ* + *api* → *dutiyampi* 'a second time'
- *dukkhaṁ* + *iti* → *dukkhanti* 'thus, suffering'
- *sukhaṁ* + *eva* → *sukhameva* 'happiness indeed'

The canon's definition of the word *pātimokkha* puts both outcomes into one sentence:

- **Pātimokkhanti** ādimetaṁ mukhametaṁ pamukhametaṁ kusalānaṁ dhammānaṁ. Tena vuccati **pātimokkhanti**. (Kd 2 — Vin i 103)
- *pātimokkhanti* < *pātimokkhaṁ* the Monastic Code · *iti* (the quotative) · *ādimetaṁ* < *ādiṁ* first · *etaṁ* this · *mukhametaṁ* < *mukhaṁ* front · *etaṁ* this · *pamukhametaṁ* < *pamukhaṁ* foremost · *etaṁ* this · *kusalānaṁ* of wholesome · *dhammānaṁ* qualities · *tena* therefore · *vuccati* it is called
- "'**Monastic Code**': this is the first, this the front, this the forefront of wholesome qualities. Therefore it is called '**Monastic Code**'."

Three times over, the niggahīta before *e-* becomes *m*; and four words later *pātimokkhaṁ* meets the *t-* of *iti* and takes the dental nasal of that consonant's own group instead.[^adimetam-dpd]

[^adimetam-dpd]: DPD resolves *ādimetaṁ* as *ādiṁ* + *etaṁ*, and glosses the three words as 'first, initial', 'front, foremost, facing' and 'at the head' respectively. *Ādimetaṁ* has 1 root-text occurrence, and so have *mukhametaṁ* and *pamukhametaṁ*: the three stand together in the Uposatha chapter and nowhere else in the canon.

#### The niggahīta meets a consonant

Two cases, and both are the assimilation rule of the previous chapter applied across a word boundary. Before a nasal the niggahīta is absorbed into it; before any other consonant it becomes the nasal of that consonant's group:[^nigg-cons]

- *taṁ* + *ca* → *tañca* 'and that'
- *taṁ* + *eva* → *taññeva* 'that very thing'
- *evaṁ* + *hi* → *evañhi* 'for thus'
- *paṭi* + *saṁ* + *līna* → *paṭisallīno* 'gone into seclusion'
- *saṁ* + *lekha* → *sallekho* 'self-effacement'

[^nigg-cons]: Perniola §234, p. 288; Kaccāyana 31–33 and 39–41 (Thānuttamo pp. 31–32). *Evañhi* and *taññeva* show *h* and *y* taking the palatal nasal, the two consonants that belong to no group. The *y* of *taññeva* is the hiatus glide of the next section, and the editions print the join at both stages: *taṁyeva* 31 times in the root texts, *taññeva* 61.

But the writing does not always follow. *Yaṁ* + *nūna* is analysed as *yannūna*, and the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana prints *yaṁnūna* — 94 times in the root texts, niggahīta intact. The rule describes what a reciter does; whether an editor writes it is a separate question, and the answer varies by edition. Expect the spelling to record the pronunciation and you will be surprised twice: once by the joins that are written and once by the ones that are not.

### What bridges a hiatus

When two vowels meet and a consonant turns up between them, the consonant has one of two quite different origins, and the sources that treat them as one make the language look more arbitrary than it is.

Sometimes it is a **glide**: a consonant with no history, inserted to close the gap because a gap is awkward to say. *Y* and *v* are the commonest, and *m* occurs too:

- *añña* + *añña* → *aññamañña* 'one another'[^annamanna-m]
- *dvi* > *di* + *aḍḍha* → *diyaḍḍha* 'one and a half'
- *na* + *idaṁ* → *nayidaṁ* 'this is not'

Sometimes it is a **relic**: an Old Indic final consonant that had been lost at the end of the word and reappears when the word is no longer at the end of anything:

- *yāva* + *eva* → *yāvadeva* 'just as far as', from *yāvad*
- *puna* + *eti* → *punareti* 'comes back again', from *punar*

Oberlies puts both under one heading and keeps them apart inside it: a hiatus "can be bridged by (a) a glide or (b) a (so-called) *sandhi* consonant, which may be a relic from OIA or analogically inserted".[^oberlies-hiatus] That last qualification matters most. Once a language has a stock of words whose *d* or *r* comes back before a vowel, it can extend the habit to words that never had one, so the class is mixed, and no rule shows which member is in front of you. What you can do is stop expecting the consonant to belong to either word, for that expectation makes such forms impossible to look up.

[^oberlies-hiatus]: Oberlies §25, p. 124. His examples of the glide inside words include *aññamañña*, *diyaḍḍha* ~ *duvaḍḍha* 'one and a half' and *piṇḍiyālopa* 'a morsel of food'. Perniola §231(d), p. 287, had already noticed half the distinction, listing *dhīratthu*, *pāturahosi*, *tasmātiva*, *yāvadeva* and *punarahosi* under the observation that "often there is no new consonant inserted, but the original Sanskrit final consonant is restored".

[^annamanna-m]: The classification is Oberlies's (§25, p. 124), and DPD does not share it: its Sanskrit column gives *anyam* + *anya*, which makes the *m* the old accusative ending of the first member, not a consonant inserted to fill a gap. *Lahumessati* (Dhp 369) goes the same way: DPD reads *lahuṁ* + *eti*, again an accusative. Both are in the mixed class this section is about, and neither reading can be read off the form of the word.

> **A wrong division invents a rule**
>
> Geiger cites *ādicco-v-udayaṁ* (It 85,4) as an instance of a *v*-glide. Oberlies: "But this is, of course, *ādicco v' udayaṁ* 'like the rising sun'." The *v* is what is left of the particle *iva*, not a glide, and once the phrase is divided correctly there is nothing to explain. He reports the same about *ubhaya-v-okiṇṇa-*, which is *ubhaya-vokiṇṇa-*.
>
> The warning generalises, and it applies directly to what this chapter teaches. Undoing a sandhi wrongly does not produce a wrong word; it produces a rule that does not exist, and the rule then looks like evidence for itself. Where a division is uncertain, the test is whether both of the resulting pieces are words that occur elsewhere, not whether the division is possible.

### Consonants meeting consonants

Inside a word, two consonants that come together usually do not stay as they are. Which of them gives way is not a matter of chance: Perniola ranks the consonants by strength (the mutes strongest, then the sibilant *s*, then the nasals, then *l*, *v*, *y* and *r*), and the rule follows in two clauses.[^strength] If the two are of **equal** strength, the first assimilates to the second. If they are of **unequal** strength, the weaker assimilates to the stronger.

[^strength]: Perniola §12, p. 15. He notes that *h* enters only a few combinations, and that *ḷ* and *ḷh* enter none.

- *sat* + *kāra* → *sakkāra* 'honour': two mutes, the first gives way
- *sak* + *noti* → *sakkoti* 'he is able': mute against nasal, the nasal gives way
- *vac* + *ssati* → *vakkhati* 'he will speak': palatal to guttural, then assimilation
- *labh* + *ta* → *laddha* 'obtained': both change: the second is voiced by the first, then the first assimilates to it
- *rāj* + *nā* → *raññā* 'by the king': the second is palatalised by the first, then the first assimilates to it

The direction has names, and the grammars use them. Assimilation is **progressive** when the first consonant makes the second like itself, as in *sakkoti*; **regressive** when the second makes the first like itself, as in *sakkāra*; and **reciprocal** when both move, as in *laddha* and *raññā*.[^directions] The nasal of a group behaves slightly differently and has its own name, **adaptation**: a nasal before a mute simply shifts to the nasal of that mute's group, the niggahīta rule seen once more: *saṁ* + *tiṭṭhati* gives *santiṭṭhati*, *saṁ* + *carati* gives *sañcarati*, *saṁ* + *ṭhāna* gives *saṇṭhāna*.

[^directions]: Perniola §11(a)–(b), pp. 13–15. He lists four further processes under the same section which belong to word formation, not to reading, and are treated in this book under compounds and word formation: metathesis (*gah-ṇāti* → *gaṇhāti*), epenthesis (*rāj-no* → *rājino*), aspiration (*as-ti* → *atthi*) and simplification (*bhed-ssati* → *bhecchati*).

The practical value of all this to a reader is narrow but real. A doubled consonant in the middle of a word very often marks a join, and knowing which single consonants collapse into which double one lets the join be found.

> **Assimilation across manuscript traditions**
>
> Since there are no strict rules, different manuscript traditions have resolved the same junction differently. *Kho ahaṁ* appears as *khvāhaṁ* in Burmese manuscripts, as *khohaṁ* in others, and unassimilated as *kho ahaṁ* elsewhere. It is therefore common to find several spellings of one junction, internal or external, with none of them more correct than the rest.
>
> Older grammars tend to treat Pāli as a derivative of Sanskrit and to explain its sandhi accordingly. Pāli is now generally understood as a sister of Sanskrit, not a daughter, influenced by Sanskrit rules later, at the period of writing. So a Sanskritising spelling like *vyākaroti* can stand in a grammar for a form the canon does not have.

#### Sandhi in verse

Verse takes more liberties than prose, because the words have to fit the metre. Junctions appear there that would be unusual otherwise: *yaṁ taṁ santaṁ* → *yantasantaṁ*, *navaṁ na atthi* → *navanatthi*.

The pressure also runs the other way. The Metta Sutta's *puttamāyusā* is written unassimilated as *puttaṁ āyusā* in several editions, and the unassimilated reading breaks the metre, a useful reminder that an editor's word division is an editorial act. It also explains how a form can be canonical and yet hard to find in a digital text: a search for *āyusā* as a word finds nothing in the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana, because the line is printed joined. Chapter 18 takes up what the metre does to these lines; here it is enough that a junction which looks irregular in verse is often the metre's work, not the language's.

### The canon undoing a join

Undoing a join is not only a modern reader's problem. The Vinaya does it on its own pages: each rule of the Pātimokkha is followed by a word-analysis, the *padabhājanīyaṁ*, 'the analysis of the rule', literally 'the sentence to be divided', which takes the rule apart word by word and states what each word covers.[^padabhajaniya] Every entry opens by quoting its word as the rule prints it, and the rule prints it joined.

- Yo **panāti** yo yādiso … Eso vuccati '**yo panā**'ti. (Bu Pj 1 — Vin iii 23)
- *yo panāti* < *yo* whoever · *pana* moreover · *iti* (the quotative) · *yo* whoever · *yādiso* of whatever kind · *eso* this · *vuccati* is called · *yo panā'ti* the same three elements again, the join now marked, not written
- "'**Whoever**': whoever, of whatever kind … This is what is called '**whoever**'."

[^padabhajaniya]: DPD, s.v. *padabhājanīya*: '(vinaya) analysis of the rule', literally 'sentence to be divided'. The texts have the neuter *padabhājanīyaṁ*, and more often the shorter *padabhājanaṁ*, almost entirely in the commentaries. The analysis has a name in the tradition, not in the canon. Brahmāli, in the general introduction to his Vinaya translation ("The Sutta-vibhaṅga"), renders the section this belongs to as 'the Analysis of the Sutta', noting that the *sutta* meant is the Pātimokkha and not a discourse.

The entry opens with the lemma joined (*pana* has swallowed the *i* of *iti* and lengthened its own *a* in compensation) and closes by giving the lemma back with the join marked instead of written. This is the *-ti* that gave *passatīti* above, acting on a short *a* instead of a short *i*. The closing form is instructive. The *ā* of *panā* belongs to neither *pana* nor *iti* but to their meeting, and an editor who divides the phrase has to leave it standing somewhere.[^pana-leftover] A division does not restore the words.

[^pana-leftover]: A leftover piece can look like a word and still not be one. *Panā* has 6 root-text occurrences, every one of them in the Mahāniddesa and every one in a single verse line, *Saddhammapūjāpi panā tatheva*; DPD carries no headword for it. That qualifies the test given below, which checks a division by asking whether both resulting pieces occur elsewhere. The test rules a division out. It does not rule one in.

The other entries do the same on other vowels. The next lemma is *bhikkhūti*, from *bhikkhu* + *iti* (Bu Pj 1 — Vin iii 24); and the *sekhiya* rules, 'the rules of training', are built on a first-person future welded the same way:

- “Parimaṇḍalaṁ **nivāsessāmīti** sikkhā karaṇīyā”ti. (Bu Sk 1 — Vin iv 185)
- *parimaṇḍalaṁ* evenly all round · *nivāsessāmīti* < *nivāsessāmi* I shall dress · *iti* (the quotative) · *sikkhā* the training · *karaṇīyā* to be done
- "'**I shall dress** evenly all round': this is the training to be done."

Three vowels and one junction: whatever short vowel stands before the quotative lengthens, and *iti* loses its *i*.

For a reader searching a text this is worse than the case of *āyusā* above, where the word was hidden inside a joined form but every letter of it was still there to be found. Here the word's own last vowel has changed, so the form a dictionary files it under is not even contained in the printed text. *Nivāsessāmi* does not occur in the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana at all, while *nivāsessāmīti* has 2 root-text occurrences, one in each Vibhaṅga's sekhiya section. The sekhiya rules are built this way almost without exception, so a search for any of them under its dictionary form finds nothing.

One rule breaks the pattern, and it shows where the fault lies. At Bu Sk 36 the future is not the last word before the quotative, and it stands there in the form a dictionary would give it.[^sekhiya-position] The position hides the form, not the verb.

[^sekhiya-position]: *Na sūpaṁ vā byañjanaṁ vā odanena paṭicchādessāmi bhiyyokamyataṁ upādāyāti sikkhā karaṇīyā* (Bu Sk 36 — Vin iv 192), 'I shall not cover curry or condiment with rice out of a desire for more'. The quotative here attaches to the absolutive *upādāya* and not to the verb, so the verb is left bare, and *paṭicchādessāmi* has 1 root-text occurrence, which is this one. DPD glosses *bhiyyokamyatā* as 'desire for more'.

### Where a sandhi gets resolved

One rule of this book's own follows from everything above, and it is stated here because this is the chapter that earns it.

A resolved sandhi belongs in the gloss, never in the quotation. When a passage is cited, the first line gives the text of the edition, *Esevanto dukkhassā* (SN 12.51 — S ii 84), and the resolution goes on the line beneath, where it is true: *esevanto* < *eso* + *eva* + *anto*. The impulse to print the unpacked form on the quotation line is a teacher's impulse and a sound one, but *eso eva anto* is a sequence no manuscript contains, and a book that puts it inside quotation marks has composed a Pāli example, not cited one.

That is why this book gives every example in three layers, not two. The middle layer is where each join this chapter teaches is undone.

### Two habits

Two habits carry the whole of this chapter, and they pull against each other. When a word will not parse, suspect a junction before you suspect the dictionary: take off a *ti*, a *pi* or an *eva*, put back the vowel the junction ate, and look up what is left. And when two vowels stand side by side, do not go looking for the rule that failed. *Ko imaṁ* is two words that met and did nothing. With both habits you undo the joins the canon made and leave alone the ones it did not.

**Exercises for this chapter are in the workbook**, under "Exercises: Sandhi", with worked answers at the back of that volume.

**Principal sources.** Perniola chapter XVII for the patterns of external sandhi and chapter II §§11–12 for consonant change; Thānuttamo for the tradition's categories and the Kaccāyana rules behind them; Oberlies §25 for what bridges a hiatus.

## Nouns: Stems and Declension

A Pāli noun is a **stem** plus an **ending**. The stem carries the meaning and does not change; the ending carries the grammar and changes constantly. *Buddha* is a stem, and it is not a word: what the texts contain is *buddho*, *buddhaṁ*, *buddhena*, *buddhassa*, *buddhe*, *buddhānaṁ*. The consequence is one a reader meets on the first page of any sutta. No Pāli word ends in a bare consonant, and no Pāli word is a bare stem; every word in the canon ends in a vowel or in the niggahīta *ṁ*. When a grammar prints *rājan* or *bhagavant* or *satthar*, it is printing something the texts never say.

The set of endings a stem takes is its **declension** (*vibhatti*, 'the division'), and stems that take the same endings form a **declension group**. There are around twenty of these, which sounds worse than it is: three of them account for most of the canon, and the rest are variations legible from those three.

### Cases, numbers and genders

Every inflected noun in the canon simultaneously answers three questions: what job is it doing in its sentence, how many of it are there, and what gender is it.

**Seven cases.** Nominative, vocative, accusative, instrumental, ablative, genitive/dative, locative. This book's tables print seven rows and not eight, because the genitive and the dative have fallen together: the same ending serves both almost everywhere.[^gen-dat] What each case *does* is the business of the chapter on case syntax; here it is only their forms.

**Two numbers**, singular and plural. Old Indic had a third, the dual, and Pāli has lost it entirely. Only a single word survives, *ubho* 'both', which still inflects as one: the same fact met from the other side in the chapter on numerals, where it explains why 'two' has no paradigm of its own.[^dual]

**Three genders**, masculine, neuter and feminine, abbreviated m., n. and f. throughout. Gender here is a grammatical category and not a statement about the world: the chapter on gender and adjectives takes that up, and shows three words for 'road' in three different genders.

[^gen-dat]: Oberlies §28.6, p. 137, treats this under *case syncretism*, the general term for two cases collapsing into one form. A separate dative survives in strength only in the *a*-stem singular, where `-āya` stands beside genitive `-assa`: *hitāya* 'for the welfare', *sukhāya* 'for the happiness', *dassanāya* 'in order to see' (Perniola §25e, p. 31). Ñāṇatusita marks the same form `āya (= Dat.)` in his own table, reproduced at Appendix A.1.

[^dual]: Oberlies §28.1, pp. 130–131. Perniola §24, p. 29, states the two numbers without comment on the loss.

### The one fact that organises the rest

Before any paradigm, the fact that makes them learnable instead of memorisable. Perniola states it in a sentence and then moves on, and it deserves more weight than he gives it:

> the masculine and the neuter nouns are declined in the same way with the exception of the nominative, vocative and accusative singular and plural. The feminine nouns have different case endings.[^perniola-24]

So the masculine and neuter are one declension with a small exception, not two declensions. Everywhere except the three cases Perniola names, *buddhena* and *cittena*, *buddhassa* and *cittassa*, *buddhesu* and *cittesu* (a masculine and a neuter) are simply the same. The gender difference lives in the nominative, vocative and accusative, and nowhere else. That is a great deal less to learn than a table implies, and it holds across every stem class in the language, not merely the *a*-stems where it is easiest to see.

[^perniola-24]: Perniola §24, p. 29. The same division underlies Ñāṇatusita's layout at Appendix A.1, where masculine and neuter share a table and the feminine is given its own.

### The *a*-stems

By far the largest class, and the one every other stem is read against. The masculine goes like *purisa* 'man', the neuter like *rūpa* 'form'.

| Case | Masc. | Neut. |
|---|---|---|
| Nom. sg. | *puriso* | *rūpaṁ* |
| Voc. sg. | *purisa* | *rūpaṁ* |
| Acc. sg. | *purisaṁ* | *rūpaṁ* |
| Instr. sg. | *purisena* | *rūpena* |
| Abl. sg. | *purisā*, *-asmā*, *-amhā*, *-ato* | *rūpā*, *-asmā*, *-amhā*, *-ato* |
| Gen./Dat. sg. | *purisassa*, *-āya* (dat.) | *rūpassa*, *-āya* (dat.) |
| Loc. sg. | *purise*, *-asmiṁ*, *-amhi* | *rūpe*, *-asmiṁ*, *-amhi* |
| Nom./Voc. pl. | *purisā* | *rūpāni*, *rūpā* |
| Acc. pl. | *purise* | *rūpāni*, *rūpe* |
| Instr./Abl. pl. | *purisehi* | *rūpehi* |
| Gen./Dat. pl. | *purisānaṁ* | *rūpānaṁ* |
| Loc. pl. | *purisesu* | *rūpesu* |

Read the two columns against each other and Perniola's rule is visible: they differ in four rows and are identical in the other seven.

A caution about how to read that table, and about every paradigm table in this book and in every other grammar. A paradigm teaches endings, and its forms are only specimens of them. *Purisasmā* and *purisamhi* occur nowhere in the canon or its commentaries; they are found only in the grammarians' own texts, where somebody has done what the table above does and completed the paradigm by rule. The *endings* are real and common (*kāyasmā*, *kāyamhi* and *lokasmiṁ* are ordinary canonical Pāli, and *lokasmiṁ* alone occurs over four hundred times in the root texts), but nobody in the canon happens to have said *purisasmā*.[^specimen-forms]

This holds for every table below, and it is not a defect in them. A paradigm has to be complete to be a paradigm. It only misleads if it is read as a list of words the texts contain, and the rule for this book is accordingly narrow: where a specific form is asserted in the prose and not merely displayed in a table, it has been checked against the texts first. Appendix A.1 sidesteps the question altogether by printing bare endings and never a whole word.

[^specimen-forms]: Counted over the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana, in four groups: *purisasmā* (root texts 0, aṭṭhakathā 0, ṭīkā 0, later works 13) and *purisamhi* (root texts 0, aṭṭhakathā 0, ṭīkā 0, later works 6), where 'later works' are the grammatical and chronicle literature outside the commentaries. Against these, *lokasmiṁ* (root texts 466, aṭṭhakathā 134, ṭīkā 10, later works 107). Perniola §25, pp. 30–31, prints the full *purisa* paradigm without distinguishing them, which is ordinary practice in a grammar and is not an error; the distinction only matters when a paradigm is mistaken for a word list.

- Nāhaṁ, bhikkhave, aññaṁ ekarūpampi samanupassāmi yaṁ evaṁ **purisassa** cittaṁ pariyādāya tiṭṭhati … (AN 1.1 — A i 1)
- Mendicants, I do not see a single sight that so occupies **a man's** mind …

- Idh'evam eso **lokasmiṁ**, mūlaṁ khaṇati attano. (Dhp 247 — Dhp p. 36)
- Right here **in this world** this man digs up his own root.[^idhevameso]

- Nibbindaṁ virajjati; **virāgā** vimuccati. **Vimuttasmiṁ** vimuttamiti ñāṇaṁ hoti. (SN 22.59 — S iii 68)
- Being disillusioned, desire fades; **through the fading of desire** he is freed. **When he is freed**, the knowledge comes that he is freed.

[^idhevameso]: The Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana prints the second word solid, *Idhevameso*, and it divides as *idha* + *evaṁ* + *eso*, the niggahīta of *evaṁ* becoming *m* before the vowel. This book divided it *idheva meso* until the Buddha Jayanti edition was consulted, which prints the line word-divided as *Idh'evam eso lokasmiṁ* in one of its two Dhammapada texts and reads *Idheva poso lokasmiṁ*, "this man", in the other, either way with a subject for *khaṇati* and no *me* in the line. DPD's *meso*, *me* + *eso* 'this is my', is a real sandhi form and belongs to the *na meso attā* passages, not here.

The last of those has two *a*-stem obliques with different functions in consecutive clauses: *virāgā* an ablative of cause, *vimuttasmiṁ* a locative, and both of them ordinary canonical forms, not paradigm fillers.

#### Why the endings look as they do

Four rules account for most of the pattern of that table, and Perniola sets them out where a reader can use them.[^perniola-25g] The stem is *purisa-* throughout, and:

- Before the nominative singular marker *-s*, the stem vowel and the marker together give *-o*: *purisa-s* > *puriso*.
- Before the nominative plural *-as*, they give *-ā*: *purisa-as* > *purisā*.
- The stem vowel **lengthens** before an ending beginning with *n-*: *purisa-naṁ* > *purisānaṁ*, *rūpa-ni* > *rūpāni*.
- The stem vowel **drops** before an ending beginning with *e-*: *purisa-ena* > *purisena*, *purisa-ehi* > *purisehi*, *purisa-esu* > *purisesu*.

None of this has to be applied consciously while reading. It is here because, once you have seen it, you stop experiencing *purisānaṁ* and *purisesu* as two unrelated forms and start hearing one stem under both, and that is the whole difficulty of the declension in miniature.

[^perniola-25g]: Perniola §25(g), p. 32, where the four are given as one list. The rules are stated as synchronic descriptions of the Pāli, not as historical derivations; for the history see Perniola chapter 13, *Evolution of the declensions*.

#### Where the *a*-stems vary

Three variants a reader will meet and should not be thrown by.

A nominative singular in *-e* instead of *-o* turns up occasionally in both masculine and neuter: *purise*, *manusse*, *jīvite*, *sukhe*, *dukkhe*.[^nom-e] A nominative plural in *-āse* appears in verse — *paṇḍitāse*, *gatāse*. And an ablative singular in *-to* is available to every stem in the language, not only this one; it is common enough to meet on its own, below.

[^nom-e]: Perniola §25(a), p. 31, who gives eleven instances. Ñāṇatusita marks the *-e* nominative as rare in both genders (Appendix A.1). Oberlies §30.1, p. 142, treats it as the Māgadhī nominative surviving in the Pāli of the canon, and so it is more frequent in some texts than in others.

### The *ā*-stems

The feminine counterpart, and the third of the three paradigms that carry the canon. It goes like *kaññā* 'girl'.

| Case | Fem. |
|---|---|
| Nom. sg. | *kaññā* |
| Voc. sg. | *kaññe* |
| Acc. sg. | *kaññaṁ* |
| Instr. sg. | *kaññāya* |
| Abl. sg. | *kaññāya*, *-āto* |
| Gen./Dat. sg. | *kaññāya* |
| Loc. sg. | *kaññāya*, *-āyaṁ* |
| Nom./Voc./Acc. pl. | *kaññā*, *kaññāyo* |
| Instr./Abl. pl. | *kaññāhi* |
| Gen./Dat. pl. | *kaññānaṁ* |
| Loc. pl. | *kaññāsu* |

What is striking here is a collapse, not a form: in the singular, *-āya* serves four cases (instrumental, ablative, genitive, dative), and *-āya* or *-āyaṁ* the locative besides. Five of the seven cases are one ending. A feminine *-ā* noun in an oblique case therefore shows almost nothing about which case it is in; only the sentence around it does. This is the sharpest instance in the nominal system of a general truth about reading Pāli, that an ending narrows the possibilities and the context decides among them.

- … pubbe ananussutesu **dhammesu** cakkhuṁ udapādi, ñāṇaṁ udapādi, **paññā** udapādi, **vijjā** udapādi, āloko udapādi. (SN 56.11 — S v 422)
- … regarding **teachings** not heard before, vision arose, knowledge arose, **wisdom** arose, **realisation** arose, light arose.

Two feminine *ā*-stems there, *paññā* and *vijjā*, both nominative singular and both recognisable as feminine by their ending alone, and nowhere else in this paradigm is the ending decisive.

### The *i*- and *u*-stems

These two behave so alike that they are best met together: whatever the *i*-stem does with *i*, the *u*-stem does with *u*. Masculine *aggi* 'fire' and *bhikkhu* 'monk'; neuter *akkhi* 'eye' and *cakkhu* 'eye'.

| Case | *i*- m. | *i*- n. | *u*- m. | *u*- n. |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Nom./Voc. sg. | *aggi* | *akkhi*, *akkhiṁ* | *bhikkhu* | *cakkhu*, *cakkhuṁ* |
| Acc. sg. | *aggiṁ* | *akkhi*, *akkhiṁ* | *bhikkhuṁ* | *cakkhu*, *cakkhuṁ* |
| Instr. sg. | *agginā* | *akkhinā* | *bhikkhunā* | *cakkhunā* |
| Abl. sg. | *agginā*, *-ismā*, *-imhā* | *akkhinā*, *-ismā* | *bhikkhunā*, *-usmā*, *-umhā* | *cakkhunā*, *-usmā* |
| Gen./Dat. sg. | *aggino*, *aggissa* | *akkhino*, *akkhissa* | *bhikkhuno*, *bhikkhussa* | *cakkhuno*, *cakkhussa* |
| Loc. sg. | *aggismiṁ*, *-imhi*, *aggini* | *akkhismiṁ*, *akkhini* | *bhikkhusmiṁ*, *-umhi* | *cakkhusmiṁ*, *cakkhumhi* |
| Nom./Voc. pl. | *aggī*, *aggayo* | *akkhīni*, *akkhī* | *bhikkhū*, *bhikkhavo* | *cakkhūni*, *cakkhū* |
| Acc. pl. | *aggī*, *aggayo* | *akkhīni*, *akkhī* | *bhikkhū*, *bhikkhavo* | *cakkhūni*, *cakkhū* |
| Instr./Abl. pl. | *aggīhi* | *akkhīhi* | *bhikkhūhi* | *cakkhūhi* |
| Gen./Dat. pl. | *aggīnaṁ* | *akkhīnaṁ* | *bhikkhūnaṁ* | *cakkhūnaṁ* |
| Loc. pl. | *aggīsu* | *akkhīsu* | *bhikkhūsu* | *cakkhūsu* |

Two observations justify the table. The plural lengthens the stem vowel: *aggi-hi* > *aggīhi*, *bhikkhu-su* > *bhikkhūsu*. The specimen is *aggi* because it declines cleanly, not because the canon has occasion to speak of fires in the instrumental plural; the same ending on commoner stems gives *isīhi* 'by the seers' and *gahapatīhi* 'by the householders'. And in the nominative and accusative plural the vowel is strengthened, not lengthened: *aggi-o* becomes *aggayo* by way of *agge-o*, and *bhikkhu-o* becomes *bhikkhavo* by way of *bhikkho-o*.[^guna-plural] That explains *bhikkhavo*, and the same operation produces the vocative *bhikkhave*, which a reader meets in the first line of most suttas in the canon.

[^guna-plural]: Perniola §26(a), p. 33, who derives *aggi-o* > *agge-o* > *aggayo* and *taru-o* > *taro-o* > *taravo*. On the two vocative plurals, Perniola §26(g), p. 34, notes that *bhikkhu* has both *bhikkhavo* and *bhikkhave*, "the latter form is more common", decisively so: *bhikkhave* occurs 26,716 times in the root texts against *bhikkhavo*'s 180.

- Te taṁ rattiṁ tikkhattuṁ paccuṭṭhāya pañjalikā **aggiṁ** namassanti. (AN 10.119 — A v 234)
- Getting up three times in the night, with palms joined, they worship **the fire**.

- Idha pana, upavāṇa, **bhikkhu cakkhunā** rūpaṁ disvā rūpappaṭisaṁvedī ca hoti. (SN 35.70 — S iv 41)
- Here, Upavāṇa, **a mendicant**, having seen a form **with the eye**, experiences the form.

The second of those shows the two stems side by side doing different work in one clause: *bhikkhu* is a masculine *u*-stem in the nominative, subject of the sentence, and *cakkhunā* a neuter *u*-stem in the instrumental, the means by which the seeing is done.

### Feminine *ī*- and *ū*-stems

The feminines in *-ī* and *-ū* (*nadī* 'river', *vadhū* 'daughter-in-law') differ from the feminines in *-i* and *-u* in the nominative singular and almost nowhere else.[^fem-i-ii] The oblique singular is *nadiyā*, *vadhuyā*, and the plural *nadiyo*, *vadhuyo*, *nadīhi*, *vadhūhi*, *nadīnaṁ*, *nadīsu*.

Because the difference is so slight, a good many words are found in both shapes: *hiri* beside *hirī* 'moral shame', *itthi* beside *itthī* 'woman'. Nothing hangs on which a manuscript prints.

[^fem-i-ii]: Perniola §30, p. 36, whose *jāti* / *nadī* / *dhenu* / *vadhū* paradigm shows the four columns diverging only in the nominative and vocative singular. Oberlies §36, pp. 160–164, treats *ī/ū*-inflexion as one section for the same reason, and his *Rem.* to §36.8, p. 164, takes up *itthī-*, *sirī-* and *hirī-* specifically as words that waver.

The really rare stems here are the two with a *u*: the **feminine in *-ū*** and its short-vowel partner in ***-u***. Perniola's paradigm supplies a specimen of each (*vadhū* 'daughter-in-law', *dhenu* 'cow'), and the root texts barely use either.[^fem-i-ii] *Vadhū* reaches them only inside the compound *kulavadhū*; *dhenu* reaches them as *dhenuṁ* and *dhenuyo* and scarcely another case. The clearest instance of a *-ū* stem in the early texts is the river-name *Sarabhū*, not a common noun at all. Both paradigms are more of a curiosity than a tool.

### The odd one: *go*

One stem in the language ends in *-o*: *go* 'cow, ox'. In the singular it declines much like *purisa* apart from the nominative, and in the plural the sequence *-ava-* has contracted, giving *gāvo*, *gohi*, *gunnaṁ*, *gosu*.

It gets a paragraph because Oberlies explains where its stranger forms came from, and the explanation demonstrates a process at work throughout the nominal system. A new stem *gava-* was built out of the instrumental *gavā*, which had come to be understood as an ablative; and another stem, *goṇa-*, was extracted from the newly-created feminine *goṇī-*.[^go-oberlies] Forms are not only inherited. They are also **back-formed**, reverse-engineered by speakers from a form they had in front of them, and a paradigm that looks chaotic is often several such rebuildings laid on top of one another.

[^go-oberlies]: Oberlies §37.2–3, p. 165. He gives *gāvuṁ* at Vin I 150,9 as itself a transformation of *gavaṁ* under the influence of the neighbouring words, citing Saddanīti V 1358. Of the two genitive plurals Perniola §28, p. 35, prints, *gunnaṁ* is canonical, with 11 occurrences in the root texts, and *gonaṁ* is found only in the grammarians' texts.

### The stem inventory

With the three main paradigms in place, the rest of the system can be seen whole. These are the stems that exist, and the genders each is found in:

| Stem | m. | n. | f. |
|---|---|---|---|
| *-a* | ✓ | ✓ | |
| *-ā* | | | ✓ |
| *-i* | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ |
| *-ī* | ✓ | | ✓ |
| *-u* | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ |
| *-ū* | ✓ | | ✓ |
| *-o* | ✓ | | |
| *-(n)t* | ✓ | ✓ | |
| *-in* | ✓ | ✓ | |
| *-an* | ✓ | ✓ | |
| *-ar* | ✓ | | ✓ |
| *-as*, *-is*, *-us* | ✓ | ✓ | |

The remaining sections work through the consonant stems, which is the whole of the second half of that table.

### Consonant stems

Old Indic had a large and productive consonant declension. Pāli has the wreckage of it, and of the sources consulted here only Oberlies says so outright:

> Only traces of (a) the root nouns and (b) the consonant stems without vowel alternation — mainly neuter nouns in *-as*, *-is* and *-us*, and very few masc./fem. *as*-nouns — have survived.[^ob-38-1]

*Traces*, and the direction of travel is towards the *a*-stems. Several consonant stems have a set of forms built the old way and a second set built as though the word were an ordinary *a*-stem: beside the accusative singular *rājānaṁ* stands *rājaṁ*, and beside *attānaṁ* stands *attaṁ*.[^transfer] Expect the consonant stems to be tidy and you will be puzzled; expect them to be halfway through a migration and you will not.

How far the migration has gone can be measured instead of assumed: less far than the grammars imply. Perniola gives six transferred forms for these two words, and only the two accusatives above are found in the canon at all; the genitives *rājassa* and *attassa* and the plurals *rājehi* and *attehi* are constructions.[^transfer-measured] The transfer is real, and in the canon it is still mostly confined to the accusative.

[^ob-38-1]: Oberlies §38.1, p. 165. His cross-reference is to §28.2, on the transfer of stems, which is the mechanism.

[^transfer]: Perniola §36(a), p. 42, who gives the doubled forms for *rājan* and *attan* and calls them declension "like the stems in *-a*". Oberlies §28.2, pp. 131–136, gathers the same phenomenon across the whole nominal system under *transfer of stems*.

[^transfer-measured]: Counted over the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana. Canonical: *rājaṁ* 3 in the root texts, *attaṁ* likewise present. Not canonical: *rājassa* (root texts 0, aṭṭhakathā 0, ṭīkā 0, later works 56) and *rājasmā* (root texts 0, aṭṭhakathā 0, ṭīkā 0, later works 2), both grammarians' texts only; *attassa* (root texts 0, aṭṭhakathā 3, ṭīkā 2, later works 14), commentary only; *rājehi* (root texts 0, aṭṭhakathā 0, ṭīkā 0, later works 12), *attehi* (root texts 0, aṭṭhakathā 0, ṭīkā 0, later works 6) and *attanehi* (root texts 0, aṭṭhakathā 0, ṭīkā 0, later works 15), grammarians' texts only. Perniola's list (§36(a), p. 42) describes what the declension permits, a different claim from what the canon contains, and he does not distinguish them.

#### Stems in *-(n)t*

Words in *-(n)t* are adjectives or present participles by origin, though several of the commonest are used as nouns. This class has **two stems, a strong one in *-ant* and a weak one in *-at***, and it uses both: *bhagavant-* gives *bhagavanto* and *bhagavatā*, *sīlavant-* gives *sīlavanto* and *sīlavato*. The nominative singular is shorter than either, ending in a bare *-ā*.

| Case | Sg. | Pl. |
|---|---|---|
| Nom. | *bhagavā* | *bhagavanto* |
| Voc. | *bhagavā*, *bhagava* | *bhagavanto* |
| Acc. | *bhagavantaṁ* | *bhagavante* |
| Instr./Abl. | *bhagavatā*, *bhagavantena* | *bhagavantehi* |
| Gen./Dat. | *bhagavato* | *bhagavataṁ*, *bhagavantānaṁ* |
| Loc. | *bhagavati* | *bhagavantesu* |

- … **bhagavati** pasādo. (SN 55.30 — S v 390)
- … confidence **in the Blessed One**.

- Mahantasmiṁ kho pana lokasannivāse santi samaṇabrāhmaṇā **iddhimanto** dibbacakkhukā paracittaviduno. (AN 3.40 — A i 148)
- In this vast human society there are ascetics and brahmins **with psychic power**, with clairvoyance, who know the minds of others.

The lower right of that table is thinner than it looks, and no case in the chapter more clearly shows a paradigm completed by rule. The *-(n)t* class largely has no oblique plural at all. Perniola says so directly: there are no forms for the instrumental, ablative or locative plural except *sabbhi*, the instrumental and ablative plural of *sant* 'good, true'. The texts agree with him (*bhagavantehi* and *bhagavantesu* occur only in the grammarians' texts, and *bhagavantena* nowhere at all), while *sabbhi* is thoroughly canonical, with 36 occurrences in the root texts.[^sabbhi] A reader will meet *sabbhi* in verse long before meeting the paradigm it belongs to.

[^sabbhi]: Perniola §34, p. 40: "There are no forms for the instrumental, ablative and locative plural, with the exception of *sabbhi* which is the instrumental and ablative plural of *sant* 'good'." Counted over the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana: *sabbhi* (root texts 36, aṭṭhakathā 38, ṭīkā 7, later works 42); *bhagavantehi* (root texts 0, aṭṭhakathā 0, ṭīkā 0, later works 4) and *bhagavantesu* (root texts 0, aṭṭhakathā 0, ṭīkā 0, later works 1), grammarians' texts only; *bhagavantena* 0 throughout. The oblique plural cells printed above are therefore the *a*-stem pattern applied by rule, and are given because a reader needs somewhere to put an ending, not because the texts supply them.

#### Stems in *-in*

All words in *-in* are adjectives by origin (*ātāpin* 'ardent', *sukhin* 'happy', *saññin* 'perceiving'), though some, like *gihin* 'householder', have settled into nouns. The stem is a **possessive suffix**: *-in* added to a noun makes an adjective meaning 'having that thing', so *ātāpin* is 'having ardour' and *gihin* 'having a house'. Two related suffixes, *-min* and *-vin*, serve the same purpose: *sassatasamin*, *medhāvin* 'having wisdom, wise'.[^in-suffix]

| Case | Sg. | Pl. |
|---|---|---|
| Nom. | *hatthī* | *hatthino*, *hatthī* |
| Voc. | *hatthī* | *hatthino* |
| Acc. | *hatthinaṁ* | *hatthino*, *hatthī* |
| Instr./Abl. | *hatthinā* | *hatthīhi* |
| Gen./Dat. | *hatthino* | *hatthīnaṁ* |
| Loc. | *hatthini* | *hatthīsu* |

The nominative singular *-ī* is the point of difficulty, because it looks like a feminine *ī*-stem and is not one. *Hatthī* 'elephant' is masculine; so is *bhikkhu*'s near-neighbour *dhammacārī* 'one who lives by the Dhamma'.

- **Ātāpino** saṁvegino bhavātha. (Dhp 144 — Dhp p. 21)
- Be **ardent** and full of urgency.

[^in-suffix]: Oberlies treats all three together as *i(n)-/mi(n)-/vi(n)-*inflexion at §34, so they are given together here, not as three unrelated stem types. Perniola §35, p. 42, has only *-in*.

#### Stems in *-an*

A small class, and two of its members are among the most frequent words in the canon: *rājan* 'king' and *attan* 'self'.

| Case | *rājan* sg. | *rājan* pl. | *attan* sg. |
|---|---|---|---|
| Nom. | *rājā* | *rājāno* | *attā* |
| Voc. | *rāja* | *rājāno* | *atta* |
| Acc. | *rājānaṁ* | *rājāno* | *attānaṁ* |
| Instr./Abl. | *raññā*, *rājinā* | *rājūhi* | *attanā* |
| Gen./Dat. | *rañño*, *rājino* | *raññaṁ*, *rājūnaṁ* | *attano* |
| Loc. | *raññe*[^rajini] | *rājūsu* | *attani* |

The *raññā* / *rañño* forms are the ones to fix in memory, because they do not look as though they belong to *rājā* at all and they are everywhere.

[^rajini]: The table gives *raññe*, not *rājini*, for the locative singular. *Rājini* is not canonical (none in the root texts, 7 in the commentaries and 33 in the grammarians' texts), whereas *raññe* has 16 in the root texts and is the locative a reader will meet. Both are Ñāṇatusita's, whose *an*-stem locative singular gives four endings, *-ni*, *-ismiṁ*, *-imhi* and *-ne*; on this word only the last of them is Pāli anyone wrote. The *-in* forms of *rājan* are real elsewhere in the paradigm (*rājino* has 33 root-text occurrences and *rājinā* one), so the stem transfer is real and it is the locative specifically that did not take.

- **Attā** hi **attano** nātho. (Dhp 160 — Dhp p. 24)
- **Oneself** is indeed **one's own** protector.

Others of this class are *brahman* 'Brahmā', *yuvan* 'youth', *addhan* 'road', *puman* 'man', and the neuters *kamman* 'action' and *muddhan* 'head'.[^an-others] Most of these have gone over to the *a*-declension almost completely (*kamma*, *kammaṁ*, *kammena*), and their *-an* forms survive chiefly in verse and in compounds.

[^an-others]: Perniola §36(e), p. 43, who lists them and prints the few cases each is actually found in. His list is the fullest of any source read for this chapter; Oberlies §39, pp. 168–171, covers the same ground historically.

#### Stems in *-ar*

The *-ar* stems are two groups wearing one paradigm, and Perniola makes a distinction here that no other source consulted makes.

**Agent nouns** (*satthar* 'teacher', *dātar* 'giver', *netar* 'leader', *sotar* 'hearer') are formed from verbs and mean 'the one who does this'. **Relationship nouns** (*pitar* 'father', *mātar* 'mother', *bhātar* 'brother', *dhītar* 'daughter') are inherited kinship terms. They decline alike except in the accusative singular and the nominative, vocative and accusative plural, where the agent nouns have a long stem vowel and the relationship nouns a short one: *satthāraṁ*, *satthāro* against *pitaraṁ*, *pitaro*, *mātaraṁ*, *mātaro*.[^ar-perniola]

| Case | *satthar* | *pitar* | *mātar* |
|---|---|---|---|
| Nom./Voc. sg. | *satthā* | *pitā* | *mātā* |
| Acc. sg. | *satthāraṁ* | *pitaraṁ* | *mātaraṁ* |
| Instr./Abl. sg. | *satthārā* | *pitarā* | *mātarā* |
| Gen./Dat. sg. | *satthu* | *pitu* | *mātu* |
| Loc. sg. | *satthari* | *pitari* | *mātari* |
| Nom./Voc./Acc. pl. | *satthāro* | *pitaro* | *mātaro* |
| Instr./Abl. pl. | *satthūhi* | *pitūhi* | *mātūhi* |
| Gen./Dat. pl. | *satthūnaṁ* | *pitūnaṁ* | *mātūnaṁ* |
| Loc. pl. | *satthūsu* | *pitūsu* | *mātūsu* |

- Punappunaṁ vaccho upeti **mātaraṁ**. (SN 7.12 — S i 174)
- Again and again the calf goes to **its mother**.

- … anuttaro purisadammasārathi **satthā** devamanussānaṁ. (DN 2 — D i 49)
- … the unsurpassed trainer of those who can be trained, **teacher** of gods and humans.

[^ar-perniola]: Perniola §37(b), p. 44. His §37(c), p. 45, adds that the masculine stems form the instrumental and genitive singular on the *u*-stem pattern (*satthunā*, *satthuno*, *pitunā*, *pituno*), and that the feminine *mātar* forms the locative singular that way too, *mātuyā*, *mātuyaṁ*. *Dhītar* 'daughter' is often declined instead as an *ā*-stem: *dhītā*, *dhītaṁ*, *dhītāya*.

#### Stems in *-as*, *-is* and *-us*

A dozen or so nouns end in *-as*: *manas* 'mind', *cetas* 'mind, heart', *tapas* 'ardour', *tejas* 'fire, majesty', *yasas* 'fame', *vacas* 'speech', *siras* 'head', *ayas* 'iron', *vayas* 'age', *uras* 'breast', *jaras* 'old age'. Beside the *-as* stems stand a very few in *-is* and *-us*.

Their distinguishing forms are a nominative and accusative in *-o*, and an oblique singular in *-asā*, *-aso*, *-asi*:

| Case | Sg. |
|---|---|
| Nom./Acc. | *mano* |
| Instr./Abl. | *manasā* |
| Gen./Dat. | *manaso* |
| Loc. | *manasi* |

Every one of those is common. What is not stable is anything else about the class.

These nouns are in the middle of changing declension, and their gender is disputed because of it. Alongside the forms above, the same words are declined throughout as ordinary neuter *a*-stems: *manaṁ*, *manena*, *manasmā*, *manassa*, *manasmiṁ*, *manāni*, *manehi*, *manānaṁ*, *manesu*. Oberlies gives the phenomenon its own section, *transfer of °as- into a-stems*, and it underlies the sources' disagreement about gender. Perniola and Ñāṇatusita call the class neuter, which is what it was; the Digital Pāli Dictionary calls *manas*, *cetas* and *tapas* masculine, as a word whose nominative is *mano* looks once it has arrived in the *a*-declension. Oberlies's formula ("mainly neuter nouns in *-as*, *-is* and *-us*, and very few masc./fem. *as*-nouns") is the accurate one, and it is accurate partly because it declines to be tidy.[^as-gender]

For a reader, the practical consequence is small: treat these words as neuter, expect masculine agreement occasionally, and do not be surprised by either.

- **Manasā** ce paduṭṭhena, bhāsati vā karoti vā. (Dhp 1 — Dhp p. 1)
- If **with a corrupted mind** one speaks or acts.

- Mātā yathā niyaṁ puttam **āyusā** ekaputtamanurakkhe. (Snp 1.8 — Sn p. 26)
- As a mother would guard her own son, her only son, **with her life**.

[^as-gender]: Oberlies §38.1, pp. 165–166, for the formula; his §38.2, pp. 166–167, for the mechanism. Perniola §33, pp. 39–40: "There are a few stems in *-s* which are neuter in gender and are declined only in the singular", with §33(a) noting that "these stems are often declined like neuter stems in *-a*" and §33(c) allowing one masculine, *candimas* 'moon', nominative *candimā*. DPD, s.v. *manas*, *cetas*, *tapas*, classes all three as masculine, "mano group". Ñāṇatusita marks his *as*- and *us*-columns neuter (Appendix A.1).

The second example above is a small lesson in reading a digital edition. Searched for as a word, *āyusā* finds nothing in the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana, which would ordinarily suggest a form built by grammarians, not used by anyone. It is nothing of the kind: the edition prints the line with the words run together as *puttamāyusā*, so a search for the word cannot see it, and the form sits in one of the best-known passages in the canon.[^ayusa-search]

The word *āyu* 'life, lifespan' is itself the clearest case of the drift this section describes. It is historically a *-us* stem, and Oberlies flags its gender as an open question in the scholarship; in the canon it behaves as a neuter *u*-stem (*āyunā*, *āyuno*), with the older *āyusā* surviving in verse.[^ayu]

[^ayusa-search]: *Āyusā* has 0 root-text occurrences as a word. The line is found only by looking for the string inside a longer one: Snp 1.8 — Sn p. 26 prints *Mātā yathā niyaṁ puttamāyusā ekaputtamanurakkhe*. A word that cannot be found has to be looked for again as part of a longer word before anyone concludes it does not exist, and this shows why.

[^ayu]: Oberlies §38.1 n. 5, p. 166, referring the gender question to Caillat, *IF* 74 (1969) 224. DPD makes *āyu* neuter, from *ā* + √*i* > *ay* + *u*. In the root texts *āyunā* occurs 43 times and *āyuno* 23, against one occurrence of *āyusā*.

#### Root stems

Finally, and mostly for recognition, not use: a handful of words in the canon are **root nouns**, in which the case ending was added directly to a verbal root with no stem-forming vowel between. Pāli has fragments of these and no complete paradigm.

What survives is a scattering of individual forms, chiefly in verse: instrumental *vācā* 'by speech' and *padā* 'with the foot', locative *parisati* 'in the assembly', nominative plural *āpo* 'waters', genitive plural *dvipadaṁ … catuppadaṁ* 'of two-footed and four-footed beings'.[^root-nouns] Met in isolation these look like errors, or like forms of some other word. They are neither, and knowing the class exists is enough.

[^root-nouns]: Oberlies §38.1, pp. 165–166, who gives each with its reference: *diso-disaṁ* (Ja III 459,22\*), *padā*, *vācā* (Sn 130, 232), *parisati* (Ja V 61,24\*), *saṁsati* (M I 68,8), *āpo* (Sn 307), *dvipadaṁ … catuppadaṁ* (S I 6,22\*/24\*). Perniola §32, p. 38, calls them "a few remnants of the Sanskrit root stems declensions" and gives an overlapping list. Oberlies also gives the locative *divi* 'in heaven', citing D II 206,8, though under the diphthong stems (§37.1, p. 165), not among the root nouns; it is left out of the list above because it does not occur as a word in the root texts of the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana edition, and the longer words that contain the string (*ādivisodhanā*, *nandivisālo*) are unrelated. An edition difference is the likeliest explanation and the form is not in doubt, but this book cites what it can check.

### The ablative in *-to*

One ending cuts across every class in the chapter, and it is easier to meet once than five times. **Any noun can form an ablative singular in *-to***, whatever its stem: *dūrato* 'from afar', *gharato* 'from the house', *gāmato* 'from the village', *dukkhato* 'from suffering', *rūpato* 'from form', *jātito* 'by birth', *kammato* 'by action', *aggito* 'from fire', *cakkhuto* 'from the eye'. The last two are the point of the suffix: an *i*-stem and a *u*-stem forming an ablative with the same ending an *a*-stem uses, so it is met here as one fact, not as one fact per declension.[^to-suffix]

There is a companion to it in *-so*, no longer really a case ending at all but a maker of adverbs: *yoniso* 'thoroughly, from the root', *dhātuso* 'according to nature', *hetuso* 'by way of cause', *mattaso* 'in moderation'. Anyone who has met *yoniso manasikāra* 'proper attention' before meeting any grammar has already met this suffix.

[^to-suffix]: Oberlies gathers it at §28.4 as a general feature of the case system, not a fact about any one declension, and so it is given once here. Perniola states it separately at §25(f), §26(d), §29(b) and §30(c) (pp. 31, 33, 36 and 37), once per stem class. Two of the forms he gives do not occur in the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana and have been left out above: *nāvato* 'from the boat' occurs nowhere in it, and neither does *ādiso* 'from the beginning'; the longer words that contain the latter, *yādiso* and *tādiso*, are unrelated. *Bārāṇasito* 'from Benares' is real but commentarial, with 48 occurrences in the aṭṭhakathā and none in the root texts.

### What belongs elsewhere

Three subjects belonging to the noun are treated elsewhere on purpose. **What the cases mean** (why an instrumental and not a locative, what a dative of purpose expresses) is the chapter on case syntax. How gender is assigned, and how a feminine stem is formed from a masculine one, is the next chapter, along with the adjectives, which share every ending in this chapter and add agreement. And the **complete tables**, with every rare and poetic variant that has been left out here, are in Appendix A.1.

**Exercises for this chapter are in the workbook**, under "Exercises: Nouns", with worked answers at the back of that volume.

**Principal sources.** Perniola chapter III for the paradigms and their arrangement; Oberlies §§28–41 for which forms are actually attested and for the transfers between one stem class and another; Ñāṇatusita's inflection tables for the complete inventory of endings, which Appendix A.1 reproduces.

## Gender and Adjectives

The previous chapter set out the endings. This one is about the two things that decide which endings a word takes: its **gender**, which for a noun is fixed and must simply be known, and **agreement**, which is how an adjective borrows the gender, number and case of whatever it describes.

### Gender is grammatical

Every Pāli noun is masculine, neuter or feminine, and that assignment is a fact about the word, not about the thing it names. The clearest demonstration is a set of words meaning the same thing in three genders:

| | | |
|---|---|---|
| *maggo* | masculine | road |
| *vīthi* | feminine | road, street |
| *vaṭumaṁ* | neuter | road |

Nothing about a road is masculine, feminine or neuter. The gender belongs to the word.[^road-triple]

Where a noun does name something with a sex, the grammatical gender usually agrees (*kassako* 'farmer' is masculine, *bhikkhunī* 'nun' feminine), and it is safest to treat that as a convenience, not a rule, because the exceptions are common. *Dāra* 'wife' is masculine, and is met in the plural: *sehi dārehi asantuṭṭho*, 'not content with his own wives'. *Itthiratanaṁ*, 'the woman-treasure' in the list of a wheel-turning monarch's seven treasures, is neuter.[^dara]

[^dara]: DPD, s.v. *dāra*, masculine, 'wife', citing the Parābhava Sutta; it also records a feminine *dārā* built from the same stem. In the root texts the word is found in oblique forms (*dāraṁ* 17 occurrences, and the *dārehi* of the verse quoted), not in the nominative singular *dāro*, which occurs only in the grammarians' texts. *Itthiratanaṁ* has 25 occurrences in the root texts.

[^road-triple]: Perniola §24, p. 29, makes the same point with *maggo*, *vattani*, *vaṭumaṁ*. His feminine has been replaced here: *vattani* occurs only in the grammarians' texts and *vaṭṭanī* not at all, whereas *vīthi* 'street, road' is ordinary canonical Pāli: *vīthiṁ* has 35 occurrences in the root texts, among them MN 33 — M i 220, where the incompetent cowherd "does not know the road". DPD, s.v. *vīthi*, gives 'street; road' and 'path; track'; s.v. *vaṭuma*, 'road, path', neuter.

#### How to tell a noun's gender

Most of the time a reader does not need to. The case is what carries the meaning, and the case can usually be read off the ending without settling the gender at all. When it does matter, there are three ways to find out.

**The dictionary says so.** Most mark m., n. and f. The PTS *Pali-English Dictionary* misleads here, because it omits 'm.': an entry with no gender given is masculine.

**The nominative singular usually shows it.** This is the practical method, and it works because the previous chapter found the three genders differing in the nominative. In the *a*-declension, *-o* is masculine and *-aṁ* neuter; *-ā* is a feminine *ā*-stem.

- … pubbe ananussutesu dhammesu **cakkhuṁ** udapādi, **ñāṇaṁ** udapādi, **paññā** udapādi, **vijjā** udapādi, **āloko** udapādi. (SN 56.11 — S v 422)
- … regarding teachings not heard before, **vision** arose, **knowledge** arose, **wisdom** arose, **realisation** arose, **light** arose.

Five nouns, all nominative singular, all subjects of the same verb. Their endings sort them without any need for a dictionary: *cakkhuṁ* and *ñāṇaṁ* are neuter, *paññā* and *vijjā* feminine, *āloko* masculine.

An adjective agreeing with it shows it. If the noun's own ending is ambiguous, a word describing it may not be, and that is the subject of the rest of this chapter.

> **When the manuscripts disagree**
>
> A fair number of nouns are found in more than one gender, and the commonest reason is that the manuscript traditions were undecided, not Pāli. These are variants of the same order as the *ṭ*/*t* and *ḷ*/*l* spellings met in the chapter on the alphabet: real, recorded and of no consequence for reading.
>
> A second and more interesting reason was met in the previous chapter. The *-as* stems (*mano*, *cetas*, *tapas*) are in the middle of moving into the *a*-declension, and a word whose nominative is *mano* invites masculine agreement whatever its history. The canon supplies both. In the list of the six senses at SN 35.2, five sense-bases take the agreement their own gender requires and the sixth takes *mano dukkho*, masculine.

#### A noun keeps its gender when it describes another noun

Nouns are fixed in gender, and they do not surrender it when they are set beside another noun to characterise it. So a perfectly ordinary Pāli sentence can equate two nouns of different genders, with different endings, without error:

- **Moho** **nidānaṁ** kammānaṁ samudayāya. (AN 3.34 — A i 134)
- **Delusion** is a **source** for the origination of deeds.

- **Puññāni** paralokasmiṁ, **patiṭṭhā** honti pāṇinaṁ. (AN 5.36 — A iii 41)
- **Good deeds** are a **support** for beings in the world beyond.

*Moho* is masculine and *nidānaṁ* neuter; *puññāni* is neuter plural and *patiṭṭhā* a feminine singular. An adjective could not do this. That is the difference between a noun and an adjective, and the next section states it more formally.

### Adjectives

An adjective describes a noun. The Pāli grammarians called it *guṇanāma*, 'quality-name', against *nāmanāma*, 'name-name', for the noun proper — and they drew the line between the two where this chapter has been heading: a noun has one gender and an adjective has three.[^collins-guna]

[^collins-guna]: Collins §2.1, who sets out the indigenous distinction and its two criteria: that adjectives take the gender of the noun they qualify, and that they can be compared. He adds that a noun standing as the final member of a compound acquires all three genders too, so the line is thinner in practice than in principle, a point the chapter on compounds takes up.

An adjective **agrees** with its noun: it takes that noun's gender, its number and its case. The stem stays what it is; only the ending moves.

- **Cakkhuṁ**, bhikkhave, **dukkhaṁ**. … **jivhā dukkhā** … **kāyo dukkho** … **mano dukkho**. (SN 35.2 — S iv 2)
- **The eye**, mendicants, is **suffering**. … **the tongue** is **suffering** … **the body** is **suffering** … **the mind** is **suffering**.

One adjective, *dukkha*, four times, in three genders. It is neuter with *cakkhuṁ*, feminine with *jivhā*, masculine with *kāyo*, and masculine again with *mano*, the last for the reason given in the box above.

A single sentence in the Vinaya's ordination procedure shows the same, and adds number to it. Straight after an ordination five things are to be settled, and each of them is put in a gerundive:

- Tāvadeva chāyā metabbā, utuppamāṇaṁ **ācikkhitabbaṁ**, divasabhāgo **ācikkhitabbo**, saṅgīti **ācikkhitabbā**, cattāro nissayā **ācikkhitabbā**. (Kd 1 — Vin i 95)
- *tāvadeva* straightaway · *chāyā*<sub>a</sub> the shadow (f. nom. sg.) · *metabbā*<sub>a</sub> is to be measured (f. nom. sg.) · *utuppamāṇaṁ*<sub>b</sub> the date (n. nom. sg.) · *ācikkhitabbaṁ*<sub>b</sub> is to be told (n. nom. sg.) · *divasabhāgo*<sub>c</sub> the part of the day (m. nom. sg.) · *ācikkhitabbo*<sub>c</sub> (m. nom. sg.) · *saṅgīti*<sub>d</sub> the recitation (f. nom. sg.) · *ācikkhitabbā*<sub>d</sub> (f. nom. sg.) · *cattāro nissayā*<sub>e</sub> the four supports (m. nom. pl.) · *ācikkhitabbā*<sub>e</sub> (m. nom. pl.)
- "Straightaway the shadow is to be measured, the date **told**, the part of the day **told**, the recitation **told**, and the four supports **told**."

*Ācikkhitabba* 'to be told' stands four times in one sentence and in four shapes: neuter singular beside *utuppamāṇaṁ* 'the date', masculine singular beside *divasabhāgo* 'the part of the day', feminine singular beside *saṅgīti* 'the recitation', masculine plural beside *cattāro nissayā* 'the four supports'.[^acikkhitabba-variant] The stem never moves.

Laid out pair by pair, the sentence shows something the running text hides:

| Noun | Its predicate | What settles the predicate's form |
|---|---|---|
| *chāyā* (f. nom. sg.) | *metabbā* | the noun |
| *utuppamāṇaṁ* (n. nom. sg.) | *ācikkhitabbaṁ* | its own ending |
| *divasabhāgo* (m. nom. sg.) | *ācikkhitabbo* | its own ending |
| *saṅgīti* (f. nom. sg.) | *ācikkhitabbā* | the noun |
| *cattāro nissayā* (m. nom. pl.) | *ācikkhitabbā* | the noun |

The last two are spelled alike, and the word itself cannot decide between them. *Ācikkhitabbā* is the feminine nominative singular, and it is also the masculine nominative plural. *Saṅgīti* on the one side and *cattāro nissayā* on the other decide it, so an adjective can settle a noun's gender (the third of the three methods given above), and a noun can equally settle an adjective's. Agreement runs in both directions.

[^acikkhitabba-variant]: The apparatus disagrees about what follows, and no variant touches the running text quoted here. The Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana's own note gives *cattāri akaraṇīyāni ācikkhitabbāni* as an addition after *saṅgīti ācikkhitabbā, cattāro nissayā ācikkhitabbā*, and sources it to *ka.*, 'in some manuscripts'. DPD's entry for *utuppamāṇa* cites this sentence as its illustration, and prints the sandhi divided, *tāva'd'eva*.

Number and case work the same way:

- Idha, bhikkhave, **pāpaṇiko kusalo** hoti paṇiyaṁ ketuñca vikketuñca. (AN 3.20 — A i 116)
- Here, mendicants, **a shopkeeper** is **skilful** at buying and selling goods.

- … riñcati **kusalaṁ ṭhānaṁ**. (MN 60 — M i 403)
- … he neglects **the skilful course**.

- Seyyathāpi, bhikkhave, **paṇḍito byatto kusalo sūdo** rājānaṁ vā rājamahāmattaṁ vā nānaccayehi sūpehi paccupaṭṭhito assa … (SN 47.8 — S v 151)
- Suppose, mendicants, **a wise, capable, skilful cook** were to serve a king or a king's minister with various sauces …

*Kusalo* masculine nominative singular in the first, *kusalaṁ* neuter accusative singular in the second, and in the third one noun with three adjectives, all four words in step.

#### An adjective's stem does not change

Agreement moves the ending, not the stem. An adjective in *-a* describing a masculine *u*-stem noun becomes masculine, but it stays an *a*-stem: *kusalo bhikkhu*, not *kusalu bhikkhu*. So the previous chapter's paradigms are the whole apparatus needed for adjectives too: an adjective in *-a* declines like *purisa*, *rūpa* and *kaññā* according to the gender it has taken on, one in *-i* like *aggi* or *jāti*, one in *-(n)t* like *bhagavant*.

Perniola puts it in one line: the adjective *kusala* "is declined like *puriso* in the masculine, like *vanitā* in the feminine, and like *rūpaṁ* in the neuter".[^perniola-adj]

[^perniola-adj]: Perniola §31, p. 37, whose opening sentence he has already made once, at the end of §24 on p. 30. His §31 sets out *dīgha* 'long' in all three genders and both numbers, and notes that "when the adjective ends in *-i*, the adjective is declined like *nadī*" in the feminine.

### Forming the feminine

An adjective needs a feminine form, and so does any noun naming a role that women fill. Pāli builds both from the masculine stem, and which suffix it uses depends on what the stem ends in. Perniola gathers the rules; the texts decide between the versions where sources differ.[^fem-perniola]

| Masculine stem | Feminine | Examples |
|---|---|---|
| noun in *-a* | *-ī* | *dāsa* → *dāsī* 'servant'; *cora* → *corī* 'thief'; *deva* → *devī* 'goddess, queen'; *kumāra* → *kumārī* 'girl'; *brāhmaṇa* → *brāhmaṇī* |
| **adjective** in *-a* | *-ā* | *dīgha* → *dīghā* 'long'; *manāpa* → *manāpā* 'agreeable'; *gata* → *gatā* 'gone' |
| *-aka* | *-ikā* | *janaka* → *janikā* 'mother'; *kumāraka* → *kumārikā*; *upāsaka* → *upāsikā* 'laywoman'; *sāvaka* → *sāvikā* 'female disciple' |
| *-in* | *-inī* | *hatthin* → *hatthinī* 'she-elephant'; *musāvādin* → *musāvādinī* 'liar' |
| *-u*, *-ū* | *-nī* | *bhikkhu* → *bhikkhunī* 'nun'; *abhijjhālū* → *abhijjhālunī* 'covetous' |
| *-(n)t*, adjective | *-tī* (weak stem) | *mahant* → *mahatī* 'great'; *sīlavant* → *sīlavatī* 'virtuous'; *guṇavant* → *guṇavatī* |
| *-(n)t*, present participle | *-ntī* (strong stem) | *gacchant* → *gacchantī* 'going'; *vasant* → *vasantī* 'dwelling' |

The second row is the most useful, and the most easily missed. A noun in *-a* makes its feminine in *-ī* and an adjective in *-a* makes its feminine in *-ā*. So *dāsa* gives *dāsī* but *dīgha* gives *dīghā*, and the difference is grammatical, not phonetic. Perniola notes that a few words do both, according to which they are being used as: *kalyāṇa* 'beautiful' gives the adjective *kalyāṇā* but the noun *kalyāṇī* 'a beautiful woman', and both are canonical.[^dahara]

[^dahara]: Perniola §38, N.B., p. 46. His other instance of the pair, *dahara* 'young' → adjectival *daharā* but nominal *daharī* 'young woman', is only half supported: *daharā* has 54 occurrences in the root texts and *daharī* none anywhere but in the grammarians' texts. The rule is sound; that particular illustration of it is not, and it has been left out.

- **Sukhiniyo** hontu tā, mahārāja, somā ca bhaginī sakulā ca bhaginī. (MN 90 — M ii 126)[^sukhiniyo]
- May they be **happy**, great king — sister Somā and sister Sakulā.

- Tena kho pana samayena sukkā bhikkhunī **mahatiyā** parisāya parivutā dhammaṁ deseti. (SN 10.9 — S i 212)
- Now at that time the nun Sukkā was teaching the Dhamma surrounded by **a large** assembly.

[^sukhiniyo]: *Sukhinī* is the feminine of *sukhin* 'happy', an *-in* stem, and *sukhiniyo* its nominative plural: the wish is addressed to two nuns. The form is rare: one occurrence in the root texts, this one.

The Vinaya is two codes, one for monks and one for nuns, and although they differ in what they forbid they are built on one frame. That puts *bhikkhu* and *bhikkhunī* (the *-u* row of the table) into sentences which hold everything else constant:

- Yo pana **bhikkhu** atirekacīvaraṁ dhāreyya, nissaggiyaṁ pācittiyan'ti. (Bu Np 1 — Vin iii 195)
- *yo* whoever (m. nom. sg.) · *pana* moreover · *bhikkhu* a monk (m. nom. sg.) · *atirekacīvaraṁ* a surplus robe (n. acc. sg.) · *dhāreyya* should keep (3 sg. opt.) · *nissaggiyaṁ* entailing relinquishment (n. nom. sg.) · *pācittiyaṁ* an offence entailing confession (n. nom. sg.) · *ti* (quotation)
- "Whatever **monk** should keep a surplus robe: an offence entailing relinquishment and confession."

- Yā pana **bhikkhunī** pattasannicayaṁ kareyya, nissaggiyaṁ pācittiyan'ti. (Bi Np 1 — Vin iv 243)
- *yā* whoever (f. nom. sg.) · *pana* moreover · *bhikkhunī* a nun (f. nom. sg.) · *pattasannicayaṁ* a stash of bowls (m. acc. sg.) · *kareyya* should make (3 sg. opt.) · *nissaggiyaṁ pācittiyaṁ* as above · *ti* (quotation)
- "Whatever **nun** should make a stash of bowls: an offence entailing relinquishment and confession."

Two different offences and one frame. *Yo* becomes *yā* and *bhikkhu* becomes *bhikkhunī*; the optative, and the verbless clause that states the penalty, do not move at all. Even that clause is an agreement: *nissaggiyaṁ* is itself a gerundive, 'to be relinquished', taking the neuter of *pācittiyaṁ*.

The parallel is not confined to the rules. The formal act that opens a meeting of the Sangha begins *Suṇātu me, bhante, saṅgho* among monks and *Suṇātu me, ayye, saṅgho* among nuns (Bu Np 1 — Vin iii 196, Bi Np 1 — Vin iv 244): one word apart, and the word is the term of address.

A handful of nouns form the feminine irregularly, and three are common: *nara* 'man' → *nārī*, *assa* 'horse' → *vaḷavā* 'mare', *go* 'ox' → *gāvī* 'cow'.[^fem-irregular]

[^fem-perniola]: Perniola §38, pp. 45–47, whose nine rules are the basis of the table. Two of his examples have been dropped because the texts do not support them. Counted over the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana, *tāpasī* 'female ascetic' is (root texts 0, aṭṭhakathā 0, ṭīkā 0, later works 10), the grammarians' texts alone, and *sāminī* 'mistress' (root texts 0, aṭṭhakathā 15, ṭīkā 0, later works 1), the commentaries alone. Every form printed in the table above is canonical.

[^fem-irregular]: Perniola §38(h), p. 47, gives a list of twelve. Of them a reader will meet *nārī*, *vaḷavā* and *gāvī*: 71, 1 and 39 occurrences respectively in the root texts. Two others he prints are not canonical and are left out; *rājinī* 'queen' occurs only in the grammarians' texts, though *devī* does the same job and is common.

### Comparative and superlative

An adjective can be compared, and this is the second of the two criteria by which the tradition separated adjectives from nouns. Pāli forms the comparative and the superlative in three ways, and keeps a small stock of irregulars besides.[^comparative]

**The ordinary suffixes are *-tara* and *-tama***, added to the stem: *piya* 'dear' → *piyataro*, *paṇḍita* 'wise' → *paṇḍitataro*, *bahu* 'much' → *bahutaro*. Of the two the comparative is much the commoner; several stems form a *-tara* freely and a *-tama* barely at all.[^tara-tama] These suffixes are productive, and they attach to a good deal more than plain adjectives: to past participles (*abhikkantataro* 'more surpassing'), to compounds (*sīlasampannataraṁ* 'endowed with greater virtue'), and even to nouns (*sappurisataro* 'a better man').

**An older pair, *-iya* and *-iṭṭha***, survives in a few words: *pāpa* 'bad' → *pāpiyo*, *pāpiṭṭha*.[^papittha]

[^tara-tama]: *Piyataro* is canonical; *piyatamo* occurs nowhere, and the superlative shows up once in the whole of the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana, as the instrumental *piyatamena*. A grammar's neat pairs invite the conclusion that both members are equally available, and they are not.

[^papittha]: The superlative *pāpiṭṭha* is a curious case. In the nominative singular *pāpiṭṭho* it occurs only in the grammarians' texts, but its own doubled comparative *pāpiṭṭhatara* is thoroughly canonical, with *pāpiṭṭhataraṁ* at 17 occurrences in the root texts and four further inflected shapes beside it. The canon uses the comparative of the superlative more readily than the superlative.

**A third suffix, *-ma***, makes superlatives from a small set, often with the stem vowel changed to *-i*: *majjha* 'middle' → *majjhima*, *anta* 'end' → *antima* 'last', *pacchā* 'after' → *pacchima* 'last', *para* → *parama* 'highest', *pura* → *purima* 'former'.

The irregulars have to be learnt as vocabulary, because they are frequent and because they do not look like the words they belong to:

| Positive | Comparative | Superlative |
|---|---|---|
| *sant* 'good' | *seyya* 'better' | *seṭṭha* 'best' |
| *yuva* 'young' | *kaniya* | *kaniṭṭha* 'youngest' |
| *vuḍḍha* 'old' | *jeyya* | *jeṭṭha* 'eldest' |
| *bahu* 'much' | *bhiyyo* 'more' | — |

*Seṭṭha* and *jeṭṭha* and *kaniṭṭha* are everywhere in the canon, the first in the Buddha's own titles and the other two in every account of a family. *Bhiyyo* is likewise common, and it does not decline: it is an adverb, 'more, further'.

A curiosity, and evidence of how far these had drifted from the words they came from: several have themselves acquired a fresh comparative. The canon has *pāpiṭṭhataraṁ*, a comparative built on a superlative meaning 'worst'; *seyyataraṁ*, one built on 'better'; and *purimatarāni*, one built on 'former'. The suffix had stopped being felt as a suffix.[^double-comparative]

The comparative and superlative endings also attach to prefixes and adverbs, making adjectives out of them: *ud-* 'up' gives *uttara* 'higher' and *uttama* 'highest'; *adha-* 'down' gives *adhara* and *adhama* 'lowest'; *upari* 'above' gives *uparima* 'uppermost'.[^prefix-comparative]

[^comparative]: Perniola §39, pp. 48–52, twelve subsections, which is the fullest treatment in any source consulted. Oberlies handles the comparatives under §38.2, *transfer of °as- into a-stems*, because the *-iya* comparative is historically an *-yas*-stem, and so *seyya* and *bhiyyo* look as they do. The old instrumental of *bhiyyo* survives in one place, the fixed phrase *yebhuyyena* 'for the most part', common enough at 129 occurrences in the root texts, but *bhuyyena* is never found standing on its own.

[^double-comparative]: Perniola §39(g), p. 49, gives four: *seyyatara*, *seṭṭhatara*, *pāpiṭṭhatara*, *purimatara*. Three of the four are found in the texts; only *seṭṭhatara* is not, occurring in the grammarians' texts alone. All three of the survivors are canonical only in inflected forms (*pāpiṭṭhataraṁ* 17 occurrences in the root texts, *purimatarāni* 10, *seyyataraṁ* 1), so the chapter prints them as the canon has them, not as bare stems. Each of the three is missing in its nominative singular and is found only through the stem. A word the canon uses only in oblique cases will always read as absent if it is looked up in a nominative the canon never uses.

[^prefix-comparative]: Perniola §39(l), p. 51, for the forms built on prepositions and adverbs; *majjhima* and *antima* come from his §39(d), p. 49, which is the *-ma* superlative with the stem vowel raised to *-i*. Of the forms he prints, *uttaraṁ*, *uttamo*, *pacchimo*, *paramo*, *purimo* and *majjhimo* are all canonical; *adhamo* and *antimo* occur in the commentaries but not the root texts, and are given above as glosses, not as canonical forms.

How a comparison is expressed in a sentence (what case the thing compared against goes into) is a question about the cases and belongs with them; the chapter on case syntax takes it up under the ablative.

### Nouns made from adjectives

The line between adjective and noun is thin in English and thinner in Pāli. English can say 'the grass is green' and 'green is a beautiful colour', using one word two ways, and Pāli does this with almost every adjective it has: a word listed in the dictionary as an adjective can nearly always stand on its own as a noun.

When such a noun names a thing or a quality it is generally **neuter**: *dukkhaṁ* 'suffering', *kusalaṁ* 'the wholesome'. When it names a person it takes the gender of the person, masculine or feminine.

- Loke samaññaṁ **kusalo** viditvā … (SN 1.25 — S i 15)
- Having understood **the skilled one's** designation in the world …

Context is the only guide. In *riñcati kusalaṁ ṭhānaṁ* above, *kusalaṁ* is an adjective describing *ṭhānaṁ*; standing alone it would be the noun 'the wholesome'.

### Adverbs made from adjectives

English turns an adjective into an adverb with *-ly*: *kind* becomes *kindly*. Pāli uses the accusative singular of the adjective. *Visama* 'uneven, unjust' gives *visamaṁ* 'unjustly'; *dīgha* 'long' gives *dīghaṁ* 'at length'; *sādhuka* 'good' gives *sādhukaṁ* 'well'; *nicca* 'constant' gives *niccaṁ* 'constantly'; *cira* 'long' gives *ciraṁ* 'for a long time'.

Only context distinguishes such an adverb from the accusative it is made of, and the test is simple: ask what the word is describing. If it describes a noun it is an adjective in the accusative; if it describes a verb it is an adverb. The pairs below make the test concrete.

- Gottena mattā **visamaṁ** caranti. (SN 35.132 — S iv 117)
- Drunk on their lineage, they behave **unjustly**.

- **Visamaṁ** maggamāruyha … (SN 2.22 — S i 57)
- Having taken **the uneven** road …

- Dīghaṁ vā assasanto '**dīghaṁ** assasāmī'ti pajānāti. (MN 118 — M iii 82)
- Breathing in long, he knows 'I breathe in **long**'.

- **Dīghaṁ** santassa yojanaṁ. (Dhp 60 — Dhp p. 9)
- **Long** is a league to the weary.

In the first of each pair the word answers "how?" and modifies the verb; in the second it answers "which?" and modifies a noun. *Visamaṁ caranti* is 'they walk unevenly'; *visamaṁ maggaṁ* is 'an uneven road'.

Strictly, such adverbs are no longer in a case at all. They are **indeclinables** that happen to have the form of an accusative, frozen in it; the chapter on indeclinables returns to them.

### Where this chapter stops

What the cases mean and how comparison is constructed are in the chapter on case syntax. How a noun keeps or changes its gender inside a compound is in the chapter on compounds, where the *bahubbīhi* type turns whole phrases into adjectives. And the **complete declensions**, for adjectives as for nouns, are in Appendix A.1, since an adjective has no endings of its own.

**Exercises for this chapter are in the workbook**, under "Exercises: Gender and Adjectives", with worked answers at the back of that volume.

**Principal sources.** Perniola §§24, 31, 38 and 39 for gender, agreement, the formation of the feminine, and the comparative; Oberlies §§28.2 and 38.2 for where the comparatives came from; the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana itself for which of the forms the grammars print are actually used.

## Pronouns

### What a pronoun is

A **pronoun** is a word that stands in the place of a noun: *he*, *this*, *who*, *anyone*. Pāli has them in quantity, and one fact about the language shapes everything in this chapter: a Pāli verb already carries its own person, so a pronoun subject is never structurally required. *Gacchāmi* is a complete sentence, "I go". When *ahaṁ* 'I' turns up in front of it, then, it does more than fill a gap. It emphasises, or contrasts, or picks one referent out of several, and reading Pāli well means asking which.

The indigenous grammarians call the whole class *sabbanāma*, 'all-name', and their inventory is a closed list of twenty-seven words.[^sabbanama-list] It is not the list a European grammar would draw up. It counts the numerals *eka* 'one', *ubha* 'both', *dvi* 'two', *ti* 'three' and *catu* 'four' among the pronouns, and the directional adjectives *dakkhiṇa* 'southern, right', *uttara* 'northern, upper' and *adhara* 'lower' as well. That matters for the section on **pronominal adjectives** below, because the awkward category it has to describe is the residue: the words the tradition counts as pronouns and a European description does not.

[^sabbanama-list]: *Sabba, katara, katama, ubhaya, itara, añña, aññatara, aññatama, pubba, para, apara, dakkhiṇa, uttara, adhara, ya, ta, eta, ima, amu, kiṁ, eka, ubha, dvi, ti, catu, tumha, amha*: Saddanīti I 150, reported by Thānuttamo, *Māgadhabhāsā*, p. 55, with Collins, *A Pali Grammar for Students* §2.2.

Two general facts hold across the whole class. Pronouns decline for case and number like nouns, and the demonstratives and interrogatives decline for gender as well; but no pronoun has a vocative, so the tables below run six cases and not seven. And a great many of them share one set of endings, the *pronominal declension*, so the *ta-*, *eta-*, *ya-* and *ka-* paradigms will soon start to look repetitive. That is a mercy, not a coincidence: learn one and you have most of four.

### Personal pronouns

Strictly, Pāli has personal pronouns for two persons only. The first person is built on the stem *amha-* and the second on *tumha-*; for the third it simply uses the demonstrative *ta(d)* 'that', so that "he", "she", "it" and "that one" are one and the same word. **Demonstrative pronouns** below is where that word is set out, and so the two tables here are complete as they stand, not missing a third.

Neither the first nor the second person inflects for gender. In Pāli as in English, a man and a woman alike say *ahaṁ*.

#### First person: *ahaṁ* 'I'

|            | sg.                            | pl.                      |
|------------|--------------------------------|--------------------------|
| nom.       | *ahaṁ*                         | *mayaṁ*                  |
| acc.       | *maṁ*, *mamaṁ*, *me*           | *amhe*, *no*             |
