So *ubho* is the last dual in the language, not an irregular plural, and the *-o* that looks like a masculine nominative singular is nothing of the kind.[^ubho-dual] Beside it stands *ubhaya-*, which means the same thing and declines as an ordinary *a*-stem: the regularised replacement, which is how a language usually disposes of a category it no longer has.

- **Appamādo** kho, mahārāja, eko dhammo, yo **ubho** atthe samadhiggayha tiṭṭhati. (SN 3.17 — S i 86)
- *appamādo* diligence · *kho* indeed · *mahārāja* great king (voc.) · *eko* one · *dhammo* thing · *yo* which · *ubho* both (acc.) · *atthe* benefits (m. acc. pl.) · *samadhiggayha* having secured · *tiṭṭhati* stands
- "Diligence, great king, is the one thing that secures **both** benefits."

The next sentence says which two: *diṭṭhadhammikañceva atthaṁ samparāyikañca*, the benefit belonging to this visible life and the benefit belonging to what comes after. *Ubho* is used as a dual exists to be used: pointing at a pair already known to both speakers, not counting to two.

[^dual-loss]: Oberlies §43(2), p. 192. He gives the stems from which 'two' was reassembled (*d[u]vā-*, *d[(u)v]i-*, *d[v]e-*, *du[v]-* and *bā-*) and notes that the nominative/accusative feminine and neuter *d(u)ve* (compare Old Indic *duvé*) was transferred to the masculine, its ending then matching *ime*, *te*, *sabbe*. The locative *duve* is used only in the late Apadāna (443,1/3), and the 'eastern' locative *duvesu* stands beside *dvīsu*.

[^ubho-dual]: Oberlies §43(2), pp. 192–193: "*ubha-* has generalised the *o* of the nom. (< *ubhau*)", giving *ubho*, *ubhohi*, *ubhinnaṁ*, *ubhosu*. He notes *ubhaya-* inflects as an *a*-stem, used in both singular and plural with a plural verb, and that it is contaminated with *dvi-* to give *dubhaya-* (Saddanīti V 1451). The indigenous tradition counts *ubha* among the twenty-seven *sabbanāmāni* (see the pronoun chapter), so a reader meets it in lists of pronouns as often as in lists of numerals. Perniola p. 65 prints the same four forms independently, classing *ubho* with *dvi* as "declined only in the plural".

### Five till eighteen

From five to eighteen the difficulty stops. These numerals have no gender at all, they exist only in the plural, and they all take one set of endings:

| | | | |
|---|---|---|---|
| *pañca* | five | *dvādasa* | twelve |
| *cha* | six | *terasa* | thirteen |
| *satta* | seven | *cuddasa* | fourteen |
| *aṭṭha* | eight | *paṇṇarasa* | fifteen |
| *nava* | nine | *soḷasa* | sixteen |
| *dasa* | ten | *sattarasa* | seventeen |
| *ekādasa* | eleven | *aṭṭhārasa* | eighteen |

The teens are each a unit compounded with 'ten', and the second element shows up in two shapes: *-dasa* in *ekādasa*, *dvādasa* and *cuddasa*, and *-rasa* in *terasa*, *paṇṇarasa*, *sattarasa* and *aṭṭhārasa*. Both are inherited, and neither is a misprint for the other.[^teens]

They are inflected as follows:

| gender     | m. & n. & f. |
|------------|--------------|
| nom./acc.  | *-a*         |
| inst./abl. | *-ahi*       |
| gen./dat.  | *-annaṁ*[^pancannam-dental] |
| loc.       | *-asu*       |

[^teens]: Oberlies §43(1), p. 196, gives the variants and their derivations: 'thirteen' is *terasa-* / *telasa-* < *trayadaśa-*, with a further form in *-ḷ-* < *-ḍ-*; 'fourteen' *catuddasa-* also shows "abnormal loss of *-t-*", giving *cuddasa-*; and "the genuine MIA forms of 'fifteen' are *paṇṇarasa-* and *pannarasa-*". So the two forms are several separate developments that happen to converge on two endings, not one rule applied unevenly, and the list has to be learnt, not derived.

[^pancannam-dental]: The genitive/dative is dental *-annaṁ*, not retroflex *-aṇṇaṁ*. DPD derives the form transparently (*pañca* + *ānaṁ* > *annaṁ*, Sanskrit *pañcānām*), and *pañcaṇṇaṁ* does not occur anywhere in the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana, against ready attestation of *pañcannaṁ*, *channaṁ*, *sattannaṁ*, *aṭṭhannaṁ* and *dasannaṁ*.

- **Pañca** saññā bhāvitā bahulīkatā mahapphalā honti mahānisaṁsā. (AN 5.62 — A iii 79)
- **Five** perceptions, developed and cultivated, are of great fruit, great benefit.

- **Aṭṭhahi**, bhikkhave, ākārehi itthī purisaṁ bandhati. (AN 8.17 — A iv 196)
- Bhikkhus, a woman binds a man **in eight** ways.

### Nineteen and upwards

Something changes at nineteen, and it is not that the words merely get longer. Up to eighteen a numeral is an **adjective**: it has no gender of its own, it takes the gender of whatever it counts, and it stands in that noun's case. From nineteen upwards a numeral is a **noun**. It has a gender of its own, it is normally singular however many things it counts, and the thing counted must be attached to it by some other means than agreement.

Perniola states the boundary as a flat rule ("The numerals from 1 to 18 are adjectives, from 20 onwards are nouns") and leaves nineteen unassigned. Oberlies puts it at nineteen and supplies the reason: nineteen is *ekūnavīsati*, 'twenty less one', a word built on 'twenty'. It belongs with what follows it, not with what precedes.[^nineteen-boundary]

| | | | |
|---|---|---|---|
| 19 | *ekūnavīsati* | 70 | *sattati* |
| 20 | *vīsati*, *vīsa* | 80 | *asīti* |
| 30 | *tiṁsati*, *tiṁsa* | 90 | *navuti* |
| 40 | *cattālīsa*, *cattārīsa* | 100 | *sataṁ* |
| 50 | *paññāsa*, *paṇṇāsa* | 1,000 | *sahassaṁ* |
| 60 | *saṭṭhi* | 100,000 | *satasahassaṁ*, *lakkhaṁ* |

Between the decades the unit and the decade simply compound: *ekavīsati* 'twenty-one', *dvattiṁsa* 'thirty-two', *caturāsīti* 'eighty-four'. Where 'two' is the unit the compounding form is normally *dvā-*, not *dve-*, and its vowel shortens before a double consonant: hence *dvāvīsa* 'twenty-two' beside *dvattiṁsa*. That *dvā-* has a further development to *bā-*, and for 'twenty-two' the canon prefers it: *bāvīsati* and *bāvīsa* are both commoner than *dvāvīsa*.[^dvavisa-forms]

[^dvavisa-forms]: The grammars give *dvāvīsati* and *dvattiṁsati*, and neither is root-text Pāli. They are the grammars' forms, carrying the *-ti* of *vīsati* through into the compound, and the canon drops it: *dvattiṁsa* stands ten times in the root texts against *dvattiṁsati*'s none (three occurrences in the whole of the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana, all in the grammatical literature), and *dvāvīsati* has a single root-text occurrence, in the Visuddhimagga, not the canon proper. For 'twenty-two' the attested forms are *bāvīsa* (15), *dvāvīsa* (4) and *bāvīsati* (3 outside the Visuddhimagga); DPD derives the third from the first two, *dvi* > *dva* > *bā* + *vīsati*, so they are one word and not three. All of *bāvīsa*'s occurrences and all of *dvāvīsa*'s but one are in the Paṭṭhāna's counting summaries, standing in series with *aṭṭhārasa* 18, *dvādasa* 12, *tevīsa* 23 and *pañcavīsa* 25: cardinals, though DPD lemmatises the bare *dvāvīsa* as an ordinal and gives *dvāvīsā* for the cardinal. The rule the paragraph teaches is unaffected (*dvā-* keeps its long vowel before a single consonant and shortens before a double one), and the attested pair shows it more cleanly than the grammars' pair did. The edition itself sets a trap: the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana files the Visuddhimagga among the root texts, so a small root-text count may be Buddhaghosa in the fifth century, not the canon, and here it was.

Three formations do what a modern reader expects arithmetic to do. All three should be recognisable on sight, but they are not equally canonical, and the difference matters. *Ekūna-* 'less one' counts **down** from the next round number, and it is everywhere in the suttas: *ekūnavīsati* 19, *ekūnatiṁsa* 29, *ekūnapaññāsa* 49. *Uttara-* 'over' counts up from the round number instead, so *vīsuttarasataṁ* is 'a hundred with twenty over', 120 — but no canonical text says it, or any of its fellows. That formation belongs to the commentaries and the later chronicles.[^uttara-postcanonical] And the halves are handled by prefixing *aḍḍha-* 'half' to an **ordinal**, which names the hundred being half-completed, not the one already finished: *diyaḍḍha-* is 'one and a half', so *diyaḍḍhasataṁ* is 150, literally 'a hundred and the second half-done'.[^halves-ordinal]

That last formation is more than a curiosity. It is how the canon states the size of the Pātimokkha:

- Sādhikamidaṁ, bhante, **diyaḍḍhasikkhāpadasataṁ** anvaddhamāsaṁ uddesaṁ āgacchati. (AN 3.84 — A i 230)
- *sādhikaṁ* with more besides · *idaṁ* this · *bhante* venerable sir · *diyaḍḍha·sikkhāpada·sataṁ* one-and-a-half-hundred training rules (n. nom. sg.) · *anvaddhamāsaṁ* every fortnight · *uddesaṁ* to the recitation · *āgacchati* comes
- "Sir, **this hundred and fifty training rules** comes up for recitation every fortnight, and more besides."

The Vajjian monk who says this is complaining that he cannot train in so many rules. Grammatically, *diyaḍḍhasikkhāpadasataṁ* is a single neuter **singular** (one word, one ending) for a hundred and fifty things, and that the verb *āgacchati* is singular to match. A numeral of this size does not agree with what it counts; it swallows it.

The declension follows the ending, not the number, and there are four patterns:[^higher-declension]

| Ending | Declines as | Example |
|---|---|---|
| *-ti* (*vīsati*, *saṭṭhi*, *asīti*, *navuti*, *koṭi*) | feminine *i*-stem, like *jāti* | *vīsati*, *vīsatiṁ*, *vīsatiyā*, *vīsatiyaṁ* |
| *-ā* (*cattālīsā*, *paññāsā*) | feminine *ā*-stem, like *vanitā* | *vīsā*, *vīsaṁ*, *vīsāya*, *vīsāyaṁ* |
| *-aṁ* (*sataṁ*, *sahassaṁ*) | neuter *a*-stem, singular and plural | *sataṁ*, *satena*, *satassa*; *satāni*, *satehi*, *satānaṁ*, *satesu* |
| *-a* (*vīsa*, *tiṁsa*) | usually uninflected | *vīsa* |

[^nineteen-boundary]: Perniola §50, p. 64; Oberlies §43(1), p. 196: "The numerals 'one' through 'eighteen' are used as adjectives, unless they inflect as neuters (sg.) in analogy with *vīsaṁ* (< *viṁśat*). The numerals 'nineteen' and upwards are neuter or feminine substantives in *-aṁ* or *-ā* and *-ti* respectively." The two are not in conflict: Perniola's silence about nineteen is what Oberlies's derivation fills.

[^halves-ordinal]: Oberlies §43(3), p. 198: "To denote fractional sense *aḍḍha-* is added to the next higher ordinal: *aḍḍhatiya-* 'two and a half', *aḍḍhuḍḍha-* 'three and a half'." Perniola §53, p. 68, gives the series as *diyaḍḍha*, *aḍḍhatiya* / *aḍḍhateyya*, *aḍḍhuḍḍha* for 1½, 2½, 3½, and p. 64 gives *diyaḍḍhasataṁ* 150 and *diyaḍḍhasahassaṁ* 1500. Both halves of the system are attested: *diyaḍḍhasikkhāpadasataṁ* at AN 3.84, and *aḍḍhateyyāni niruttisatāni* '250 kinds of language' in the Paṭisambhidāmagga, where the surrounding count (*pañcavīsatisataṁ* 125, then 250, then *pañca ñāṇasatāni* 500) confirms the arithmetic from the passage itself. Perniola's examples for 108 are *aṭṭhādhika sataṁ* and *aṭṭhuttara sataṁ*, which he prints as two words; the texts write them as single words, and as such *aṭṭhuttarasata* occurs three times and *aṭṭhādhikasataṁ* once. The note on the *uttara-* numerals below says where they occur.

[^uttara-postcanonical]: The *uttara-* numerals are **post-canonical**. Across the whole of the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana the type occurs only outside the root texts: *vīsuttarasataṁ* once in the commentaries, *dasuttarasataṁ* three times there and three more in the later grammatical and chronicle works, *aṭṭhuttarasata* three times in those works, *catuttarasate* and *dvādasuttarasata-* likewise, and *aṭṭhādhikasataṁ* once in the commentaries, and nothing at all in the four nikāyas or the Vinaya. The *ekūna-* type, by contrast, is canonical and common. Nor is there anything to choose between *vīsuttarasataṁ* and Perniola's *aṭṭhuttara sataṁ* (p. 63) on grounds of attestation: the two stand in the same relation to the canon, which is outside it.

[^higher-declension]: Perniola §50(e)–(h), pp. 65–66. *Koṭi* 'ten million' is feminine and declines with the *-ti* group. The fourth row is the fact Oberlies reports from the syntactic side ("Often, however, the numerals are not inflected at all", p. 196), and it explains why a higher numeral so often gives a reader no case information at all.

### How a numeral joins its noun

The chapter opened with a warning, and here it is made good. It is often said that a numeral takes the case of the noun it counts. For *eka* through *aṭṭhārasa* that is true, and for everything above it is one option among four. Oberlies sets the four out in a single sentence: the numeral "may be used appositionally in the same case as the substantive, or else the qualified substantive may be put in the genitive plural. Often, however, the numerals are not inflected at all. Finally, a determinative compound may be formed."[^four-constructions]

**Agreement** is the first, and needs no comment beyond an example. The Mahāpadāna Sutta happens to give it twice over, in two different cases in consecutive sentences:

- … lābhinī bodhisattamātā hoti **pañcannaṁ** kāmaguṇānaṁ. Sā **pañcahi** kāmaguṇehi samappitā samaṅgībhūtā paricāreti. (DN 14 — D ii 13)
- *lābhinī* one who obtains (f. nom. sg.) · *bodhisattamātā* the Bodhisatta's mother · *hoti* is · *pañcannaṁ* of five (gen. pl.) · *kāmaguṇānaṁ* of the kinds of sensual pleasure (m. gen. pl.) · *sā* she · *pañcahi* with five (inst. pl.) · *kāmaguṇehi* with the kinds of sensual pleasure (m. inst. pl.) · *samappitā* provided · *samaṅgībhūtā* endowed · *paricāreti* amuses herself
- … the Bodhisatta's mother obtains the **five** kinds of sensual pleasure. Provided and endowed with the **five** kinds of sensual pleasure, she amuses herself.

*Pañcannaṁ* against *pañcahi*: one word, two cases, each matching its noun, and nothing more.

**The genitive** is the second, and it catches a reader trained on the first. Here the numeral is a noun in its own right and the thing counted hangs off it in the genitive plural: 'a thousand *of* moons', not 'a thousand moons':

- Tasmiṁ **sahassadhā** loke **sahassaṁ** candānaṁ **sahassaṁ** sūriyānaṁ … **cattāri** mahāsamuddasahassāni. (AN 10.29 — A v 59)
- *tasmiṁ* in that · *sahassadhā* thousandfold · *loke* world · *sahassaṁ* a thousand (n. nom. sg.) · *candānaṁ* of moons (m. gen. pl.) · *sūriyānaṁ* of suns (m. gen. pl.) · *cattāri* four (n. nom. pl.) · *mahāsamudda·sahassāni* thousands of great oceans (n. nom. pl.)
- "In that thousandfold world there are a **thousand** moons, a **thousand** suns … and **four** thousand great oceans."

The Buddha is describing the thousandfold world-system, and the sentence is a gift to a grammarian: it runs the genitive construction eleven times and then, without a break, switches to the fourth construction for the oceans. *Sahassaṁ candānaṁ* is a singular neuter noun governing a genitive; *mahāsamuddasahassāni* is a compound, plural, with *cattāri* agreeing with it. Two constructions, one sentence, and the reason for the switch is arithmetic: four thousand cannot be said with one *sahassaṁ*.

**No inflection at all** is the third, and it is less a construction than the absence of one. The numerals in *-a* commonly stand as they are, and a reader gets no case from them; the noun has to be read first and the numeral attached to it afterwards.

**The compound** is the fourth, and in practice the canon reaches for it whenever a large round number qualifies a noun:

- Tānipi kho **pañcabhikkhusatāni** … tikkhattuṁ citakaṁ padakkhiṇaṁ katvā bhagavato pāde sirasā vandiṁsu. (DN 16 — D ii 163)
- *tāni* those (n. nom. pl.) · *pi* too · *kho* indeed · *pañca·bhikkhu·satāni* five hundreds of bhikkhus (n. nom. pl.) · *tikkhattuṁ* three times · *citakaṁ* the pyre (m. acc. sg.) · *padakkhiṇaṁ katvā* having circumambulated · *bhagavato pāde* the Blessed One's feet · *sirasā* with the head · *vandiṁsu* paid homage
- "Those **five hundred bhikkhus** too … circumambulated the pyre three times and paid homage with their heads at the Blessed One's feet."

Note what the compound costs and what it buys. *Pañcabhikkhusatāni* is **neuter**, because *sata* is neuter, even though the five hundred are monks; *tāni* is neuter to agree with it, and only the verb *vandiṁsu* remembers that people are meant. Expect the gender of the counted noun to survive and you will misread the pronoun.

Small numerals compound too, and there the gender comes out the other way. The Vinaya's definition of 'inhabited area' runs four of them in series:

- **Ekakuṭikopi** gāmo, **dvikuṭikopi** gāmo, **tikuṭikopi** gāmo, **catukuṭikopi** gāmo … (Bu Pj 2 — Vin iii 46)
- *ekakuṭiko* having one hut (m. nom. sg.) · *pi* also · *gāmo* an inhabited area (m. nom. sg.) · *dvikuṭiko* having two huts · *tikuṭiko* having three · *catukuṭiko* having four
- "An area with **one hut** is an inhabited area, an area with **two huts** is an inhabited area, with **three huts**, with **four huts** …"

The stems in composition are *eka-*, *dvi-*, *ti-* and *catu-*, and none of them is a cell of the table above; a numeral stands there in its bare stem, as any other first member does. The gender goes the other way from *pañcabhikkhusatāni* because the compound is of a different class. *Ekakuṭika* is possessive ('having one hut'), so it takes the gender of what it describes, and *gāmo* is masculine; *pañcabhikkhusatāni* is determinative, so it keeps the neuter of its own last member *sata*, and Chapter 13 sets the two classes out. What decides is the class of the compound, not the numeral inside it.[^numeral-compound-class]

[^numeral-compound-class]: Oberlies's fourth construction (§43, p. 197) names the determinative compound specifically, and the possessive is not among his four. It has the same syntactic function (a numeral and its noun in a single word) and differs only in what the word then agrees with, so it is given here as an extension of his fourth, not as a fifth. DPD reads *ekakuṭika* as *eka* + *kuṭi* + *ka*, an adjective 'with one hut', and cites this passage for it. None of the four is printed bare anywhere in the canon, because the edition joins *pi* to each: *ekakuṭikopi* has 1 root-text occurrence, and *dvikuṭiko*, *tikuṭiko* and *catukuṭiko* do not occur at all. The resolution on the gloss line above is an analysis and not a quotation, which is the rule the chapter on sandhi states.

[^four-constructions]: Oberlies §43(1), pp. 196–197. His footnote there adds a fifth possibility from outside the canon: in texts like the Dīpavaṁsa a numeral (and *ubho* with it) can keep its case ending as the first member of a compound, *tīṇi-vassamhi nigrodho catuvassamhi bhātaro* (Dīp VII 31–32). Perniola reaches the third of the four from the morphological side, not the syntactic, at §50(h): "Numerals ending in *-a* are usually uninflected."

### Ordinal numbers

The first ten ordinals are:

| | | | |
|---|---|---|---|
| *paṭhama* | first | *chaṭṭha* | sixth |
| *dutiya* | second | *sattama* | seventh |
| *tatiya* | third | *aṭṭhama* | eighth |
| *catuttha* | fourth | *navama* | ninth |
| *pañcama* | fifth | *dasama* | tenth |

Only the first four have to be learnt as items. *Paṭhama* is built on the prefix *pa-* 'in front', not on *eka* at all; *dutiya* and *tatiya* are formed with a suffix *-tiya*; and *catuttha* and *chaṭṭha* are formed with *-tha*, so the fourth and sixth break the pattern the fifth establishes.[^ordinal-formation] From five upwards the rule is simply *-ma* added to the cardinal, and it does not stop: *ekādasama* 'eleventh', *vīsatima* 'twentieth', *sattatima* 'seventieth'. Between eleven and eighteen the suffix may also be dropped altogether, so that the cardinal serves as its own ordinal: *terasa* both 'thirteen' and 'thirteenth'. All of them inflect as ordinary *a*-stem adjectives, agreeing with their noun in gender, number and case.

The feminine deserves care, because the two grammars this chapter leans on most disagree about it, and the canon settles the question against the one that is usually right. Oberlies states that the feminine "is always in *-ā*, while those in *-ī* are used to denote dates". Perniola gives a different rule: the first three are in *-ā* (*paṭhamā*, *dutiyā*, *tatiyā*), and from the fourth upwards the feminine is in *-ī*, *catutthī*, *pañcamī*, *ekādasamī*. The Mahānidāna Sutta runs both across three consecutive sentences, with the same feminine noun each time:

- Ayaṁ **tatiyā** viññāṇaṭṭhiti. … Ayaṁ **catutthī** viññāṇaṭṭhiti. … Ayaṁ **pañcamī** viññāṇaṭṭhiti. (DN 15 — D ii 69)
- *ayaṁ* this (f. nom. sg.) · *tatiyā* third (f. nom. sg.) · *catutthī* fourth (f. nom. sg.) · *pañcamī* fifth (f. nom. sg.) · *viññāṇaṭṭhiti* station of consciousness (f. nom. sg.)
- "This is the **third** station of consciousness. … This is the **fourth** station of consciousness. … This is the **fifth** station of consciousness."

Nothing about a lunar fortnight is in view; these are stations of consciousness, being counted. The third is *tatiyā* and the fourth and fifth are *catutthī* and *pañcamī*, which is Perniola's rule set out by the text itself. So the feminine of the first three ordinals is in *-ā*, and from the fourth upwards it is normally in *-ī*.[^ordinal-feminine]

What is true in Oberlies's account is narrower than he states it, and the *-ī* forms are most often met within that narrower claim. Three of them have hardened into names for **dates**: the eighth, fourteenth and fifteenth days of the lunar fortnight, the uposatha days:

- Cātuddasī pañcadasī aṭṭhamī ca pakkhassa. (MN 4 — M i 20)
- *cātuddasī* the fourteenth (f.) · *pañcadasī* the fifteenth (f.) · *aṭṭhamī* the eighth (f.) · *ca* and · *pakkhassa* of the fortnight (m. gen. sg.)
- The fourteenth, the fifteenth, and the eighth of the fortnight.

The Bodhisatta is choosing the nights on which to go and sit in the frightening shrines of the forest, and he picks the three the tradition already marks. That is a lexical fact about three words, not a grammatical one about a whole class: *aṭṭhamī* still means simply 'eighth' elsewhere, and DPD accordingly carries it twice, once as the ordinal and once as the day.

[^ordinal-formation]: Perniola §51(a)–(e), pp. 66–67: *paṭhama* from the prefix *pa-* (Sanskrit *pra-*) 'in front' plus *ṭhama*; *dutiya*, *tatiya* with the suffix *-tiya*; *catur-tha* > *catuttha* and *cha-tha* > *chaṭṭha*; *pañc-ama*, *satt-ama*, *aṭṭh-ama*, *nav-ama*, *das-ama* with *-ama*; and for eleven to eighteen either *-ama* or bare *-a*, *ekādas-ama* or *ekādas-a*. Oberlies §43(2), p. 197, states the higher series independently ("The higher numbers (except for '60th') may be formed by adding *°ma-* to the cardinal: *vīsa-*, *vīsatima-* …, *saṭṭhitama-*. They are all inflected as *a*-stems"), and both he and Perniola make 'sixtieth' the exception, *saṭṭhitama-*, where Duroiselle p. 107 gives *saṭṭhima*. **Neither form is canonical.** *Saṭṭhitama-* does not occur at all in the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana, under any ending; *saṭṭhimo* occurs once, in a grammarian's own list of the numerals (*paññāso, paññāsamo. Saṭṭhyādito purimasuttena mo, saṭṭhimo, sattatimo*), a derivation, not a use. Three grammars therefore disagree about a form the canon never has occasion to write, and 'sixtieth' is better treated as a gap in the attested series than as an irregularity in it. Thānuttamo p. 75, citing Kaccāyana 373 and 666, derives *paṭhama* instead from *putha* + *ama*, and DPD gives a third analysis, *pa* + *tama* > *ṭhama*, that is, the superlative suffix of Chapter 4, which Perniola's bare *ṭhama* leaves unanalysed.

    Duroiselle's second column is not safe to take forms from. At p. 106 he prints a doubled ordinal for four, five, six, seven and ten: *catuttha, turīya*; *pañcatha, pañcama*; *chaṭṭha, chaṭṭhama*; *sattha, sattama*; *dasma*. Of the extra forms, only *chaṭṭhama* is attested, and chiefly in verse (*chaṭṭhamaṁ bhagavā brūhi*, Snp 1.6 — Sn p. 19; also the Buddhavaṁsa, and a Kathāvatthu feminine *chaṭṭhamī*); *turīya*, *pañcatha* and *dasma* do not occur anywhere in the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana, and *turīya* is not in DPD at all, while *sattha* is attested only in its unrelated senses. He was working from the indigenous grammarians, who generate such forms, and he does not mark which of them the canon uses.

[^ordinal-feminine]: Oberlies §43(2), p. 197: "Their feminine is always in *-ā*, while those in *-ī* are used to denote dates (*aṭṭhamī-* '8th day', *cātuddasī-* '14th day' [see CDIAL 4606], *pañcadasī-* '15th [day]')." Perniola §51(g) and p. 68: "The feminine of the first three numbers ends in *-ā*: *paṭhamā, dutiyā, tatityā* [so printed; read *tatiyā*]. The feminine of all the other numeral ordinals ends in *-ī*: *catutthī, pañcamī, ekādasamī / ekādasī, tiṁsatimī / timī*." (His last form is printed *timī*, checked against the page image; it is a second slip of the *tatityā* kind, for *tiṁsī*. Neither spelling is canonical (*tiṁsī* and *tiṁsatimī* occur nowhere and *timī* once, in a ṭīkā), so these are paradigm completions, not forms the texts use.) DN 15 — D ii 69 decides it, and the Kathāvatthu's *antarābhava* discussion decides it again with a masculine in the same breath: *pañcamī sā yoni, chaṭṭhamī sā gati, aṭṭhamī sā viññāṇaṭṭhiti, dasamo so sattāvāso*, three feminines in *-ī* against one masculine in *-o*. DPD gives *aṭṭhamī* under two headwords, 'eighth' (from the Sīvaka Sutta) and 'eighth day of the lunar fortnight' (from the Bhayabherava Sutta), the same division of labour. The *-ā* feminine is not extinct above the third (the Dhammasaṅgaṇī has *catutthāya bhūmiyā* 'of the fourth plane' beside *dutiyāya* and *tatiyāya*), so *-ī* is the norm, not the only option.

- **Dutiyo** pi samaṇo n'atthi. **Tatiyo** pi samaṇo n'atthi. **Catuttho** pi samaṇo n'atthi. (DN 16 — D ii 152)
- There is no **second** (type of) renunciant. There also is no **third** renunciant. There also is no **fourth** renunciant.

### What an ordinal does besides count

Three uses of the ordinal have nothing to do with ranking a series, and all three are common enough to trip anyone who knows only the counting sense.

The accusative is a temporal adverb. *Paṭhamaṁ* means 'the first time', not 'the first', and *dutiyaṁ* 'the second time'. The form is an ordinary neuter accusative singular used adverbially, as the accusative is throughout the language; what makes it need naming is that the English is a different part of speech.[^ordinal-adverb]

Compounded with *atta(n)-* 'self', an ordinal counts a company including oneself. *Attadutiya-* is 'oneself with one companion', literally 'self as second', and *attacatuttha-* is 'oneself with three others'. The idiom counts the speaker in, so the number is always one higher than the number of companions. For a reader of the Vinaya and the Suttas this is live material, since it is how the texts state the size of a travelling party: Ānanda takes his bowl and robe and enters Kusinārā *attadutiyo*, with one companion.[^atta-ordinal]

And an ordinal is occasionally read where a cardinal would be expected. The stock example is *kaṇṭhapañcamehi bandhanehi*, said of a child tied at the four limbs and the neck, and Oberlies takes it as an ordinal standing in for a cardinal: 'with five bonds'. The compound will not quite carry that. Its first member is *kaṇṭha-* 'neck', which the abbreviated citation drops, and with it in place the phrase reads 'with bonds of which the neck is the fifth': the ordinal numbering the last member of a set, not replacing a count. DPD glosses the compound both ways at once, 'in five places' for the sense and 'with neck as the fifth' for the literal, and that is the sound position. So the use is real but this is not its clearest case, and meeting an ordinal where the sense demands a cardinal, look first for a compound of this kind.[^ordinal-for-cardinal]

[^ordinal-adverb]: Oberlies §43(2), p. 197 n. 3: "The acc. of the ordinals is used to form temporal adverbs: *paṭhamaṁ* 'the first time', *dutiyaṁ* 'the second time' (see Geiger §119.3)." Perniola reaches the same forms from the other end, listing them at §54(a) as one of the five ways of forming a **multiplicative**: "by using the accusative neuter of the ordinal numerals: *paṭhamaṁ* 'for the first time', *dutiyaṁ* 'for the second time'". The two descriptions are of one form, and the difference is only whether it is filed under the ordinal or under the multiplicative.

[^atta-ordinal]: Oberlies §43(2), p. 197 n. 4: "Noteworthy are compounds of ordinal numbers with *atta(n)-*: *attaduttiya-* 'oneself with one companion', D II 147,21, *attacatuttha-* 'oneself with three others', M I 393,21." His *attaduttiya-* carries a double *t* that the texts do not. The Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana has *attadutiyo* and its inflections throughout and no instance of the doubled form; DPD's headword is *attadutiya*, from *atta* + *dutiya*, cited from the Mahāparinibbāna Sutta (Oberlies's own D II reference), where Ānanda enters Kusinārā *attadutiyo*. *Attacatuttha-* is right as he gives it, attested as *attacatuttho* and *attacatutthe*.

[^ordinal-for-cardinal]: Oberlies §43(2), p. 197 n. 4: "Sporadically the ordinal number is used instead of the cardinal number: *pañcamehi bandhanehi* 'with five bonds', S IV 201,22, 202,9 (see Geiger §118.4 and Oberlies 1997: 10-11)." The canonical phrase is *kaṇṭhapañcamehi bandhanehi*, not *pañcamehi bandhanehi*. Bare *pañcamehi* occurs nowhere in the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana; *kaṇṭhapañcamehi* occurs fourteen times in the canon, including the Yavakalāpi Sutta at his own S IV reference and the Vekhanasa Sutta at MN 80. DPD reads *kaṇṭhapañcama* as 'in five places; in arms, legs and neck', literally 'with neck as the fifth'. On that reading the ordinal is the final member of a bahubbīhi with an ordinal sense, so the phrase does not demonstrate what it is cited for. The use itself is reported by Geiger §118.4 independently and is not in doubt; the example is.

### Numerals beyond counting

Four further classes are built on the numerals, and the indigenous tradition treats all four as part of the topic, not as afterthoughts to it. Thānuttamo divides the field into six kinds where a Western grammar tends to give two; the four below are the ones the previous sections have not covered.[^six-classes]

**Multiplicatives** answer 'how many times'. The main formation adds *-kkhattuṁ* to the stem (*sakiṁ* 'once', *dvikkhattuṁ* 'twice', *tikkhattuṁ* 'three times', *dasakkhattuṁ* 'ten times'), and the third of these is one of the most frequently read words in the canon, because it is what a visitor does on arrival and on departure:

- … **tikkhattuṁ** citakaṁ padakkhiṇaṁ katvā bhagavato pāde sirasā vandi. (DN 16 — D ii 163)
- *tikkhattuṁ* three times · *citakaṁ* the pyre (m. acc. sg.) · *padakkhiṇaṁ* keeping to the right · *katvā* having done · *bhagavato* of the Blessed One (m. gen. sg.) · *pāde* the feet (m. acc. pl.) · *sirasā* with his head (n. inst. sg.) · *vandi* paid homage
- … having circumambulated the pyre **three times**, he paid homage with his head at the Blessed One's feet.

Beside *-kkhattuṁ* stand two other means to the same end: the noun *vāra* 'turn, occasion' with a cardinal or an ordinal (*cattāro vārā* 'four times', *catuttha vāre* 'on the fourth occasion'), and the ordinal accusative already met above.

**Adverbs in *-dhā*** divide something into parts: *dvidhā* 'in two parts', *sattadhā* 'in seven parts', *bahudhā* 'in many ways', *sahassadhā* 'in a thousand ways'. The series has no canonical member for 'one', which is reasonable enough in a suffix whose business is dividing.[^ekadha]

[^ekadha]: *Ekadhā* 'in one way' does not head this list, though the pattern of the series invites it. It is a real Pāli word and a common one in the commentaries, but its seven occurrences in the root texts are all in the Visuddhimagga, and it has none in the canon. The corresponding gap in the *-kkhattuṁ* series is filled (*sakiṁ* 'once' serves), so the absence is specific to *-dhā*. A reader meets the seven first, in the warning the Buddha gives Ambaṭṭha when he will not answer a legitimate question put to him twice, with the yakkha Vajirapāṇi standing overhead, blazing iron thunderbolt in hand, waiting for the third:

- … ettheva te **sattadhā** muddhā phalissati. (DN 3 — D i 94)
- *ettheva* < *ettha* here · *eva* right · *te* your (gen. sg.) · *sattadhā* into seven parts · *muddhā* head (m. nom. sg.) · *phalissati* will split
- … **into seven pieces** your head will split, right here.

The related suffix *-so* is **distributive**, not divisive: *anekaso* 'repeatedly', *pañcaso* 'in fives'. Neither of those two is common (the suffix is met far more often on nouns than on numerals), so know the plainer way to distribute, which is simply to say the numeral twice: *aṭṭha aṭṭha* 'eight each'.[^distributive]

**Fractions** are usually built by naming a numeral and a word for 'part': *bhāga*, *kalā*, *aṁsa*, *koṭṭhāsa*. The two may stand apart, so that *kalaṁ … soḷasiṁ* is 'a sixteenth part', which the canon says forty times and never once in the nominative;[^solasi-case] or they may compound, so that *catu* and *bhāga* give *catubhāgaṁ* 'a quarter'.[^fraction-cardinal]

[^fraction-cardinal]: The numeral is not always an ordinal. Perniola §53, p. 68, gives only *soḷasi kalā*, where *soḷasī* is ordinal in shape, and generalising from that one example excludes the commoner type: DPD constructs *catubhāgaṁ* as *catu* + *bhāga*, literally 'four part', on the plain cardinal. 'Half' has words of its own, *aḍḍha* and *upaḍḍha*, and 'a quarter' is *pāda*, literally 'a foot'. The trap is that *aḍḍha-* prefixed to an **ordinal** gives one and a half, two and a half, three and a half, as the section on the higher numerals showed, but prefixed to a **cardinal** it means *half of* that number, so *dasaddha-* is **five**, not 'ten and a half'. Oberlies §43(3), p. 198 with n. 6: *aḍḍha-* added to the next higher ordinal gives *aḍḍhatiya-* 'two and a half' and *aḍḍhuḍḍha-* 'three and a half'; but "If *aḍḍha-* is added to a cardinal the whole compound denotes *half of* the numeral: *dasaddha-* (sic) 'five', Th 1244 (see Geiger §119.2)". Perniola §53 supplies the fraction words *bhāga*, *kalā*, *aṁsa*, *koṭṭhāsa*, the example *soḷasi kalā*, and *aḍḍha* / *upaḍḍha* for 'half' against *pāda* for 'a quarter'.

**Collectives** name a group as a thing in itself, with the suffix *-ka* or *-ya*: *duka* or *dvaya* 'a pair', *tika* or *taya* 'a triad', then *catukka*, *pañcaka*, *chakka*, *sattaka*, *aṭṭhaka*, *dasaka*. These are everywhere in the Abhidhamma, whose sections are named by them, and one of them titles a sutta: the Chachakka Sutta, MN 148, is 'the six sixes'. They also name the largest structure in the Sutta Piṭaka: the Aṅguttara's eleven books are the Book of the Ones, the Twos, the Threes and so on up, and *duka*, *tika*, *catukka* and *pañcaka* are the words that do the naming. Related are the adjectives in *°guṇa-* and *°vidha-*, which say how many kinds or folds: *diguṇa-* 'twofold', *catugguṇa-* 'fourfold', *pañcavidha-* 'of five kinds'.

[^six-classes]: Thānuttamo, *Māgadhabhāsā*, "Numerals (*saṅkhyā*)", p. 72, divides them into cardinal, ordinal, distributive, fractional, multiplicative and substantive, citing Kaccāyana throughout (Kacc 133, 134, 373–6, 380, 397, 646, 666). Oberlies §43(3), p. 198, reaches the same material as a single list of "numeral derivatives" and Perniola gives each a numbered section, §§52–55. The classes are the tradition's; the order here is not, since a reader meets the multiplicatives far more often than the fractions.

[^distributive]: Oberlies §43(3), p. 198 n. 4: "The adverbial suffix *-so* is added to numerals in a distributive sense (see Geiger §119.3). Quite often it is added also to nouns and adjectives (see Norman 1992: 204 [ad Sn 288])." Perniola §52, p. 68, gives the repetition type with *aṭṭha aṭṭha there amacce ca pesayi* 'to each he sent eight elders and eight court officials', and adds *ekekaso* 'one by one'. The *-so* numerals are thinner on the ground than any of these sources suggests, and each should be checked in the texts before it is taught as ordinary. *Ekekaso* occurs once, in a later work. *Anekaso* occurs six times, none of them in the canon — twice in the commentaries and four times in the grammatical texts. *Pañcaso* occurs nowhere, though DPD carries it as a headword, ablative singular of *pañca* against Sanskrit *pañcaśas*, with a grammarian's *pañcapañcaso* for its example. All three are real words of the language; none is a word a reader will meet in a sutta.

[^solasi-case]: The grammars cite this fraction in a nominative the canon does not have. Perniola §53, p. 68, gives it as *soḷasi kalā* and DPD lemmatises it *soḷasī*, feminine, 'one sixteenth', from *soḷasa* + *ī*. Neither nominative occurs in the root texts: *soḷasī* is absent from the root texts and the commentaries alike, with one occurrence apiece in the ṭīkās and outside them. The fraction is only ever the accusative *soḷasiṁ*, forty times in the root texts, agreeing with an accusative *kalaṁ*: *kalaṁ nāgghanti soḷasiṁ* 'they are not worth a sixteenth part' (SN 10.8 — S i 211), and the same idiom closing Dhammapada 70. Bare *soḷasi* attests six times and is not a word. Every one of the six is *soḷasi'nti*, which is *soḷasiṁ* before *ti* with the niggahīta assimilated: a sandhi at the end of a word hiding its true form as thoroughly as one at the beginning would.

### Why this chapter is longer than it looks

A numeral seems the least grammatical thing in a language: a closed list, learnable in an afternoon. Pāli spends more than that. Its numerals decline in three genders below five and stop declining above eighteen; they agree with their noun, or govern it in the genitive, or absorb it into a compound, or stand as an abstract noun and take it as a modifier, and a reader expects only the first of those four. The ordinals form their feminine one way below five and another way above it.

None of that is difficulty for its own sake. A small closed class old enough to have been reshaped several times, and common enough that none of the reshapings was ever tidied away, ends up like this. And the practical return is large, because this canon counts. The four truths, the five aggregates, the six senses, the eightfold path, the twelve links: unable to parse *channaṁ* or *catunnaṁ*, a reader has trouble with the titles of the lists the teaching is built from, and not merely with an occasional sentence.

**Exercises for this chapter are in the workbook**, under "Exercises: Numerals", with worked answers at the back of that volume.

**Principal sources.** Oberlies §43 for the forms and for which of them are attested; Perniola chapters 5 and 15 for the numerals above eighteen and for their history; Thānuttamo for the classes the tradition itself distinguishes.

## Verbs: The Present System

A **finite verb** is one that inflects for person: it has separate forms for 'I', 'you', 'he'. Everything else in the verbal system is **infinite**: the participles, which inflect for gender, case and number but not for person, and the absolutives and infinitives, which do not inflect at all. Those are Part IV's. This part is about the forms that carry a person, and this chapter is about the three of them that are built on one stem.

How much of the Old Indic verb Pāli has let go is the first thing to establish, because a reader arriving from Sanskrit will otherwise spend some time looking for things that are not there. The dual is gone completely. The subjunctive and the precative are gone from the moods.[^no-subjunctive] The middle voice survives only in scattered forms, and its old work has largely passed to the causative. The perfect has been reduced to two or three stray forms in the whole canon.[^stray-perfect] What is left is a system a reader can hold in the head: three tenses (present, past and future) and four moods, of which the indicative, the imperative and the optative are formed on the present stem and belong here.

[^no-subjunctive]: Oberlies §44 (p. 199): "The subjunctive and the precative are missing from the moods", with a remark at §46.2 on what survives of the Old Indic subjunctive in a handful of imperative forms.

[^stray-perfect]: Perniola §61 (p. 74): "Further there are in the Pali Canon two or three stray forms of the perfect tense. In the postcanonical literature these forms are more numerous." Oberlies §44, p. 199, reaches the same place from the other direction, treating imperfect, aorist and perfect as having collapsed into a single **preterite**, and so they are handled together in the next chapter, not as three tenses.

### The present stem

Almost everything in this part follows from one fact, and it is a fact about Pāli, not about how Pāli is taught. In Old Indic the root was the centre of the verb and every form was derived from it. In Pāli the root has lost that place. Oberlies states it clearly: "The focus of the verbal conjugation is no longer the root but the present stem, i.e. the 3sg. of the indicative without the ending *-ti*. All regular verb forms are based on it; forms that are not so constructed are chiefly historical relics."[^stem-focus]

[^stem-focus]: Oberlies §44, p. 200. The claim is historical and structural at once, and it is what licenses a reference grammar to teach the stem before the root. Thānuttamo p. 86 reaches the same practical rule from the tradition, quoting Collins p. 79: "Pāḷi follows the convention that it — generally — forms the present indicative, the imperative and the optative from the active stem and everything else from the root." That sentence is the reason this chapter contains the imperative and the optative and the next one does not.

That gives the reader a procedure, not a fact to memorise. A dictionary gives a verb in its third person singular, *gacchati* 'goes'. Remove the *-ti* and what is left is the stem, *gaccha-*, and the stem is what every form in this chapter is built on:

| Dictionary form | Stem | Gloss |
|---|---|---|
| *gacchati* | *gaccha-* | goes |
| *deseti* | *dese-* | teaches |
| *karoti* | *karo-* | does, makes |
| *jānāti* | *jānā-* | knows |
| *suṇāti* | *suṇā-* | hears |

Look down the stem column and one feature is immediately visible: the stems end either in a short *-a* or in a long vowel. A reader needs no other division. Oberlies distinguishes two conjugations on this basis: one, much the larger, with the stem in *-a*; the other with the stem in a long vowel, "most commonly *-e*, fairly often *-ā*, rarely *-ī* or *-o*".[^two-conjugations] The rare *-ū* type is a handful of forms of one verb, *brūti* 'says', which a reader meets often enough in verse to recognise and never needs to conjugate.

[^two-conjugations]: Oberlies §44, p. 201. His "second" conjugation is the old athematic presents, made uniform by suppressing the alternations that Old Indic had between strong and weak stems, so *sunomi … sunoma*, *jahāmi … jahanti* with one stem where Sanskrit had two. The *-o* type is *karoti* and a very few others; the *-ī* type is smaller still.

#### Why the stems differ: vowel gradation

The stems do not differ arbitrarily, and two mechanisms account for nearly all of it. The first is **vowel gradation**: the systematic lengthening of a root vowel that runs through the whole of Pāli word-formation, nominal as well as verbal.[^gradation-placement] A root vowel appears in one of three grades:

[^gradation-placement]: Perniola makes vowel gradation §60 (pp. 73–74), inside his verb chapter and two sections ahead of the conjugation classes, which open on p. 75, because there the phenomenon has visible effects. Ñāṇatusita likewise sets his gradation table at the head of the verb sheet, and so Appendix A.3 opens with it. It is truly a fact about phonology, and it is taught here, not in Part I, because in Part I there is nothing yet for it to explain.

| Zero grade | *Guṇa* grade | *Vuddhi* grade |
|---|---|---|
| *i*, *ī* | *e*, *ay* | *āy*, *e* |
| *u*, *ū* | *o*, *av* | *āv*, *o* |
| *a* (or none) | *a* | *ā* |

The *guṇa* 'quality' grade adds an *a* to the vowel; the *vuddhi* 'growth' grade adds another. So the root *bhū-* 'be' in the *guṇa* grade is *bhav-*, giving *bhavati* 'is'; *jut-* 'shine' gives *jotati* 'shines'; *nī-* 'lead' gives *nayati* 'leads'. Push the same roots to the *vuddhi* grade and they yield the causatives of Chapter 9: *bhāveti* 'develops', literally 'causes to be', and *kāreti* 'makes another do'. The vowel *a* is already at the *guṇa* grade and so does not change there, and so the largest class of verbs shows no gradation at all.[^perniola-gradation]

[^perniola-gradation]: Perniola §60 (p. 73), with the worked series *lih-* → *lehati* 'licks', *jut-* → *jotati*, *bhū-* → *bhavati*, *kṛ-* → *karoti*. He adds the limiting rule at p. 74: only a final vowel, long or short, and a medial short vowel followed by one consonant are liable to be raised. So *jīv-* stays *jīvati* (the medial vowel is long by nature) and *nind-* stays *nindati* (long by position).

#### Why the stems differ: the conjugation classes

The second mechanism is that most verbs insert something between the root and the ending. The tradition calls these class affixes *vikaraṇa*, and sorts every root in the language by which one it takes. *Suṇāti* has its long *-ā-* because the root *su-* 'hear' takes the affix *-ṇā-*; *jhāyati* has its *-ya-*; *muñcati* has a nasal pushed inside the root itself.

| Class | Name | Affix | Example |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | *bhūvādigaṇa* | *-a-* | *pacati* 'cooks' |
| 2 | *rudhādigaṇa* | nasal infix + *-a-* | *muñcati* 'releases' |
| 3 | *divādigaṇa* | *-ya-* | *jhāyati* 'meditates, burns' |
| 4 | *svādigaṇa* | *-ṇu-*, *-ṇā-*, *-uṇā-* | *suṇāti* 'hears' |
| 5 | *kiyādigaṇa* | *-nā-* | *jānāti* 'knows' |
| 6 | *gahādigaṇa* | *-ppa-*, *-nhā-* | *gaṇhāti* 'takes' |
| 7 | *tanādigaṇa* | *-o-*, *-yirā-* | *karoti* 'does' |
| 8 | *curādigaṇa* | *-e-*, *-aya-* | *deseti* 'teaches' |

The grammars do not agree on how many classes there are, and the disagreement instructs more than it troubles. Kaccāyana and the *Saddanīti* give the eight above, each with its own rule; the *Padarūpasiddhi* folds the sixth into the fifth and gets seven; Perniola gives ten, because he follows the Sanskrit division and says so ("the Pali verbal system is directly connected with the Sanskrit verbal system; hence we can follow the Sanskrit division of the verbs into classes"); Warder arrives at seven on a different cut again.[^class-counts] They count the same forms and divide them at different joints, as happens whenever a classification is imposed on a continuum that has been eroding for centuries. This book does not depend on the number.

[^class-counts]: Thānuttamo pp. 85–87 for the eightfold scheme, with Kacc 445–452 against the classes in order, and p. 87 n. 133 for the observation, quoting Warder p. 79, that "sometimes it is not easy to decide whether to class a verb as an independent seventh conjugation root or as the causative form of some other verb of perhaps widely divergent meaning." Perniola's ten are at §62 ff. (p. 75). The parallel with the three-way disagreement about the size of the alphabet, in Chapter 1, is exact: a traditional enumeration, a Western one, and a difference of framework, not of fact.

What the classes are *for*, in a book about reading, is to stop a reader being surprised. *Suṇāti*, *jānāti*, *karoti* and *deseti* look nothing like each other and behave identically once the stem is isolated. The class explains the stem; the stem is what carries the endings; the endings are the same for all of them.

### Voice

Before the paradigms, one distinction that Appendix A.3 presents in a second column and that the reader will otherwise take for a misprint.

Pāli inherits two sets of personal endings. The tradition calls them *parassapada*, 'the word for another', and *attanopada*, 'the word for oneself': what Western grammars call the **active** and the **middle**.[^pada-terms] In principle the middle marks an action that returns to the agent, done for oneself instead of passing to something else. In practice it barely survives. Oberlies says the middle "survives only in some forms" and that its function has been partly taken over by the causative; Duroiselle is blunter, and describes what a reader will find:

[^pada-terms]: Thānuttamo pp. 82–84, following Kacc 406–407, where the active endings are the "former six personal suffixes" (*pubbakāni cha padāni*) and the middle the "latter six" (*parāni cha padāni*): a purely positional description, as befits their place in a traditional table.

> It must here be remarked that the Reflective Voice has lost very much of its importance, and that the distinction between Active and Reflective has been almost if not altogether effaced, and that the choice between the Active or Reflective is mostly determined now by metrical exigencies.

So the middle is largely a verse form, chosen for its sound, not its sense, and the reader's practical need is only to recognise it. A third singular in *-te* where *-ti* was expected, a first plural in *-mhe* or *-māse*, a third plural in *-re*: these are middle endings, not errors.

- Pare ca na vijānanti, mayamettha **yamāmase**. (MN 128 — M iii 154)
- *pare* others (m. nom. pl.) · *ca* and · *na* not · *vijānanti* understand · *mayaṁ* we · *ettha* here · *yamāmase* let us restrain ourselves (1 pl. imperative middle)[^yamamase-sense]
- Others do not understand that here **we must restrain ourselves**.

Whatever *yamāmase* means (and its grammar is settled where its sense is not), it is the middle voice, first person plural, and it is a verse line, which is where such forms live.[^yamamase] The same verse is Dhammapada 6, and whoever has met it in either place has met the *attanopada*, named or not.

[^yamamase]: The form is canonical, with six occurrences in the root texts. The neighbouring *saṁyamāmase* and *bhaṇāmase* at SN 1 show the same ending on two further verbs, likewise in verse. The simple present *yamati* is **not** attested in the root texts at all: this verb reaches the canon in this one form and in the compound *saṁyamāmase*, which is a caution about reading a dictionary headword as though it were a word one will meet.

[^yamamase-sense]: The sense is contested, and the rendering above is the translators', not the commentaries'. Ñāṇamoli and Bodhi render the MN 128 line "That here we should restrain ourselves". The commentary on that verse reads it the other way: *mayaṁ yamāmase … nassāma satataṁ samitaṁ maccusantikaṁ gacchāmā*, "we perish, we go continually into the presence of death" (Papañcasūdanī on MN 128, Ps IV 205). The Dhammapada commentary on Dhp 6 says the same with *uparamāma vinassāma* (Dhp-a I 65), and then offers a second reading, *yamāmase bhaṇḍanādīnaṁ vuddhiyā vāyamāmā*, 'we strive for the growth of quarrels and the like' (Dhp-a I 66). DPD gives the headword *yamati* the meaning 'dies', citing this very verse, which is the commentaries' first reading. The root √yam leans towards restraint: its Pāli derivatives are overwhelmingly the vocabulary of restraint (*saṁyama*, *saññata*, *vāyamati* 'makes effort'), and no other word in the family means 'die'. This chapter does not depend on the choice, since the form is a first person plural middle either way, and the disagreement is recorded here, not resolved.

There is a second distinction that is easy to run together with this one and must not be. Voice in the sense of active against passive is carried by the *stem*, not by the endings. A passive stem is made with an affix *-ya-* and then takes whatever personal endings it needs, active-looking ones included. That is Chapter 9's subject; what matters here is that *vuccati* 'is called' has an ordinary-looking *-ti* and is passive, so the endings alone will never tell a reader whether a verb is active or passive.[^stem-carries-voice]

[^stem-carries-voice]: Thānuttamo p. 81, citing Oberlies (2019) p. 555 and Warder p. 51: "it is the stem which indicates if a verb is active or passive … and not the two sets of personal voice markers." He adds the traditional analysis of the mismatch, in which an active-looking ending on a passive stem is a "reversed *attanopada*" form: historically, a middle ending that has taken active dress. It is a tidy account of a real oddity, and a reader needs only its consequence.

### The present indicative

The endings, active and middle, are these:

| Person | Active | Middle |
|---|---|---|
| 1 sg. | *-mi* | *-e* |
| 2 sg. | *-si* | *-se* |
| 3 sg. | *-ti* | *-te* |
| 1 pl. | *-ma* | *-mhe*, *-mase* |
| 2 pl. | *-tha* | *-vhe* |
| 3 pl. | *-nti* | *-nte*, *-re* |

Perniola calls these the **primary** endings, and the term is useful across the whole of Part III: the primary endings serve the present indicative *and* the future, the secondary endings serve the aorist and the conditional, the optative takes both, and the imperative has its own.[^primary-secondary] Hold that rule and the future in the next chapter is simply the present endings on a different stem, not a new paradigm to learn.

[^primary-secondary]: Perniola §57 (p. 71), which sets out the four sets (primary and secondary, each in an active and a middle column) and then assigns them: "The present indicative and the future take the primary personal endings. The aorist and the conditional take the secondary personal endings. The optative takes both the primary and the secondary personal endings. The imperative takes special endings." It is the single most economical page any source consulted has offered on the Pāli verb.

Attached to a stem in *-a*, the active endings give:

|     | singular | plural |
|-----|----------|--------|
| 1st | *gacchāmi* 'I go' | *gacchāma* 'we go' |
| 2nd | *gacchasi* 'you go' | *gacchatha* 'you go' |
| 3rd | *gacchati* 'he, she, it goes' | *gacchanti* 'they go' |

Two changes to the stem vowel are visible there, and neither is arbitrary. **The stem vowel lengthens before *-mi* and *-ma***, so the two first-person forms have *gacchā-* where the rest have *gaccha-*.[^kacc-478] And in the third plural the stem vowel is absorbed into the ending, so that a stem in a long vowel appears with a short one: *jānāti* but *jānanti*, *suṇāti* but *suṇanti*. These alternations are a rule and not an accident of the verbs, and it applies everywhere in the chapter, the imperative and the optative included.

[^kacc-478]: Kacc 478, reported at Thānuttamo p. 90, which gives the lengthening before *-hi*, *-mi* and *-ma*; and Duroiselle p. 154 for the complementary rule that the stem vowel drops before an ending that begins with a vowel. The same two rules account for the imperative *gacchāhi* beside *gacchatu* below.

The three other stem types take the same endings without further comment:

|     | *dese-* | *karo-* | *jānā-* |
|-----|---------|---------|---------|
| 1 sg. | *desemi* | *karomi* | *jānāmi* |
| 2 sg. | *desesi* | *karosi* | *jānāsi* |
| 3 sg. | *deseti* | *karoti* | *jānāti* |
| 1 pl. | *desema* | *karoma* | *jānāma* |
| 2 pl. | *desetha* | *karotha* | *jānātha* |
| 3 pl. | *desenti* | *karonti* | *jānanti* |

Because each person has its own ending, the personal pronoun is not needed and is usually absent. *Dhammaṁ desenti* is 'they teach the Dhamma' with no *te* in it; the *-nti* has already said 'they'. When a pronoun does appear it adds something (contrast, or emphasis of the kind set out in Chapter 5), and it should be noticed, not translated away.

#### What the present tense does

The present indicative states a fact, and its plainest use is what its name suggests: something is the case now. English has to choose between 'he teaches' and 'he is teaching'; Pāli does not, and either may be the right rendering.[^present-progressive]

[^present-progressive]: Thānuttamo pp. 90–92 lists eight senses for the present indicative, attributed to Collins pp. 81–83, Duroiselle (§611, p. 320 in the 1921 edition), Hendriksen p. 13 n. 2 and Perniola pp. 349–350. Three of them are given here, and the remaining five (interrogative use at the head of a sentence, the recent past, the hypothetical, and the future with the particles *yāva*, *pure*, *purā* and with *kadā*) are real but too thinly attested in the four Nikāyas to earn a section in a chapter this size.

Beyond that it does two things a reader must be ready for. The first is to state what is always the case. A general truth has no particular time, and Pāli puts it in the present:

- Jātassa maraṇaṁ **hoti**, jāto dukkhāni phussati; (SN 5.6 — S i 132)
- *jātassa* for one who is born (m. gen. sg.) · *maraṇaṁ* death (n. nom. sg.) · *hoti* is · *jāto* one born (m. nom. sg.) · *dukkhāni* sufferings (n. acc. pl.) · *phussati* encounters
- For one who is born there **is** death; one born encounters sufferings.

No action in that line is happening at the moment of speaking. *Hoti* means 'is the case', not 'is dying now', and the sentence reaches into the future (for one who is born there *will* be death) without any future form in it.

The second is the **historic present**, and it is very common. Pāli narrates past events in the present tense, where English can do so only in a consciously colloquial register.[^historic-present] Every sutta opens this way:

[^historic-present]: Thānuttamo p. 92 marks it "very common". The English parallel is real but stylistically marked — "so I walk into his office and say…" — which is why a translator almost always renders it as a past. Bodhi does exactly that in the line below, giving "was living" for *viharati*.

- ekaṁ samayaṁ bhagavā kurūsu **viharati** kammāsadhammaṁ nāma kurūnaṁ nigamo. (MN 10 — M i 55)
- *ekaṁ samayaṁ* at one time · *bhagavā* the Blessed One (m. nom. sg.) · *kurūsu* among the Kurus (m. loc. pl.) · *viharati* dwells · *kammāsadhammaṁ nāma* named Kammāsadhamma · *kurūnaṁ* of the Kurus (m. gen. pl.) · *nigamo* a town (m. nom. sg.)
- At one time the Blessed One **was dwelling** among the Kurus, at a town of the Kurus named Kammāsadhamma.

The verb is present and the event is centuries past. To render *viharati* as 'dwells' here is an error of English, not of grammar; but to conclude from the tense that the Buddha is described as presently resident is to misread the sentence.

### The imperative

The imperative commands, and it also invites, blesses, permits and entreats: a range much wider than the English term suggests. Its endings go on the same present stem:

| Person | Active | Middle |
|---|---|---|
| 1 sg. | *-mi* | *-e* |
| 2 sg. | *-hi*, or the bare stem | *-ssu* |
| 3 sg. | *-tu* | *-taṁ* |
| 1 pl. | *-ma*, *-mu* | *-āmase*, *-mhase* |
| 2 pl. | *-tha* | *-vho* |
| 3 pl. | *-ntu* | *-ntaṁ*, *-ruṁ* |

Three of the active endings are identical to the present indicative's (the first person singular and plural and the second plural), so *gacchāma* is 'we go' or 'let us go' and only the context decides.[^impv-syncretism] On the stem *gaccha-*:

[^impv-syncretism]: The overlap is real and total in those three cells, and it is not a defect of the table: the first-person imperative is a hortative ('let me', 'let us'), which is close enough to a statement of intention that the two never needed separate forms. The second plural *gacchatha* covers both 'you go' and 'go!' the same way.

|     | singular | plural |
|-----|----------|--------|
| 1st | *gacchāmi* 'let me go' | *gacchāma* 'let us go' |
| 2nd | *gacchāhi*, *gaccha* 'go!' | *gacchatha* 'go!' |
| 3rd | *gacchatu* 'let him go' | *gacchantu* 'let them go' |

Look at the second person singular. It has two forms: the ending *-hi* with the stem vowel lengthened before it, and the bare stem with no ending at all. Both are ordinary; *gaccha* and *gacchāhi* are the same command.[^impv-2sg] Only stems in *-a* take the bare form, because otherwise nothing would distinguish it from the stem itself.

[^impv-2sg]: Kacc 479, at Thānuttamo p. 88: the *a* of the stem is lengthened before *-hi*, and the ending is "sometimes elided altogether". Both *gaccha* and *gacchāhi* are well attested in the root texts, as are *karohi* and *suṇohi*, where the *-o-* stem shows the ending clearly.

The commonest use is the plain one, and the Buddha's most quoted instruction to enquirers is a string of them:

- **Etha** tumhe, kālāmā, **mā** anussavena, **mā** paramparāya … **mā** samaṇo no garūti. (AN 3.65 — A i 189)
- *etha* come! (2 pl. imperative) · *tumhe* you (nom. pl.) · *kālāmā* Kālāmas (voc. pl.) · *mā* do not · *anussavena* by oral transmission (m. inst. sg.) · *paramparāya* by lineage (f. inst. sg.) · *samaṇo* the ascetic (m. nom. sg.) · *no* our · *garu* teacher · *ti* end-quote
- **Come**, Kālāmas: **do not** go by oral transmission, **nor** by lineage … **nor** by thinking 'the ascetic is our teacher'.

The third person is where the range widens, because a third-person imperative in English needs a paraphrase ('let him', 'may he'), and the Pāli carries no such politeness marker of its own. It covers instruction, invitation, blessing and entreaty alike, and who is speaking to whom decides which is meant.[^impv-senses] The best-known instance in the canon is a benediction:

[^impv-senses]: Thānuttamo pp. 92–93 lists ten senses (command, prohibition, advice, invitation, wish, curse, benediction, entreaty, reflection, hope), with an illustration for all but prohibition and wish, which he names and leaves bare. His illustrations are largely Kaccāyana's and the commentaries', so the classification is used here and the examples are not.

- Sukhino va khemino hontu, sabbasattā **bhavantu** sukhitattā. (Snp 1.8 — Sn p. 25)[^sabbe-satta]
- *sukhino* happy (m. nom. pl.) · *va* indeed · *khemino* safe (m. nom. pl.) · *hontu* let them be (3 pl. imperative) · *sabba·sattā* all beings (m. nom. pl.) · *bhavantu* let them be (3 pl. imperative) · *sukhit·attā* happy in themselves (m. nom. pl.)
- May they be happy and safe; **may** all beings **be** happy in themselves.

[^sabbe-satta]: The editions divide the words differently here. The Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana prints the compound *sabbasattā*; the Buddha Jayanti, the Syāmese and the Pali Text Society all print *sabbe sattā*, two words, which is also how the line is set in most chanting books. Nothing depends on it (the sense and the grammar are identical, and *sabbe* is simply the same stem uncompounded), but if you know the verse by heart in one form, do not conclude that the other is a mistake. No edition varies *sukhitattā*.

Two imperatives in one line, *hontu* and *bhavantu*, from the two stems of the same verb 'to be'. Neither is a command to anyone. The mood is the same one that orders a monk to go; the sense is entirely from the setting.

The singular is at its most formal in the Vinaya, where every act of the Sangha opens with the same four words:

- **Suṇātu** me, bhante, saṅgho. (Bu Np 1 — Vin iii 196)
- *suṇātu* let it listen (3 sg. imperative) · *me* to me (1 sg. dat.) · *bhante* venerable sirs (voc.) · *saṅgho* the Sangha (m. nom. sg.)
- "**Let** the Sangha **listen** to me, venerable sirs."

No command is given here either, and none could be: a monk has no standing to order the Sangha. The sentence is a request for the floor, and it opens every *kammavācā*, 'act-statement', in the Vinaya: the formula by which a community does anything formally at all.[^kammavaca] A nun's version differs in one word, *ayye* for *bhante*. So in two examples the third person has moved from benediction to formal request with no change in the form, the point this section makes and the reason the ten senses are a list, not a paradigm.

[^kammavaca]: *Kammavācā* has 11 root-text occurrences and the accusative *kammavācaṁ* 14, and every one of them stands in a Vinaya file. It is one of the few technical terms of the tradition that is canonical, not commentarial, as one would expect of a word naming a procedure the canon has to legislate.

### The optative

The optative, also called the potential, covers what may, might, could, would or should happen. Its endings are added to the present stem, and unlike the imperative's they are unmistakable, because every one of them carries *-eyy-* or its contraction:

| Person | Active | Middle |
|---|---|---|
| 1 sg. | *-eyyaṁ*, *-e* | *-eyyaṁ* |
| 2 sg. | *-eyyāsi*, *-e* | *-etho* |
| 3 sg. | *-eyya*, *-e* | *-etha* |
| 1 pl. | *-eyyāma* | *-emase* |
| 2 pl. | *-eyyātha* | *-eyyavho* |
| 3 pl. | *-eyyuṁ* | *-eraṁ* |

On the stem *gaccha-* these give *gaccheyyaṁ* 'I might go', *gaccheyyāsi*, *gaccheyya*, *gaccheyyāma*, *gaccheyyātha*, *gaccheyyuṁ*. The short forms in *-e* alongside the full ones in *-eyya-* are contractions of the same ending and mean the same thing; the tradition also records an optative in *-yā*, which survives in a few common verbs: *vajjā* 'he would say', *dajjā* 'he would give'.[^opt-ya]

[^opt-ya]: Thānuttamo p. 93, reporting from Collins p. 85 that double formations also occur, *-eyya* being added to a base already in *-yā* to give *dajjeyya*. That double form is a grammarians' construction and should not be looked for in the suttas: *dajjā* itself has 44 occurrences in the root texts, while *dajjeyya* and *dajjeyyaṁ* appear only in the grammatical literature and *dajjeyyāti*, the form as Thānuttamo prints it, nowhere in the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana at all. Appendix A.3 carries the contracted optative forms in its own column; both series are Ñāṇatusita's.

The optative's commonest job in the canon is the conditional clause, where it is regularly preceded by *sace*, *ce* or *yadi*, all meaning 'if':

- **Sace** maṁ, bhante, evaṁ **puccheyyuṁ** … (DN 9 — D i 200)
- *sace* if · *maṁ* me (acc. sg.) · *bhante* venerable sir (voc.) · *evaṁ* thus · *puccheyyuṁ* they should ask (3 pl. optative)
- **If** they were to **ask** me thus, venerable sir …

Its second use is the polite or provisional statement, and in the Buddha's speech the mood is used most this way: a rule stated as what one *should* do, an enquiry framed as what one *might* find. The Kālāma Sutta puts an optative directly after the imperatives quoted above, and the shift is the point of the passage: having told them what not to rely on, the Buddha moves to what they may come to know for themselves:

- Yadā tumhe, kālāmā, attanāva **jāneyyātha** … (AN 3.65 — A i 189)
- *yadā* when · *tumhe* you (nom. pl.) · *kālāmā* Kālāmas (voc. pl.) · *attanā* by oneself (m. inst. sg.) · *va* just, only · *jāneyyātha* you should know (2 pl. optative)
- But when you **come to know** for yourselves …

One caution, against seeing a distinction that is not there. The imperative and the optative overlap in use to the point that Oberlies calls them "syntactically often exchangeable".[^impv-opt-overlap] A wish may appear in either; a mild instruction may appear in either. The forms are always distinguishable and the senses frequently are not, so having parsed *gaccheyya* correctly as an optative you still have to decide from the context whether it is 'he might go', 'he should go' or simply 'let him go'.

[^impv-opt-overlap]: Oberlies (2019) p. 399, quoted at Thānuttamo p. 92. The same page adds, from a personal communication of Oberlies's dated 30 October 2020, that imperatives occur chiefly in main clauses, the only structural cue that separates the two when the sense will not.

### *Atthi* and *hoti*

One verb is irregular enough in the present to be learnt whole, and it is among the most frequent words in the canon. *Atthi* 'is, exists' does not take a stem in the way the verbs above do; the endings attach to the root itself, with the results that Old Indic left behind:

|     | singular | plural |
|-----|----------|--------|
| 1st | *asmi*, *amhi* 'I am' | *amha* 'we are' |
| 2nd | *asi* 'you are' | *attha* 'you are' |
| 3rd | *atthi* 'he, she, it is; there is' | *santi* 'they are' |

Its imperative is *atthu*, *santu*, and its optative is *siyā* or *assa*, plural *siyuṁ* or *assu*, the last of these among the most common verb forms in the whole canon, since a very large part of the Buddha's conditional and hypothetical speech runs through it.[^atthi-optative]

[^atthi-optative]: *Siyā* has 3,843 occurrences in the root texts and *assa* 812, against 1 for the imperative *atthu*. Appendix A.3's optative endings account for both series: *siyā* is the *-yā* type mentioned above, and *assa* the ordinary *-eyya* contracted onto the root *as-*.

Two practical notes about this paradigm, because the table above is tidier than the page a reader will meet. The first-person forms are usually printed joined to the word before them. Standing alone, *asmi* occurs just once in the root texts; joined, it is everywhere: *ahamasmi* 'I am' forty-five times, and *asmī'ti* sixty-five.[^asmi-sandhi] Search a text for *asmi*, find nothing, and you have met a fact about how the editions set sandhi, not about the language.

[^asmi-sandhi]: Counted across the root texts of the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana. The same pattern is visible in the doctrinal term *asmimāna*, 'the conceit "I am"', where the form is embedded in a compound. Chapter 2's rules for junction are what undo it.

And the doublets the handbooks print beside these are real, although they do not occur as words of their own. The first singular is often given as *asmi* or *amhi*, the first plural as *amha* or *amhā*. *Amhi* and *amhā* do not occur as words in the root texts at all, as one would expect of a pair of grammarians' constructions. They are hidden by the sandhi just described, and hidden more completely, because a joined *amhi* gives up its own initial vowel and leaves only *-mhi* behind. *Kho* + *amhi* gives *khomhi* 'I am indeed', which stands in the root texts a hundred and thirty-four times; *na* + *amhi* gives *nāmhi* 'I am not', which is how the Buddha asks to be left undisturbed for a fortnight.[^amhi-amha] So the first person singular has two living forms, not one. The first plural does not, and there the handbooks are nearly right: *amhā* has two occurrences in the whole of the root texts against *amha*'s eight, and is the rarer word by a long way.

[^amhi-amha]: Counted across the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana. As words standing alone in the root texts: *asmi* 1, *amhi* 0, *amha* 1, *amhā* 0. Joined, *amhi* occurs in 136 distinct words and 495 places in the root texts: *khomhi* 134, *pattomhi* 'I have attained' 36, *somhi* 27, *nāmhi* 5, the last at SN 54.11, *nāmhi kenaci upasaṅkamitabbo*, 'I am not to be approached by anyone'. Joined *asmi* is commoner still, its two largest hosts being the *anattā* formula's *esohamasmi* and *nesohamasmi*, 157 and 193. *Amhā*'s two root-text occurrences are *jhāyinomhā* 'we are meditators' (AN 6.46) and *dassanakāmamhā* 'we wish to see' (Ud 1.10); *amha*'s eight are one bare, six as *khomha* and one as *bhātaromha*. DPD confirms each analysis: *khomhi* as *kho* + *amhi*, *nāmhi* as *na* + *amhi*, s.vv.

The other common verb to name here is *hoti* 'is, becomes', a contraction of *bhavati*, which supplies most of the ordinary copula and whose imperative *hotu* 'so be it' a reader meets constantly. *Hoti* and *bhavati* say that A is B; *atthi* says that A exists, or that there is an A. The distinction is not absolute, but it is the safe assumption.

Beyond these, a minority of verbs are irregular in the present and a much larger number are irregular in the past. Since almost all of that irregularity falls in the aorist, the irregular verbs are gathered in the next chapter, where they can be met alongside the tense that makes them irregular.

**Exercises for this chapter are in the workbook**, under "Exercises: The Present System", with worked answers at the back of that volume.

**Principal sources.** Oberlies §44 for the structure of the verbal system and for what Pāli has lost from it; Thānuttamo for voice and for the conjugation classes the tradition distinguishes; Perniola §§56–62 for the personal endings taken as one system, not five tables. The endings themselves are Appendix A.3's throughout.

## Verbs: The Past and the Future

The three forms built on the present stem (the indicative, the imperative and the optative) are Chapter 7's. This chapter takes what is built from the root instead: the past, the future, and the conditional, together with the negation and prohibition that go with them.

One line of the *Mahāparinibbāna Sutta* has all three tenses in a row, and it is a good place to begin, because the form of each is visible against the other two:

- *na cāhu na ca bhavissati na cetarahi vijjati añño samaṇo vā brāhmaṇo vā bhagavatā bhiyyobhiññataro yadidaṁ sambodhiyaṁ.* (DN 16 — D ii 82)
- *na* not · *ca* and · *āhu* there has been (3 sg. aor.) · *na* not · *ca* and · *bhavissati* there will be (3 sg. fut.) · *na* not · *ca* and · *etarahi* now · *vijjati* there is found (3 sg. pres.) · *añño* another (m. nom. sg.) · *samaṇo* ascetic (m. nom. sg.) · *vā* or · *brāhmaṇo* brahmin (m. nom. sg.) · *bhagavatā* than the Blessed One (m. inst. sg.) · *bhiyyobhiññataro* of greater direct knowledge (m. nom. sg.) · *yadidaṁ* that is · *sambodhiyaṁ* as to awakening (f. loc. sg.)
- "There has not been, there will not be, and there is not now, another ascetic or brahmin whose direct knowledge is greater than the Blessed One's when it comes to awakening."

*Āhu*, *bhavissati*, *vijjati*: past, future, present, and each carries its tense in a different place. The present is the bare stem plus an ending, as Chapter 7 had it. The future inserts *-ssa-* between the stem and the same ending. The past does neither: *āhu* is a form of the root with nothing recognisable added at all.[^ahu-note] That asymmetry is the subject of this chapter.

[^ahu-note]: *Ahu* is the root aorist of *hoti/bhavati*, from *bhū-*; it has 460 occurrences in the root texts. The form here is *cāhu*, from *ca* + *ahu*, with the vowels merged by the sandhi of Chapter 2.

### The augment

Both of the past formations in this chapter and the conditional share one mark: a prefixed *a-*, called the **augment** (*vuddhi* in the Sanskrit tradition's sense of increase, though the Pāli grammarians simply call it *a-kāra-āgama*, 'the coming of an *a*'). It is inherited from Old Indic, where it was obligatory, and in Pāli it is not.

Two facts about it matter more than its history. It is not a negation. *Agamā* is 'he went', not 'he did not go'; the negative is the separate word *na*, treated at the end of this chapter. Having learnt the prefix *a-* as a negative on nouns and adjectives (*anicca* 'impermanent', *asukha* 'unpleasant'), you will misread a great many past-tense verbs unless the two are firmly separated.

And when a prefix is present, the augment goes between the prefix and the root, not in front of the whole word: *ud* + *a* + *pādi* gives *udapādi* 'it arose'; *saṁ* + *a* + *bhavi* gives *samabhavi* 'it came to be'. Here a reader is most likely to lose it, because the augment is then buried in the middle of a long word and looks like part of the stem.[^augment-prefix]

Whether it appears at all is not a free choice, and two sources state the pattern as a rule instead of a list of examples, arriving at it from opposite ends.[^augment-rule] Perniola grades it by the type of aorist. The root aorist **always** takes the augment; the *a*-aorist and the *s*-aorist **generally** do; and it is often dropped when the verb carries a prefix ending in *-a*, so *pahāsi* and *upapajjatha* stand beside augmented forms of the same verbs. Geiger grades it by the length of the word instead: short forms keep the augment, long ones shed it, and the shedding spreads as the language gets later. The two meet where it matters most, since the root aorist's forms are the monosyllables: *adā*, *agā*, *ahu*. In verse, the metre decides.

[^augment-prefix]: Both examples are canonical, and the obvious third one is not. *Udapādi* has 840 root-text occurrences (it is the verb of *cakkhuṁ udapādi* at SN 56.11 — S v 422, quoted in Chapters 3 and 4), and DPD constructs it *ud* + *a* + √*pad* + *i*; *samabhavi* is DPD's *saṁ* + *a* + *bhava* + *i*. But *paccadesesi* 'he confessed' does not occur in the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana at all, and neither does any other augmented aorist of *paṭideseti*: the verb is the Vinaya's own word for confessing an offence (*paṭidesemī* has 16 root-text occurrences and the gerundive *paṭidesetabbaṁ* 11), and the canon simply never puts it in this tense. The form is well made and it is a grammarians' construction, so it is not used here. Chapter 14 makes the same cross-reference with *udapādi*.

[^augment-rule]: Perniola §82, pp. 91–92, whose subsections a–d grade it by aorist type (always for the root aorist, generally for the other three), with two further conditions at e and f: the augment is often dropped after a prefix ending in *-a*, and in verse its use "often depends on the requirements of the metre". Geiger §158 (p. 153) sets out a five-part rule he credits to Wackernagel, *Wortumfang und Wortform*, GN 1906, pp. 154 ff., graded by syllable count: monosyllabic forms keep the augment (*adaṁ* 'I gave', *agā* 'he went'); dissyllabic forms from the imperfect, the simple aorist or the *s*-aorist keep it "also in the later language" (*adāsi*, *avocuṁ*); dissyllabic forms from the *is*-aorist take it arbitrarily in the two oldest periods and drop it in post-canonical prose; trisyllabic forms of the extended type keep it (*agamāsi*); and everything else of three syllables or more began early to drop it, at random first and then as a rule. Oberlies §48, p. 242, gives the same rule in three parts, also after Wackernagel: the augment is prefixed where the aorist would otherwise be monosyllabic (*adā*); it is used with all disyllabic aorists except continuations of the *is*-aorist, where it is facultative (*alabhi* or *labhi*); and it is facultative with longer aorists, generally apart from those enlarged within Pāli (*agamāsi*). Thānuttamo p. 94 says the augment is frequently left out of the aorist and gives, from Oberlies (2019), the two plainest cases where it is used, a disyllabic aorist and one that would otherwise be monosyllabic; Duroiselle §417, p. 172, gives examples on either side and says only that the augment is "of much more frequent occurrence in prose than in poetry", where metre decides it. That a rule exists at all matters, because an augment described only as "frequently left out" invites a reader to think the two forms are free variants of each other, and in the third person singular they are not: *agamā* and *gamā* do not occur in equal numbers.

### The aorist

Pāli had inherited three separate past tenses from Old Indic: the imperfect, the perfect and the aorist. It kept one. The aorist absorbed the work of all three, and it is the ordinary past tense of the canon; a page of narrative prose is made of it. Oberlies accordingly heads his §48 "the preterite", not "the aorist", because in Pāli the two words have come to mean the same thing.

The complication is that the aorist is four formations, not one. Old Indic formed its aorist in several ways, and Pāli inherited the several ways without inheriting the reasons for choosing between them, so which type a verb takes is a fact about that verb and has to be learnt with it. The four are conventionally numbered, and Appendix A.3 sets out all four in full. The account below gives what a reader needs in order to recognise them. To learn the commonest forms before the system, begin at the end of the chapter instead, under "Irregular finite verbs", where five verbs are given whose aorists are best learnt whole.

Two features run through all four. Every type uses the **secondary endings**: the shorter set, contrasted in Chapter 7 with the primary endings that the present and the future take. And in every type the third person singular is the form a reader meets most, because so much of the canon is narrative about what somebody did.

#### Type 1: the root aorist

The endings go straight onto the root, with the augment, and no other element comes between.

| Person | Active |
|---|---|
