- *puna caparaṁ* furthermore · *bhikkhave* monks (m. voc. pl.) · *bhikkhu* a monk (m. nom. sg.) · *gacchanto* walking (m. nom. sg. prp.) · *vā* or · *gacchāmi* I walk (1 sg. pres.) · *ti* (quotation) · *pajānāti* understands (3 sg. pres.) · *ṭhito* standing (m. nom. sg. pp.) · *ṭhitomhi* < *ṭhito* + *amhi* I am (1 sg.) · *nisinno* seated (m. nom. sg. pp.) · *sayāno* lying down (m. nom. sg. prp.)
- "Furthermore, monks, a monk, when **walking**, understands 'I am walking'; when **standing**, understands 'I am standing'; when **seated**, understands 'I am seated'; when **lying down**, understands 'I am lying down'."

That second passage carries the whole point in one sentence. *Ṭhito* and *nisinno* are past participles of intransitive verbs ('having come to stand', 'having come to sit'), and both are read actively, as ordinary descriptions of the monk's own posture; *gacchanto* and *sayāno* beside them are present participles of the same verbs, active in the ordinary way present participles are. None of these is passive, because there is no object for a passive to act on.

Just as with adjectives, the "thing" a participle describes is sometimes an unstated "it": the neuter singular carrying the whole clause:

- Evaṁ me **sutaṁ**. (MN 26 — M i 160)
- *evaṁ* thus · *me* by me (gen./inst. sg.) · *sutaṁ* heard (n. nom. sg. pp.)
- "Thus (it) was **heard** by me."

**Less common than the *-ta* type are past participles in *-na/-ṇa***, which inflect the same way.

| Form | From | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| *ucchinno* | *ucchindati* 'cuts off' | cut off |
| *upapanna* | *upapajjati* 'takes rebirth' | took rebirth |
| *khīṇa* | *khīyati* 'ends, is exhausted' | ended, exhausted |
| *dinna* | *dadāti* 'gives' | given |

- '**Khīṇā** jāti, vusitaṁ brahmacariyaṁ, kataṁ karaṇīyaṁ, nāparaṁ itthattāyā'ti pajānātī"ti. (SN 35.28 — S iv 20)
- *khīṇā* ended (f. nom. sg. pp.) · *jāti* birth (f. nom. sg.) · *vusitaṁ* lived (n. nom. sg. pp.) · *brahmacariyaṁ* the spiritual life (n. nom. sg.) · *kataṁ* done (n. nom. sg. pp.) · *karaṇīyaṁ* what was to be done (n. nom. sg. grd.) · *na aparaṁ* no further · *itthattāya* for this state of being (n. dat. sg.) · *ti* (quotation) · *pajānāti* understands (3 sg. pres.)
- "He understands: 'Birth **is ended**, the spiritual life **has been lived**, what had to be done **has been done**, there is no more of this state of being.'"

This is the canon's formula for full awakening, and it is four participles serving as finite verbs in one breath, three of them past (*khīṇā*, *vusitaṁ*, *kataṁ*) and the last a gerundive (*karaṇīyaṁ*, met properly below).

A third group is assimilated: *-ṭha*, *-dha* and *-ḷha* are all sound changes on *-ta*, produced when the root ends in a consonant that the *t* cannot follow directly.

| Form | From | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| *diṭṭha* | *dassati* 'sees' | seen |
| *buddha* | *bujjhati* 'wakes up (to), understands' | woken up (to), understood |
| *laddha* | *labhati* 'gets, obtains' | got, obtained |
| *virūḷha* | *virūhati* 'grows up'[^rulha-fix] | grown up |

[^rulha-fix]: The plain, unprefixed *rūḷha* is a grammarians' abstraction: it does not occur bare anywhere in the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana. The canon has it only compounded (*virūḷha* 'grown up', *samārūḷha* 'mounted, climbed', *ārūḷha* 'mounted', *abhirūḷha* 'mounted', the last of these Oberlies's own example at §56, p. 262), and a reader will meet those forms.

The most famous verse in the language is built almost entirely from this group, and it is given whole here, not as isolated headwords:

- Gahakāraka **diṭṭhosi**, puna gehaṁ na kāhasi; sabbā te phāsukā **bhaggā**, gahakūṭaṁ **visaṅkhataṁ**; visaṅkhāragataṁ cittaṁ, taṇhānaṁ khayamajjhagā. (Dhp 154 — Dhp p. 23)
- *gahakāraka* house-builder (m. voc. sg.) · *diṭṭho* seen (m. nom. sg. pp.) · *asi* you are (2 sg. pres.) · *puna* again · *gehaṁ* a house (n. acc. sg.) · *na* not · *kāhasi* you will make (2 sg. fut.) · *sabbā*<sub>a</sub> all (f. nom. pl.) · *te* your (gen. sg.) · *phāsukā*<sub>a</sub> rafters (f. nom. pl.) · *bhaggā*<sub>a</sub> broken (f. nom. pl. pp.) · *gahakūṭaṁ*<sub>b</sub> the roof-peak (n. nom. sg.) · *visaṅkhataṁ*<sub>b</sub> demolished (n. nom. sg. pp.) · *visaṅkhāragataṁ*<sub>c</sub> gone to the unconditioned (n. nom. sg. pp.) · *cittaṁ*<sub>c</sub> the mind (n. nom. sg.) · *taṇhānaṁ* of craving (f. gen. pl.) · *khayaṁ* the ending (m. acc. sg.) · *ajjhagā* has reached (3 sg. aor.)
- "House-builder, **you are seen**! You will not build a house again. All your rafters **are broken**, the roof-peak **demolished**; the mind **has gone** to the unconditioned, it has reached the ending of craving."

*Diṭṭhosi* is one printed word standing for two: *diṭṭho* + *asi*, 'seen' + 'you are', the *-o* and *a-* fusing as Chapter 2 predicts. The de-sandhied pair belongs in the gloss line, as here, never in the line quoted as the verse; the tradition holds that the joined form was said, and the manuscripts have only the joined form.

### The past active participle

A participle can also be built *from* a past participle, by adding *-vant-* or *-in-*, and the result is active regardless of transitivity.[^tavant-oberlies] *Vusitavā* 'one who has lived (the spiritual life)', from *vusita* 'lived' plus *-vant-*, means simply 'accomplished': an epithet, not a passive report of having-been-lived. It inflects like the *(m/v)ant*-stems of Appendix A.1, the very paradigm this chapter's present participles use, since *bhagavant-* 'the Blessed One' is built the same way; the *-in* type (*bhuttāvī* 'one who has eaten', from *bhutta* + *-in-*) inflects like the *ī*/*in*-stems of the same appendix.

[^tavant-oberlies]: Oberlies §56, its closing paragraph (p. 263): "By adding the suffix *°va(nt)*- to a verbal adjective an active participle is formed." Thānuttamo (pp. 113–114) gives it its own section, "Past Active Participle", and reports that the absolutive has mostly displaced it in ordinary use, so no coursebook consulted for this chapter teaches it, although it is common enough in the canon to be met on purpose, not by accident.

This is no marginal formation. *Vusitavā* has sixty occurrences in the root texts, *bhuttāvī* thirty-six, *katāvī* fifteen, *vijitāvī* forty-three: more common, cell for cell, than most of the paradigm this book has taught so far. It shows up chained together with other participles in the standard description of an arahant:

- … yo hoti bhikkhu arahaṁ khīṇāsavo **vusitavā** katakaraṇīyo ohitabhāro anuppattasadattho parikkhīṇabhavasaṁyojano sammadaññāvimutto, so nesaṁ aggamakkhāyati dhammeneva, no adhammena. (DN 27 — D iii 83)
- *yo hoti bhikkhu* whichever monk (m. nom. sg.) · *arahaṁ* an arahant (m. nom. sg.) · *khīṇāsavo* one whose defilements are ended (m. nom. sg.) · *vusitavā* who has lived (the spiritual life) (m. nom. sg. p. act. p.) · *katakaraṇīyo* who has done what was to be done (m. nom. sg.) · *ohitabhāro* who has laid down the burden (m. nom. sg.) · *anuppattasadattho* who has attained the goal (m. nom. sg.) · *parikkhīṇabhavasaṁyojano* whose fetters of existence are exhausted (m. nom. sg.) · *sammadaññāvimutto* who is rightly freed through knowledge (m. nom. sg.) · *so* he (m. nom. sg.) · *nesaṁ* among them (m. gen. pl.) · *aggam* foremost · *akkhāyati* is said to be (3 sg. pres. pass.) · *dhammena* by principle (m. inst. sg.) · *eva* just · *no* not · *adhammena* by what is against principle (m. inst. sg.)
- "… whichever monk is an arahant, one whose defilements are ended, **who has lived the spiritual life**, who has done what was to be done, who has laid down the burden, who has attained the goal, whose fetters of existence are exhausted, who is rightly freed through knowledge — he is said to be foremost among them by principle, not by what is against principle."

Every element of that chain except *vusitavā* is itself a past participle used adjectivally: five ordinary ones and a compound (*katakaraṇīya*, past participle plus gerundive, 'done-what-was-to-be-done'). Only *vusitavā* is built as this section describes, and without the formation you will parse it as a stray irregular, not as a member of a real, if minor, class.

### The present participle

The present participle stem is formed with *-māna* or with *-(n)t*. *-māna* is the commoner of the two in a beginner's early reading; *-(n)t* is the ending this book's own inherited chapters lean on more heavily, and it deserves the fuller treatment.

#### Present participle in *-māna*

| Form | From | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| *gacchamāno* | *gacchati* 'goes' | going |
| *jānamāno* | *jānāti* 'knows, understands' | knowing, understanding |
| *vedayamāno* | *vedeti* = *vedayati* 'feels' | feeling |
| *samāno* | *atthi* 'is, exists' | being, existing |

They inflect like *-a* stem adjectives (*gacchamāno*, *gacchamānaṁ*, *gacchamānena* …). A handful of verbs (including, oddly, *atthi* and *karoti*) keep an older, historical form of the same ending, bare *-āna*, not *-māna*, with no *m*.[^ana-not-mana] *Sayāno* 'lying down', already met above, is built this way straight on the root, *saya-* + *-āna*, not by dropping an *m* from a longer form that never existed for this verb.

[^ana-not-mana]: Oberlies §54 (p. 257): as the middle voice stopped being a living category and the passive took over active endings, the two suffixes *-māna* and *-āna* (one originally the middle-voice ending, the other originally the passive's) became "true alternatives", the *-āna* type often hidden behind the commoner *-māna* one. *Karoti*'s own present middle participle is a further irregularity: *kurumāno* or *kubbamāno*, not the expected *kārayamāna*, built on the old stem *kubba-*. Oberlies names *kubba(nt)-/kubba(m)āna-* explicitly as "one of the very few exceptions" to the regular present-stem-plus-*-māna* rule. *Kārayamāna* is a real word all the same, and it means what its shape says: the middle participle of the *causative* *kāreti*, 'having something made'. DPD gives it twice, both "prp of *kāreti*", √*kar*, *kāraya* + *māna*, glossed 'making; causing to make' and 'building; causing to do', and cites it from the *Kuṭikārasikkhāpada* and the *Dvebhāgasikkhāpada*, the Vinaya rules on having a hut and a sitting-mat made. Geiger §191 (p. 182) had it as well, filed under the causative's uncontracted stems. In the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana it has twenty-two occurrences, ten of them in the root-text Vinaya, and every one is oblique: *bhikkhunā kuṭiṁ kārayamānena*, 'by a monk having a hut built'. There is no nominative *kārayamāno* in the canon at all. This chapter's first draft therefore called the form unattested, having looked it up in the one shape it never takes.

- Evaṁ **viharamānassa**, suddhikāmassa bhikkhuno; khīyanti āsavā sabbe, nibbutiñcādhigacchati. (Thag 10.6 — Th p. 61)
- *evaṁ* thus · *viharamānassa* dwelling (m. gen. sg. prp.) · *suddhikāmassa* longing for purity (m. gen. sg.) · *bhikkhuno* for a monk (m. gen. sg.) · *khīyanti* are exhausted (3 pl. pres.) · *āsavā* the taints (m. nom. pl.) · *sabbe* all (m. nom. pl.) · *nibbutiṁ* extinguishment (f. acc. sg.) · *ca* and · *adhigacchati* reaches (3 sg. pres.)
- "For a monk **dwelling** thus, longing for purity, all defilements are ended, and he reaches extinguishment."

#### Present participle in *-(n)t*

| Form | From | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| *gaccha(n)t* | *gacchati* 'goes' | going |
| *jāna(n)t* | *jānāti* 'knows, understands' | knowing, understanding |
| *sa(n)t* | *atthi* 'is, exists' | being, existing |

The *-(n)t* type inflects like the *(m/v)ant*-stems of Appendix A.1, and four things about it need care. The nominative singular has a special short form, *-aṁ*, standing alongside the regular *-anto*. The weak stem appears through the oblique singular and the genitive plural (*-atā*, *-ato*, *-ati*, *-ataṁ*) beside the thematised endings, so the locative singular of *sa(n)t* is *sati*, spelt like the noun *sati* 'mindfulness'; the locative absolute of *atthi* takes that form, and Chapter 16 has the construction. The feminine strengthens to *-antī*, not the plain *-atī* an *ī*-stem would predict, and only *sa(n)t* among the participles also builds a feminine on the weak stem.[^weak-stem-sant] The pronominal endings *-asmā*, *-amhā*, *-ato*, *-asmiṁ* and *-amhi* attach only to the thematised stem in *-anta*, and even there the root texts print them seldom.[^ant-pronominal] The whole paradigm is in Appendix A.1.

[^weak-stem-sant]: Oberlies §41 (pp. 178–179): the feminines of this stem type "are usually derived from the strong stem (*kubbantī-*); only adjectives have a feminine in *°atī-* (*sīlavatī-*, *mahatī-*) as has *sa(nt)-* (*satī-*)". Perniola §38(f), pp. 46–47, draws the same line between adjectives and participles without naming the exception (an *-ant* adjective takes *-ī* on the weak stem, *sīlavatī*, *mahatī*; a present participle takes it on the strong, *gacchantī*, *carantī*), and Ānandajoti's *Practical Guide*, "Verbs", p. 10, puts the strong stem in the nominative and accusative (*gacchantī*, *gacchantiṁ*) but the weak stem in the oblique singular (*gacchatiyā*), a form the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana does not print; the canon has *gacchantiyā*. The canon has both feminines. *Satī* stands in Jātaka verse, of a hope that succeeds however far off it has gone: *tāva dūragatā satī* (Ja 380 — J iii 251), agreeing with *āsā*, feminine. The prose uses *santī*, built regularly on the strong stem: *indasālaguhā visamā santī samā samapādi* (DN 21 — D ii 269), of the Indasāla cave that was uneven and became even. So the weak-stem feminine is real, and it is not the commoner of the two.

[^ant-pronominal]: Oberlies prints the locative *arahantamhi* in his own paradigm (§41, p. 178), and Perniola §34, p. 40, sets out the full *-a*-stem declension of *sīlavant* beside the consonantal one, *sīlavantasmā* and *sīlavantasmiṁ* among it. In the root texts of the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana these endings are rare and mostly in verse: *himavantamhi* sixteen times, *arahantamhi* twice, *mahantasmiṁ* and *mahantasmā* once each, and five present participles standing in locative absolutes: *sampādayantamhi*, *gaḷantamhi*, *sarantamhi*, *uppajjantamhi*, *nidhāyantamhi*. None of them occurs on the weak stem; *gacchatasmiṁ* and *gacchatamhi* do not occur anywhere in the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana.

Present participles of verbs whose stem ends in *-e* or *-o* inflect the same way but keep their stem vowel, and have no special short *-aṁ* form: *dento* 'giving', *desento* 'teaching', *karonto* 'doing, making, working'.

- So taṁ itthiṁ passeyya aññena purisena saddhiṁ **santiṭṭhantiṁ** **sallapantiṁ** **sañjagghantiṁ** **saṁhasantiṁ**. (MN 101 — M ii 223)
- *so* he (m. nom. sg.) · *taṁ*<sub>a</sub> that (f. acc. sg.) · *itthiṁ*<sub>a</sub> woman (f. acc. sg.) · *passeyya* might see (3 sg. opt.) · *aññena*<sub>b</sub> with another (m. inst. sg.) · *purisena*<sub>b</sub> man (m. inst. sg.) · *saddhiṁ* together with · *santiṭṭhantiṁ*<sub>a</sub> standing (f. acc. sg. prp.) · *sallapantiṁ*<sub>a</sub> chatting (f. acc. sg. prp.) · *sañjagghantiṁ*<sub>a</sub> joking (f. acc. sg. prp.) · *saṁhasantiṁ*<sub>a</sub> laughing (f. acc. sg. prp.)
- "He might see that woman **standing**, **chatting**, **joking** and **laughing** together with another man."

- Tena kho pana samayena āyasmā udāyī mahatiyā gihiparisāya parivuto dhammaṁ **desento** nisinno hoti. (AN 5.159 — A iii 184)
- *tena samayena* at that time · *kho pana* now · *āyasmā udāyī*<sub>a</sub> the venerable Udāyī (m. nom. sg.) · *mahatiyā gihiparisāya* by a large lay assembly (f. inst. sg.) · *parivuto*<sub>a</sub> surrounded (m. nom. sg. pp.) · *dhammaṁ* the teaching (m. acc. sg.) · *desento*<sub>a</sub> teaching (m. nom. sg. prp.) · *nisinno*<sub>a</sub> seated (m. nom. sg. pp.) · *hoti* is (3 sg. pres.)
- "Now at that time the venerable Udāyī, surrounded by a large lay assembly, was seated **teaching** the Dhamma."

One use of the present participle has no equivalent in the *-māna* type and is easy to miss: it can carry a conditional sense, marking what would happen, not what is happening.[^prp-irrealis] The reading is rare enough that it should be suspected only where the ordinary progressive sense makes no sense of the sentence, but where it applies, the participle functions as Chapter 8's conditional mood does, without any of that mood's own morphology.

[^prp-irrealis]: Oberlies's *rem.* to §54 (p. 257): "Pāli shows the incipient use of the present participle as conditional (i.e. to denote the *irrealis*)", with the example *adhammaṁ sārathi kayirā, maṁ ce tvaṁ nikhaṇaṁ vane* 'you **would do** wrong, charioteer, if you were to bury me in the forest', a present participle, *kayirā* being read as if it carried the counterfactual sense a conditional would.

### The gerundive

The third participle means *to be done*, *should be done*, *must be done*. It carries an obligation and not a time, and everything awkward about its names follows from that one fact.

Earlier grammars call it the "future participle", which is wrong twice over: it is not built on the future stem, and it does not mean 'about to'.[^future-participle-misnomer] Its more exact name is the **gerundive**, or *participium necessitatis*, 'the participle of necessity', and that is the name this book uses and the sense the gloss `grd.` carries throughout. Thānuttamo's chapter heads it "Future **Passive** Participle (aka gerundive, participle of necessity)", which at least restores the passivity the common label drops.

[^future-participle-misnomer]: A real, if vanishingly rare, active participle built on the *future* stem does exist: Oberlies §54, p. 256, gives *(fut.) marissaṁ* 'who is dying, about to die' as his only example, the *-nt-* suffix added to the future instead of the present stem. It is not what "future participle" means in ordinary use, and a reader is unlikely to meet it outside a grammar's own footnotes.

**The commonest ending is *-tabba*.**

| Form | From | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| *gantabbaṁ* | *gacchati* 'goes' | to be gone |
| *kātabbaṁ* | *karoti* 'does' | to be done |
| *pahātabbaṁ* | *pajahati* 'abandons' | to be abandoned |
| *veditabbaṁ* | *vedeti* 'feels, experiences' | to be felt, experienced |

They inflect like *-a* stem adjectives, and the two DN 17 examples below show the whole moral vocabulary of a precept built from nothing but this one suffix:

- Pāṇo na hantabbo, adinnaṁ na **ādātabbaṁ**, kāmesu micchā na caritabbā, musā na bhaṇitabbā, majjaṁ na pātabbaṁ, yathābhuttañca bhuñjathā'ti. (DN 17 — D ii 173)
- *pāṇo* a living being (m. nom. sg.) · *na* not · *hantabbo* to be killed (m. nom. sg. grd.) · *adinnaṁ* what is not given (n. nom. sg.) · *na* not · *ādātabbaṁ* to be taken (n. nom. sg. grd.) · *kāmesu* in sensual pleasures (m. loc. pl.) · *micchā* wrongly · *na* not · *caritabbā* to be indulged in (grd.) · *musā* falsely · *na* not · *bhaṇitabbā* to be spoken (grd.) · *majjaṁ* intoxicant (n. nom. sg.) · *na* not · *pātabbaṁ* to be drunk (n. nom. sg. grd.) · *yathābhuttaṁ* what is properly eaten (n. acc. sg.) · *ca* and · *bhuñjatha* eat! (2 pl. impv.) · *ti* (quotation)
- "A living being **is not to be killed**, what is not given **is not to be taken**, one **should not** wrongly **indulge** in sensual pleasures, one **should not speak** falsely, intoxicants **are not to be drunk**, and you should eat only what is properly given."

- Dveme, bhikkhave, antā pabbajitena na **sevitabbā**. (SN 56.11 — S v 421)
- *dve*<sub>a</sub> two (m. nom. pl.) · *ime*<sub>a</sub> these (m. nom. pl.) · *bhikkhave* monks (m. voc. pl.) · *antā*<sub>a</sub> extremes (m. nom. pl.) · *pabbajitena* by one gone forth (m. inst. sg.) · *na* not · *sevitabbā*<sub>a</sub> to be practised (m. nom. pl. grd.)
- "Monks, these two extremes **are not to be practised** by one who has gone forth."

*Pabbajitena* there is the agent, in the instrumental, where a gerundive puts an agent when it names one. The Khandhaka's account of what a student owes his preceptor runs to 158 gerundives in 1,364 words, and names the student, in that same instrumental, fourteen times. One agent for every eleven obligations.[^upajjhayavatta] The rest read like this:

- Sace upajjhāyo gāmaṁ pavisitukāmo hoti, nivāsanaṁ **dātabbaṁ**, paṭinivāsanaṁ **paṭiggahetabbaṁ**, kāyabandhanaṁ **dātabbaṁ**, saguṇaṁ katvā saṅghāṭiyo **dātabbā**, dhovitvā patto sodako **dātabbo**. (Kd 1 — Vin i 46)
- *sace* if · *upajjhāyo* the preceptor (m. nom. sg.) · *gāmaṁ* the village (m. acc. sg.) · *pavisitukāmo* wanting to enter (m. nom. sg.) · *hoti* is · *nivāsanaṁ* a sarong (n. nom. sg.) · *dātabbaṁ* is to be given (n. nom. sg. grd.) · *paṭinivāsanaṁ* the one being worn (n. nom. sg.) · *paṭiggahetabbaṁ* is to be received (n. nom. sg. grd.) · *kāyabandhanaṁ* a belt (n. nom. sg.) · *saguṇaṁ* the two as one (adverbial acc.) · *katvā* having made (absol.) · *saṅghāṭiyo* the outer robes (f. nom. pl.) · *dātabbā* are to be given (f. nom. pl. grd.)[^saguna] · *dhovitvā* having rinsed (absol.) · *patto* the bowl (m. nom. sg.) · *sodako* still wet (m. nom. sg.) · *dātabbo* is to be given (m. nom. sg. grd.)
- "If the preceptor wants to enter the village, a sarong **is to be given** and the one he is wearing **received**, a belt **is to be given**, the outer robes folded together and **given**, the bowl rinsed and **given** to him still wet."

Five gerundives, and not one of them names who must do any of it. *Dātabba* alone stands three times in three forms (neuter singular beside *nivāsanaṁ*, feminine plural beside *saṅghāṭiyo*, masculine singular beside *patto*), and each time it agrees with the thing to be given and never with the person who must give it. That is the whole grammar of the form. A gerundive is passive, so its subject is what the action is done to; the agent is an extra, and Pāli supplies it only when it is not obvious.[^grd-agent]

[^grd-agent]: The translator has no such option, and the gap shows how the Pāli works. Brahmāli renders this sentence "the student should give him a sarong and receive the one he's wearing in return … He should give him a belt", naming the student and then carrying him through four more clauses as *he*: five agents in the English where this sentence's Pāli has none. Nothing has been added to the sense: the section is headed with the student's duties, and the Pāli can leave him unsaid for a page together where an English finite verb must have a subject.

[^upajjhayavatta]: Counted over Kd 1 — Vin i 44, the *upajjhāyavatta* or 'duties towards a preceptor': 158 forms in *-tabb-*, and *saddhivihārikena* 'by the student' fourteen times. That is 116 gerundives per thousand words, against the 14.7 of the Vinaya taken as a whole, so whoever can follow the whole section can follow the Khandhaka's procedural chapters generally.

[^saguna]: DPD's headword *saguṇa* is 'with good qualities', *sa* + *guṇa*, and it is a different word: a collision of a kind to watch for, where the form is right and the sense belongs to something else. The Vinaya commentary glosses the phrase where it stands, in its own *Upajjhāyavattakathā* (Sp V 978): *Saguṇaṁ katvāti dve cīvarāni ekato katvā*, "*saguṇaṁ katvā* means having made the two robes into one". It then explains the plural this example depends on: folded together they are still *saṅghāṭiyo*, plural, *sabbañhi cīvaraṁ saṅghaṭitattā 'saṅghāṭī'ti vuccati*, "for every robe, being stitched together, is called a *saṅghāṭī*".

A second ending is *-anīya*, with a family of near-relations: *-aniya*, *-aneyya*, *-eyya*.

| Form | From | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| *gamanīyo* | *gacchati* 'goes' | to be gone |
| *karaṇīyaṁ* | *karoti* 'does' | to be done |
| *pariññeyyo* | *parijānāti* 'fully understands' | to be fully understood |
| *vedanīyaṁ* | *vedeti* 'feels, experiences' | to be felt, experienced |

- Rūpaṁ, bhikkhave, **pariññeyyo** dhammo, vedanā pariññeyyo dhammo, saññā pariññeyyo dhammo, saṅkhārā pariññeyyo dhammo, viññāṇaṁ pariññeyyo dhammo. (SN 22.23 — S iii 26)
- *rūpaṁ* form (n. nom. sg.) · *bhikkhave* monks (m. voc. pl.) · *pariññeyyo*<sub>a</sub> to be fully understood (m. nom. sg. grd.) · *dhammo*<sub>a</sub> a thing (m. nom. sg.) · *vedanā* feeling (f. nom. sg.) · *saññā* perception (f. nom. sg.) · *saṅkhārā* volitional formations (m. nom. pl.) · *viññāṇaṁ* consciousness (n. nom. sg.)
- "Monks, form is a thing **to be fully understood**, feeling is a thing to be fully understood, perception is a thing to be fully understood, volitional formations are a thing to be fully understood, consciousness is a thing to be fully understood."

That is the five aggregates, each in turn declared *pariññeyyo*: the whole of a practitioner's task, stated as a single repeated gerundive.

The Vinaya's use of the same ending is a good deal less exalted, and it is interesting for the repetition, not the sense. Every training rule governing a monastic's deportment closes on the same two words:

- “Sakkaccaṁ piṇḍapātaṁ paṭiggahessāmīti **sikkhā karaṇīyā**”ti. (Bu Sk 27 — Vin iv 190)
- *sakkaccaṁ* respectfully · *piṇḍapātaṁ* almsfood (m. acc. sg.) · *paṭiggahessāmi* I shall receive (1 sg. fut.) · *ti* (quotative) · *sikkhā*<sub>a</sub> the training (f. nom. sg.) · *karaṇīyā*<sub>a</sub> is to be done (f. nom. sg. grd.) · *ti* (quotation)
- "'I shall receive almsfood respectfully' — **this is the training to be done**."

*Karaṇīyā* is feminine because *sikkhā* is, and it is the gerundive proper of the same verb whose substantivised neuter, *karaṇīyaṁ* 'duty, work', appears in the drift table at the end of this chapter. The pair stands unaltered at the close of seventy-nine sentences in the root texts.[^sekhiya-count] Everything before the quotative changes and everything after it does not, the property Appendix B measures as a low type/token ratio and recommends a reader seek out, arrived at here by nothing cleverer than repetition.

[^sekhiya-count]: Counted in the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana root texts, where *sikkhā karaṇīyā* occurs 79 times, all of them in the Vibhaṅga. Seventy-six are in the monks' analysis, one more than the seventy-five rules, because the last rule is given twice: once as first laid down and once as amended to exempt the sick, *na udake agilāno uccāraṁ … karissāmi*. The remaining three are where the nuns' analysis restates a rule in its own words.

A third, less obvious type is formed with a bare *-a*, no *-tabba* or *-(a)nīya* affix at all: a direct survival of the Sanskrit pattern *sukara-* 'easy to do', *duṣkara-* 'hard to do'.[^a-gerundive] *Dukkara* 'hard to do' and *sulabha* 'easy to get' are built this way, the *su-* and *du-* prefixes in the role a suffix fills everywhere else.

[^a-gerundive]: Oberlies §55 (p. 259). The type is unproductive (new examples were made by analogy to the old ones, not freely), so it survives only with *su-* or *du-* attached and not as a general-purpose gerundive suffix.

[^grd-density]: Counted over the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana root texts by shelf, as forms in *-tabb-* against the shelf's own word count: 6,170 in the five Vinaya books (420,685 words, 14.7 per thousand), 4,578 in the forty-one files of the Sutta Piṭaka (1,549,966 words, 2.95), and 4,829 in the Abhidhamma (830,590 words, 5.8). The two lists in the text are illustrative, so the ranking itself is given here, each gerundive counted across all its inflected forms. The Vinaya's ten commonest are *dātabba* 488, *kātabba* 486, *gantabba* 321, *jānitabba* 194, *vattabba* 192, *ñāpetabba* 166, *nikkhipitabba* 163, *upasampādetabba* 160, *dhāretabba* 144, *kāretabba* 126; and *jānitabba* is procedural too, the word for ascertaining whether a building site is fit before it is approved. The Sutta Piṭaka's are *pahātabba* 537, *veditabba* 484, *bhāvetabba* 402, *sikkhitabba* 295, *sevitabba* 240, *sacchikātabba* 157, *vitthāretabba* 95, *kātabba* 91, *niddisitabba* 86, *paccavekkhitabba* 68. One form, *kātabba*, is in both tens, which states the disjunction as starkly as possible. Widening to eighteen a side adds only *dātabba*, *gantabba* and *vattabba*, the three other verbs no Pāli of any kind can do without.

[^cca-yha]: The two are not quite the same formation, and DPD separates them. *Gārayha* it derives as √*garah* > *gārah* + *ya*, Sanskrit *garhya*: the *y* and the *h* changing places, the *-ya* suffix clearly. *Kicca* it derives as √*kar* > *kir* + *tya*, Sanskrit *kṛtya*, so the *cc* is an assimilated *ty*, not an assimilated *y*. The endings converge; the suffixes behind them do not.

A last, small group has the endings *-cca* or *-yha*: assimilations of *-(i)ya*, the same sound changes that produced *-ṭha/dha/ḷha* on the *-ta* past participle. *Kicca* 'to be done', from *karoti*, and *gārayha* 'to be reproached', from *garahati*, both inflect like *-a* stem adjectives, and neither needs a table of its own.[^cca-yha]

The gerundive is not spread evenly through the canon, and where it collects says what it is for. In the root texts *-tabba* runs at about fifteen occurrences per thousand words in the Vinaya against three in the Sutta Piṭaka, and the verbs it attaches to in the two are all but disjoint. The Vinaya's commonest are *dātabba* 'to be given', *kātabba* 'to be done', *gantabba* 'to be gone to', *upasampādetabba* 'to be given the full ordination', *nissajjitabba* 'to be relinquished'. The suttas' are *pahātabba* 'to be abandoned', *bhāvetabba* 'to be developed', *veditabba* 'to be known', *sevitabba* 'to be cultivated', *sacchikātabba* 'to be realised'.[^grd-density] One list is a procedure and the other is a practice. The suffix is the same; the obligations are not.

### Participles on the page

Three participles, four formations and a paradigm make up the system. What follows is the rest a reader meets: a participle negated, a participle leaning on a helping verb, a participle that has stopped needing a noun, and a participle whose sense has walked away from its derivation.

**Negated with *a-*.** Participles are occasionally negated with the adverb *na*, but usually with the prefix *a-*, which becomes *an-* before a vowel.

- … **asantaṁ** vā ajjhattaṁ kāmacchandaṁ: 'natthi me ajjhattaṁ kāmacchando'ti pajānāti … (AN 3.121 — A i 272)
- *asantaṁ* not being present (n. acc. sg. prp.) · *vā* or · *ajjhattaṁ* internal (n. acc. sg.) · *kāmacchandaṁ* sensual desire (n. acc. sg.) · *na atthi* there is not · *me* for me (dat. sg.) · *ti* (quotation) · *pajānāti* he understands (3 sg. pres.)
- "Or, sensual desire **not being present** internally, he understands: 'There is no sensual desire in me.'"

*Asantaṁ* is itself a present participle, the negated form of *santaṁ*, 'being', from *atthi*: the *-nt* type this chapter has just given a full paradigm, used here in a way that paradigm alone would not predict.

**With helping verbs.** Pāli can, though it rarely must, add a helping verb (*atthi*, *bhavati*, *hoti*) to a participle, much as English adds 'is' or 'has been'. *Diṭṭhosi*, already met above, is the fused case: *asi* 'you are' welded onto *diṭṭho* 'seen', with no space between them on the page. Translation of the helping verb is often not literal: *hoti* can as easily give 'has' or 'has been' as 'is':

- Idha, bhikkhave, ekaccassa kāyaduccaritaṁ **kataṁ** hoti, akataṁ hoti kāyasucaritaṁ. (AN 2.3 — A i 49)
- *idha* here · *bhikkhave* monks (m. voc. pl.) · *ekaccassa* for someone (m. gen. sg.) · *kāyaduccaritaṁ* bodily misconduct (n. nom. sg.) · *kataṁ* done (n. nom. sg. pp.) · *hoti* is (3 sg. pres.) · *akataṁ* not done (n. nom. sg. pp.) · *kāyasucaritaṁ* bodily good conduct (n. nom. sg.)
- "Here, monks, for someone bodily bad conduct **has been done**, and bodily good conduct has not been done."

A participle can even serve as the helping verb of another participle:

- So sabyābajjhehi phassehi **phuṭṭho** **samāno** sabyābajjhaṁ vedanaṁ vedayati ekantadukkhaṁ, seyyathāpi sattā nerayikā. (AN 3.23 — A i 122)
- *so*<sub>a</sub> he (m. nom. sg.) · *sabyābajjhehi*<sub>b</sub> afflictive (m. inst. pl.) · *phassehi*<sub>b</sub> by contacts (m. inst. pl.) · *phuṭṭho*<sub>a</sub> touched (m. nom. sg. pp.) · *samāno*<sub>a</sub> being (m. nom. sg. prp.) · *sabyābajjhaṁ*<sub>c</sub> afflictive (f. acc. sg.) · *vedanaṁ*<sub>c</sub> feeling (f. acc. sg.) · *vedayati* feels (3 sg. pres.) · *ekantadukkhaṁ*<sub>c</sub> entirely painful (f. acc. sg.) · *seyyathāpi* just as · *sattā nerayikā* beings in hell (m. nom. pl.)
- "He, **being** **touched** by afflictive contacts, feels afflictive feeling, entirely painful, just as beings in hell do."

And a periphrasis carries the tenses English's own perfect system covers, one step further than 'is' and 'has been' reach. A past participle plus *bhavissati* (future in form, but not necessarily future in sense here) gives a pluperfect or a future-perfect: *patto abhavissaṁ* 'I would have attained', *gato bhavissati* 'he will have gone' (or, depending on context, 'he must have gone').[^periphrastic-perfect]

[^periphrastic-perfect]: Oberlies §56(d) (p. 262), calling the construction a *futurum exactum* when read as future-perfect and noting it can equally carry the pluperfect sense, past participle plus a past-tense auxiliary supplying 'had'.

**As nouns.** Like adjectives generally, a participle can stand alone as a noun when no noun is named for it to agree with:

- **Buddho** dhammamadesesi, jātiyā samatikkamaṁ. (SN 5.6 — S i 132)
- *buddho* the Awakened One (m. nom. sg. pp., substantivised) · *dhammaṁ* the teaching (n. acc. sg.) · *adesesi* taught (3 sg. aor.) · *jātiyā* of birth (f. gen. sg.) · *samatikkamaṁ* the going-beyond (m. acc. sg.)
- "**The Awakened One** taught the teaching, the going-beyond of birth."

- Pāṇo na hantabbo, **adinnaṁ** na ādātabbaṁ … (DN 17 — D ii 173, opening of the same precept quoted above)
- *pāṇo* a living being (m. nom. sg.) · *na* not · *hantabbo* to be killed (m. nom. sg. grd.) · *adinnaṁ* what is not given (n. nom. sg. pp., substantivised) · *na* not · *ādātabbaṁ* to be taken (n. nom. sg. grd.)
- "A living being is not to be killed, **what is not given** should not be taken …"

*Buddho* is the plainest possible case: a past participle, 'awakened', that has become a proper name so completely that its participial origin is easy to forget, though it is the reason the word means what it means.

**With non-literal meanings.** A participle's ordinary sense sometimes drifts a long way from its literal derivation, and the drift is better known than re-derived each time.

| Form | Sense | Literally | Built from |
|---|---|---|---|
| *arahaṁ* (prp.) | worthy, an arahant | "(being) worthy" | *arahati* 'is worthy' |
| *kamanīyo* (grd.) | lovely, desirable | "to be desired" | *kāmeti* 'desires' |
| *karaṇīyaṁ* (grd., n.) | duty, work | "to be done" | *karoti* 'does, makes' |
| *kuddho* (pp.) | angry | "angered" | *kujjhati* 'is angry' |
| *dassanīyo* (grd.) | beautiful | "to be seen" | *dassati* 'sees' |
| *paṭipanno* (pp.) | practising | "entered upon" | *paṭipajjati* 'enters upon' |
| *phoṭṭhabbaṁ* (grd., n.) | a tangible object | "to be touched" | *phusati* 'touches' |
| *bhojanīyaṁ* (grd., n.) | food | "to be eaten" | *bhuñjati* 'eats' |
| *saṅkhittaṁ* (pp.) | in brief | "shortened" | *saṅkhipati* 'shortens' |
| *santo* (prp.) | true, good | "existing" | *atthi* 'exists' |

### What this chapter is for

The largest thing a participle does was promised at the start and belongs to Chapter 16: put a participle beside a subject of its own, in an oblique case, and the pair becomes a clause with no finite verb anywhere in it: the locative and genitive absolutes. That is syntax and it needs the room. Here it is useful only to say which of this chapter's participles are eligible for it, because not all of them are. The present participle and the past passive participle head absolutes freely and account for nearly all of them. The past active participle does so too, rarely, one reason it has a section above, not a footnote. The gerundive never does.

That is the chapter's practical outcome. Tell a past participle from a present one, and which voice a past participle carries, and you have most of what the rest of the book assumes, because participles are a large part of how canonical Pāli makes statements at all, not an ornament of it. The awakening formula quoted above says *khīṇā jāti, vusitaṁ brahmacariyaṁ, kataṁ karaṇīyaṁ* and has no finite verb in it until *pajānāti* arrives to report the whole thing. Four participles carry the most important sentence in the canon. This chapter exists mainly to teach you to hear them as verbs, instead of parsing them as adjectives and then wondering where the verb went.

**Exercises for this chapter are in the workbook**, under "Exercises: Participles", with worked answers at the back of that volume.

**Principal sources.** Oberlies §§54–56 for the participle's place in the non-finite verb system and for the rule that says which verbal adjectives are active and which are passive; Thānuttamo for the past active participle, missing from every coursebook consulted; DPD for every gloss in the worked examples.

## Absolutives and Infinitives

A brahmin farmer, meeting the Buddha at the edge of his field, tells him to go and earn his food as everyone else does:

- Ahaṁ kho, samaṇa, **kasāmi** ca **vapāmi** ca, **kasitvā** ca **vapitvā** ca bhuñjāmi. (SN 7.11 — S i 172)
- *ahaṁ* I (nom. sg.) · *kho* indeed · *samaṇa* ascetic (m. voc. sg.) · *kasāmi* I plough (1 sg. pres.) · *ca* and · *vapāmi* I sow (1 sg. pres.) · *kasitvā* having ploughed (absol.) · *vapitvā* having sown (absol.) · *bhuñjāmi* I eat (1 sg. pres.)
- "I, ascetic, **plough** and **sow**, and **having ploughed** and **sown**, I eat."

The first half of that sentence is two finite verbs joined by *ca*, which says something at the outset: Pāli can coordinate finite verbs, and does. The second half is the same two actions said again in the form Pāli reaches for far more readily: two indeclinable forms leading into a third, not two more finite verbs. That form is the **absolutive** (*pubbakiriyā*, 'the prior action'), also called the gerund, and it names an action completed before the finite verb it depends on.[^ger-vs-grd]

[^ger-vs-grd]: The gerund (ger.) is this chapter's absolutive. It is not the **gerundive** (grd.), Chapter 10's name for the future passive participle. The terms differ by one letter and name different things: an action already complete, against an obligation not yet discharged.

The preference is strong enough to shape how a sutta sentence looks on the page. Long stretches of narrative in the canon consist of a chain of absolutives with a single finite verb at the end, and the practical consequence for a reader is a reading strategy, not a rule to memorise: when a sentence will not resolve, find the finite verb first (it is usually last) and read everything in front of it as a sequence of completed actions leading up to it.

### The absolutive

An absolutive takes no ending for case, gender, number or person. It is an indeclinable, and so it cannot agree with anything; it modifies the action of the clause instead of describing any noun in it. Oberlies counts eight suffixes that form it, of which two account for nearly everything a reader will meet; the remainder are in Appendix A.4.[^abs-eight-suffixes]

[^abs-eight-suffixes]: Oberlies §58, p. 265, lists *-(i)tvā*, *-(i)tvāna(ṁ)*, *-(t)tu(ṁ)*, *-tūna*, *-(i)yă*, *-(i)yāna(ṁ)*, *-eyya* and *-aṁ*, noting that all of them can be added to the present stem. Appendix A.4 gives Ñāṇatusita's own grouping of the same material, which differs in arrangement, not in content.

#### The *-tvā* type

The commonest suffix by a wide margin is *-tvā*, joined to the root or the present stem, often through a connecting *-i-*:

| Form | From | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| *katvā* / *karitvā* | *karoti* | having done |
| *gantvā* | *gacchati* | having gone |
| *datvā* / *daditvā* | *dadāti* / *deti* | having given |
| *vatvā* | *vadati* | having said |
| *sutvā* / *suṇitvā* | *suṇāti* | having heard |
| *hitvā* / *jahitvā* | *jahati*[^jahetva-unattested] | having abandoned |

[^jahetva-unattested]: A third variant, *jahetvā*, is sometimes printed beside these two. It does not occur in the root texts of the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana at all, and has a single commentarial attestation, a gloss in the Mahāniddesa-aṭṭhakathā (Nidd-a II 286). *Hitvā* (186 root-text occurrences) and *jahitvā* (85) are both ordinary.

Many verbs offer two absolutives (*katvā* beside *karitvā*, *sutvā* beside *suṇitvā*), and the choice between them carries no difference in meaning. The longer variant *-tvāna* belongs almost entirely to verse, and the formula introducing a sutta's closing stanzas puts it there:

- Idaṁ **vatvāna** sugato athāparaṁ etadavoca satthā. (DN 16 — D ii 90)
- *idaṁ* this (n. acc. sg.) · *vatvāna* having said (absol.) · *sugato* the Well-Gone One (m. nom. sg.) · *atha* then · *aparaṁ* further · *etad* this (n. acc. sg.) · *avoca* said (3 sg. aor.) · *satthā* the Teacher (m. nom. sg.)
- "**Having said** this, the Well-Gone One, the Teacher, then said this further."

#### The *-(i)ya* type

A second suffix, *-ya*, attaches mostly, though not only, to verbs carrying a prefix. Where the root ends in a long *ā*, the suffix simply follows it; where it ends in a consonant or a short vowel, the resulting cluster assimilates, and the outcome is a doubled consonant that can look nothing like a suffix at all:

| Form | From | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| *āgamma* | *āgacchati* | having come |
| *upādāya* | *upādiyati* | having taken up |
| *pahāya* | *pajahati* | having abandoned |
| *paccakkhāya* | *paccakkhāti* (*paṭi* + *ā* + √*khā*) | having renounced |
| *samatikkamma* | *samatikkamati* | having passed beyond |
| *upasampajja* | *upasampajjati* | having entered upon |
| *pecca* | *peti* (*pa* + *eti*) | having died |

The last of these shows the assimilation at its most opaque: *pa* + *i* + *tya* gives *pecca*, in which nothing of the suffix survives as a recognisable syllable.[^ya-assimilation] Keep *pecca* apart from *paṭicca*, which the footnote names beside it: the two differ only in their prefix and are otherwise the same formation from the same root, so *pecca* is *pa-* 'onward' (one has *gone on*, and Sanskrit writes it *pretya*), while *paṭicca* is *paṭi-* 'back' and belongs to *pacceti*, Sanskrit *pratītya*.[^pecca-paticca] The reduplication that a present stem may carry does not transfer (*pajahati* has *pahāya*, built on the bare root), the behaviour Chapter 10 noted for the gerundive *pahātabba*.

[^ya-assimilation]: Oberlies §58(5), p. 265: roots in short vowels insert a *-t-* before the suffix, and the cluster is then assimilated. The *-cca* outcome is the most visible product of the rule, in *paṭicca*, *pecca*, *vivicca* and *sakkacca*.

[^pecca-paticca]: DPD, s.v. *pecca*: the gerund of *peti*, *pa* + √*i* > *e* + *tya*, Sanskrit *pretya*; and s.v. *paṭicca*: the gerund of *pacceti*, *pati* + √*i* + *tya*, Sanskrit *pratītya*. The distinction is easy to lose because both are *-cca* absolutives of the same root and only the prefixes separate them; the Sanskrit settles it, *pra-* against *prati-*.

Either type negates with *a-*, becoming *an-* before a vowel, as the participle does in Chapter 10. The first rule of the Pātimokkha turns on two of them in a row, one of each type:

- Yo pana bhikkhu bhikkhūnaṁ sikkhāsājīvasamāpanno sikkhaṁ **apaccakkhāya** dubbalyaṁ **anāvikatvā** methunaṁ dhammaṁ paṭiseveyya … pārājiko hoti asaṁvāso. (Bu Pj 1 — Vin iii 23)
- *yo pana bhikkhu* whatever monk (m. nom. sg.) · *bhikkhūnaṁ* of the monks (m. gen. pl.) · *sikkhāsājīvasamāpanno* having undertaken the training and way of life (m. nom. sg.) · *sikkhaṁ* the training (f. acc. sg.) · *apaccakkhāya* not having renounced (absol., negated) · *dubbalyaṁ* his weakness (n. acc. sg.) · *anāvikatvā* not having disclosed (absol., negated) · *methunaṁ dhammaṁ* sexual intercourse (m. acc. sg.) · *paṭiseveyya* should engage in (3 sg. opt.) · *pārājiko* expelled (m. nom. sg.) · *hoti* is · *asaṁvāso* excluded from the community (m. nom. sg.)
- "Whatever monk, having undertaken the monks' training and way of life, **without having renounced** the training and **without having disclosed** his weakness, engages in sexual intercourse … is expelled and excluded from the community."

*Apaccakkhāya* is the negation of the *paccakkhāya* in the table above, and *anāvikatvā* of *āvikaroti* 'makes clear'.[^neg-absolutive] The negation gives the rule its whole force: the two absolutives describe two things the monk could have done and did not, not things he did. A negated absolutive names the road not taken, and here it is the road that would have made the act no offence at all, since a monk who renounces the training first is no longer bound by it.

[^neg-absolutive]: DPD parses *apaccakkhāya* as the gerund of *na paccakkhāti*, literally 'not speaking against', and *anāvikatvā* as *na* + *an* + *āvi* + *katvā*, the absolutive of the compound verb *āvikaroti*. *Apaccakkhāya* has 11 root-text occurrences and *anāvikatvā* 9, and both are rare outside this rule and the passages that quote it. The Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana and the Pali Text Society both print the single *p* at this rule, and so does DPD in its own citation of the line. A doubled *appaccakkhāya* is canonical elsewhere, but it is not the reading here.

#### Whose action is it?

In most sentences the absolutive's unexpressed agent is the subject of the main verb, and a chain of them shares that one subject throughout. The formula closing a teaching encounter is the standard case:

- Atha kho bhagavā soṇadaṇḍaṁ brāhmaṇaṁ dhammiyā kathāya **sandassetvā samādapetvā samuttejetvā sampahaṁsetvā** uṭṭhāyāsanā pakkāmīti. (DN 4 — D i 126)
- *atha kho* then · *bhagavā* the Blessed One (m. nom. sg.) · *soṇadaṇḍaṁ brāhmaṇaṁ* the brahmin Soṇadaṇḍa (m. acc. sg.) · *dhammiyā kathāya* with a talk on the Dhamma (f. inst. sg.) · *sandassetvā* having instructed (absol.) · *samādapetvā* having roused (absol.) · *samuttejetvā* having inspired (absol.) · *sampahaṁsetvā* having gladdened (absol.) · *uṭṭhāya* having risen (absol.) · *āsanā* from the seat (n. abl. sg.) · *pakkāmi* departed (3 sg. aor.) · *ti* (quotation)
- "Then the Blessed One, **having instructed, roused, inspired and gladdened** the brahmin Soṇadaṇḍa with a talk on the Dhamma, rose from his seat and departed."

Five absolutives, one aorist. Every one of the five is the Buddha's action, and the sentence does not need to say so once.

The rule that follows from this, that the absolutive's agent is the main verb's subject, is the one most pedagogical grammars give, and it is right about the great majority of sentences. It is not, however, exceptionless, and the Pāli commentators noticed the exception and gave it a name. Consider the sentence that closes the description of the liberated person:

- Puna caparaṁ, bhikkhave, bhikkhu sabbaso nevasaññānāsaññāyatanaṁ **samatikkamma** saññāvedayitanirodhaṁ **upasampajja** viharati. Paññāya cassa **disvā** āsavā parikkhīṇā honti. (MN 25 — M i 160)
- *puna caparaṁ* furthermore · *bhikkhave* monks (m. voc. pl.) · *bhikkhu* a monk (m. nom. sg.) · *sabbaso* entirely (adv.) · *nevasaññānāsaññāyatanaṁ* the dimension of neither perception nor non-perception (n. acc. sg.) · *samatikkamma* having passed beyond (absol.) · *saññāvedayitanirodhaṁ* the cessation of perception and feeling (m. acc. sg.) · *upasampajja* having entered upon (absol.) · *viharati* dwells (3 sg. pres.) · *paññāya* with wisdom (f. inst. sg.) · *ca* and · *assa* for him, his (m. gen./dat. sg.) · *disvā* having seen (absol.) · *āsavā* the defilements (m. nom. pl.) · *parikkhīṇā* exhausted (m. nom. pl. pp.) · *honti* are (3 pl. pres.)
- "Furthermore, monks, a monk, **having passed** entirely beyond the dimension of neither perception nor non-perception, **having entered** the cessation of perception and feeling, dwells there. And **having seen** with wisdom, his defilements are exhausted."

The first sentence is ordinary: *samatikkamma* and *upasampajja* both belong to *bhikkhu*, the subject of *viharati*. The second is not. The subject of *honti* is *āsavā*, 'the defilements', and the defilements do not do the seeing. The seer is the monk, present in the sentence only as *assa*, a form that is genitive and dative alike and cannot be told apart here.[^assa-case]

[^assa-case]: *Cassa* is *ca* + *assa*. The third-person pronoun's genitive and dative are identical in the singular (Appendix A.2), so the case cannot be settled from the form, and the sentence does not settle it either. For the construction both readings come to the same: whichever case it is, it is not the nominative, and it is not *āsavā*.

The tradition analysed this directly. Dhammapāla, commenting on the Visuddhimagga, sets out the ordinary construction and then turns to this one with the phrase *asamāne pana kattari*, "but when the agent is not the same", and gives two illustrations: the sentence above, and a made-up one that the grammatical tradition passes from hand to hand, *pisācaṁ disvā bhayaṁ hoti*, "having seen a goblin, fear arises."[^asamane-kattari] The example is well chosen, because in English the mismatch passes unnoticed. Fear does not see the goblin. A person does, and the fear results.

[^asamane-kattari]: Visuddhimagga-mahāṭīkā, Paññābhūminiddesa, on the exposition of dependent origination (¶580). *Kattari* is itself a locative absolute (Kaccāyana's own grammatical idiom, Ākhyātakappa 445), so *asamāne kattari* is literally 'the agent being not the same'. Later ṭīkās turn the phrase into a single adjective and call such a form *asamānakattuka*, 'having a different agent'. The *pisāca* sentence is the commentator's illustration, not a canonical quotation.

A second commentary is blunter still. Glossing the same line, the Pañcapakaraṇa-mūlaṭīkā says outright: *na āsavā paññāya passanti*, "the defilements do not see with wisdom", and explains that they are called 'exhausted having seen' because their exhaustion depends on the seeing.[^mulatika-asava] Hold on to that last point, because it shows something the grammar alone would not. Here the absolutive marks *why*, and not merely what happened first. The commentaries gloss *disvā* in this construction with *dassanahetu*, 'by reason of seeing', and set beside it a standing pair of illustrations: *sappiṁ pivitvā balaṁ hoti, sīhaṁ disvā bhayaṁ hoti*, "having drunk ghee, strength arises; having seen a lion, fear arises."[^hetu-attha] Neither strength nor fear performs the action in front of it. Both are its result.

[^mulatika-asava]: Pañcapakaraṇa-mūlaṭīkā, on the Puggalapaññatti's first exposition, the *Ekakaniddesa* (¶24); the same gloss recurs, in nearly the same words, in the Dīgha-nikāya ṭīkā on the *Mahānidānasutta* (Sv-pṭ II 152).

[^hetu-attha]: Udāna-aṭṭhakathā on the *Soṇasutta*, Ud 5.6 (Ud-a 314), which gives all three examples together as the model for the verse's own *disvā* and *ñatvā*, and says those are to be taken as causal in sense (*hetuatthatā*). The Visuddhimagga-mahāṭīkā gives the pair as *ghataṁ pivitvā balaṁ hoti, sīhaṁ disvā bhayaṁ hoti*, calling the *-tvā* form *hetuattha* (Saṅkhārupekkhāñāṇakathā, ¶802); its *pisāca* sentence is at ¶580. The ṭīkā on MN 111 repeats the pair and cites the mahāṭīkā for it (¶96), and the Sāratthadīpanī on the Vinaya's *Sudinnabhāṇavāra* repeats it without a reference (¶24); both describe the form as *hetuattha* 'causal in sense' and *asamānakattuka* 'of a different agent'.

So the rule has two halves. Ordinarily the absolutive's agent is the subject of the main verb, and to assume so is right nearly every time. Where the main clause's subject cannot plausibly have performed the action (where it is an abstract noun like *bhayaṁ* or *āsavā*, and the sentence describes a consequence, not a sequence), the agent is someone else, usually named in the genitive or dative, and the absolutive gives a cause.

The absolutive can also carry the weight of a conditional clause, with no verb in the conditional mood anywhere in the sentence:

- Mañhi, bhante, aññatitthiyā sāvakaṁ **labhitvā** kevalakappaṁ nāḷandaṁ paṭākaṁ parihareyyuṁ. (MN 56 — M i 379)
- *maṁ* me (acc. sg.) · *hi* indeed · *bhante* venerable sir (m. voc. sg.) · *aññatitthiyā* followers of another sect (m. nom. pl.) · *sāvakaṁ* as a disciple (m. acc. sg.) · *labhitvā* having gained (absol.) · *kevalakappaṁ* the whole of (adj., acc. sg.) · *nāḷandaṁ* Nāḷandā (f. acc. sg.) · *paṭākaṁ* a banner (n. acc. sg.) · *parihareyyuṁ* they would carry around (3 pl. opt.)
- "For if followers of another sect **were to gain** me as a disciple, venerable sir, they would carry a banner around the whole of Nāḷandā."

Chapter 8 gave the optative as the ordinary vehicle for the *apodosis*, 'the "then" half', of a conditional sentence. Here the *protasis*, the "if" half, has no verb of its own at all; an absolutive stands in for it.

#### Absolutives that have stopped being verbs

A number of absolutives have hardened into other parts of speech, and are best learned as vocabulary, not parsed each time. The largest group functions as postpositions, governing a case of its own:

| Form | From | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| *uddissa* | *uddisati* | for, on account of (+ acc.) |
| *paṭicca* | *pacceti* | dependent on, because of (+ acc.) |
| *ārabbha* | *ārabhati* | concerning, about (+ acc.) |
| *nissāya* / *upanissāya* | *nissayati* | depending on, close to (+ acc.) |
| *upādāya* | *upādiyati* | out of, in consequence of |
| *vivicca* | *viviccati* | secluded from |
| *ṭhapetvā* | *ṭhapeti* | except for, apart from (+ acc.) |

- Cakkhuñca **paṭicca** rūpe ca uppajjati viññāṇaṁ, cakkhuviññāṇantveva saṅkhyaṁ gacchati. (MN 38 — M i 259)
- *cakkhuṁ* the eye (n. acc. sg.) · *ca* and · *paṭicca* dependent on (postpos. + acc.) · *rūpe* forms (n. acc. pl.) · *uppajjati* arises (3 sg. pres.) · *viññāṇaṁ* consciousness (n. nom. sg.) · *cakkhuviññāṇaṁ* eye-consciousness (n. nom. sg.) · *tu* but · *eva* just · *saṅkhyaṁ* reckoning (f. acc. sg.) · *gacchati* goes (3 sg. pres.)
- "**Dependent on** the eye and forms, consciousness arises; it is reckoned as just eye-consciousness."

*Paṭicca* is the most consequential word in this list. It is an absolutive used as a preposition, and it names the doctrine on which the whole of the canon's causal analysis is built: *paṭiccasamuppāda*, dependent origination.

*Ṭhapetvā*, literally 'having set aside', has become the ordinary word for *except*, and it is how the Vinaya's definitions carve out what they do not cover:

- **Ṭhapetvā** gāmañca gāmūpacārañca avasesaṁ araññaṁ nāma. (Bu Pj 2 — Vin iii 46)
- *ṭhapetvā* except for (postpos. + acc.) · *gāmaṁ* an inhabited area (m. acc. sg.) · *ca* and · *gāmūpacāraṁ* the access to an inhabited area (m. acc. sg.) · *ca* and · *avasesaṁ* the remainder (n. nom. sg.) · *araññaṁ* wilderness (n. nom. sg.) · *nāma* is called
- "**Except for** an inhabited area and its access, the remainder is called 'wilderness'."

Nothing in that sentence is being set anywhere. The verb has given up its own sense and kept only the case it governs, which is the process the whole table records.

A second group has become adverbs outright. *Sakkaccaṁ* 'respectfully, carefully' (112 root-text occurrences) is the absolutive of *sakkaroti*, and *asañcicca* 'unintentionally' (98) is the absolutive of a negated *sañceteti*. Neither is felt as a verb any longer.

The second matters in the Vinaya, and a rule read together with its exemption shows why. The rule against killing runs *Yo pana bhikkhu **sañcicca** pāṇaṁ jīvitā voropeyya, pācittiyaṁ* (Bu Pc 61 — Vin iv 124), 'whatever monk should **intentionally** deprive a living being of life: an offence entailing confession'. The list of exemptions that follows it opens *Anāpatti — asañcicca* (Bu Pc 61), 'there is no offence: unintentionally'. One negated absolutive makes the difference between an offence and none, and it is an adverb in both places, agreeing with nothing and governing nothing. Oberlies notes that the process can go one step further and yield an ordinary noun: *upanidhā* 'comparison' is a feminine noun abstracted from the absolutive *upanidhāya*.[^abstracted-nouns]

[^abstracted-nouns]: Oberlies §58, *rem.* (b), p. 269, which gives a noun stem *asañcicca-* alongside *upanidhā-*. The adverbial *asañcicca* quoted above is the absolutive itself, not that derived noun; the two should be kept apart.

### The infinitive

The infinitive names the bare idea of a verb, with no indication of when it happens: English "to do." Like the absolutive it is indeclinable, and the two share an origin: both are frozen case-forms of an old action noun in *-tu-*, the infinitive from its accusative or dative, the absolutive from its instrumental. The commonest suffix is *-tuṁ*:

| Form | From | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| *kātuṁ* | *karoti* | to do |
| *gantuṁ* | *gacchati* | to go |
| *desetuṁ* | *deseti* | to teach |
| *bhāvetuṁ* | *bhāveti* | to develop |
| *sotuṁ* | *suṇāti* | to hear |

One consequence of that shared origin shows on the page. When an infinitive is compounded with *kāma-* 'wanting', it drops its final *-ṁ*: *daṭṭhuṁ* 'to see' plus *kāma* gives *daṭṭhukāmo*, 'wanting to see'. The pattern is very productive (the root texts carry 138 distinct words ending in *-ukāmo*), and recognising it on sight matters, because if you meet *sotukāmo* or *gantukāmo* and look for a stem *sotuka-* you will not find one.[^kama-compounds]

[^kama-compounds]: Oberlies §57 n. 4, p. 263, citing Geiger §207, with *jīvitukāma-* (Dhp 123) and *daṭṭhukāma-* (Sn 685); DPD gives the construction of each as *jīvituṁ + kāma* and *daṭṭhuṁ + kāma*. *Daṭṭhuṁ* belongs to Oberlies's third absolutive suffix, *-(t)tu(ṁ)*, which also supplies the bare absolutive *daṭṭhu* 'having seen': one more sign of how close the two categories are.

Pāli reaches for the infinitive less often than English reaches for "to," because the cases already cover much of the same ground. The dative of an action noun in *-āya* serves the same purpose, and so, less often, does the accusative; Ñāṇatusita lists the dative among the infinitive formations outright.[^dative-infinitive] Chapter 15 takes up the overlap.

[^dative-infinitive]: Appendix A.4 gives "5. (an)āya (dative)" in its list of infinitive suffixes. Oberlies §57, p. 264, gives one example of each, glossing them 'go out in order to see …' (accusative) and *na ca mayaṁ labhāma bhagavantaṁ dassanāya*, "and we did not get an opportunity to see the Blessed One" (dative).

#### Infinitive syntax

An infinitive ordinarily shares its agent with the main verb, and usually precedes it. It attaches most naturally to a predicate of ability, worthiness or ease, and those predicates are frequently impersonal, with no grammatical subject at all. Where that happens, the agent appears in the instrumental, even though the infinitive itself is read actively:

- Na dāni sukaraṁ amhehi lābhasakkārasiloke **pariccajituṁ**. (MN 76 — M i 524)
- *na* not · *dāni* now · *sukaraṁ* easy (n. nom. sg.) · *amhehi* by us (1 pl. inst.) · *lābhasakkārasiloke* gain, honour and fame (m. acc. pl.) · *pariccajituṁ* to give up (inf.)
- "It is not easy for us now **to give up** gain, honour and fame."

There is no nominative anywhere in that sentence for *amhehi* to compete with, and that makes room for it: the instrumental marks the one by whom the giving-up would be done. It is the case, with the same function, that Chapter 9 gave for the agent of a passive, appearing here beside an infinitive that is not passive at all.

The contrast between the two forms this chapter has treated is easiest to see where the canon sets them side by side. A monk considers giving up the training:

- Yannūnāhaṁ sikkhaṁ **paccakkhāya** hīnāyāvattitvā bhoge ca **bhuñjeyyaṁ** puññāni ca **kareyyan**ti. (AN 4.122 — A ii 125)
- *yaṁ nūna* what if · *ahaṁ* I (nom. sg.) · *sikkhaṁ* the training (f. acc. sg.) · *paccakkhāya* having renounced (absol.) · *hīnāya* to the lower (n. dat. sg.) · *āvattitvā* having returned (absol.) · *bhoge* possessions (m. acc. pl.) · *ca* and · *bhuñjeyyaṁ* I might enjoy (1 sg. opt.) · *puññāni* merits (n. acc. pl.) · *kareyyaṁ* I might make (1 sg. opt.) · *ti* (quotation)
- "What if I, **having renounced** the training and returned to the lower life, **were to enjoy** possessions and **make** merit?"

The Buddha's reply, a few lines earlier in the same discourse, uses the same two verbs and puts neither in the optative:

- Sakkā bhoge ca **bhuñjituṁ** puññāni ca **kātuṁ**. (AN 4.122 — A ii 125)
- *sakkā* it is possible (indecl.) · *bhoge* possessions (m. acc. pl.) · *ca* and · *bhuñjituṁ* to enjoy (inf.) · *puññāni* merits (n. acc. pl.) · *kātuṁ* to make (inf.)
- "It is possible **to enjoy** possessions and **to make** merit."

Two absolutives and two optatives in the first; two infinitives and no finite verb at all in the second.[^sakka-indecl] The monk frames a course of action he might take and wants; the answer states, flatly, what can be done. Between them they use most of what this chapter contains.

[^sakka-indecl]: *Sakkā* is an indeclinable meaning 'it is possible', historically an old optative, and it takes an infinitive to complete it. It carries no person or number of its own, so the sentence has no finite verb.

**Exercises for this chapter are in the workbook**, under "Exercises: Absolutives and Infinitives", with worked answers at the back of that volume.

**Principal sources.** Oberlies §§57–58 for the suffix inventories and for which formations are really rare; the Visuddhimagga-mahāṭīkā and the Pañcapakaraṇa-mūlaṭīkā for the tradition's analysis of the absolutive whose agent is not the sentence's subject; Thānuttamo for the absolutives frozen into postpositions.

## Indeclinables and Particles

Every chapter so far has been about endings. A noun takes a case, a verb takes a person, an adjective agrees, and even the participle, which has no person, declines like an adjective. This chapter is about the words that do none of that.

The tradition calls them ***nipātā***, 'those that fall in'. Collins gives the image its full force: a *nipāta* is a word which falls into the language whole and complete, needing no grammatical transformation to fit.[^ind-nipata-etym] They are also called ***avyaya***, 'unchanging', the word behind *avyayībhāva*, the adverbial compound type in the chapter on compounds. Duroiselle counts about two hundred of them.

[^ind-nipata-etym]: Collins p. 121: "The word *nipāta* means falling, and a *nipāta* is a word which falls into language whole and complete, not needing grammatical transformation. All of them, especially the sub-category which western grammar calls adverbs, are sometimes called *avyayībhāva*, not subject to change."

One curious thing about the class needs saying at the outset. The two grammars this book leans on hardest for deciding whether a form is real have no section on indeclinables at all. Oberlies's grammar ends at §58, the absolutive; his index has one entry under *indeclinables*, and it points at a footnote. Geiger's ends at §214, the gerunds in *-yāna*. Neither omission is careless. A historical grammar is organised by inflection, tracking a sound change through a set of endings, and an indeclinable has no endings for the method to work on. So a word class supplying some of the commonest words in the canon falls straight through the middle of both.

The consequence for this chapter is that the sources which do treat it are coursebooks and reference grammars, not philological ones, and that every form below has had to be checked against the canon directly, with no grammarian having done it first. Seventeen forms offered by those sources turned out not to be canonical Pāli. They are not taught here.

### What the tradition does with them

Pāli's own grammarians recognise four kinds of word: ***nāma*** (nouns, adjectives, pronouns, numerals), ***ākhyāta*** (verbs), ***upasagga*** (prefixes) and ***nipāta*** (particles).[^ind-four-classes] The first two inflect; the last two do not. Prefixes are treated in the chapter on word formation, because a prefix cannot stand alone as a word, and standing alone is the property this chapter is about. Everything else that does not inflect is here.

[^ind-four-classes]: Collins pp. vii–viii: "Pali and Sanskrit grammar recognize four kinds of word: *nāma* (nouns, adjectives, pronouns, numerals), *ākhyāta* (verbs), *upasagga* (prepositions, prefixes), and *nipāta* (particles, conjunctions, adverbs), and the book is organized in those terms."

Where a Western grammar sorts these words by part of speech (adverb, conjunction, interjection), the Saddanīti sorts them by the job they do in a sentence. The traditional scheme is the better one to learn first, and the reason is that it has a fourth category. Calling *kho* an "emphatic" says what it feels like; calling it a **filler** says what it is for.[^ind-sadd-886]

[^ind-sadd-886]: Saddanīti 886 f., translated in Collins §4.1: "*samuccaya-vikappana-paṭisedha-pūraṇatthaṁ asatva-vācaka nepātika*, *nipāta* words are used for conjunction, disjunction, negation/prohibition, or as fillers, and do not denote any substantial existent."

| The job | Traditional name | Specimens |
|---|---|---|
| Joining | *samuccaya* | *ca*, *pi*, *atha* |
| Separating | *vikappana* | *vā*, *udāhu* |
| Denying and forbidding | *paṭisedha* | *na*, *no*, *mā*, *alaṁ* |
| Filling | *pūraṇattha* | *kho*, *hi*, *vata*, *sudaṁ*, *khalu* |

Three of those four are what any reader would expect. The fourth is treated in its own section below, because it changes how a page is read.

### Joining and separating

***Ca*** 'and' and ***vā*** 'or' are enclitic, which in practice means they follow the word they attach instead of standing between the two things joined. The commonest arrangement repeats the particle after each member:

- **Santussako ca subharo ca**, appakicco ca sallahukavutti. (Snp 1.8 — Sn p. 25)
- *santussako* content (m. nom. sg.) · *ca* and · *subharo* easy to support (m. nom. sg.) · *ca* and · *appakicco* of few duties · *ca* and · *sallahukavutti* living lightly
- "**Content and easy to support**, of few duties and living lightly."

That is 'A *ca* B *ca*'. Two other arrangements are ordinary, 'A *ca* B' and 'A B *ca*', and the second of these, with a single *ca* at the end, catches a new reader out, because nothing in the first half of the phrase signals that a conjunction is coming.[^ind-ca-three] Bare 'A B', with no *ca* at all and 'and' understood, is also common. The same three arrangements hold for *vā*.

[^ind-ca-three]: Collins §4.1(i): "Horses and elephants can be (i) *assā ca hatthino ca*, (ii) *assā ca hatthino*, or (iii) *assā hatthino ca*." He gives the same three for *vā* at (ii), and adds that *ca* can in context mean 'but' and *vā* can in context mean 'and'.

Because these words are enclitic they can fall inside a phrase that English would keep together, splitting an adjective from its noun:

- … iti **purāṇañca** vedanaṁ paṭihaṅkhāmi **navañca** vedanaṁ na uppādessāmi … (MN 2 — M i 10)
- *iti* thus · *purāṇaṁ*<sub>a</sub> old (f. acc. sg.) · *ca* and · *vedanaṁ*<sub>a</sub> feeling (f. acc. sg.) · *paṭihaṅkhāmi* I shall ward off · *navaṁ*<sub>a</sub> new (f. acc. sg.) · *ca* and · *na* not · *uppādessāmi* I shall produce
- "… thinking, 'I shall ward off **old** feeling **and** shall not produce **new**.'"

Read straight through, *purāṇañca vedanaṁ* looks like 'old, and feeling'. It is not: *ca* has landed after the first word of the phrase because it is enclitic and could land nowhere else, and it joins the whole clause to the one after it. The rule to carry is that a *ca* is not a boundary. Look for what it joins; do not assume it joins whatever stands immediately either side.

*Ca* also combines with the emphatic *eva*, usually assimilating to ***c'eva***, and the pair marks the first member of a list without adding much of its own:

- Ayoniso, bhikkhave, manasikaroto anuppannā **ceva** āsavā uppajjanti, uppannā **ca** āsavā pavaḍḍhanti. (MN 2 — M i 7)
- *ayoniso* unwisely · *bhikkhave* monks (voc. pl.) · *manasikaroto* for one attending (m. gen. sg. pres. part.) · *anuppannā* unarisen (m. nom. pl.) · *ca* and · *eva* just · *āsavā* defilements (m. nom. pl.) · *uppajjanti* arise · *uppannā* arisen · *ca* and · *pavaḍḍhanti* increase
- "For one who attends unwisely, monks, unarisen defilements arise **and** arisen defilements increase."

***Udāhu*** 'or' does a job *vā* does not: it introduces the second limb of a question offering two alternatives.

- Kiṁ **nu** kho, āvuso gotama, taṁ jīvaṁ taṁ sarīraṁ, **udāhu** aññaṁ jīvaṁ aññaṁ sarīran'ti? (DN 7 — D i 159)
- *kiṁ* what · *nu* (question) · *kho* indeed · *āvuso* friend (voc.) · *gotama* Gotama (voc.) · *taṁ* that (n. nom. sg.) · *jīvaṁ* soul · *taṁ* that · *sarīraṁ* body · *udāhu* or · *aññaṁ* other · *jīvaṁ* soul · *aññaṁ* other · *sarīraṁ* body · *ti* (quotation)
- "Is the soul the same as the body, friend Gotama, **or** is the soul one thing and the body another?"

### Enclitics, and how the editions write them

A word is **enclitic** when it cannot begin a sentence and leans instead on the word in front. *Ca*, *vā*, *pi*, *ce*, *pana*, *kho*, *hi* and *eva* are all enclitic, and the grammars state this as a rule about word order.[^ind-enclitic-rule]

[^ind-enclitic-rule]: Thānuttamo, *Māgadhabhāsā*, p. 120, citing Duroiselle's 1997 re-edition: "Never used as sentence initials are *ca*, *vā*, *yadi*, *sace*, *ce* …, *atha*, *atho*." In the 1921 edition, §538, pp. 243–244, Duroiselle says this of *ca*, *vā* and *ce* only, and lists *yadi*, *sace*, *atha* and *atho* without it; p. 330 repeats it of *ca* and *vā*. Thānuttamo's Warder citation, pp. 117–118, belongs to the clause that follows, not to this one, and it is as well that it does: Warder's synopsis there marks each of these words *enclitic* or *initial* one by one, and he makes *sace* and *atha* **initial**. Seven of the eight words called enclitic here are marked enclitic by Warder; the eighth, *eva*, is not a connective and does not appear in his synopsis at all.

The texts state it more strongly, and in a way that changes what a reader has to do about it. Counted as free-standing words in the root texts, *pi* occurs seven times and *eva* ninety-seven, figures so far below what anyone who has read a page of the canon would expect that the first response should be to doubt the count. The count is right. These words are not usually printed as words at all: they are written joined to whatever precedes them, and the joined forms are everywhere. *Sopi*, *ahampi*, *tvampi*, *mayampi*; *tatheva*, *sova*, and the string *-yeva* inside six hundred and ninety-three distinct root-text words.

One sentence of the Sāmaññaphala's account of the psychic powers makes the point seven times over:

- … **ekopi** hutvā bahudhā hoti, **bahudhāpi** hutvā eko hoti … **udakepi** abhijjamāne gacchati seyyathāpi pathaviyā; **ākāsepi** pallaṅkena kamati seyyathāpi pakkhī sakuṇo … (DN 2 — D i 78)
- *eko* one (m. nom. sg.) · *pi* even · *hutvā* having been (absol.) · *bahudhā* manifold · *hoti* becomes · *bahudhā* manifold · *pi* even · *eko* one · *hoti* becomes · *udake* on water (n. loc. sg.) · *pi* even · *abhijjamāne* not breaking apart (loc. sg. pres. part.) · *gacchati* goes · *seyyathā* just as · *pi* even · *pathaviyā* on earth (f. loc. sg.) · *ākāse* in space (m. loc. sg.) · *pi* even · *pallaṅkena* cross-legged (m. instr. sg.) · *kamati* moves · *pakkhī* winged · *sakuṇo* bird
- "… having been **one** he becomes many, having been **many** he becomes one … he goes **on water** without sinking as though on earth; he travels **through space** cross-legged like a bird on the wing."

Every one of those bolded words is a noun or numeral with *pi* fused onto it, and the fusion is the ordinary spelling, not a poetic licence. So the practical skill is seeing *pi* when it is not printed separately. A word ending in *-pi* that will not parse should be tried without it. The same holds for *-eva*, *-yeva* and *-ñeva*, for *-ti* at the end of a quotation, and for the *-ssu* below.

### Denying and forbidding

***Na*** is the ordinary negative, placed before the word it denies, and it negates a whole clause as readily as a single word. ***No*** is a stronger or more emphatic negative and also serves as the negative answer, 'no'. Both attach freely to a series:

- '**Neva** davāya, **na** madāya, **na** maṇḍanāya, **na** vibhūsanāya …' (MN 2 — M i 10)
- *na* not · *eva* just · *davāya* for amusement (m. dat. sg.) · *na* not · *madāya* for intoxication · *na* not · *maṇḍanāya* for beautifying · *na* not · *vibhūsanāya* for adorning
- "'**Not** for amusement, **not** for intoxication, **not** for beautifying, **not** for adorning …'"

The first member takes *n'eva*, as the first member of a positive list takes *c'eva*.

***Mā*** forbids; it does not deny, and it does not take an imperative. **It takes the aorist**:

- '**Mā** evaṁ, āvuso sāti, **avaca**, **mā** bhagavantaṁ **abbhācikkhi**.' (MN 38 — M i 256)
- *mā* do not · *evaṁ* thus · *āvuso* friend (voc.) · *sāti* Sāti (voc.) · *avaca* you said (2 sg. aor.) · *mā* do not · *bhagavantaṁ* the Blessed One (m. acc. sg.) · *abbhācikkhi* you misrepresented (2 sg. aor.)
- "'**Do not** speak so, friend Sāti, **do not** misrepresent the Blessed One.'"

*Avaca* and *abbhācikkhi* are aorists, and the sentence means neither 'you did not say' nor 'you were not saying'. It means 'do not say'. The aorist here has lost its past reference entirely, and what it has gained it has gained from the particle. Chapter 8 reaches the same rule from the tense's side, where it stands as the one exception to the aorist being a past tense at all. If you have learnt the aorist as the past tense, *mā* is the signal to stop reading it that way.

***Alaṁ*** means 'enough', and by extension 'enough of', a refusal. With a dative it means 'fit for, sufficient for', which is the sense in the Saṁyutta's definition of what makes someone a speaker on the Dhamma:

- 'Dhammakathiko bhikkhū'ti **alaṁvacanāya**. (SN 12.16 — S ii 18)
- *dhammakathiko* speaker on the Dhamma (m. nom. sg.) · *bhikkhu* monk (m. nom. sg.) · *ti* (quotation) · *alaṁ* enough, sufficient · *vacanāya* for saying (n. dat. sg.)
- "**Enough** to call him 'a monk who speaks on the Dhamma'."

Negation is also done without a particle at all, by the prefix *a-* (*an-* before a vowel), which the chapter on word formation treats and which every reader meets first in *anicca*, *anattā*, *asaṅkhata*. A negated participle almost always uses the prefix, not *na*.

### Asking

Pāli marks a question with a particle, not with word order, and the particle carries information English puts into intonation.

***Nu***, usually *nu kho*, marks a plain question and expects nothing in particular. ***Nanu***, which is *na* + *nu*, a negative folded into a question, expects the answer yes, as English 'surely you …?' or 'don't you …?' does:[^ind-nanu]

[^ind-nanu]: Duroiselle p. 242: "*Nanu*, used in asking questions to which an affirmative answer is expected. *Nu*, used in asking simple questions." DPD, s.v. *nanu*: "surely?; but wouldn't?; is it true?; wasn't?", from *na* + *nu*.

- **Nanu** tvaṁ sālavatikaṁ ajjhāvasasī'ti? (DN 12 — D i 227)
- *nanu* surely (interrog.) · *tvaṁ* you (nom. sg.) · *sālavatikaṁ* Sālavatikā (f. acc. sg.) · *ajjhāvasasi* you dwell in · *ti* (quotation)
- "**Surely** you live in Sālavatikā?"

The Buddha is asking Lohicca something he already knows, and Lohicca duly answers *Evaṁ, bho Gotama*, 'Just so, master Gotama'. The particle predicted the answer.

***Kacci*** asks a question with concern in it: a hope that all is well, or a fear that it is not:

- '**Kacci** te, tāta, aggi na nibbuto'ti? (DN 23 — D ii 341)
- *kacci* I hope, is it that (interrog.) · *te* your (gen. sg.) · *tāta* dear one (voc.) · *aggi* fire (m. nom. sg.) · *na* not · *nibbuto* gone out (m. nom. sg. p.p.) · *ti* (quotation)
- "'**I trust**, my dear, that the fire has not gone out?'"

***Su***, and its doubled form ***ssu***, never stand as free words in the canon. The particle survives fused to the word in front, and a reader meets it constantly in *kudāssu nāma*, 'when, oh when', the Bodhisatta's question at the head of dependent origination:

- **Kudāssu** nāma imassa dukkhassa nissaraṇaṁ paññāyissati jarāmaraṇassā'ti? (SN 12.10 — S ii 10)
- *kudā* when · *ssu* (emphatic) · *nāma* indeed · *imassa* of this (n. gen. sg.) · *dukkhassa* of suffering (n. gen. sg.) · *nissaraṇaṁ* escape (n. nom. sg.) · *paññāyissati* will be discerned (3 sg. fut. pass.) · *jarāmaraṇassa* of ageing-and-death (n. gen. sg.) · *ti* (quotation)
- "**When, oh when** will an escape be discerned from this suffering, from ageing and death?"

*Kudā* is not otherwise printed on its own in the root texts. It exists there in this compound and one or two like it, which fairly illustrates what "enclitic" costs anyone who searches for the bare word and concludes it does not exist.

### The fillers

The Saddanīti's fourth class is ***pūraṇattha***, 'for the purpose of filling', and it divides in two. ***Padapūraṇa*** particles fill out the **line**; ***atthapūraṇa*** particles fill out the **meaning**.[^ind-puranattha] It is the first kind that a reader needs to be told about, because they include several of the commonest words in the canon and because the natural instinct on meeting them is the wrong one.

[^ind-puranattha]: Collins §4.1(iv), translating Saddanīti: "*pūraṇatthaṁ duvidhaṁ: padapūraṇañca atthapūraṇañca*, (a *nipāta* used) as a filler can be of two kinds: filling up a verse or sentence or filling out the meaning." His list of *padapūraṇa* particles runs *atha*, *atho*, *assu*, *ā*, *enaṁ*, *kahaṁ*, *kīva*, *khalu*, *kho*, *carahi*, *tato*, *naṁ*, *pana*, *yagghe*, *yathā*, *vata*, *ve*, *sudaṁ*, *seyyathidaṁ*, *have*, *hi*.

***Kho*** is the clearest case: thirty-one thousand occurrences in the root texts, and no translation. It is faintly emphatic, it is enclitic, and in the overwhelming majority of its appearances (*atha kho*, *evaṁ kho*, *yo kho*, *tena kho pana samayena*) the right rendering is nothing at all. The same holds, in varying degrees, for ***hi***, ***pana***, ***vata***, ***ve*** and ***have***, ***khalu***, ***assu*** and ***sudaṁ***.

The tradition's account of why they exist is not the account a reader would guess: they are there for the sound. Collins ends the section by saying so: the particles join words together "without expressing a specific meaning", and although their semantic value may seem small, "they contribute to the sound and rhythm of the sentence, especially in verse."[^ind-rhythm] In a literature composed to be recited and memorised, a syllable that fills out a line has a use. Its use is not to mean something.

[^ind-rhythm]: Collins pp. 121, 123. The Pāli phrase he is translating at (iv.a) is *asati atthavisesābhidhāne vacasiliṭṭhatāya*, roughly 'for smoothness of speech, there being no special sense expressed'.

- **Tena sudaṁ**, bhante, devā tāvatiṁsā attamanā honti pamuditā pītisomanassajātā. (DN 19 — D ii 221)
- *tena* by that (n. instr. sg.) · *sudaṁ* (emphatic) · *bhante* sir (voc.) · *devā* gods (m. nom. pl.) · *tāvatiṁsā* of the Thirty-Three · *attamanā* pleased (m. nom. pl.) · *honti* become · *pamuditā* delighted · *pītisomanassajātā* filled with rapture and joy
- "**At that**, sir, the gods of the Thirty-Three became pleased, delighted, filled with rapture and joy."

*Sudaṁ* contributes emphasis and two syllables, but nothing that can be put into English.[^ind-sudam] The instruction that follows from all this is a negative one, and it saves a great deal of wasted effort: when a short word will not yield a meaning, consider that it may not have one.
