Two conditionals, two optatives and two indicatives, and the doctrine rests on the difference between them. The grammar is central to that passage: its whole force is that the first sentence is contrary to fact and the second is not, and only the mood of the verbs says so.

A relative clause carries a condition too, and most of the Pātimokkha is built that way. A rule states its case as a generic relative with an optative, and its consequence as briefly as the class of offence allows, often in a clause with no verb in it at all. The nuns' rule about garlic runs to six words:

- **Yā pana bhikkhunī** lasuṇaṁ **khādeyya**, **pācittiyaṁ**. (Bi Pc 1 — Vin iv 259)
- *yā* whatever (f. nom. sg.) · *pana* (filler) · *bhikkhunī* nun (f. nom. sg.) · *lasuṇaṁ* garlic (nt. acc. sg.) · *khādeyya* should eat (3 sg. opt.) · *pācittiyaṁ* an offence requiring confession (nt. nom. sg.)
- "**Whatever nun should eat** garlic: **an offence requiring confession**."

There is no *sace*, no *ce* and no *yadi*. The optative carries the condition by itself, and the relative carries the generality: *yā pana bhikkhunī* is any nun there may be, not some particular nun. The consequence then arrives as a single noun, with *hoti* understood as it was in *appamādo amatapadaṁ* at the head of this chapter.[^cls-pacittiya] A rule is a conditional with the 'if' left to a mood and the 'then' left to a case ending.

[^cls-pacittiya]: DPD, s.v. *pācittiya*, neuter, '(vinaya) offence requiring confession'. The junction this rule depends on (an optative in *-eyya* followed directly by *pācittiya-*) stands 115 times in the Vinaya's root texts, counted as a phrase.

A locative absolute can also carry a condition without any particle at all, and Bodhi notes a case where the situation is so unlikely that 'if' translates better than 'when':

- **Āditte**, bhikkhave, **cele vā sīse vā** kimassa karaṇīyan ti? (SN 56.34 — S v 440)
- *āditte*<sub>a</sub> ablaze (nt. loc. sg., past part.) · *cele*<sub>a</sub> cloth, garment (nt. loc. sg.) · *vā … vā* either … or · *sīse*<sub>a</sub> head (nt. loc. sg.) · *kiṁ* what · *assa* would be (3 sg. opt. of *atthi*) · *karaṇīyaṁ* to be done (nt. nom. sg., fpp) · *ti* end of question
- '**If one's turban or one's head were ablaze**, monks, what should be done?'

### The simile, and the sentence that answers it

The discourses make constant use of similes, and the simile has a fixed shape: ***seyyathāpi***, 'just as', opens the first term, and ***evam eva*** or ***evam evaṁ***, 'just so', opens the second. The verbs in the first term are usually optative, because the case is imagined, not reported.[^cls-simile]

[^cls-simile]: Perniola §306.a, p. 392; Bodhi, "Similes", p. 52, who notes the optative convention. Perniola adds three further ways of building the second term: with the correlative adverbs *yathā … tathā*, with a *bahubbīhi* in *-upama*, and with *seyyo* … *yañ ce* for a comparison of superiority.

- **Seyyathāpi**, bhante, purisassa agāraṁ ekādasadvāraṁ, so tasmiṁ **agāre āditte** ekamekenapi dvārena **sakkuṇeyya** attānaṁ sotthiṁ kātuṁ. (MN 52 — M i 353)
- *seyyathāpi* just as · *purisassa* of a man (m. gen. sg.) · *agāraṁ* a house (nt. nom. sg.) · *ekādasadvāraṁ* with eleven doors (nt. nom. sg., *bahubbīhi*) · *so* he (m. nom. sg.) · *tasmiṁ agāre* that house (nt. loc. sg.) · *āditte* ablaze (nt. loc. sg., past part.) · *ekamekena dvārena* by any one door (nt. ins. sg.) · *sakkuṇeyya* would be able (3 sg. opt.) · *attānaṁ* himself (m. acc. sg.) · *sotthiṁ kātuṁ* to make safe
- **Just as**, sir, if a man had a house with eleven doors, and **that house were ablaze**, he **would be able** to get himself to safety by any one of the doors.

Three of this chapter's constructions are in that one sentence: the simile frame, a locative absolute carrying a condition, and an infinitive of purpose. It is also a good measure of how much of the grammar a reader has to have available to follow one ordinary sentence of the canon at reading speed.

### Quotation, and the *ti* that is not one

Where English says 'he knew that the cat was in the house', Pāli almost always quotes: the thought is given as direct speech and closed with ***ti***.[^cls-ti] The construction belongs with the indeclinables and is treated there; what belongs here are the two things it does at the level of the clause.

[^cls-ti]: Perniola §307, p. 395, who notes that it is nearly always *ti*, not *iti*; Bodhi, "Embedded direct speech", pp. 51–52. The sandhi that *ti* produces on the preceding word (the lengthened vowel, the assimilated final niggahīta) is in the chapter on sandhi, and the particle itself in the chapter on indeclinables.

The first is that Pāli does have a real indirect statement, and it is easy to miss because it looks like an ordinary object: a noun or pronoun in the accusative with a participle agreeing with it, the two together forming the object of the main verb. *Addasā kho nigrodho paribbājako sandhānaṁ gahapatiṁ dūratova āgacchantaṁ* (DN 25 — D iii 37) is not 'the wanderer Nigrodha saw the householder Sandhāna from afar, coming', but 'the wanderer Nigrodha saw **that** the householder Sandhāna **was coming** from afar'.[^cls-indirect] The participle carries what English would put in a subordinate clause.

[^cls-indirect]: Perniola's own example is *addasā bhagavantaṁ dūrato va āgacchantaṁ*, which he prints twice: at §307, p. 395, uncited and without the *va*, and again at §309.a, p. 399, with it and cited to MN II 2:2. The wording quoted here is the second. The construction is why a reader should be slow to detach an accusative participle from its accusative noun, and the same ambiguity makes the accusative absolute hard to identify.

The second is that a *ti* clause need not be a quotation at all. It can carry **purpose**: an imperative or optative closed with *ti* states the aim for the sake of which the main action is done.

- '**Pasaṁsā me āgacchatū**' ti sīlaṁ rakkheyya paṇḍito. (Iti 76 — It p. 67)
- *pasaṁsā* praise (f. nom. sg.) · *me* to me (dat./gen. sg.) · *āgacchatu* let it come (3 sg. imp.) · *ti* end of quotation · *sīlaṁ* virtue (nt. acc. sg.) · *rakkheyya* would guard (3 sg. opt.) · *paṇḍito* the wise one (m. nom. sg.)
- The wise one would guard his virtue **so that praise might come to him**.

Read as a quotation that sentence is nonsense, since nobody is speaking. Read as a purpose clause it is exact, and it is the ordinary Pāli way of saying 'in order that'.[^cls-ti-purpose]

[^cls-ti-purpose]: Perniola §311.a, p. 404, who cites it as Itv 67:6; the sutta is Itivuttaka 76. His §311.b gives two more (the infinitive and the dative of an action noun in *-na*, *nadiṁ gamissāma sināyituṁ* and *bhagavantaṁ dassanāya upasaṅkami*), which are treated in the chapters on infinitives and on case syntax respectively; and his §311.c a fourth, a *tappurisa* built on an action noun with *atthaṁ* in the accusative, *nayidaṁ brahmacariyaṁ vussati janakuhanatthaṁ*, which belongs with the compounds.

### What is left, and where it is

Four kinds of subordinate clause remain, and all four are built the same way: a relative word in the subordinate clause answered by a demonstrative in the main one. That correlative construction is the next chapter's subject, and this section is a map, not a treatment.[^cls-correlatives]

[^cls-correlatives]: Perniola §§310–314, pp. 401–407, treats all four; the *ya-*/*ta-* forms themselves, with their paradigms, are in the chapters on pronouns and on relative clauses.

| Kind | Introduced by | Answered by |
|---|---|---|
| Time | *yadā*, *yāva*, *yasmiṁ samaye* | *tadā*, *tāva*, *atha*, *tasmiṁ samaye* |
| Cause | *yasmā*, *yato*, *yatvādhikaraṇaṁ* | *tasmā*, *tato* |
| Result | *yathā*, *yena*, *yatra hi nāma* | *tathā*, *yāva*, *sādhu* |
| Concession | *api* or *pi*, alone or with *ce* | — |

Three notes on that table, because each hides something a reader will meet.

*Yatvādhikaraṇaṁ* looks like a rarity and is not. It occurs eighty-five times in the root texts, and never as a separate word: the canon has ***yatvādhikaraṇamenaṁ***, joined to the pronoun that follows, in the formula of sense restraint. A search for the bare form will suggest it belongs to the grammarians.

*Yatra hi nāma* takes a future and carries astonishment, not result. The monks at DN 14, in the conversation this chapter quoted for the genitive absolute, exclaim at the Buddha's power of recollection: *yatra hi nāma tathāgato atīte buddhe parinibbute … jātitopi anussarissati* (DN 14 — D ii 8), '**that** the Realised One should recollect, of past Buddhas who have attained final Nibbāna, even their birth'. The future there is not future time, and there is a locative absolute inside it.[^cls-yatra]

And the concessive clause has no conjunction at all. It is marked by *pi* or *api* attached to a word in a clause whose verb is usually a participle: *Chinnopi rukkho punareva rūhati* (Dhp 338 — Dhp p. 49), 'though the tree is cut, it grows again'. The relation is left for the reader to supply, which is the habit this whole chapter has been describing.

[^cls-yatra]: Perniola §312, pp. 404–405, for the construction. His own illustration of it, *yāva pāpo ayaṁ devadatto alakkhiko, yatra hi nāma samaṇassa gotamassa vadhāya parakkamissati*, does not occur in the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana in that wording; the DN 14 sentence is given here in its place.

### Where clause syntax is treated elsewhere

**The particles** (*na* and *mā* and the moods they take, *ca* and *vā* and *pi*, the interrogatives *nu kho*, *kacci*, *nanu* and *api nu*, and *iti* with its sandhi) are each treated in the chapter on indeclinables, where their forms and their enclitic behaviour belong. This chapter has added only their effect on a whole clause.

**The relative correlatives** (the whole *ya-*/*ta-* apparatus, and the adjectival clause built with *yo … so*) are the next chapter's, and it treats them with paradigms.

The participles and the absolutive, whose forms every construction here depends on, are in the chapters on participles and on absolutives and infinitives. This chapter uses them and derives none of them; where it names a form as a present participle or a past passive one, that is the claim those chapters establish.

**The cases themselves** are in the chapter on case syntax, which left the absolutes here on purpose, on the ground that an absolute construction is a dependent clause and a reader meets it as one. It kept the observation this chapter has spent its middle third unfolding: these constructions are named after their case because only the case marks them off, and when the case will not decide, no other word in the sentence will.

### Where to start in a long sentence

Begin with the finite verbs. They are few, and each is a place the sentence could stop. Then take every absolutive as an action finished before the next, by the same subject, and every participle standing in an oblique case with its own noun as a clause of its own, joined to the rest by nothing but that case. Where no finite verb is printed, supply *hoti* and see whether the sentence closes. Every remaining word is read against one of these. The next chapter ends by doing that to a single sentence, word by word.

**Exercises for this chapter are in the workbook**, under "Exercises: Clause and Sentence Syntax", with worked answers at the back of that volume.

**Principal sources.** Wijesekera, *Syntax of the Cases in the Pāli Nikāyas*, §§26, 56, 158–159 and 182–186, for the absolute constructions, which are half this chapter and on which he is the fullest authority in any language; Perniola chapter XXI, "Syntax of the sentence", for the range of sentence types and for the conditionals; Collins pp. 37–41 for the only treatment that gathers all five absolutes into a single place; Bodhi's syntax overview in *Reading the Buddha's Discourses in Pāli* for the rules a reader needs at the moment of reading; Duroiselle §§588–592 for word order and concord, which no other source in the collection treats. The tradition's rules (Kaccāyana 305 and 313, *Saddanīti* 325–327, Moggallāna 34–35) were read in the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana, not through the grammars that quote them.

## Relative Clauses

### Two clauses, and one term between them

A **relative clause** is a subordinate clause that can only be read against the main clause, because a word in it points there. In Pāli that word almost always begins with *y-*, and the word it points to almost always begins with *t-*. Warder calls the resulting articulation "an outstanding characteristic of Pāli"; he is being restrained. In Lesson 27 he calls the relative clause "the most important and most frequent of all the elements in Pāli period building", and the reason is arithmetical as much as stylistic: Pāli has few conjunctions and no relative pronoun that can be dropped, so a long sentence has to be built out of pairs.

The tradition's names for the two halves describe the construction better than the English ones do. A relative pronoun is *aniyamattha*, "of undetermined meaning": it posits a term without saying which one.[^rel-aniyama] The answering word in the main clause determines it. So the sentence advances by holding something open and then closing it, and only at the closing can it be understood at all.

[^rel-aniyama]: Collins, *A Pali Grammar for Students*, p. 68, who glosses it "undetermined, that is, until they are determined by their correlative". The term is the tradition's: the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana uses *aniyamattha* of a word left open: *Vā-saddo cettha aniyamattho*, "the word *vā* here is of undetermined meaning", in the Pañcapakaraṇa-anuṭīkā on the Paṭṭhāna, *Anantarapaccayaniddesavaṇṇanā* (¶4). Warder p. 71 gives *niyamato paṭiniddesa* for the correlative demonstrative; that phrase does not occur anywhere in the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana, and although both its halves are current words there, *paṭiniddesa* is used in the Netti tradition for a detailed re-exposition and never as a name for a correlative. Take it as his own coinage, not a citation.

That is a different habit from English, and the difference should be stated flatly at the outset, because it governs how a Pāli sentence is read. English subordinates by embedding; Pāli subordinates by pairing. English builds *the mendicant who saw this went away* by dropping a clause inside the main one and leaving the noun where it stood. Pāli says, in effect, *which mendicant saw this, he went away*: two clauses side by side, the noun standing in the first, and a pronoun holding its place in the second. The relative clause regularly comes first. So a reader meets the subordinate clause before there is anything to subordinate it to, and cannot begin to construe until the answering word arrives.

That is the practical difficulty of the whole chapter, and it does not go away with familiarity. It is only managed, by learning what to expect.

### The rule that decides every relative pronoun

Three sources state it in nearly the same words, and Collins calls it "the vital principle to remember":[^rel-principle]

> A relative pronoun takes its number and gender from the antecedent, and its case from its own clause.

[^rel-principle]: Collins p. 68; Warder p. 71, "must be in the same number and gender as the noun or demonstrative pronoun it refers to, but it may be in any case — connecting it with the words in its own relative clause or sentence"; Thānuttamo p. 57, who cites Collins for it.

The rule has two parts, and they should be separated. Gender and number are **agreement**: they identify what the relative is pointing at, and they are the reader's only means of doing so when the antecedent is several words away. Case is **function**: it says what the relative does inside the clause it opens. The two halves answer different questions, and reading a relative pronoun means asking both.

Here is the rule with nothing complicating it. The Buddha has just left Pāṭaligāma by a particular gate:

- Atha kho bhagavā ***yena*** **dvārena nikkhami**, *taṁ* gotamadvāraṁ nāma ahosi. (DN 16 — D ii 89)
- *atha kho* then · *bhagavā* the Blessed One · *yena dvārena*<sub>a</sub> by which gate (n. instr. sg.) · *nikkhami* went out (aor.) · *taṁ*<sub>a</sub> that (n. nom. sg.) · *gotamadvāraṁ*<sub>a</sub> the Gotama Gate (n. nom. sg.) · *nāma* by name · *ahosi* became (aor.)
- The gate by which the Blessed One went out came to be called the Gotama Gate.

*Yena* and *taṁ* are the same word twice, neuter and singular both times because *dvāra* is neuter and singular. Their cases differ because their functions differ: *yena* is instrumental because you leave *by* a gate, *taṁ* is nominative because it is the subject of *ahosi*. Neither case shows anything about the other. Expect the pair to match in case and you will look for a second neuter instrumental and not find one.

Warder gives this sentence as his sole illustration of the rule, and it is well chosen: it is the smallest possible sentence in which the two halves of the rule visibly pull in different directions.

#### Four places the rule holds and still surprises

Of the sources in the collection, only Bodhi treats agreement as a difficulty and not merely a fact, and in all four cases he assembles the rule is obeyed exactly and the result is not what a reader expects.[^rel-bodhi] They belong together.

[^rel-bodhi]: Bodhi, *Reading the Buddha's Discourses in Pāli*, "Relative clause and main clause correlation", pp. 46–49. Two of the four sentences below are his; the others are given here in the form the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana prints them.

The case changes across the pair. This is the ordinary situation and it has just been shown; it is listed first because an unwarned reader takes a case mismatch for a mistake.

The gender changes across the pair, because each word agrees with its own noun. The relative takes the gender of the noun in the relative clause; the correlative takes the gender of the noun in the main clause; if those two nouns differ in gender, so do the pronouns.

- ***Yo***, **bhikkhave, rāgakkhayo dosakkhayo mohakkhayo** — *idaṁ* vuccati, bhikkhave, asaṅkhataṁ. (SN 43.12 — S iv 362)
- *yo*<sub>a</sub> which (m. nom. sg.) · *bhikkhave* monks · *rāgakkhayo*<sub>a</sub> the ending of greed (m. nom. sg.) · *dosakkhayo* the ending of hate · *mohakkhayo* the ending of delusion · *idaṁ*<sub>b</sub> this (n. nom. sg.) · *vuccati* is called · *asaṅkhataṁ*<sub>b</sub> the unconditioned (n. nom. sg.)
- The ending of greed, the ending of hate, the ending of delusion: this, monks, is called the unconditioned.

*Yo* is masculine because *khaya* is masculine. *Idaṁ* is neuter because *asaṅkhata* is neuter. They refer to the same thing and agree with different words, which is the rule working correctly and looking as though it has failed. Notice also that the correlative here is not from the *ta-* stem at all.

A relative standing before a run of nouns of different genders agrees with the first of them.

- ***Yā*** **kho, bhikkhave, imesaṁ tiṇṇaṁ dhammānaṁ saṅgati sannipāto samavāyo**, *ayaṁ* vuccati cakkhusamphasso. (SN 35.93 — S iv 68, and again at each of the other sense bases)
- *yā*<sub>a</sub> which (f. nom. sg.) · *imesaṁ tiṇṇaṁ dhammānaṁ* of these three things (m. gen. pl.) · *saṅgati*<sub>a</sub> the meeting (f. nom. sg.) · *sannipāto* the coming together (m. nom. sg.) · *samavāyo* the concurrence (m. nom. sg.) · *ayaṁ*<sub>b</sub> this (m. nom. sg.) · *cakkhusamphasso*<sub>b</sub> eye-contact (m. nom. sg.)
- The meeting, the coming together, the concurrence of these three things is called eye-contact.

*Saṅgati* is feminine and *sannipāta* and *samavāya* are masculine; *yā* is feminine because the first of the three is. The main clause's *ayaṁ* is masculine because it agrees with *cakkhusamphasso*. One noun phrase, two genders, and each pronoun taking the gender of the word standing next to it.

The relative clause is completed by *sabba*, not by a demonstrative. When a relative clause is generalised, the main clause is very often closed by *sabba* "all", not by a bare *ta-* form, with the demonstrative attached to it, or by *sabba* alone.

- ***Yāni*** **kānici, bhikkhave, bhayāni uppajjanti** *sabbāni tāni* bālato uppajjanti, no paṇḍitato. (AN 3.1 — A i 101)
- *yāni kānici*<sub>a</sub> whatever (n. nom. pl.) · *bhayāni*<sub>a</sub> dangers (n. nom. pl.) · *uppajjanti* arise · *sabbāni tāni*<sub>a</sub> all those (n. nom. pl.) · *bālato* from the fool (m. abl. sg.) · *no paṇḍitato* not from the astute one
- Whatever dangers arise, monks, all of them arise from the fool, not from the astute one.

#### The one place the rule is broken

Oberlies gives a sentence in which the relative does not agree with its noun at all, and, being Oberlies, he gives the reason as well.[^rel-oberlies-gender] Māgaṇḍiya is speaking:

[^rel-oberlies-gender]: Oberlies §42(3) *rem.* (a), pp. 186–187, citing Sn 838 and a second case at Ja VI 274. His explanation is at the head of the same remark: "Owing to dialect mixture the endings of the nom./acc. pl. were identical for masc. and neuter (*-ā*, *-āni*). This contributed to the confusion of the masc. and neuter plural forms, frequently encountered when a relative pronoun refers to a noun."

- **Vinicchayā** ***yāni*** **pakappitāni**, (iti māgaṇḍiyo) *Te* ve munī brūsi anuggahāya. (Snp 4.9 — Sn p. 164)
- *vinicchayā* judgements (m. nom. pl.) · *yāni* which (n. nom. pl.) · *pakappitāni* have been formed (n. nom. pl., pp. of *pakappeti*) · *iti māgaṇḍiyo* said Māgaṇḍiya · *te* those (m. acc. pl.) · *ve* indeed · *munī* sage (m. voc. sg.) · *brūsi* you speak of · *anuggahāya* without adopting (them)
- "The judgements that have been formed — those, sage, you speak of without adopting any of them," said Māgaṇḍiya.

One noun, and two pronouns disagreeing about its gender in consecutive lines: *yāni* neuter, *te* masculine. Oberlies's account is that in the plural the masculine and neuter nominative and accusative endings had already fallen together under dialect mixture, so the distinction the rule depends on was not being maintained. The lexicon shows the same thing from the other side: DPD carries *vinicchaya* as masculine at this verse and as neuter three verses later in the same *vagga*, printing the neuter plural *vinicchayāni*.[^rel-vinicchaya] The word is of both genders, and its relative pronoun is free to choose.

[^rel-vinicchaya]: DPD, s.v. *vinicchaya* 1 (masc., citing this verse) and *vinicchaya* 3 (nt., citing *hitvāna sabbāni vinicchayāni* at the Cūḷabyūha, Snp 4.12). Construction *vi* + *nī* + √*ci* > *chay* + *a*.

Two conclusions follow for a reader, and only the second is comfortable. A relative that does not agree is not necessarily a corruption. And the rule remains the right one to reason with, because the exception is confined to a place where the language itself had stopped distinguishing.

### The forms

The relative pronoun is the stem *ya(d)-*, and it inflects as the demonstrative *eta(d)-* does; Oberlies establishes the identity, and that allows a table to be given at all.[^rel-paradigm-authority]

[^rel-paradigm-authority]: Oberlies §42(3), p. 186: "The stems *eta(ṁ)-* and *ya(ṁ)-* … inflect in the same way", with the stem-forms attested at Vin I 57,35 and Ja III 131,12\*. Appendix A carries no *ya-* paradigm, because Ñāṇatusita's pronoun sheets do not print one; so unlike every other paradigm in this book, this table is not checked against the appendix. Every cell in it has instead been run through the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana, and the two cells that did not survive are marked below.

|            | sg. m.                    | sg. n.                    | sg. f.               | pl. m.  | pl. n.  | pl. f.  |
|------------|---------------------------|---------------------------|----------------------|---------|---------|---------|
| nom.       | *yo*                      | *yaṁ* (*yad-*)            | *yā*                 | *ye*    | *yāni*  | *yā*    |
| acc.       | *yaṁ* (*yad-*)            | *yaṁ* (*yad-*)            | *yaṁ*                | *ye*    | *yāni*  | *yā*    |
| instr.     | *yena*                    | *yena*                    | *yāya*               | *yehi*  | *yehi*  | *yāhi*  |
| abl.       | *yasmā*, *yamhā*, *yato*  | *yasmā*, *yamhā*, *yato*  | *yāya*               | *yehi*  | *yehi*  | *yāhi*  |
| gen./dat.  | *yassa*                   | *yassa*                   | *yassā*              | *yesaṁ* | *yesaṁ* | *yāsaṁ* |
| loc.       | *yasmiṁ*, *yamhi*         | *yasmiṁ*, *yamhi*         | *yāyaṁ*, *yassaṁ*    | *yesu*  | *yesu*  | *yāsu*  |

Three notes, each of which a reader will need before they need the rest of the table.

*Yad-* is not a word. It is what *yaṁ* becomes before a vowel, and the texts never print it standing alone, not once, in the root texts, the commentaries, the sub-commentaries or the later works. They print the result: *yadidaṁ* 953 times in the root texts, *yadā* 603, *yadeva* 120, *yadaniccaṁ* 66. The junction consonant is the old *d* of the stem, surfacing when a vowel gives it somewhere to go.[^rel-yad]

[^rel-yad]: The same *d* is visible in *ta(d)-*: *tadidaṁ*, *tadiṅgha*, *tadubhayaṁ*. See the chapter on sandhi for the general rule and the chapter on pronouns for the parallel with *eta(d)-*.

The genitive plural has a longer form that this book does not teach. Grammars print *yesānaṁ* beside *yesaṁ*, and the ending itself is real: the root texts carry *katamesānaṁ* twelve times, and *sabbesānaṁ* and *etesānaṁ* once each. But *yesānaṁ* occurs nowhere in the root texts, the commentaries or the sub-commentaries. Its seven occurrences in the whole Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana are six declension runs in Kaccāyana, Padarūpasiddhi and the *Saddanīti*, and one line of a mediaeval chronicle. It is a cell the grammarians completed by rule and nobody used.[^rel-yesanam]

[^rel-yesanam]: Oberlies §42(2), p. 186, calls the ending a blend and cites *esānaṁ* at M II 154,2, *katamesānaṁ* at D I 206,5 and *sabbesānaṁ* at M III 60,24. The Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana confirms the second and third and does not print the first as a separate word at all; what it has there is *etesānaṁ*, once. The seven occurrences of *yesānaṁ* are in the grammatical literature and in the Thūpavaṁsa. This is the same profile as *amuyo* and *amūsānaṁ* in the chapter on pronouns, and it is flagged for the same reason.

The feminine plural forms are thin, and that is a fact about sentences, not forms. *Yāhi* stands seven times in the root texts and *yāsaṁ* five. A Pāli sentence rarely needs a feminine instrumental plural relative pronoun; when it needs one, this is the word.

### The word that answers

The default answering word is the corresponding form of *ta(d)-*, and the pairs are so regular that a reader learns to hear the second as soon as the first arrives. But four things happen often enough that expecting only *ta-* will mislead.

The answer may be a different pronoun. *Idaṁ*, *ayaṁ* and *eta-* all serve, as in the two sentences above; so do the personal pronouns, and so does *sabba*.

The answer may be a noun. Where the main clause names the thing, it does not also need a pronoun for it. The relative clause then reads as an ordinary adjectival modifier.

The answer may be missing altogether. Warder notes that this happens especially where the main clause carries a proper name with a title, and that it is otherwise the exception, not the rule.[^rel-no-correlative] The sharpest case is one Wijesekera isolates, where the missing word is the relative, not the correlative: the same nominative serves as subject of the main sentence and of a subordinate clause with nothing to introduce it:

[^rel-no-correlative]: Warder pp. 71–72: "Sometimes the main clause has no correlative word (this may of course depend on its having a special form, such as direct speech), but as a rule the correlative demonstrative is used. A proper name (with title) in the main clause is quite often used without a demonstrative." And at Lesson 27, p. 291, on the subordinating indeclinables: "the use of correlative demonstratives is fairly free, and quite frequently they are omitted altogether."

- Ayaṁ vuccati, bhikkhave, **bhikkhu phegguṁ aggahesi brahmacariyassa**. (MN 29 — M i 196)
- *ayaṁ* this one (m. nom. sg.) · *vuccati* is called · *bhikkhu* a monk (m. nom. sg.) · *phegguṁ* the softwood (m. acc. sg.) · *aggahesi* took (aor. of *gaṇhati*, *a* + *gahe* + *si*, √*gah*) · *brahmacariyassa* of the spiritual life (n. gen. sg.)
- This one, monks, is called a monk who has taken the softwood of the spiritual life.

There is no *yo* in the sentence, and English cannot manage without one. *Bhikkhu* is the complement of *vuccati* and at the same time the subject of *aggahesi*, and the two clauses are simply set down side by side.[^rel-wijesekera-nom]

[^rel-wijesekera-nom]: Wijesekera §18.d, citing M I.192: "It is also not unusual in Pāli to find the same nom. appearing as subject of the principal sentence and of a relative clause at the same time, the connecting relative pronoun being absent." He supplies the missing *yo* in brackets in his own translation, the right way to render it.

The Vinaya sharpens Warder's "exception rather than the rule", because the Pātimokkha is built on the omission. Most of its rules open with a relative, and the shorter classes close without answering it:

- **Yā pana bhikkhunī** lasuṇaṁ khādeyya, **pācittiyaṁ**. (Bi Pc 1 — Vin iv 259)

Nothing answers *yā*. The main clause is the single word *pācittiyaṁ*, and it names an offence, not a nun, so there is nowhere for a correlative to stand even if one were wanted. Chapter 16 takes the same sentence for what it does to the conditional.

Where a correlative *does* turn up in a rule, it adds something besides closing the clause. The second *pārājika* ends *ayampi pārājiko hoti asaṁvāso* (Bu Pj 2 — Vin iii 46), "**this one too** is expelled and not in communion", and the *pi* joined to the demonstrative is the point of it. The first *pārājika* has no correlative at all; the second, third and fourth all carry *ayampi*, six times between them in the root texts, once in each rule's original formulation and once in its amendment.[^rel-ayampi] Each is being added to a list already running. The demonstrative appears where 'also' is wanted, and it closes the relative clause as well.

[^rel-ayampi]: The six occurrences of *ayampi pārājiko* in the Vinaya root texts are all in the Pārājika section, two apiece under the second, third and fourth *pārājika*, and none under the first. The first rule's final form ends *pārājiko hoti asaṁvāso* with no demonstrative (Bu Pj 1 — Vin iii 23), which Chapter 11 cites for a different reason.

The relative clause may follow the main clause. The order is reversible, and the reversal is not free: it emphasises the main clause, and Warder describes the effect as elevated speech.[^rel-inverted] Thānuttamo gives a clean instance where the demonstrative and its clause come first and the relative answers:[^rel-than-inverted] *amataṁ tesaṁ, bhikkhave, sacchikataṁ yesaṁ kāyagatāsati sacchikatā* — "the deathless has been realised by those, monks, by whom mindfulness of the body has been realised."

[^rel-inverted]: Warder p. 72, on *hoti kho so samayo yaṁ … ayaṁ loko vivaṭṭati*: "Here the relative clause follows the main clause = elevated speech or emphasis of main clause." Bodhi says the same more plainly, p. 47: "Although the relative clause usually precedes the main clause, occasionally, for emphasis, the sequence may be inverted."

[^rel-than-inverted]: Thānuttamo p. 57, citing AN I 29 (AN 1.627 in this book's numbering) after Dhammajoti. The point is his; the sentence is the canon's.

So expect a closure of some kind, not necessarily a *ta-* form: a demonstrative, a noun, a *sabba*, or nothing at all. The reader's business is to keep the relative clause open until one of them arrives.

### The relatives, from emptiest to fullest

Warder's arrangement is the useful one, and no other source has anything like it. He ranks the relative words by how much each of them means: *yaṁ* is "the most general or 'empty' relative"; *yathā* is "the next most general or empty relative after *yaṁ*"; and then "the remaining relatives are more specialized in meaning."

The ranking is a difficulty ranking as much as a semantic one. A relative that means "where" shows what kind of clause is coming. A relative that means nothing shows only that a clause is coming, and leaves the relation to be worked out from the sentence. So the emptiest word is the hardest, and it is much the commonest.

#### *Yaṁ*, and what the tradition does with it

At its emptiest, *yaṁ* is a marker: it says that what follows is subordinate, much as *ti* says that what precedes is quoted, and it carries no case relation a reader can use directly. Warder lists the senses it covers (indirect speech, a supposition, a concession, a cause, or "merely a qualification") and adds that the optative appears in it as usual for hypothetical cases.

It is sometimes said that it is not always clear whether such relatives are pronouns or adverbs, and that for translation this does not usually pose a problem. The tradition disagrees with both halves of that, and the disagreement is the most valuable thing in this chapter.

Commenting on the opening of the Mahāpadāna Sutta, Buddhaghosa meets a *yaṁ* with no visible work to do:

- Ito so, bhikkhave, ekanavutikappe ***yaṁ*** **vipassī bhagavā arahaṁ sammāsambuddho loke udapādi**. (DN 14 — D ii 2)
- *ito* from now · *so* that (m. nom. sg.) · *ekanavutikappe* ninety-one eons (m. loc. sg.) · *yaṁ* which (n. acc. sg.) · *vipassī bhagavā* the Blessed One Vipassī · *arahaṁ* perfected · *sammāsambuddho* fully awakened · *loke* in the world (m. loc. sg.) · *udapādi* arose (aor., *ud* + *a* + √*pad* + *i*)
- Ninety-one eons ago, monks, the Blessed One Vipassī, perfected and fully awakened, arose in the world.

His first move is to resolve the word instead of leaving it: *Tattha yaṁ vipassīti yasmiṁ kappe vipassī*, "*yaṁ vipassī* means *in which eon* Vipassī".[^rel-buddhaghosa] The empty relative has a case after all, and the case is locative; the sentence means "ninety-one eons ago is the eon in which Vipassī arose", not "ninety-one eons ago and, separately, Vipassī arose".

His second move is to generalise, and it is a four-way classification of the word, each use named by its case in the tradition's vocabulary and each supported by a citation:

| His term | The case | His illustration |
|---|---|---|
| *paccattavacane* | nominative | *yaṁ me, bhante, devānaṁ tāvatiṁsānaṁ sammukhā sutaṁ … ārocemi taṁ, bhagavato* |
| *upayogavacane* | accusative | *Yaṁ taṁ apucchimha akittayī no, aññaṁ taṁ pucchāma tadiṅgha brūhi* |
| *karaṇavacane* | instrumental | *Aṭṭhānametaṁ, bhikkhave, anavakāso, yaṁ ekissā lokadhātuyā* |
| *bhummatthe* | locative | the sentence above |

[^rel-buddhaghosa]: Sumaṅgalavilāsinī, Mahāvaggaṭṭhakathā, Mahāpadānasuttavaṇṇanā, Pubbenivāsapaṭisaṁyuttakathā (Sv II 410), read directly in the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana. His citations are DN 2.203, Sn 881 and AN 1.277 respectively. Wijesekera §50.d reports the passage, alone among the sources in this collection; his transcription of the second illustration is loose (he prints *appucchimha akkittayī no aññaṁ, yaṁ pucchāma tad iṅgha brūhi*, with doubled consonants the commentary does not have and a different word order), but his reading of it is exact.

So the tradition's position is that the empty relative is never really empty: it always has a case, the case is recoverable from the sentence, and recovering it is what reading the sentence consists of. That is a considerably more demanding claim than "this does not pose a problem", and it is also a better description of what a translator actually does.

One of the four is disputed, and both sides have something to be said for them.

> **Grammars differ on the instrumental *yaṁ*.** Buddhaghosa reads *Aṭṭhānametaṁ … yaṁ ekissā lokadhātuyā dve arahanto … uppajjeyyuṁ* as a nominative used in the sense of an instrumental (*kāraṇatthe paccattaṁ*, "the nominative in the sense of cause") and glosses it *yena kāraṇena*, "by which reason". Wijesekera holds that the form is rather an adverbial accusative on the Sanskrit model, and supports the reading with sentences in which the *yaṁ* clause is clearly the content of a demonstrative accusative: *Tasmātihānanda, tuyhevetaṁ dukkaṭaṁ … yaṁ tvaṁ … na tathāgataṁ yāci* (DN 16), "therefore, Ānanda, this is your own misdeed … that you did not entreat the Realised One". The two analyses agree on the translation and disagree on the grammar behind it. Wijesekera's has the merit of accounting for all four of Buddhaghosa's uses by one mechanism.[^rel-yam-dispute]

[^rel-yam-dispute]: Wijesekera §50.c, and the same section for the sentences on the other side (D II.115, M I.389, D II.166). His own summary is generous to the commentator: "The commentator is therefore aware of the syntactical distinctions of the uses of *yaṁ*."

And the temporal *yaṁ* answers a question this book has walked past for sixteen chapters. Wijesekera assembles the sentences in which *yaṁ* simply means "when" — *So kho, ānanda, samayo yaṁ mahāvātā vāyanti*, "that is the time when great winds blow" — and observes that they explain the accusative in the phrase every reader of this book has met a thousand times.[^rel-ekam-samayam] ***Ekaṁ samayaṁ*, the accusative that opens nearly every sutta in the canon, is an adverbial accusative of exactly this kind.** It is not "one occasion" as an object of anything. It is "on one occasion", the same construction as *yaṁ* for "when", frozen into a formula.

[^rel-ekam-samayam]: Wijesekera §50.d: "All these examples go to show that the acc. in *ekaṁ samayaṁ* occurring at the beginning of all suttas has its origin in the above-discussed temporal adverbial use." He also notes that the commentary on one of them supplies the resolution explicitly, *yaṁ imesanti yasmiṁ samaye imesaṁ* (Sv III 853, on DN 26), Buddhaghosa's locative *yaṁ* again.

*Yaṁ* also fuses freely with what follows it, and two of the results are common enough to be treated as words. *Yadidaṁ*, "which is this", corresponds to English "that is to say" or "namely", and is often best left almost untranslated. *Yadagge* or *yadaggena*, "from which point", means "since" of a stretch of time reckoned from a starting point.[^rel-yadidam]

[^rel-yadidam]: Both are Collins p. 69 and Warder Lesson 27 p. 292. *Yadidaṁ* < *yad* + *idaṁ*; *yadagge* DPD resolves as *yad* + *agga* + *e*, literally "in which point", and its own illustration is the Mahāli sentence Warder gives. *Yadidaṁ* is common, with 953 root-text occurrences, while *yadagge* has four and *yadaggena* four, so a reader will meet the first constantly and the second twice in a lifetime of reading.

#### *Yathā*, and the relatives that mean something

*Yathā* is the next word up the scale. Its core sense is manner ("as", "in the way that"), but Warder records it as also carrying consecutive, comparative, causal and final senses, a range wide enough that the answering word is usually needed to fix it. The commonest answer is *tathā*, and *evaṁ* serves as well.

Doubled, *yathā yathā … tathā tathā* generalises the manner over every instance of it:

- ***Yathā yathā*** **vā panassa kāyo paṇihito hoti** *tathā tathā* naṁ pajānāti. (MN 10 — M i 57)
- *yathā yathā* in whatever way · *vā pana* or moreover · *assa* his (m. gen. sg.) · *kāyo* body (m. nom. sg.) · *paṇihito* is disposed (pp.) · *hoti* is · *tathā tathā* in just that way · *naṁ* it (m. acc. sg.) · *pajānāti* he understands
- Or however his body is disposed, he understands it accordingly.

That sentence is from the Satipaṭṭhāna Sutta's section on postures, and the doubling matters in it: the instruction is that the understanding tracks the posture whatever the posture happens to be.

The remaining relatives are specialised, and a reader can take their senses from the pairs directly. What follows treats the three kinds of clause they build.

### Time

Perniola distinguishes three ways of introducing a temporal clause, and the distinction is useful because the three behave differently.[^rel-perniola-time] *Yadā* answers to *tadā* or *atha* and marks a point. *Yāva* answers to *tāva* and marks an extent. And *yasmiṁ samaye* answers to *tasmiṁ samaye*, a locative noun phrase with a relative adjective in front of it, not a relative adverb at all: the construction Buddhaghosa reached for when he resolved the empty *yaṁ*.

[^rel-perniola-time]: Perniola §310.a, p. 401.

Warder adds two observations, both of which save a reader trouble. The correlative *tadā* is optional and is often absent. And the same *yadā* construction can carry a condition instead of a time, in which case the tenses behave as they do in a conditional sentence.

The extent construction carries one of the best-known passages in the canon. The word is not *yāvakīvaṁ*, which is how grammars cite it and which occurs nowhere; the canon has *yāvakīvañca*, with *ca* joined on, 95 times in the root texts.

- ***Yāvakīvañca***, **ānanda, vajjī** ***ye te*** **vajjīnaṁ vajjimahallakā**, *te* **sakkarissanti garuṁ karissanti mānessanti pūjessanti tesañca sotabbaṁ maññissanti**, *vuddhiyeva*, ānanda, vajjīnaṁ pāṭikaṅkhā, no parihāni. (DN 16 — D ii 74)
- *yāvakīvañca* and so long as · *vajjī* the Vajjians (m. nom. pl.) · *ye te* those who (m. nom. pl.) · *vajjīnaṁ* of the Vajjians (m. gen. pl.) · *vajjimahallakā* the Vajjian elders (m. nom. pl.) · *te* them (m. acc. pl.) · *sakkarissanti* will honour (fut.) · *garuṁ karissanti* will respect · *mānessanti* will esteem · *pūjessanti* will venerate · *tesañca* and of them (m. gen. pl. + *ca*) · *sotabbaṁ* what is to be listened to (n. nom. sg., fpp.) · *maññissanti* will think (fut.) · *vuddhiyeva* growth alone (f. nom. sg. + *eva*) · *pāṭikaṅkhā* is to be expected (f. nom. sg., fpp.) · *no parihāni* not decline
- And so long as the Vajjians honour, respect, esteem and venerate the Vajjian elders among them, and think their word worth listening to, only growth is to be expected for the Vajjians, not decline.

There are two relative clauses here, one inside the other, and their answering words are of different kinds. The outer *yāvakīvañca* is answered by nothing at all: the main clause simply states what is to be expected. The inner *ye te* is answered by *te*, and stands in the middle of the *yāva* clause as its object. Look only for *yāva … tāva* and you will find nothing to pair and conclude the sentence is broken.

### Cause

Two words do this work, *yato* and *yasmā*, and every grammar in the collection treats them as interchangeable synonyms answering to *tasmā* or *tato*. Warder alone says otherwise: "*yasmā*, 'because', 'since', is a rarely used synonym of *yato*."

He is right, and he is right in a more interesting way than he says. In the root texts *yato* stands 1,370 times against *yasmā*'s 374, which is the ratio he describes. In the commentaries it reverses, and more sharply (*yasmā* 3,317 against *yato*'s 590), and in the sub-commentaries 1,605 against 327. *Yasmā … tasmā* is the exegetes' connective. A commentator writes it when setting out a reason, and the canon, which argues less and narrates more, reaches for *yato*.

That matters for two quite different reasons. It shows a reader what to expect where, and it explains why the grammars disagree: a grammar written mainly from commentarial Pāli will teach *yasmā* as the ordinary word for "because", and will be describing the language it read.

Neither is rare in absolute terms, and the canonical instances of *yasmā* include one of the most-quoted sentences in the Saṁyutta:

- Kittāvatā nu kho, bhante, suñño lokoti vuccatī ti? — ***Yasmā*** **ca kho, ānanda, suññaṁ attena vā attaniyena vā** *tasmā* suñño lokoti vuccati. (SN 35.85 — S iv 54)
- *kittāvatā* in what respect (abl., adverbial) · *suñño* empty (m. nom. sg.) · *loko* the world (m. nom. sg.) · *vuccati* is called · *yasmā* because · *suññaṁ* empty (n. nom. sg.) · *attena* of a self (m. instr. sg.) · *attaniyena* of what belongs to a self (n. instr. sg.) · *tasmā* therefore
- "Sir, in what respect is it said that the world is empty?" — "Because it is empty of a self or of what belongs to a self, Ānanda, it is said that the world is empty."

The question and the answer are built from the same stem in three shapes: *kittāvatā* interrogative, *yasmā* relative, *tasmā* demonstrative. Bodhi points out that this triad runs right through the pronominal adverbs (*kattha*, *yattha*, *tattha*; *kadā*, *yadā*, *tadā*; *kuto*, *yato*, *tato*; *kathaṁ*, *yathā*, *tathā*), and once you see the pattern you have learnt three sets of words for the price of one.[^rel-triad]

[^rel-triad]: Bodhi, *Reading the Buddha's Discourses in Pāli*, "Interrogative sentences", p. 54: "Interrogative words often begin with the consonant *k-*, and several correspond to relative and demonstrative words." The interrogative forms and their behaviour are in the chapter on pronouns; what belongs here is only that the middle member of each triad opens a subordinate clause.

*Yato* also keeps its older sense of "whence", the place a thing comes from, and Warder gives sentences in which the two senses are hard to separate, as one would expect of an ablative that has become a conjunction.

### Place, and the connective *yena*

*Yattha* answers to *tattha* and is the ordinary relative of place, in both the literal and the extended sense: *yattha* also covers "in which case", "in which matter". It is the commonest of the specialised relatives — 3,936 occurrences in the root texts, more than *yadā* and *yāva* together.

*Yena* has two distinct jobs and they should not be confused. With a verb of motion it means "to where", and the thing arrived at stands in the nominative: *yena bhagavā tenupasaṅkami*, "he went up to where the Blessed One was". That idiom belongs to the chapter on case syntax, which treats it as a use of the instrumental.

The other use belongs here. Wijesekera shows that *yena* is also a **connective**, parallel to the empty *yaṁ*, in the sense "whereby, in that, such that".[^rel-yena-connective] It ordinarily follows a question about cause:

- Ko nu kho, bhante, hetu ko paccayo, ***yena*** **mātugāmo neva sabhāyaṁ nisīdati, na kammantaṁ payojeti, na kambojaṁ gacchatī**ti? (AN 4.80 — A ii 82)
- *ko* what (m. nom. sg.) · *hetu* cause (m. nom. sg.) · *paccayo* condition (m. nom. sg.) · *yena* whereby (m. instr. sg.) · *mātugāmo* a woman (m. nom. sg.) · *na eva* not at all · *sabhāyaṁ* in the assembly (f. loc. sg.) · *nisīdati* sits · *kammantaṁ* work (m. acc. sg.) · *payojeti* undertakes · *kambojaṁ* to Kamboja (m. acc. sg.) · *gacchati* goes
- "What is the cause, sir, what is the condition, whereby a woman does not sit in the assembly, does not undertake work, does not go to Kamboja?"

The Buddha's reply closes the sentence with the same word, not with a demonstrative: *ayaṁ kho, ānanda, hetu ayaṁ paccayo, yena mātugāmo …* (AN 4.80 — A ii 83). So *yena* here does not answer an interrogative at all; it attaches a clause to a noun, and that makes it a connective, not a case form in its case function.[^rel-yena-connective]

[^rel-yena-connective]: Wijesekera §75.d, who calls it "mostly employed as connective corresponding to the acc. *yaṁ* already dealt with", and cites this sentence as A II.82 along with *appamattakaṁ etaṁ … yena puthujjano tathāgatassa vaṇṇaṁ vadamāno vadeyya* (D I.3). His transcription of the first reads *sabhāya*; the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana prints the locative *sabhāyaṁ* and records *sabhāya* in its own apparatus as the Sinhalese, Syāmese and Pali Text Society reading, which is where his form comes from.

### Tense across the pair

Warder devotes a paragraph to the tenses of correlative sentences that no other source in the collection attempts, and although he writes it about conditionals it holds for the construction generally.[^rel-tense] There are four rules, and the same tense is normally used in both clauses:

[^rel-tense]: Warder, Lesson 27, "Notes on Tenses", p. 295. The three types of conditional sentence, with their examples, are in the chapter on clause syntax; what is added here is the observation that the rules are not special to conditionals.

- If the relation is **purely hypothetical**, both verbs are optative.
- If the result is **certain and always true**, both are present.
- If the result is **certain in a particular case**, both are future.
- A **past participle** may stand for the subordinate verb.

Two of those four need a further sentence. The present is the tense of what Warder calls an eternal truth, a result that holds whenever the condition does, and so much doctrinal statement stands in it. The past participle does what the tenses cannot: it marks the subordinate action as complete before the main one begins, a relation of order, not of time.

The third rule accounts for a feature of the canon that puzzles readers: whole passages in the future tense that are clearly not about the future. The Vajjian sentence above is one: *sakkarissanti*, *karissanti*, *mānessanti*, *pūjessanti*, four futures in a row, closed by *pāṭikaṅkhā*, a future passive participle, "to be expected", not a future tense, with the same effect by other means. The sentence states a particular case, certain if the condition holds, and does not predict.

### Making it general

Three devices generalise a relative clause, and Pāli uses them constantly.

**Doubling the relative.** *Yo yo* is "whoever", *yaṁ yaṁ* "whatever", *yattha yattha* "wherever". The correlative is doubled to match: *tassa tassa*, *taṁ taṁ*.[^rel-doubling]

[^rel-doubling]: Warder p. 72 and Collins p. 68. Warder's illustration is the Cakkavattisīhanāda Sutta's *sace kho ahaṁ yo yo … adinnaṁ ādiyissati tassa tassa dhanaṁ anuppadassāmi*, "if I give money to whoever takes what is not given": the king's fatal reasoning in that sutta, worth reading in full, because doubling a relative is how the canon states a policy. *Anuppadassāmi* does not occur as a word, because the root texts print ***dhanamanuppadassāmi***, with the object joined to the verb. That is the junction that hides *māvamaññetha* and *khomhi*, met this time between an object and its verb.

**Adding an indefinite.** *Koci*, *kāci*, *kiñci*, *keci* attach to the relative and generalise it: *yaṁ kiñci* "whatever", *ye keci* "whoever", *yāni kānici* "whatever" (neuter plural). The pair is regularly closed by *sabba*, as in the AN 3.1 sentence above.

The sentence of this book a reader is most likely to have met already is built this way, and it deserves a pause. When the Dhamma-eye arises in Koṇḍañña at the end of the first discourse, what it sees is stated as a correlative sentence and nothing more:

- ***Yaṁ kiñci*** **samudayadhammaṁ** *sabbaṁ taṁ* nirodhadhammaṁ. (SN 56.11 — S v 423)
- *yaṁ kiñci*<sub>a</sub> whatever (n. nom. sg.) · *samudayadhammaṁ*<sub>a</sub> is of the nature to arise (n. nom. sg., adj. compound *samudaya* + *dhamma*) · *sabbaṁ taṁ*<sub>a</sub> all that (n. nom. sg.) · *nirodhadhammaṁ*<sub>a</sub> is of the nature to cease (n. nom. sg., *nirodha* + *dhamma*)
- Whatever is of the nature to arise, all that is of the nature to cease.

Every word in it is either a pronoun or a compound of *dhamma*, there is no finite verb, and the whole content lies in the relation between the two halves. The relative clause opens a class with no members named; the main clause closes it and says what holds of all of them. That is what a correlative sentence is for, and it is striking that the canon's shortest statement of insight should be a pure specimen of the construction.

**Repeating relative and antecedent together.** Collins records a third device, repeating both the relative and the noun instead of doubling the pronoun alone, and Warder gives the form with *yadi*: *yaṁ yad eva parisaṁ upasaṅkamati, yadi khattiyaparisaṁ, yadi brāhmaṇaparisaṁ …*, "whatever assembly he approaches, whether of nobles, whether of brahmins …". The *yadi* there corresponds to English "whether", its ordinary use in a disjunction.

In all three the relative names no particular thing. What differs is only how the openness is marked.

### The relative adjectives

Pāli has, as English does not, adjectives that behave like relatives. They agree with a noun and they open a clause, and each has a demonstrative partner.

| Relative | Sense | Demonstrative |
|---|---|---|
| *yāvataka* | as much as, however much | *tāvataka* |
| *yattaka* | as much as | *tattaka* |
| *yādisa*, *yādisaka* | of which kind | *tādisa*, *tādisaka* |
| *yathārūpa* | of which kind | *tathārūpa* |

Every source that lists these lists the first two pairs as equals, and they are unequal. In the root texts *yāvatak-* stands 117 times and *tāvatak-* 78; *yattak-* stands 12 times, and *tattak-* 15, of which four turn out on inspection to be *tatta-* "heated" inside a compound, not the pronoun at all, leaving eleven.[^rel-adjective-counts] That is a ratio of roughly ten to one, and it should decide where a reader spends their attention. *Yādisa* and *yathārūpa*, with their partners, are common; learn both by sight.

[^rel-adjective-counts]: Counts are of all inflected forms in the root texts, since none of these words occurs in the canon in its stem form: *yāvatakaṁ* 73, *yāvatako* 30, *yāvatakvassa* 6, *yāvatakā* 3, and four more; *tāvatakaṁ* 37, *tāvatakeneva* 29, *tāvatakvassa* 6, and four more. That is the ordinary citation-form artefact: the form a word is looked up in belongs to the grammar, and the form in the canon to the text. The four discarded *tattak-* hits are *tattakapāle* "in a heated pan" (three) and *tattakāyagginā* (one), and all four are in the Visuddhimagga, not the canon proper, which the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana files among its root texts.

- ***Yāvatakaṁ***, **bhante, sekhena ñāṇena sekhāya vijjāya pattabbaṁ**, anuppattaṁ *taṁ* mayā. (MN 73 — M i 494)
- *yāvatakaṁ* however much (n. nom. sg.) · *sekhena ñāṇena* by a trainee's knowledge (n. instr. sg.) · *sekhāya vijjāya* by a trainee's realisation (f. instr. sg.) · *pattabbaṁ* is to be attained (n. nom. sg., fpp.) · *anuppattaṁ* has been attained (n. nom. sg., pp.) · *taṁ* that (n. nom. sg.) · *mayā* by me (instr.)
- Whatever, sir, is to be attained by a trainee's knowledge, by a trainee's realisation — that has been attained by me.

### The clause you do not see

The last thing to say about relative clauses is that Pāli builds fewer of them than English does, and that the *yo … so* construction is only one of five ways the language attributes something to a noun.

Perniola is the only source in the collection to set this out as a taxonomy, and it reorganises the subject.[^rel-perniola-five] A relative clause or phrase, he says, can be expressed:

[^rel-perniola-five]: Perniola §309, pp. 399–400, whose section heading is "Adjectival or relative clauses and phrases" and whose opening sentence is the frame: "Relative clauses and phrases do the work of adjectives and hence they qualify a noun or pronoun." His illustrations are given here as the canon has them; the analysis is his.

1. By a participle agreeing with the noun. *Addasā bhagavantaṁ dūrato va āgacchantaṁ*, "he saw the Blessed One coming from afar", where English needs "who was coming".
2. **By a *tappurisa* compound.** *Sīlasampanno bhikkhu*, "a monk endowed with virtue", for "a monk who is endowed with virtue".
3. **By a *kammadhāraya* compound.** *Jātapuriso kālaṁ karissati*, "the man who is born will die".[^rel-jatapuriso]
4. **By a *bahubbīhi* compound.** *Indriyesu guttadvāro bhikkhu*, "a monk who guards the doors of his senses": the compound is itself an adjective meaning "having guarded doors".
5. **By two correlative clauses**, which is everything this chapter has been about.

[^rel-jatapuriso]: His sentence, and not the canon's: *jātapuriso* occurs nowhere in the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana, neither as a word nor inside one. That is the standing hazard with his illustrations, met here for the seventh chapter running. It bears on the example and not on the category, for an unattested sentence is evidence about a grammarian, not about a language, and the chapter on compounds has attested *kammadhārayas* in plenty.

Collins states the same fact without the taxonomy, and states the reason: relative clauses "occur less in Pali than in English because Pali, like Sanskrit … has the ability to make up compounds ad hoc which replace relative clauses."

The practical consequence is a reading habit, not a rule. When a Pāli sentence seems to lack a clause that the English rendering clearly has, the clause has usually been compounded or hung on a participle, not omitted, and the reader's job is to find which. The chapters on compounds and on participles teach the forms; this is the sentence-level reason for caring about it.

### One sentence, read whole

Every chapter of Parts IV to VI has read its examples for its own feature. The syntax ends here by reading one sentence for all of them at once, and the sentence chosen is one a reader will meet over and over. Chapter 8 used it for its aorists:

- Atha kho te bhikkhū sāvatthiyaṁ piṇḍāya caritvā pacchābhattaṁ piṇḍapātapaṭikkantā yena bhagavā tenupasaṅkamiṁsu; upasaṅkamitvā bhagavantaṁ abhivādetvā ekamantaṁ nisīdiṁsu. Ekamantaṁ nisinnā kho te bhikkhū bhagavantaṁ etadavocuṁ. (AN 10.27 — A v 49)
- *atha kho* then · *te*<sub>a</sub> those (m. nom. pl.) · *bhikkhū*<sub>a</sub> monks (m. nom. pl.) · *sāvatthiyaṁ* in Sāvatthī (f. loc. sg.) · *piṇḍāya* for alms (m. dat. sg.) · *caritvā* having wandered · *pacchābhattaṁ* after the meal · *piṇḍapātapaṭikkantā*<sub>a</sub> returned from the almsround (m. nom. pl.) · *yena … tena* to where · *bhagavā* the Blessed One (m. nom. sg.) · *upasaṅkamiṁsu* they approached (3 pl. aor.) · *upasaṅkamitvā* having approached · *bhagavantaṁ* the Blessed One (m. acc. sg.) · *abhivādetvā* having paid homage · *ekamantaṁ* to one side · *nisīdiṁsu* they sat down (3 pl. aor.) · *nisinnā*<sub>a</sub> seated (m. nom. pl.) · *etadavocuṁ* < *etaṁ* this (n. acc. sg.) + *avocuṁ* they said (3 pl. aor.)
- "Then those monks, having wandered for alms in Sāvatthī and returned from the almsround after the meal, approached the Blessed One; having approached and paid homage to him, they sat down to one side. Seated to one side, those monks said this to the Blessed One."

Laid out word by word, with each word set against the one it answers to:

| Word | What it is | Read against |
|---|---|---|
| *atha kho* | particles, 'then' | the whole sentence |
| *te bhikkhū* | m. nom. pl., the subject | every verb that follows |
| *sāvatthiyaṁ* | f. loc. sg., the place | *caritvā* |
| *piṇḍāya* | m. dat. sg., purpose | *caritvā* |
| *caritvā* | absolutive | *upasaṅkamiṁsu* |
| *pacchābhattaṁ* | frozen accusative, 'after the meal' | *piṇḍapātapaṭikkantā* |
| *piṇḍapātapaṭikkantā* | compound on a past participle, m. nom. pl. | *bhikkhū*, with which it agrees |
| *yena … tena* | instrumentals, relative and correlative of place | *upasaṅkamiṁsu* |
| *bhagavā* | m. nom. sg., the person arrived at | *yena* |
| *upasaṅkamiṁsu* | 3 pl. aorist, inside the junction *tenupasaṅkamiṁsu* | the first finite verb |
| *upasaṅkamitvā* | absolutive, taking up the verb before it | *nisīdiṁsu* |
| *bhagavantaṁ* | m. acc. sg., the object | *abhivādetvā* |
| *abhivādetvā* | absolutive | *nisīdiṁsu* |
| *ekamantaṁ* | frozen accusative, 'to one side' | *nisīdiṁsu* |
| *nisīdiṁsu* | 3 pl. aorist | the second finite verb |
| *nisinnā* | past participle, m. nom. pl. | *bhikkhū*, with which it agrees |
| *bhagavantaṁ* | m. acc. sg., the person spoken to | *avocuṁ* |
| *etadavocuṁ* | *etaṁ* the thing said + *avocuṁ*, 3 pl. aorist | the third finite verb |

There are three finite verbs, and every other word in the passage hangs from one of them, directly or through something that does. Chapter 16 began from the same observation: the finite verb is the scarce resource a long sentence is organised around. So the way into a sentence like this is to find the finite verbs first and then to ask of each remaining word what it is read against. Reading from left to right, as English is read, gets the words in the right order and their relations wrong.

Not every word answers to a finite verb. *Sāvatthiyaṁ* and *piṇḍāya* answer to *caritvā*. The monks wandered in Sāvatthī for alms; they did not approach the Buddha in Sāvatthī for alms. An absolutive governs its own words as a finite verb does, and it is the governed words that tell a reader which action each phrase belongs to. *Pacchābhattaṁ* and *ekamantaṁ* are accusatives that have stopped being anything but adverbs, which is how the Digital Pali Dictionary files them, and *yena … tena* is the instrumental pair of the case-syntax chapter, with *bhagavā* in the nominative because, as this chapter put it, the thing arrived at stands in the nominative.

The links are Chapter 16's lion over again. *Upasaṅkamiṁsu* is taken up by *upasaṅkamitvā*, and *nisīdiṁsu* by *nisinnā*, the second time by a past participle instead of an absolutive, but to the same effect. Each action is the starting point of the next, so the passage recites as one movement and not as three. And two of the three finite verbs are inside junctions, *tenupasaṅkamiṁsu* and *etadavocuṁ*, so Chapter 8 found these verbs printed alone so seldom.

Nothing in the sentence is rare, and so the chapter ends here. *Tenupasaṅkami* has 1,671 occurrences in the root texts. Account for every word of this passage and you can account for most of the narrative prose in the canon.

### Where the rest of this is

The conditional sentence is a relative clause, and Warder classes *sace*, *ce* and *yadi* among the relatives on that ground: they open a bound clause and are answered by the main one. The three types and their tenses are in the chapter on clause syntax, which also treats the concessive clause, the only subordination in this family with no relative word at all, marked only by *pi* or *api* on a word inside it.

The simile is a relative clause too. *Seyyathā* (which DPD resolves as *se* + *yathā*, the first element an old form of the *ta-* stem answering to Sanskrit *tam*) opens the comparison, and *evameva* closes it, often several sentences later. The construction is treated with the other sentence types in the chapter on clause syntax. Three forms of the word have to be kept apart: *seyyathā* alone stands 35 times in the root texts, *seyyathāpi* 2,289, and *seyyathidaṁ* ("as follows", introducing a list, not a comparison) 686.

The pronominal stems' oblique forms as adverbs (*tasmā*, *tato*, *yena … tena*) are treated as case uses in the chapter on case syntax, and their forms in the chapter on pronouns. What this chapter has added is only that each of them can open a clause.

And one form is a fossil. The *ye-* of *yebhuyyena*, "for the most part", is an eastern nominative singular neuter of *ya-*, a Māgadhī form surviving inside a common adverb long after the dialect it belongs to stopped being written.[^rel-yebhuyyena] Wijesekera and Oberlies note it independently. It is a small thing, and a reminder that a language keeps its oldest material in the closed classes.

[^rel-yebhuyyena]: Oberlies §42(3), p. 186: "An 'eastern' nom. sg. ntr. is *ye* (D II 278,16, M II 254,24) which is also part of *yebhuyyena* 'mostly, usually'." Wijesekera §75.f says the same of the compound stem *yebhuyya-*, "*ye* being the eastern pronominal form", comparing Sanskrit *yadbhūyas*. DPD corroborates the segmentation without adjudicating the dialect, resolving the adverb as *ye* + *bhūya* + *ena* against Sanskrit *yadbhūyasā* — where the Sanskrit first element is *yad-* and the Pāli is *ye-*, which is the whole of the observation.

**Exercises for this chapter are in the workbook**, under "Exercises: Relative Clauses", with worked answers at the back of that volume.

**Principal sources.** Warder, Lesson 12 and the whole of Lesson 27, for the ordering of the relatives by how much each of them means, on which this chapter is built; Collins pp. 68–69 for the rule that decides every relative pronoun, and for the observation that Pāli needs relative clauses less than English does; Bodhi's section on relative and main clause correlation in *Reading the Buddha's Discourses in Pāli*, for the four places where that rule is obeyed and still surprises a reader; Wijesekera §50 for the general *yaṁ*; Perniola §309 for the four constructions Pāli uses in a relative clause's place. Buddhaghosa's account of *yaṁ*, the centre of this chapter, was read in the Sumaṅgalavilāsinī itself, not through the grammars that cite it.

## Metre

### What a verse is made of

The canon contains one short discussion of what a verse is, and this chapter has to start there, because it names the two things the chapter is about. A deity comes to the Buddha at night and asks four questions about verses; the Buddha answers all four in a single verse.

- ***Kiṁsu*** **nidānaṁ gāthānaṁ, *kiṁsu* tāsaṁ viyañjanaṁ; *Kiṁsu* sannissitā gāthā, *kiṁsu* gāthānam-āsayo ti.** (SN 1.60 — S i 38)
- *kiṁsu* what · *nidānaṁ* source, origin (n. nom. sg.) · *gāthānaṁ* of verses (f. gen. pl.) · *tāsaṁ* of them (f. gen. pl.) · *viyañjanaṁ* distinguishing mark (n. nom. sg.) · *sannissitā* resting on, dependent (f. nom. pl.) · *āsayo* dwelling-place, home (m. nom. sg.)
- What is the source of verses? What is their distinguishing mark? What do verses rest on? What is the home of verses?

- ***Chando*** **nidānaṁ gāthānaṁ, *akkharā* tāsaṁ viyañjanaṁ; *Nāma*-sannissitā gāthā, *kavi* gāthānam-āsayo ti.** (SN 1.60 — S i 38)
- *chando* metre (n. nom. sg.) · *akkharā* syllables (n. nom. pl.) · *nāma* name, noun (n.) · *kavi* the poet (m. nom. sg.)
- Metre is the source of verses, syllables their distinguishing mark; verses rest on names, and the poet is the home of verses.

Two of those four answers are the subject of this chapter. *Chandas*, "metre", is what a verse comes out of; *akkharā*, "syllables", are what marks it off from prose. Buddhaghosa, glossing the first of them, does not stop at the abstraction: *chando nidānan-ti gāyattiādiko chando gāthānaṁ nidānaṁ*, "'metre is the source' means: metre (the Gāyattī and the rest) is the source of verses".[^met-gayatti] He answers a question about metre in general by naming a particular metre, and the one he names is Vedic. The tradition understood well that Pāli verse sits inside a much older Indian practice of counting syllables.

[^met-gayatti]: Sāratthappakāsinī on SN 1.60, Spk I 94. The Abhidhānappadīpikā ṭīkā defines the metre he names: *gāyatti nāma yassa catūsu pādesu paṭipādaṁ chaḷakkharāni santi*, "Gāyattī is that which has six syllables to each of its four lines" (¶417). The edition spells the name with a short final *-i* and only ever prints it welded, as here in *gāyattiādiko*.

The canon does *not* name its own metres. The Vuttodaya says so itself, as a point of information, not a complaint: having set out how the technical names work, Saṅgharakkhita adds that *'gāthā' icceva niddiṭṭhaṁ, munindavacane pana*, "in the word of the Lord of Sages, however, it is pointed out simply as *gāthā*".[^met-gatha] A verse in the canon is a *gāthā*, with no more particular name.

[^met-gatha]: Vuttodaya 15. The whole treatise was read for this chapter, not consulted through the grammars that cite it.

There is one exception. At the opening of the Mahāsamaya Sutta the Buddha announces what he is about to do:

- **Bhagavā etad-avoca: "*Silokam*-anukassāmi, yattha bhummā tad-assitā."** (DN 20 — D ii 255)
- *bhagavā* the Blessed One · *etad-avoca* said this · *silokaṁ* a *siloka*, a verse (m. acc. sg.) · *anukassāmi* I shall recite (1 sg. fut.) · *yattha* where · *bhummā* the earth-dwelling (gods) (m. nom. pl.) · *tad-assitā* dependent on that (m. nom. pl.)
- The Blessed One said this: "I shall recite a *siloka*, of where the earth-dwelling gods have gathered."

*Siloka* is the name of a metre (the commonest metre in the canon, and the one described below), and nowhere else in the four *nikāyas* is a metre named at all. The gloss is not obvious, because *siloka* has a second and much commoner sense, "fame, praise", which is what it carries in the stock phrase *lābhasakkārasiloka*, "gain, honour and praise". The commentary settles which sense is meant here, in the most useful possible way, by defining the word: *silokam-anukassāmī-ti akkharapadaniyamitaṁ vacanasaṅghātaṁ pavattayissāmi*, "*I shall recite a siloka* means: I shall set going an assemblage of words regulated by syllable and by line".[^met-siloka]

[^met-siloka]: Sumaṅgalavilāsinī on DN 20, Sv II 684. DPD assigns this very line to *siloka* in the sense "verse, stanza" and gives *Mahāsamayasutta* as the source passage for that sense, distinguishing it from *siloka* "fame, popularity". Walshe renders the line "I'll tell you them in verse", and Ānandajoti takes it as the metre, and gives it in his preface to the second edition, p. 3, as his reason for calling the metre *Siloka*, not *Vatta*. The other sense is available here, so the commentary's definition matters: *akkharapadaniyamita*, "regulated by syllable and line", is decisive, because praise is not regulated by syllables.

That is a definition of metrical composition in three words, and it is a good one. Prose is words in an order; verse is words in an order that a count constrains. Everything else in this chapter is an account of which counts, and of what happens to words when a count is imposed on them.

#### Why a grammar has a chapter on this

The obvious reason is quantity. Verse is a large part of the canon's bulk, not an ornamental corner of it, and some of the most-read texts in it are verse throughout. Ānandajoti's estimate, from the PTS editions, puts the Sutta Piṭaka alone well past twenty thousand verses:

| Collection | Verses | Collection | Verses |
|---|---|---|---|
| Dīghanikāya | 280+ | Suttanipāta | 1,149 |
| Majjhimanikāya | 230+ | Vimānavatthu | 1,291 |
| Saṁyuttanikāya | 1,000+ | Petavatthu | 823 |
| Aṅguttaranikāya | 570+ | Theragāthā | 1,279 |
| Khuddakapāṭha | 72 | Therīgāthā | 522 |
| Dhammapada | 423 | Jātaka | 6,905 |
| Udāna | 77 | Apadāna | 5,228 |
| Itivuttaka | 263 | Buddhavaṁsa | 960+ |

The less obvious reason matters more for this book. Metre causes some of the forms this grammar has spent seventeen chapters describing. A poet who needs a light syllable where the language offers a heavy one will lengthen a vowel, shorten a vowel, double a consonant, simplify a conjunct, or drop a niggahīta, and the result is a form that looks like an error to a reader who has learnt the paradigms and does not know why it is there. Geiger files his whole treatment of metre under phonology for this reason: for a grammarian, the interesting thing about metre is what it does to words.[^met-geiger]

[^met-geiger]: Geiger, *Pali Literature and Language* §32 (p. 78 in Ghosh's translation), headed "Quantitative Changes in Composition and under Stress of Metre" and listed in his contents as "Influence of Metre": short vowels lengthened and long ones shortened *metri causa*, with the doubling of a following consonant treated as equivalent to lengthening the vowel before it, by the law of morae. The warning against altering the orthography to suit the metre is not in that section but in a footnote two sections earlier (p. 77 n. 2, on §29's svarabhakti vowels, which verse "often ignores"), where he says bluntly that "it is however not right to change the orthography for the sake of metre."

So this chapter has two audiences in one reader. It is an introduction to Pāli prosody, and it is the place where a number of loose ends from earlier chapters are finally tied off.

### Light and heavy, short and long

Everything begins with a distinction that is easy to state and easy to blur, and blurring it makes the rest of the subject incoherent. Vowels are short or long. Syllables are light or heavy. These are different properties, and a syllable with a short vowel is very often heavy.

The tradition keeps four separate words for the four terms, and they should be learnt as two pairs:

| Of a vowel | | Of a syllable | |
|---|---|---|---|
| *rassa* | short — *a i u* | *lahu* | light |
| *dīgha* | long — *ā ī ū* | *garu* | heavy |

The Vuttodaya states the whole rule for syllabic weight in half a verse, and states it as a list of three conditions:

> *Saṁyogādi ca, dīgho ca, niggahītaparo ca, yo; garu.*
>
> That which stands at the head of a conjunct, or is long, or has the niggahīta after it: heavy. (Vuttodaya 7)

Unpacked, and put the way a reader will use it:

1. A syllable whose vowel is **long** — *ā, ī, ū* — is heavy.
2. A syllable whose vowel is followed by a **conjunct**, two or more consonants, is heavy. This is called *making position*, and the conjunct need not be in the same word: a word ending in a short vowel before a word beginning *tv-* or *br-* has a heavy final syllable.
3. A syllable whose vowel is followed by the **niggahīta** *-ṁ* is heavy.
4. Anything else — a short vowel *a, i, u* with at most one consonant after it — is **light**.

*E* and *o* are the awkward pair, and they are awkward because they are long by origin. They count as heavy, and they count as heavy whether the syllable is open or closed. Where a conjunct follows they are pronounced short (*upekkhā* has a shorter *e* than *upekhā* does), but the syllable is heavy either way, once for the vowel and once for the conjunct. There is no position in which *e* or *o* is light by rule.[^met-eo]

[^met-eo]: Ānandajoti §1.1, whose grid marks *e* and *o* heavy in both the open and the closed column. Duroiselle §630 lists *e* and *o* simply among "the long vowels" beside *ā ī ū*, which gets the weight right by accident and the description wrong; and his §626 glosses *lahu* and *garu* as "a short syllable" and "a long syllable", collapsing the distinction this section is built on. Ānandajoti's preface to his second edition records making that correction to his own first edition, "against the useful distinction made in the prosodies". Anyone coming to this subject from an older grammar should expect to meet the confusion and should not adopt it.

The whole rule fits in a small grid. Read it as: what is the vowel, and what follows it?

| Vowel | Open syllable — nothing, or one consonant, after it | Closed syllable — a conjunct or *-ṁ* after it |
|---|---|---|
| short *a i u* | **light** ⏑ | **heavy** — |
| variable *e o* | **heavy** — | **heavy** — |
| long *ā ī ū* | **heavy** — | **heavy** — |

Only the top-left cell is light. That is the whole of the rule: everything after this is either an exception to it or a pattern built out of it.

#### The two marks, and a warning about them

A light syllable is written **⏑** and a heavy one **—**. This is the European convention and the one used throughout this book.

The Indian tradition uses the opposite convention, and the reversal is total: a straight stroke marks the light syllable and a bent one the heavy. The Vuttodaya says so in the same verse that defines the terms, in two words: the heavy syllable is *vaṅka*, "bent", and the light one *uju*, "straight".[^met-marks] So a Devanāgarī or Sinhala treatment of a metre may print what looks to a European eye like the inverse of the pattern it means. The signs are not international, and there is no way to tell from the marks alone which system is in use; the only defence is to check a syllable one already knows.

[^met-marks]: Vuttodaya 7: *garu, vaṅko, pādanto vā, rasso'ñño mattiko lu'ju*: "heavy, [marked] bent, or [being] at the end of a line; the other, short, is a single measure, [marked] straight". Ānandajoti §1.3 gives the same warning as a modern observation and notes the Devanāgarī forms; the tradition had already recorded it.

### Scanning a verse

The method is mechanical and should be done mechanically at first. Divide the line into syllables (each syllable is one vowel, with the consonants that follow it up to the next vowel), then apply the four rules above. The first verse of the Maṅgalasutta is a good place to start, because it is short and familiar, and contains almost every case:

```
      ⏑    —    —    —  ¦  ⏑    —    —    ×
a     Ba   hū   de   vā    ma   nus  sā   ca

      —    ⏑    —    ⏑  ¦  ⏑    —    ⏑    ×
b     maṅ  ga   lā   ni    a    cin  ta   yuṁ

      —    —    ⏑    —  ¦  —    —    —    ×
c     Ā    kaṅ  kha  mā    nā   sot  thā  naṁ

      —    ⏑    —    ⏑  ¦  ⏑    —    ⏑    ×
d     brū  hi   maṅ  ga    la   mut  ta   maṁ
```

- **Bahū devā manussā ca, maṅgalāni acintayuṁ; Ākaṅkhamānā sotthānaṁ, brūhi maṅgalam-uttamaṁ.** (Kp 5)
- *bahū* many (m. nom. pl.) · *devā* gods · *manussā* humans · *maṅgalāni* blessings (n. acc. pl.) · *acintayuṁ* have thought about (3 pl. aor.) · *ākaṅkhamānā* desiring (pres. part., m. nom. pl.) · *sotthānaṁ* well-being (n. gen. pl.) · *brūhi* declare! (2 sg. imp.) · *maṅgalam-uttamaṁ* the highest blessing (n. acc. sg.)
- Many gods and humans have thought about blessings, desiring well-being: declare the highest blessing.

Every mark in that grid comes from one of the four rules, and each of them can be named. *Ba-* is light: short *a*, one consonant after it. *-hū* is heavy for its long vowel; so are *de-*, *-vā*, *-sā*, *-lā*, *-mā*, *-nā*, *brū-*, *-thā*. *Nus-* is heavy because *-ss-* is a conjunct, and so are *maṅ-* (*-ṅg-*), *cin-* (*-nt-*), *kaṅ-*, *sot-* (*-tth-*), *mut-*. *Yuṁ*, *naṁ* and *maṁ* are heavy for the niggahīta. *A-* at the start of *acintayuṁ* is light although a conjunct follows two letters later, because what immediately follows it is the single *c*. And *ca* at the end of line a is naturally light and is marked heavy anyway, for a reason given in a moment.

Two features of that verse are habits, not rules, and both come back later. *Brū-* begins with a conjunct, and a conjunct at the start of a line has nothing before it to make heavy; the weight of *br-* only ever matters to the syllable in front of it. And the caesura marked ¦ after the fourth syllable of each line is a **word break**, not a pause, and the tradition's name for it says so: *padaccheda*, "the cutting of words", is the Vuttodaya's gloss on *yati*.[^met-yati] Reading it as a pause produces a sing-song recitation that no reciting tradition uses.

[^met-yati]: Vuttodaya 10: *pādo ñeyyo catutthaṁso, padacchedo yatī bhave*: "a *pāda* is to be known as a fourth part; the *yati* is a word-break". Ānandajoti's glossary, p. 47, corrects the same misapprehension (*yati*, "a word break (not a pause as sometimes stated)") and adds that the break is occasionally hidden inside a compound. Note that *pāda*, the word for a line of verse, literally means a foot or a quarter: a verse has four of them, and the fourth part of a *gāthā* is a *pāda* by arithmetic.

#### The last syllable of a line

The final syllable of a *pāda* is marked heavy whatever its nature. This is a convention, not a claim about the sound, and it reflects something real: a line-end is a place where a reciter can dwell, so a light syllable there is drawn out and the difference stops mattering. Ānandajoti marks that syllable **×** instead of **—**, to record that the weight is imposed, not found, and this book follows him.[^met-padanta] The Vuttodaya states the same rule in the same verse as the others: heavy, "or [being] at the end of a line".

[^met-padanta]: Ānandajoti, preface to the third edition, p. 4, which introduced the × for this purpose. In the measure metres the same syllable also *counts* as two measures whatever its weight, and there it has a name: *pādantagaru*, "heavy by being at the end of a line".
