Ānandajoti coins the complementary *pādādigaru* for a light syllable at the start of a line that has to be counted heavy to make the sum work.

So of the eight syllables in line a above, seven are weighed and one is declared, and so *ca* is marked ×.

### What disturbs the count

Three features regularly make a line refuse to scan when the four rules are applied to it as written. In all three the spelling and the sound have come apart, and knowing them saves a great deal of time.

**Aspirates are single consonants.** In Roman script *kh, gh, ch, jh, ṭh, ḍh, th, dh, ph, bh* are written with two letters and are one sound each; *ḷh* is the same, and it looks least like a single sound. None of them makes position. So *sukhaṁ* is ⏑— and not ——: the *-u-* is followed by a single consonant, and the syllable is open. Compare *sukkaṁ*, ——, where *-kk-* is a true conjunct. This is purely an artefact of the alphabet the book is printed in: the Sinhala, Burmese and Thai scripts write each aspirate with one character and the question does not arise.

Some conjuncts fail to make position. The regular offender is *br-*, which reliably leaves the preceding syllable light in *brahma-*, *brāhmaṇa-*, *brūti* and *anubrūhaye*, although *br* does make position elsewhere in the middle of a word. *Tvaṁ*, *dvāra* and *nhātaka* sometimes fail in the same way, and *by-* (also written *vy-*) fails quite often. Beyond that list the cases are doubtful and should be treated as doubtful.[^met-position]

[^met-position]: Ānandajoti §1.5, whose list this is. The Vuttodaya has a narrower rule of the same kind at verse 8: *pare pādādisaṁyoge, yo pubbo garukakkharo; lahu sa kvaci viññeyyo*: "when a conjunct stands at the beginning of the following line, the heavy syllable before it is sometimes to be known as light". That is a statement about the line boundary, not about particular words, and the two rules are complementary, not rival.

Sarabhatti vowels are written and not counted. Some words carry a *sarabhatti* (Sanskrit *svarabhakti*), a "broken" or partial vowel inserted between two consonants that would otherwise form a conjunct, most often separating two semi-vowels, or a semi-vowel from an aspirate, nasal or sibilant. When such a word is scanned, the inserted vowel is ignored, and ignoring it restores the conjunct, which then makes position. So the effect is to make the word shorter by one syllable *and* heavier at the join, not merely shorter by one light syllable. The common ones are *ariya* (scanned *ar-ya*, —⏑), *iriyati* (—⏑⏑), *cariya* (—⏑), *viriya* (—⏑), *araha* (—⏑) and *kayira* (—⏑).

The tenth verse of the same Maṅgalasutta needs two of these three at once, which makes it the best single illustration in the chapter. Applied literally, the four rules give nine syllables in the wrong shape:

```
      ⏑    —    —    —    ⏑    ⏑    ⏑    —    ×
      Ta   po   cab  rah   ma   ca   ri   yañ  ca
```

That is one too many for a Siloka line, and the pattern belongs to no metre. Notice also where the third syllable has landed: with *br-* making position, the *b* is dragged back to close *ca*, and the division falls inside the word. Now release *br-*, so that *ca* stays light and the cluster goes wholly to the next syllable, and drop the sarabhatti vowel of *cariya*, so that it is *car-ya* in two:

```
      ⏑    —    ⏑    —  ¦  ⏑    —    —    ×
      Ta   po   ca   brah  ma   car  yañ  ca
```

- **Tapo ca brahma*cariyañ*-ca, *ariya*saccāna' dassanaṁ; Nibbānasacchikiriyā ca, etaṁ maṅgalam-uttamaṁ.** (Kp 5)
- *tapo* austerity (n. nom. sg.) · *brahmacariyaṁ* the spiritual life (n. nom. sg.) · *ariyasaccānaṁ* of the noble truths (n. gen. pl.) · *dassanaṁ* seeing (n. nom. sg.) · *nibbānasacchikiriyā* the realisation of nibbāna (f. nom. sg.) · *etaṁ* this (n. nom. sg.) · *maṅgalam-uttamaṁ* the highest blessing
- Austerity and the spiritual life, seeing the noble truths, and the realisation of nibbāna: this is the highest blessing.

Eight syllables, and the pattern a Siloka odd line should have.

The second line needs two accommodations of its own, and each does separate work. *Ariyasaccāna'* has a sarabhatti vowel like *cariya*, and it has also lost the niggahīta of *ariyasaccānaṁ*:

```
      —    ⏑    —    —  ¦  ⏑    —    ⏑    ×
      ar   ya   sac  cā    na   das  sa   naṁ
```

Keep the sarabhatti vowel and the line has nine syllables. Keep the niggahīta and it has eight, but closes ——⏑× where the Siloka's even line requires ⏑—⏑×. Neither accommodation can replace the other. That makes four separate departures from the written text in two lines, in an ordinary verse, not a difficult one.

### Metre as the cause

Pāli verse was composed in a language that offered alternatives, and the composer took whichever alternative fitted. Some of this is ordinary choice among forms the language already had, not licence at all: the genitive plural of an *-i* stem can be *-inaṁ* or *-īnaṁ*, and a poet needing a heavy syllable simply uses the second. Ānandajoti calls this **fluidity**, and it should be kept apart from what follows, because no form is being bent.[^met-fluid]

[^met-fluid]: Ānandajoti §1.7, who gives *daṭṭhu* for *disvā* as an example of an alternative form used for the metre, and it is sound: *daṭṭhu* stands sixteen times in the root texts. His other example, *ghātvā* for *ghāyitvā*, does not occur anywhere in the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana, and is not taught here.

Beyond that, forms are altered. The changes available are few, and they are applied at predictable places: end syllables most often, medial syllables only at a junction between the parts of a compound or between stem and ending, initial syllables rarely.

Vowels are lengthened or shortened. This is the commonest change of all, and the lengthening of a final *-i* is commoner than everything else together.

Consonants are doubled or simplified. A single consonant doubled closes the preceding syllable and makes it heavy; a conjunct simplified opens it and makes it light. The result is the same as changing the vowel, and the two devices are interchangeable.

The niggahīta is dropped. This is the most visible of the three, because the reader can see the apostrophe where the editor has marked it. The final line of Dhp 183 is the standard example, and the dropped nasal can be shown to matter:

```
      —    —    —    —    ⏑    —    ⏑    ×
      e    taṁ  bud  dhā  na   sā   sa   naṁ
```

- **Sabbapāpassa akaraṇaṁ, kusalassa upasampadā; Sacittapariyodapanaṁ, etaṁ *buddhāna'* sāsanaṁ.** (Dhp 183 — Dhp p. 27)
- *sabbapāpassa* of all evil (n. gen. sg.) · *akaraṇaṁ* not-doing (n. nom. sg.) · *kusalassa* of the wholesome (n. gen. sg.) · *upasampadā* undertaking (f. nom. sg.) · *sacittapariyodapanaṁ* the cleansing of one's own mind (n. nom. sg.) · *buddhānaṁ* of the Buddhas (m. gen. pl.) · *sāsanaṁ* the teaching (n. nom. sg.)
- Not doing any evil, undertaking the wholesome, cleansing one's own mind: this is the teaching of the Buddhas.

The word is *buddhānaṁ*, genitive plural. Written in full, the line scans ——————⏑×, and its last four syllables are ——⏑×. The Siloka's even line requires the cadence ⏑—⏑× and no other; ——⏑× is not a possible ending. Drop the niggahīta, and *-na* is light, and the cadence is ⏑—⏑×. One nasal decides whether the most quoted verse in the Dhammapada is in metre or out of it.

#### Where the licence is the editor's

One case ought to be raised here, not left for a reader to find, because it changes what a metrical licence is evidence of.

Ānandajoti's one example of a variable vowel scanned light in an open syllable comes from the Ratanasutta, and turns on the word *abhabbo*, "unable". He prints *abhabbŏ* with a breve, and explains that its final *-o* must be read light against its nature. The licence is necessary: scan his text as written, with *-o* heavy, and the line closes ———×, neither a Tuṭṭhubha cadence nor any other. Read the *-o* light and the cadence is —⏑—×, as it should be. On his text the licence is what makes the line scan at all.

But the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana does not print *abhabbo*. It prints *abhabba*, with a short *-a* that is light without any licence at all, and it records *abhabbo* in its own apparatus against the siglum *sī.*, the Sinhalese edition. The Buddha Jayanti and the Pali Text Society both print *abhabbo*, so the licence is a fact about the edition in front of the scanner:

- **Catūh'-apāyehi ca vippamutto, Chaccābhiṭhānāni *abhabba* kātuṁ; Idam-pi saṅghe ratanaṁ paṇītaṁ, Etena saccena suvatthi hotu.** (Kp 6)
- *catūhi apāyehi* from the four states of loss (m. abl. pl.) · *vippamutto* freed (p.p., m. nom. sg.) · *cha* six · *abhiṭhānāni* heinous deeds (n. acc. pl.) · *abhabba* unable (m. nom. sg.) · *kātuṁ* to do (inf.) · *saṅghe* in the Saṅgha (m. loc. sg.) · *ratanaṁ* jewel (n. nom. sg.) · *paṇītaṁ* sublime · *etena saccena* by this truth (n. instr. sg.) · *suvatthi hotu* may there be well-being
- Freed from the four states of loss, unable to do the six heinous deeds: this too is the sublime jewel in the Saṅgha; by this truth, may there be well-being.

Both readings scan, and both give a correct Tuṭṭhubha, because the first syllable of the line is anceps and takes either weight:

| Reading | Scansion | Licence needed |
|---|---|---|
| *Cha chābhiṭhānāni abhabbŏ kātuṁ* (Ce, Ee) | ⏑—⏑—¦—⏑⏑¦—⏑—× | the *-o* scanned light |
| *Chaccābhiṭhānāni abhabba kātuṁ* (Be) | ——⏑—¦—⏑⏑¦—⏑—× | none |

So the licence is a consequence of an editorial choice, not a fact about the verse, and the other choice removes it. The caution generalises: a claim that a poet shortened a vowel is always also a claim about which edition one is reading, and the apparatus should be consulted before the licence is asserted. The same holds in reverse: an edition that prints a metrically comfortable form may have smoothed a difficulty that the manuscripts preserve.

> **Where the sources need care.** Ānandajoti also says of this line that "the double consonant in *abhiṭṭhānāni* has been simplified to meet the requirements of the metre". *Abhiṭṭhānāni* appears in none of the three romanised editions, and DPD lexicalises the word as *abhiṭhāna*, from *abhi* + √*ṭhā* + *-ana*, with a single *ṭh*. The Sanskrit *abhiṣṭhāna* would regularly give Pāli *abhiṭṭhāna*, so the simplification is real in the word's history; what is not shown is that it happened in this verse. It seems safer to treat *abhiṭhāna* as simply the Pāli form of the word.

### Two syllables for one

Behind everything in this chapter lies an equivalence that Pāli verse takes for granted: two light syllables are worth one heavy, ⏑⏑ = —. It follows that a metre can be satisfied in two ways at any given position, and both directions are used.

**Resolution** replaces one heavy syllable with two light ones. It is common, particularly at the start of a line, and it explains why a line that should have eight syllables sometimes has nine without ceasing to be in metre. The constraint is that resolution is only allowed across the *first two syllables of a word* (including a word standing as the second member of a compound, or following a prefix), so a run of two light syllables in the middle of a word cannot be a resolution. The negative particle *na* is the only regular exception, presumably because of how tightly it binds to what it modifies.

**Replacement** is the converse: two light syllables where the metre expects them are replaced by one heavy syllable, giving a line one syllable short. It is much rarer. And it obeys the mirror image of the same constraint: it happens after a word break, which shows that the two light syllables being replaced were themselves the first two of a word.

The rule of resolution is more useful than it looks, because it is a diagnostic. When a line has an extra syllable and will not fit any pattern, finding where the resolution is often identifies the metre; and in the Siloka it can decide which variation one is looking at. The author of the Buddhavaṁsa was a considerable master of the device, and that text is where to go to see it worked hard.

### The four kinds of metre

Pāli metres are built on two different principles, and each of the two divides again, giving four families. This classification is Ānandajoti's, and it is the most useful one available because it is organised by *how a line is constructed*, not by how many syllables it happens to have.[^met-classes]

[^met-classes]: Ānandajoti §2.1, who also notes that the four are listed in the order in which they historically emerged. The alternative, Duroiselle's and the tradition's, divides verses by whether their four lines are alike (*sama*), alike in pairs (*aḍḍhasama*), or all different (*visama*): Vuttodaya 11–12, Duroiselle §§632–635. That is a classification of *verses*; this is a classification of *lines*, and a reader scanning a line needs the second.

| Family | Pāli | The line is fixed by | Examples |
|---|---|---|---|
| Flexible syllabic | *vaṇṇacchandas* (1) | the **number of syllables**, with the cadence fixed and much of the rest free | Siloka, Tuṭṭhubha, Jagatī |
| Measure | *mattāchandas* | the **number of measures**, the syllable count varying | Vetālīya, Opacchandasaka, Āpātalikā |
| Bar | *gaṇacchandas* | measures **grouped into bars** of four | Ariyā, Gīti, Gubbinī |
| Fixed syllabic | *vaṇṇacchandas* (2) | the weight of nearly every syllable | Upajāti, Vaṁsaṭṭhā, Rathoddhatā |

In the syllabic metres one counts syllables: the Siloka has eight to the line, the Tuṭṭhubha eleven, the Jagatī twelve. In the measure metres one counts *mattā*, measures, a light syllable counting one and a heavy two; the totals are fixed and the syllable count is not. The bar metres add a second layer of organisation on top of the measure count, gathering the measures into *gaṇas* of four (which may be ——, ⏑—⏑, ⏑⏑—, —⏑⏑ or ⏑⏑⏑⏑), and the effect is much more like a bar of music than a foot of verse. The fixed metres are the late arrivals, and are the flexible ones with the freedom taken out.

Almost all of the canon a reader will meet is in the first family, and much the largest part of that is in one metre.

#### A word about feet

Older grammars describe Pāli metres with the eight trisyllabic *gaṇas* of classical Indian theory, each named by a letter: *ma* ———, *na* ⏑⏑⏑, *bha* —⏑⏑, *ya* ⏑——, *ja* ⏑—⏑, *ra* —⏑—, *ta* ——⏑, *sa* ⏑⏑—. The Vuttodaya opens with them and Duroiselle's chapter is built on them.[^met-ganas] They are a compact shorthand and there is nothing wrong with them as such.

[^met-ganas]: Vuttodaya 5, which packs all eight into a single verse ending *te'te'ṭṭha gaṇā go garu, lo lahu*: "these are the eight *gaṇas*; *go* is heavy, *lo* light". Duroiselle §629 gives them with their Greek names, and calls them "the eight-syllable feet, known in Pāli as the *aṭṭhagaṇa*", but they are eight feet of three syllables each, as his own table on the same page shows, and *aṭṭhagaṇa* does not occur anywhere in the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana.

They are avoided in this chapter, for a reason Ānandajoti gives and demonstrates: they cut the line in the wrong places, and so invent a rhythm the metre does not have. Described in *gaṇas*, the Indavajirā is *ta ta ja ga ga*, which written out is ——⏑¦——⏑¦⏑—⏑¦——. That grouping suggests a repeating three-beat figure. The metre's actual pattern is ——⏑—¦—⏑⏑¦—⏑——, an opening, a break and a cadence, and it sounds nothing like the first. Where the *gaṇas* truly reflect the structure, in the bar metres, where they are bars, they are used below.

### The Siloka

The Siloka is the most prevalent metre in the canon: the metre of the Dhammapada, of the narrative verse of the Suttanipāta, and of the great bulk of verse embedded in prose suttas. It is flexible enough to carry aphorism, dialogue, narrative and epic, and once you can hear it you have most of the canon's verse in hand.

A Siloka verse is four lines of eight syllables, arranged in **dissimilar pairs**: an odd or *prior* line and an even or *posterior* line, the pair repeated. The normal form of each, the *pathyā*, is:

| | Pattern |
|---|---|
| Odd (prior) line | [⏑/—/⏑⏑] ⏓ ⏓ ⏓ ¦ ⏑ — — × |
| Even (posterior) line | [⏑/—/⏑⏑] ⏓ ⏓ ⏓ ¦ ⏑ — ⏑ × |

The first four syllables are essentially free (the first may even be resolved into two light ones, as the bracket records), and the last four are the metre. Everything that identifies a Siloka line lies in its second half. The odd line ends ⏑——, the even line ends ⏑—⏑, and the difference between them is one syllable in seventh position. That opposition makes the verse feel like a couplet, not a list.

One further constraint on the opening explains a good many forms: two successive light syllables in second and third position are normally avoided, and one occasionally catches a poet changing a word's weight to avoid them. The Dhammapada is not consistent about this.

The Maṅgalasutta verse scanned above is a Siloka, and it deserves a second look now that the pattern is known:

| Line | Scansion | |
|---|---|---|
| a | ⏑———¦⏑——× | *pathyā* — the cadence ⏑—— is the odd line's |
| b | —⏑—⏑¦⏑—⏑× | the even line's cadence ⏑—⏑ |
| c | ——⏑—¦———× | **not** *pathyā* — see below |
| d | —⏑—⏑¦⏑—⏑× | the even line again |

#### The seven variations

An odd line may depart from the *pathyā* in seven recognised ways, and each has a name taken from the *gaṇa* that characterises it. They are given here as a table, to consult when a line will not fit, not to memorise:

| Name | Pattern | Also called |
|---|---|---|
| *pathyā* (the normal form) | [⏑/—/⏑⏑] ⏓⏓⏓ ¦ ⏑——× | |
| *javipulā* | [⏑/—/⏑⏑] ⏓⏓⏓ ¦ ⏑—⏑× | Anuṭṭhubha |
| *navipulā* | [⏑/—/⏑⏑] —⏓— ¦ ⏑⏑⏑× | 1st *vipulā* |
| *bhavipulā* | [⏑/—/⏑⏑] —⏓— ¦ —⏑⏑× | 2nd *vipulā* |
| *mavipulā* | [⏑/—/⏑⏑] —⏓— ¦ ———× | 3rd *vipulā* |
| *ravipulā* | [⏑/—/⏑⏑] ⏓⏓⏓ ¦ —⏑—× | 4th *vipulā* |
| *savipulā* | [⏑/—/⏑⏑] ⏓⏓⏓ ¦ ⏑⏑—× | 5th *vipulā* |
| *tavipulā* | [⏑/—/⏑⏑] —⏑— ¦ ——⏑× | 6th *vipulā*, very sporadic |

Line c of the Maṅgalasutta verse is a *mavipulā*: syllables two to four are —⏑—, and the cadence is ———×. It even has the word break the *mavipulā* normally takes after the fifth syllable, at *Ākaṅkhamānā | sotthānaṁ*. So the opening verse of one of the best-known suttas in the canon contains a named variation, and someone who had learnt only the *pathyā* would have concluded that the line was corrupt.

The *ravipulā* takes its break after the fourth syllable instead, and that difference matters. A line running ⏓—⏑—⏑⏑⏑—× is ambiguous on the marks alone; the caesura decides it. Break after the fourth and it is *ravipulā*; break after the fifth and it is a *pathyā* whose sixth syllable has been resolved.

The proportions changed over time, which makes the Siloka a rough dating instrument. In the earlier texts the *pathyā* accounts for about sixty per cent of odd lines and the *javipulā* (the variation that makes the odd line look like an even one) for about fifteen. By the end of the canonical period the *javipulā* has all but disappeared and the *pathyā* can account for eighty-five per cent. The questions and answers of the Pārāyanavagga and the introductory verses prefixed to them sit on opposite sides of that shift and can be compared directly.

#### What the metre is called

This book calls it the Siloka, because that is the name in general use and the name the reader will meet everywhere else. It should be said that the tradition calls it something else. The Vuttodaya's section is headed *Vattappabhedo*, "the varieties of *Vatta*", and treats *pathyāvatta* and *capalāvatta* as its kinds; *vatta* stands six times in the treatise and *siloka* not once. Duroiselle, following the Vuttodaya, also calls it *Vatta*. Ānandajoti used *Vatta* in his first edition and changed to *Siloka* in his second, on the ground that *siloka* is the word the Buddha uses at DN 20, as the opening of this chapter showed it is. Both names are defensible; they answer different questions, one about the canon and one about the commentarial tradition that analysed it.

### Tuṭṭhubha and Jagatī

The second family of flexible syllabic metres is a pair, and they are best learnt together because the second is the first with one syllable added.

| | Pattern | Syllables |
|---|---|---|
| Tuṭṭhubha | [⏑/—/⏑⏑] —⏑— ¦ ⏓⏑⏓ ¦ —⏑—× | 11 |
| Jagatī | [⏑/—/⏑⏑] —⏑— ¦ ⏓⏑⏓ ¦ —⏑—⏑× | 12 |

The line has three parts, and the names for them are useful: an **opening** of four syllables, a **break** of three, and a **cadence** of four (five in the Jagatī). The opening is normally ⏓—⏑—; the break is normally —⏑⏑, which accounts for about eighty-five per cent of cases; the cadence is the fixed part. The Jagatī adds its extra light syllable in the penultimate position, so the two metres differ only in the pattern of the ending.

The opening of the Aṭṭhakavagga's Guhaṭṭhakasutta is early Tuṭṭhubha, and shows both the normal line and one of the common departures:

```
      —    —    ⏑    —  ¦  —    ⏑    ⏑  ¦  —    ⏑    —    ×
a     Sat  to   gu   hā    yaṁ  ba   hu   nā   bhi  chan  no

      —    —    ⏑    —  ¦  —    ⏑    —  ¦  —    ⏑    —    ×
b     Tiṭ  ṭhaṁ na   ro    mo   ha   nas  miṁ  pa   gā   ḷho
```

- **Satto guhāyaṁ bahunābhichanno, Tiṭṭhaṁ naro mohanasmiṁ pagāḷho; Dūre vivekā hi tathāvidho so, Kāmā hi loke na hi suppahāyā.** (Snp 4.2 — Sn p. 151)
- *satto* attached, stuck (p.p., m. nom. sg.) · *guhāyaṁ* in a cave (f. loc. sg.) · *bahunā* by much (n. instr. sg.) · *abhichanno* covered over (p.p., m. nom. sg.) · *tiṭṭhaṁ* standing (pres. part., m. nom. sg.) · *naro* a person (m. nom. sg.) · *mohanasmiṁ* in delusion (n. loc. sg.) · *pagāḷho* sunk (p.p., m. nom. sg.) · *dūre* far (adv.) · *vivekā* from seclusion (m. abl. sg.) · *tathāvidho* of such a kind · *kāmā* sensual pleasures (m. nom. pl.) · *suppahāyā* easy to give up (m. nom. pl.)
- A person stuck in a cave, thickly covered over, sunk in delusion, stands far from seclusion — for sensual pleasures in the world are not easy to give up.

Line a is textbook: opening ——⏑—, break —⏑⏑, cadence —⏑—×. Line b has the break —⏑— instead, one of the documented alternatives, and is otherwise identical. Hold the three-part pattern in mind and the substitution is heard as a variation, not a fault.

The two metres are mixed freely. A Jagatī line inside an otherwise Tuṭṭhubha verse was always acceptable, and in the middle period the mixture is so ordinary that it is better to speak of a single Tuṭṭhubha-Jagatī metre. The Ratanasutta does it in consecutive lines:

```
      ⏑    —    ⏑    —  ¦  —    ⏑    ⏑  ¦  —    ⏑    —    ⏑    ×
      Ya   thin da  khī   lo   pa   tha  vis  si   to   si   yā     Jagatī
```

- **Yath'-indakhīlo pathavis-sito siyā, Catubbhi vātehi asampakampiyo; Tath'-ūpamaṁ sappurisaṁ vadāmi, Yo ariyasaccāni avecca passati.** (Kp 6)
- *yathā* as · *indakhīlo* Indra's pillar, the boundary post (m. nom. sg.) · *pathavissito* set in the earth (m. nom. sg.) · *siyā* would be (3 sg. opt.) · *catubbhi vātehi* by the four winds (m. instr. pl.) · *asampakampiyo* not to be shaken (f.p.p., m. nom. sg.) · *tathūpamaṁ* comparable to that (m. acc. sg.) · *sappurisaṁ* the true person (m. acc. sg.) · *vadāmi* I call (1 sg. pres.) · *avecca* having penetrated (abs.) · *passati* sees
- As Indra's pillar, set in the earth, would be unshakeable by the four winds, so, I say, is the true person who sees the noble truths with penetration.

The first, second and fourth lines are Jagatī, twelve syllables; the third is Tuṭṭhubha, eleven. Only the count marks the switch, and no more is needed.

In the later canon both metres harden into fixed forms with the flexibility removed: the Tuṭṭhubha becomes **Upajāti**, ⏓—⏑—¦—⏑⏑¦—⏑—×, and the Jagatī becomes **Vaṁsaṭṭhā**, ⏓—⏑—¦—⏑⏑¦—⏑—⏑×. These are simply the normal forms of their parents with the alternatives disallowed. Tālaputta's verses at Th 1091–1145 use both, separately and mixed, and are the place to hear the difference.

### The measure metres

The second principle counts *mattā*, not syllables. A light syllable is one measure and a heavy one two, so a fixed total can be reached by many different arrangements, and lines occupying the same position in the verse vary in syllable count while coming out to the same length. The total and the pattern of the cadence distinguish one measure metre from another; the openings are variable but come in groups of two measures.

The **Vetālīya** has fourteen measures in the odd lines and sixteen in the even, with the cadence ¦—⏑—⏑× in both. The **Opacchandasaka** has sixteen and eighteen, with one extra heavy syllable in the cadence, ¦—⏑—⏑—×. The **Āpātalikā** has the same totals as the Vetālīya and a different cadence, —⏑⏑—×.

The Jarāsutta opens in Vetālīya, and counting the measures out shows the principle at work:

```
      —    —    ⏑    ⏑  ¦  —    ⏑    —    ⏑    ×
      Ap   paṁ  va   ta   jī   vi   taṁ  i    daṁ

      —    —    —    ⏑    ⏑  ¦  —    ⏑    —    ⏑    ×
      O    raṁ  vas  sa   sa   tā   pi   miy  ya   ti
```

Counted in measures, the first line is 2+2+1+1 + 2+1+2+1+2 = 14 and the second is 2+2+2+1+1 + 2+1+2+1+2 = 16.

- **Appaṁ vata jīvitaṁ idaṁ, Oraṁ vassasatā-pi miyyati; Yo ce-pi aticca jīvati, Atha kho so jarasā-pi miyyati.** (Snp 4.6 — Sn p. 158)
- *appaṁ* little (n. nom. sg.) · *vata* indeed · *jīvitaṁ* life (n. nom. sg.) · *idaṁ* this · *oraṁ* short of, within · *vassasatā* than a hundred years (n. abl. sg.) · *miyyati* one dies · *yo ce pi* even if one · *aticca* having gone beyond (abs.) · *jīvati* lives · *atha kho* then indeed · *jarasā* by old age (f. instr. sg.)
- Short indeed is this life; one dies short of a hundred years. And even one who lives beyond it dies all the same, of old age.

The odd line has nine syllables and the even has ten, and each comes out to the total its own position requires: fourteen measures and sixteen. The two lines are not the same length as each other, and the Vetālīya does not ask them to be. What the measure count fixes is that every odd line in the metre will be fourteen and every even line sixteen, however many syllables the poet spends getting there. Note that the last syllable of the even line is light and counts two anyway: the *pādantagaru* rule described earlier, without which every even line in every measure metre comes out one short.

### The bar metres

The bar metres take the measure count and add a second organisation on top of it, grouping the measures into *gaṇas* of four. A four-measure bar can be filled in five ways (——, ⏑—⏑, ⏑⏑—, —⏑⏑, ⏑⏑⏑⏑), and the rhythm of these metres comes from the alternation of two of those patterns. The effect is musical, and the traditional grouping into bars describes how the lines move instead of imposing an analysis on them.

The **Ariyā** is the common one, with eight bars to each half-verse and totals of thirty measures in the first and twenty-seven in the second; the **Gīti** repeats the first half-verse instead, giving thirty and thirty; the **Uggīti** and **Upagīti** are the other two permutations. An older and looser form, **Old Gīti**, is probably transitional between the measure metres and these, and it is the metre of the Mettāsutta and of the Tuvaṭakasutta.

These metres are considerably harder to analyse than anything above, for two reasons that compound each other: resolution can turn any bar into ⏑⏑⏑⏑, and the line divisions printed in the editions do not always fall where the *pādayugas* do. There are only about four hundred and fifty verses in the whole canon in bar metres. With the Siloka and the Tuṭṭhubha secure you have the great majority of the verse, and can reasonably leave these to a second pass with Ānandajoti's descriptions to hand.

### When a line will not scan

Three answers, in the order to try them.

The text may need one of the accommodations already described: a sarabhatti vowel to ignore, a conjunct that does not make position, a dropped niggahīta, a resolution. Most difficulties are here.

The metre may have changed mid-verse. Mixing is one of the most characteristic features of canonical Pāli verse, and it happens within the verse and not only between verses. Metres built on the same principle mix most freely: Tuṭṭhubha with Jagatī, as shown above; Vetālīya with Opacchandasaka, where the rule seems to be that the Vetālīya takes the odd lines and the Opacchandasaka the even. Even the Siloka, which is structurally unlike all of them, turns up alongside other metres constantly, usually with its odd and even lines in their proper positions, which suggests the mixing was done with the Siloka's own shape in mind. A verse with one Tuṭṭhubha line and three Siloka lines is not a corruption.

The line may simply be wrong. Ānandajoti's §1.12 is a single sentence, and it may be the most useful sentence in his book:

> It may come as a surprise that when so many changes are considered to be permissible, quite often the expected change does not in fact take place, even in cases where it appears to be easy to do so, and the metre is simply left "wrong" according to the norms that otherwise prevail.

The permissible alterations are available, not obligatory. A composer could have shortened a vowel and did not; a reciter transmitted what he received. Geiger's warning belongs here (the orthography should not be altered for the sake of the metre), and it is the right instinct. Where an edition prints a line that does not scan, the useful response is to note it, consult the apparatus, and leave it alone.

Two smaller matters belong under the same heading, because both look like metrical faults and neither is. The quotation marker *ti* at the end of a verse normally stands **outside** the metre and is not counted; it is a mark of reported speech, not part of the line. And the so-called **reciter's remarks** (interjections like *iti Bhagavā*, "so said the Blessed One", or *iti Dhaniyo gopo*, "so said Dhaniya the herdsman") are also outside the metre, and are presumed to have been added by reciters to keep track of who is speaking. A line with one of these embedded in it will be hypermetrical by exactly the length of the remark. Remove it and the line scans.

### Where to go next

Scan first the verse a reader already knows by heart, because the ear catches what the eye misses. The Maṅgalasutta, the Ratanasutta and the Karaṇīyamettasutta are short, memorised by most people who recite in Pāli at all, and between them cover the Siloka, the Tuṭṭhubha-Jagatī and the Old Gīti. After those, the Dhammapada is largely Siloka with two short runs of Vetālīya (verses 15–18 and 235–238), so it is the best place to become fluent in one metre and then meet a second by contrast.

The Lakkhaṇasutta, DN 30, is a curiosity that is also useful. It is a late text and a display piece: twenty sets of verses in seven different metres, several of which occur nowhere else in the canon, and Ānandajoti tabulates every one of them by paragraph.[^met-lakkhana] If a metre is rare, that is generally where it is.

[^met-lakkhana]: Ānandajoti §2.24, whose table lists the metre and verse-count of all twenty sets. The text of the harder ones rests on K. R. Norman's reconstructions in "The metres of the Lakkhaṇasuttanta", *Collected Papers* III, pp. 45 ff., and IV, pp. 36 ff., as Ānandajoti notes at pp. 33 and 25.

For anything beyond this chapter, go to Ānandajoti's *Outline*, which is short. Warder's *Pāli Metre* (1967) remains the fullest study of the subject, and the historical argument is made there. Norman's translations of the Theragāthā, Therīgāthā, Suttanipāta and Dhammapada carry running metrical commentary on individual verses, which is the only way to see the analysis applied at scale.

A last word on what the analysis is for. It is easy to treat scansion as a technical exercise standing at some distance from what the verses are saying, and for the first week or two it is one. But a metre is a pattern a reciter's memory was built around, and the verses in this canon were carried for centuries by people who knew them as sound before they were ever written down. Learning to hear where a line ends, where it turns, and which syllable was lengthened to make it turn there belongs to reading the texts, as a way of reading them as they were made to be heard.

**Exercises for this chapter are in the workbook**, under "Exercises: Metre", with worked answers at the back of that volume.

**Principal sources.** Ānandajoti, *An Outline of the Metres in the Pāḷi Canon*, for the whole framework of this chapter (the definition of syllabic weight, the classification of the metres by how a line is built, and the descriptions of the individual metres); it is the only modern treatment in the collection written for a reader, not a specialist, and almost everything here that is not attributed elsewhere is his. The **Vuttodaya**, Saṅgharakkhita's twelfth-century prosody, read directly in the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana, not through the grammars that summarise it, for the tradition's definitions and names. Duroiselle §§626–642 for the classical taxonomy of feet and verse-shapes, which is what a student meeting Pāli prosody in an older grammar will meet first. Geiger §32 for metre considered as a cause of sound change.

## Appendix A: Paradigm Tables

The complete declensions and conjugations, gathered here for reference. Chapters carry only the working subset a reader needs at that point; the full paradigm, in every attested variant, belongs here.

**Source.** Transcribed from Bhikkhu Ñāṇatusita's inflection tables (© 2005, Forest Hermitage, Kandy, Sri Lanka; freely distributable non-commercially), themselves a synthesis compiled against Ānanda Maitreyya, Buddhadatta, Duroiselle, Fahs, Geiger, Perniola, Warder and Wijesekera. This appendix reproduces his tables, not a union with any other grammar: where his tables and Oberlies (the first authority for morphology) diverge, Ñāṇatusita's own content stands, checked but not amended. The one exception is three places where his own tables contradict themselves — a mislabelled row, a doubled letter, and a form set in the wrong column, each plainly typographical rather than something he intended — corrected with a note where they occur below.

**Notation.** Ordinary endings are given plain; a rare, dialectal, poetic or post-canonical variant is marked with parentheses, following Ñāṇatusita's own italic marking and Oberlies's own bracket convention for the same forms. Two other uses of parentheses are Ñāṇatusita's own and are reproduced as printed rather than read as rarity markings: his explanatory shorthand — `(= Dat.)`, `(= nt.)` — and his bracketing of an optional letter *within* a form, as in `(n)ehi`, `(i)ssāmi`, `(a)nti`, `(ṭ)ṭha`. The two are told apart by position: a rarity marking follows the form it qualifies and is separated from it by a space, an optional letter sits flush inside the form.

**On the verb tables' Middle Voice column specifically:** Ñāṇatusita sets his entire Middle Voice block in italics, evidently to mark the voice as a whole rather than to flag individual rare forms within it — unlike the noun and pronoun tables, where italic marks a rare variant *of the form given beside it*. Wrapping every middle-voice ending in parentheses would misread that convention, so the Middle column below is set plain, in its own column, exactly as the Active column is. Parentheses are still used *within* either column wherever Ñāṇatusita's own italic marks one alternative as rarer than its neighbour (for instance the a-Aorist's rare active alternates `amha`, `atha`).

### A.1 Nominal declension

One table per stem class, grouped by gender where the paradigm shares its endings. Rows run singular first, then plural; the seven cases follow Ñāṇatusita's own order (nominative, vocative, accusative, instrumental, ablative, genitive/dative, locative).

#### *a*-stems

| Case | Masc. | Neutr. |
|---|---|---|
| Nom. sg. | o (e) | aṁ (e) |
| Voc. sg. | a, ā (e, o) | a, aṁ |
| Acc. sg. | aṁ | aṁ |
| Instr. sg. | ena (ā, asā) | ena (ā, asā) |
| Abl. sg. | ā, asmā, amhā, ato | ā, asmā, amhā, ato |
| Gen./Dat. sg. | assa, āya (= Dat.) (ā) | assa, āya (= Dat.) (ā) |
| Loc. sg. | e, asmiṁ, amhi (asi) | e, asmiṁ, amhi (asi) |
| Nom. pl. | ā (āse, o, āso) | āni, ā |
| Voc. pl. | ā | āni, ā |
| Acc. pl. | e (āni) | āni, e |
| Instr. pl. | ehi (ebhi, e) | ehi (ebhi, e) |
| Abl. pl. | ehi (ebhi, ato) | ehi (ebhi, ato) |
| Gen./Dat. pl. | ānaṁ (uno) | ānaṁ (uno) |
| Loc. pl. | esu (ehi) | esu (ehi) |

The locative plural `-ehi` is Ñāṇatusita's own and is kept exactly as printed: it is the one form in this table that looked, on first read, like it might be an error (Oberlies's chart does not list it, and a locative in `-ehi` — properly an instrumental/ablative ending — is unusual), but per the Ñāṇatusita-only rule settled for this appendix, a form only *he* attests is not thereby wrong, only unconfirmed elsewhere; it stands as printed.

| Gen./Dat. pl. | arānaṁ, ārānaṁ, ūnaṁ, unnaṁ, ānaṁ | arānaṁ, ārānaṁ, ūnaṁ, unnaṁ, ānaṁ |
| Loc. pl. | aresu, āresu, usu, ūsu | aresu, āsu, usu, ūsu |

[^an-neut-voc]: Ñāṇatusita's own table leaves the neuter vocative plural cell blank here, unlike the corresponding cell in the *(m/v)ant*-, *as*- and *us*-stem tables below, where the identical nominative form is written out again rather than left blank. Neuter vocative regularly matches neuter nominative throughout Pāli declension, so the blank is presumably an intentional economy rather than a gap — but since this appendix reproduces his tables rather than silently filling them, the equivalence is noted here instead of invented as printed text.

[^ant-genpl]: Ñāṇatusita's two printings of this table disagree here, and nowhere else: the one-page layout sets all three forms upright, the two-page layout marks *antaṁ* and *antānaṁ* italic. A mechanical comparison of all 294 cells of the two layouts turns up no other difference in rarity marking, so this is an isolated variation rather than a systematic one. Oberlies settles it for the upright reading: he gives *°vantānaṁ* unbracketed in the main paradigm run (§41, p. 176), and gives the *-ataṁ* type as the ordinary present-participle genitive plural, citing *sataṁ* at Ja IV 292,28 and 294,4\* (§41, p. 177); *antaṁ* is the plain composition of his bare consonantal genitive plural in *-aṁ* (§38) with this stem. (Oberlies writes the niggahīta *ṃ*; his forms are given here in the book's *ṁ*.) None of the three is a marked variant for him, so this appendix follows the one-page layout.

### A.2 Pronouns

#### *ayaṁ*/*imaṁ* ("this") — singular

| Case | Masc. | Fem. | Neutr. |
|---|---|---|---|
| Nom. | ayaṁ | ayaṁ | idaṁ, imaṁ |
| Acc. | imaṁ | imaṁ | idaṁ, imaṁ |
| Instr. | iminā, anena, aminā | imāya | iminā, aminā, anena |
| Abl. | imasmā,[^imasma-macron] imamhā, asmā, amhā | imissā, assā, imissāya, imāya, assāya | imasmā,[^imasma-macron] imamhā, asmā, amhā |
| Gen./Dat. | imassa, imissa, assa | imāya | imassa, assa |
| Loc. | imasmiṁ, imamhi, asmiṁ | imissaṁ, imissā, imāsaṁ (assaṁ, imāyaṁ) | imasmiṁ, asmiṁ, imamhi |

[^imasma-macron]: Ñāṇatusita's table prints **`imasma`** here, in both the masculine and the neuter column, without the macron that *imamhā*, *asmā* and *amhā* all carry in the same cell. Two independent authorities settle it. DPD gives *imasmā*, "masc & nt abl sg of *ima*", from *ima + asmā*, Sanskrit *asmāt*, attested at *acirapakkanto hoti imasmā dhammavinayā* (Mahāsīhanādasutta); and Oberlies §42(5), p. 188, derives the whole paradigm as the historical forms — *asmā* among them — complemented by new ones built on the old accusative *imaṁ*, which yields *imasmā* and nothing else. A short final *-a* in an ablative singular is not a Pāli form. Corrected as a typographical slip, on the same footing as the doubled *i* of his future first plural, noted below.

| Abl. | imehi, imebhi (ehi, ebhi) | imāhi, imābhi | imehi, imebhi (ehi, ebhi) |
| Gen./Dat. | imesaṁ, imesānaṁ (esaṁ, esānaṁ) | imāsaṁ (āsaṁ, imāsānaṁ) | imesaṁ, imesānaṁ (esaṁ, esānaṁ) |
| Loc. | imesu (esu) | imāsu | imesu (esu) |

| Loc. | amusmiṁ, amumhi | amussaṁ (amuyaṁ) | amusmiṁ, amumhi |

#### *asu*/*amu* ("that, yonder") — plural

| Case | Masc. | Fem. | Neutr. |
|---|---|---|---|
| Nom. | amū (amuyo[^amu-unattested]) | amū (amuyo[^amu-unattested]) | amūni (amū) |
| Acc. | amū (amuyo[^amu-unattested]) | amū (amuyo[^amu-unattested]) | amūni (amū) |
| Instr. | amūhi (amūbhi) | amūhi (amūbhi) | amūhi (amūbhi) |
| Abl. | amūhi (amūbhi) | amūhi (amūbhi) | amūhi (amūbhi) |
| Gen./Dat. | amūsaṁ (amūsānaṁ[^amu-unattested]) | amūsaṁ (amūsānaṁ[^amu-unattested]) | amūsaṁ (amūsānaṁ[^amu-unattested]) |
| Loc. | amūsu | amūsu | amūsu |

[^amu-unattested]: Oberlies rejects both of these outright, and the distinction has to be kept because this appendix cannot otherwise draw it. Of *amuyo*: "GEIGER (§ 109) gives also *amuyo*. But this is only a grammarian's form" (§42(6), p. 190 n.2). Of *amūsānaṁ*: "The gen. *amūsānaṁ* which GEIGER cites is not attested" (n.3). Neither appears in his own paradigm. **Rare and unattested are different claims**: a rare form is Pāli that a reader may meet, while these are forms the indigenous grammatical tradition constructed by analogy and no text prints. The cells are left exactly as Ñāṇatusita set them, since this appendix is his tables verified rather than a new paradigm — but a reader who takes a parenthesised form here as something to use should know that for these two, on the best current authority, there is nothing to meet.

    **Geiger says less than Oberlies's quotation of him makes it sound, and the difference is typographic.** Read off the page (§109, p. 103), his paradigm gives the feminine plural nominative and accusative as *amū, (amuyo)* and the genitive plural as *amūsaṁ, (-sānaṁ)* — both disputed forms **in brackets**, and he declares at §105 note 1 what the brackets mean: "the rarer or more archaic forms are again given in brackets." So he is not asserting them as ordinary Pāli; he is flagging them, in his own notation, much as this appendix flags them in parentheses. On the substance he and Oberlies still differ — rare is not unattested, and Oberlies has looked — but they differ by one step, not two.

    Both forms have travelled further than either. Perniola prints *amuyo* in his *asu* plural and lists *amūsānaṁ* as an alternative genitive plural for both masculine and feminine (*Pali Grammar* §41, p. 55), **with no mark of rarity at all**. That is the mechanism this note exists to name: Geiger brackets a form, the next grammar drops the bracket, and a grammarian's construction has become a standard paradigm in two moves.

| Instr. | mayā, me | amhehi, no (amhebhi) |

| Gen./Dat. | mama, mayhaṁ, me (mamaṁ, amhaṁ) | amhākaṁ, no (asmākaṁ, amhaṁ) |

Ñāṇatusita's own table labels this pronoun's row **"2 Pers. *ta(d)*"** — but *ta(d)* is the third person, correctly labelled on the paradigm below; the forms actually listed here (*tvaṁ, tuvaṁ*…) are second-person throughout. The label should read *tva(d)*, and is corrected here as a transcription slip, not a coverage difference.

### A.3 Verb conjugation

Active and Middle voice side by side. Rows follow Ñāṇatusita's own person/number order (1st, 2nd, 3rd; singular then plural).

#### Vowel gradation

The ablaut grades that recur throughout the conjugation below.

| (a) / – | a | ā |
| i, ī, y | ĕ / ay | āy / ē |
| u, ū, v | ŏ / av | āv / ō |

[^issama-typo]: Ñāṇatusita's table prints **`(i)issāma`** here (and again at the conditional 1st plural, below) — a doubled *i* against every other cell in both paradigms, which are built regularly on `(i)ssa-`. Corrected silently as a typographical slip, not a form he intended.

#### Conditional

| Person | Active | Middle |
|---|---|---|
| 1 sg. | (i)ssaṁ | (i)ssaṁ[^cond-1sg] |
| 2 sg. | (i)ssasi, (i)ssa, (i)sse | (i)ssase |
| 3 sg. | (i)ssa, (i)ssati, (i)ssā | (i)ssatha |
| 1 pl. | (i)ssāma,[^issama-typo] (i)ssamhā | (i)ssamhase, (i)ssāmhase |
| 2 pl. | (i)ssatha | (i)ssavhe |
| 3 pl. | (i)ssaṁsu | (i)ssiṁsu |

[^cond-1sg]: On this sheet the middle-voice forms of the conditional and the aorists sit in a right-hand sub-column, at a consistent horizontal position, while a rare *active* alternative is set in italics immediately after the ordinary active form. On this one line Ñāṇatusita prints *(i)ssaṁ* upright and *(i)ssaṁ* italic side by side, both in the active position, leaving the middle position empty — measured on the page at 400dpi, the two sit where the first and second *active* forms sit in every row below. Read literally that would make a form a rare variant of itself, which is not a statement the paradigm can carry; and the conditional's is the only middle cell on the sheet that would then be blank, in a paradigm whose future middle first singular is likewise *(i)ssaṁ*. It is taken here as the middle form, mis-set — the same kind of slip as the doubled *i* in *(i)issāma* two rows down.

### A.4 Derivational suffixes and prefixes

Ñāṇatusita's tables carry this apparatus alongside the conjugation proper. It is not a paradigm in the same sense as the tables above — not a closed set of inflections for one form, but an inventory of what Pāli word-formation draws on.

**A note on notation in this section.** Parentheses here are part of the printed forms themselves — `(i)tvā`, `(ṭ)ṭha`, `(an)āya` — marking an optional segment, not rarity. So rarity cannot also be shown with parentheses without ambiguity. Rare and post-canonical suffixes therefore get their own column, on the same reasoning as the Middle Voice column above: where a distinction can't share a glyph, it gets its own space. Numbered groups (1., 2., 3.) are Ñāṇatusita's own, marking alternative formations of the same category.

#### Primary suffixes

Formed directly on the verb root, giving a verbal noun, participle or derived stem.

| Formation | Ordinary | Rare / post-canonical |
|---|---|---|
| Absolutive | 1. (i)tvā, (i)tvāna, ttā; 2. (i)ya, (i)yana, (i)cca; 3. (i)tuye, (i)tūna | 3. tu(ṁ); 4. (*ṇamul*) aṁ, iṁ, akaṁ |
| Active present participle | ṁ, nt, nta, māna, āna, ana | — |
| Future middle participle | — | (i)ssant, (i)ssamāna |
| Causative | e, aya, pe, paya, āpaya, āpe | — |
| Denominative | āya, aya, e, a, ya, iya, īya | — |
| Desiderative | kha, cha, sa (with root reduplication) | — |
| Future passive participle | 1. (i)tabba; 2. anīya, aniya, aneyya, aniyya; 3. (i)ya, eyya, (i)cca; 4. tāya, tayya, teyya | — |
| Future active participle | esin | — |
| Infinitive | 1. (i)tuṁ; 2. tave; 3. (i)tuye; 4. (i)tāye; 5. (an)āya (dative) | 4. tase |
| Intensive | a, ya (with root reduplication) | — |
| Past participle | (i)ta, na, tta, (ṭ)ṭha, (t)āvin, (ta)vant | — |
| Passive past participle | 1. ya, yya; 2. iya, īya, iyya | — |

The future middle participle is the one formation Ñāṇatusita marks as *wholly* rare — both its suffixes are italicised in his table, with no ordinary alternative given.

#### Secondary suffixes

Formed on an existing noun or adjective, giving a new one.

| Formation | Suffixes |
|---|---|
| Abstract | a, ka, tta(ṁ), ttana, tā, ya, eyya |
| Action-noun | ana (aṇa), a, ti, ta, tta, tā |
| Agent-noun | (i)tā/(i)tar, i/in, āvī/āvin, aka/ika |
| Comparative | tara, īya, (i)yya, issika, iṭṭha; tama (= superlative) |
| Connective | a, ika, aka (also diminutive, pleonastic and adjectival), īna, tā (= collective) |
| Patronymic | a, aya, āyana, āna, eyya, era, ya, ava |
| Possessive | a, āla, ālu, ava, ita, ika, ima, in, iya, ila, eyya, eyyaka, mant, vant, vin |

Two of the secondary classes are indexed by sense rather than being a single list, so they are set out separately.

**Numerical:**

| Sense | Suffixes |
|---|---|
| Times | khattuṁ |
| Fold | dhā |
| Ordinal | ma, ī, tiya, ttha, ṭṭha |
| Distributive | so |
| Multiple | guṇa |
| Collection | aya, ka |

**Pronominal:**

| Sense | Suffixes |
|---|---|
| Ablative adverb | so, to |
| Accusative adverb | aṁ |
| Manner | thā, (t)thaṁ |
| Place | tra, ttha, haṁ, hiṁ, dhi |
| Time | dā, dāni, dācanaṁ, rahi, dhunā, jja, jju |
| Adjectival | tana |

#### Conjugation signs

The class markers that build a present stem from a root. `+` marks a *guṇa* or *vuddhi* grade in the root vowel (see the gradation table at A.3).

| Class | Sign |
|---|---|
| 1 | a+ |
| 2 | a (with *ṁ* inserted after the root vowel) |
| 3 | ya |
| 4 | ṇu, ṇo |
| 6 | ṇhā |
| 7 | o, u (= middle voice) |
| 8 | e, aya+ |

Class 5 is absent from Ñāṇatusita's list, which runs 1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 7, 8. The gap is his, not an omission here.[^class-five]

[^class-five]: Ñāṇatusita gives no sign for class 5 and does not say why. The traditional eight-class scheme he is following (Kaccāyana's, via the grammars he lists as consulted) does have a fifth class, so this is most likely a compositor's slip rather than a claim that Pāli lacks one. Recorded as printed rather than filled in from another grammar, per this appendix's Ñāṇatusita-only rule.

#### Prefixes

*intens.* = intensifying; *neg.* = negating.

| Prefix | Sense |
|---|---|
| *a* | non-; neg., expresses absence |
| *ati* | on, further, beyond, across, up to, over, past, very (much); intens. |
| *adhi* (*ajjh-* before a vowel) | (on)to, toward, over, above, (up)on, superior to, at, in, great; intens. |
| *anu* | after, along, near to, according to, less than, each |
| *apa* | off, away (from), forth; can express hurt, devaluation, reverence |
| *pi* | to(wards), on(to), over, close upon |
| *abhi* | to(wards), on(to), over, facing, against; intens., may express reverence, particularity |
| *ava* / *o* | down, (be)low, off, away, back, aside, little, less; neg. and diminutive |
| *ā* | at, (near) to, until, away, as far as, all round; intens. |
| *ud* | up(wards), above, forth, out, away |
| *upa* | (un)to, (up)on, to(wards), near, with, as, like, up to; intens. and diminutive; opposite of *apa* |
| *du(r)* | wrong, bad, perverse, apart; opposite of *su* |
| *ni(r)* | (with)out, forth, down(wards), into, under; neg. |
| *pa* | onward, forward to, forth, out; intens. |
| *pati* / *paṭi* | again(st), back to, opposite, in reverse direction, in return, to(wards), near(by) |
| *parā* | onto, over, away, back, opposed to, aside, beyond, through |
| *pari* | (all) around, (all) about, all over, completely; intens. |
| *vi* | asunder, apart, away, (with)out, separation, distinction, reversion, expansion; intens. and neg. |
| *saṁ* | with, along, together, fully, nearby, completely; intens.; opposite of *vi* |
| *su* | well, happily, thorough; intens.; opposite of *du(r)* |

Ñāṇatusita sets this entire prefix block in italics, as he does the Middle Voice block — marking the category as cited Pāli rather than flagging any prefix as rare. The prefixes are therefore italicised here as cited forms, and none is marked rare.

### A.5 Forms that do not look like their words

The tables above are arranged by stem, and a stem is exactly what a reader does not have when a form will not parse. This list runs the other way. It gathers the forms in the book that cannot be traced to their dictionary word by looking at them: the irregular aorists and futures, the old perfects, the comparatives that changed their stem, the defective and enclitic pronouns, and the junctions so common that the canon seldom prints their parts apart. Each row says what the form is and which chapter teaches it.

Nothing here is new, since every row restates a chapter. The order is the Pāli alphabet's, and a junction is entered as the page prints it, *etadavoca* under *e* and not under *avoca*. A form that will not parse and is not here is most often a junction of the ordinary kind; Chapter 2's two habits are the place to begin.

| Form | What it is | Chapter |
|---|---|---|
| *akā* | root aorist 3 sg. of *karoti* 'does' | 8 |
| *akāsi* | aorist 3 sg. of *karoti* | 8 |
| *agamāsi* | aorist 3 sg. of *gacchati* | 8 |
| *agā* | root aorist 3 sg. of *gacchati* 'goes' | 8 |
| *aṭṭhāsi* | aorist 3 sg. of *tiṭṭhati* 'stands' | 8 |
| *addasā* | aorist 3 sg. of *dassati* 'sees' | 8 |
| *alattha* | middle aorist 3 sg., 'he obtained', from *labh-* | 8 |
| *avoca* | aorist 3 sg. of *vadati* 'says'; usually printed inside *etadavoca* | 8 |
| *asu* | remote demonstrative, 'that one over there', nom. sg.; *amu-* in the other cases | 5 |
| *assosi* | aorist 3 sg. of *suṇāti* 'hears' | 8 |
| *ahu* | root aorist 3 sg. of *hoti*; not *āhu* | 8 |
| *ahosi* | aorist 3 sg. of *hoti* 'is, becomes' | 8 |
| *āha* | perfect 3 sg., 'he said' | 8 |
| *āhaṁsu* | perfect 3 pl., 'they said' | 8 |
| *āhu* | perfect 3 pl., 'they said'; not *ahu* | 8 |
| *iccevaṁ* | *iti* + *evaṁ* | 12 |
| *idamavoca* | *idaṁ* + *avoca*, 'said this' | 8 |
| *udapādi* | aorist 3 sg., 'it arose'; the augment stands after the prefix *ud-* | 8 |
| *etadavoca* | *etaṁ* + *avoca*, 'said this' | 2, 8 |
| *etadahosi* | *etaṁ* + *ahosi* | 8 |
| *enaṁ* | anaphoric pronoun, acc. sg., any gender | 5 |
| *katvā* | absolutive of *karoti*, 'having done' | 11 |
| *kaniṭṭha* | superlative of *yuva* 'young', 'youngest' | 4 |
| *kāhati* | verse future of *karoti* | 8 |
| *khomhi* | *kho* + *amhi*, 'I am indeed' | 7 |
| *gantvā* | absolutive of *gacchati*, 'having gone' | 11 |
| *jeṭṭha* | superlative of *vuḍḍha* 'old', 'eldest' | 4 |
| *jeyya* | comparative of *vuḍḍha* | 4 |
| *ñassati* | future of *jānāti*, built on the root *ñā-* | 8 |
| *tamenaṁ* | *taṁ* + *enaṁ* | 5 |
| *te* | enclitic 2 sg., 'you, your'; at the head of a clause, nom. pl. 'they' | 5 |
| *tenupasaṅkami* | *tena* + *upasaṅkami*, 'approached', in *yena … tena* | 2, 8 |
| *tveva* | *ti* + *eva*, as the editions print it | 12 |
| *dakkhati* | future of *dassati*, the *-ss-* assimilated | 8 |
| *daṭṭhuṁ* | infinitive, 'to see' | 11 |
| *disvā* | absolutive, 'having seen' | 11 |
| *dissati* | passive, 'is seen' | 9 |
| *naṁ* | anaphoric pronoun, acc. sg., any gender | 5 |
| *ne* | anaphoric pronoun, acc. pl. masc. | 5 |
| *nesaṁ* | anaphoric pronoun, dat. and gen. pl. | 5 |
| *no* | enclitic 1 pl.; at the head of a sentence, the negative 'not' | 5 |
| *pāpiyo* | comparative of *pāpa* 'bad' | 4 |
| *bhiyyo* | 'more', an adverb; the comparative of *bahu* | 4 |
| *bhecchati* | future of *bhindati* 'splits' | 8 |
| *me* | enclitic 1 sg.; never the first word of a sentence | 5 |
| *yadidaṁ* | *yad* + *idaṁ*, 'that is' | 17 |
| *yebhuyyena* | 'for the most part'; *ye-* an eastern nominative | 4, 17 |
| *lacchati* | future of *labhati* 'gets' | 8 |
| *vakkhati* | future of *vadati* 'says' | 8 |
| *vidū* | perfect 3 pl., 'they knew'; also an adjective, 'wise' | 8 |
| *vuccati* | passive of *vadati*, 'is called' | 9 |
| *vo* | enclitic 2 pl. | 5 |
| *sakkā* | 'it is possible', with an infinitive | 15 |
| *sakkhissati* | future of *sakkoti* 'is able' | 8 |
| *sutvā* | absolutive of *suṇāti*, 'having heard' | 11 |
| *seṭṭha* | superlative of *sant*, 'best' | 4 |
| *seyya* | comparative of *sant* 'good', 'better' | 4 |
| *seyyathāpi* | 'just as', opening a simile | 17 |
| *seyyathidaṁ* | 'as follows', opening a list | 17 |
| *sohaṁ* | *so* + *ahaṁ* | 2, 5 |
| *hitvā* | absolutive of *jahati*, 'having abandoned' | 11 |
| *hessati* | verse future of *bhavati* | 8 |
| *hehiti* | verse future of *bhavati* | 8 |

## Appendix B: A Graded Reading Pathway

A reference grammar teaches the system, and only volume of reading builds a reader. This appendix is the exit ramp.

Start it at the end of Part IV, when the participles, absolutives and indeclinables are in place, and run it alongside the remaining chapters, not after them. Everything from Part V onward (compounds, word formation, the syntax chapters, metre) is easier to learn against text you are already struggling with, and much harder to learn in the abstract. Waiting until the grammar is finished is the reliable way to never begin.

### What "graded" means here, and how it was measured

A pathway is graded if each step is mostly built out of what the last step taught. That is a measurable property, and it has not previously been measured for Pāli, so the ordering below is measurement, not taste.

Two numbers decide it. **Coverage** is the share of a text's running words that fall among the commonest words of the four Nikāyas: a text with high coverage is built from vocabulary a reader will already have met many times. **The type/token ratio** is the count of distinct words divided by the count of running words: a low ratio means the text repeats itself, and that turns the canon's formulaic habit into free graded input.

The curve for the four Nikāyas (956,218 running words, 55,602 distinct forms) runs like this:

| Coverage of running words | Commonest forms needed |
|---|---:|
| 25% | 43 |
| 50% | 502 |
| 60% | 1,153 |
| 70% | 2,530 |
| 80% | 5,637 |
| 90% | 13,601 |
| 95% | 23,762 |
| 98% | 36,578 |

A quarter of everything the Buddha is recorded as saying is forty-three word-forms, and half of it is five hundred. That is the number to hold, and it is the reason a beginner's progress feels so steep at first and then so sudden.

Two cautions attach to the other end of that table, and the first matters more than it looks. These are surface forms, not word families. Pāli inflects heavily, writes its compounds solid, and welds its particles onto the word in front of them, so a single known word turns up on the page under a great many shapes: *dhamma* is also *dhammaṁ*, *dhammena*, *dhammassa*, and it is also *dhammañca* and *dhammampi* and *dhammaṁyeva*, and it is inside every compound that contains it. Nation's threshold (98% of running words, which for general English implies a vocabulary of eight to nine thousand word *families*) therefore cannot be set beside the 36,578 in the last row. They are counting different things.

So the comparison was made properly. Resolving each form to a dictionary headword and then decomposing the headwords through the *Digital Pāli Dictionary*'s own analysis of what each is built from, the vocabulary behind the resolvable seven-tenths of the four Nikāyas comes to roughly **eight thousand distinct words**.[^lemmas] That is the same order of magnitude as the English figure, not four times it, and so the intimidating number in the last row of the table is a fact about Pāli morphology, not about how much a reader has to hold in mind. The forms are many. The words are not.

The curve itself is good for ranking texts against each other, and the rest of this appendix uses it so.

The four collections rank like this:

| | Running words | Distinct forms | Type/token | Covered by the top 1,000 |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| Dīgha | 143,464 | 17,360 | 0.121 | 52.8% |
| Majjhima | 247,885 | 21,613 | 0.087 | 57.4% |
| Saṁyutta | 263,867 | 23,045 | 0.087 | **62.1%** |
| Aṅguttara | 301,002 | 25,468 | 0.085 | 58.1% |

Both measures agree, though they were not designed to. The Saṁyutta is the most accessible of the four collections and the **Dīgha the least**, and the Dīgha also introduces new vocabulary fastest: its type/token ratio is nearly half again the others'. A collection of long framed narratives, thick with proper names and place names, looks like that from underneath. Begin with the Saṁyutta.

### The one place the measurement is misleading

Verse. The Metta Sutta draws only 29.6% of its words from the commonest thousand; the Dhammapada's opening chapter, 37.5%; and the first verse of the Theragāthā has a type/token ratio of 0.927, which means that of fifty-five words, fifty-one are different. By this measurement the texts a Buddhist is most likely to know by heart are among the hardest in the canon, and by this measurement that is correct: metre forces rare words, compressed compounds and unusual word order, and a verse line will not tell you what it means from context because it has no context to spare.

It does not follow that you should not start there. It follows that you start there for a different reason, which is the subject of the first stage.

### Stage 0 · What you already know by heart

Take a text you can already recite (the Maṅgala Sutta, Snp 2.4; the Metta Sutta, Snp 1.8; the Ratana Sutta, Snp 2.1; the Dhammacakkappavattana, SN 56.11) and work through it word by word with the grammar open.

The reason is not that these are easy. The table above says they are not. The reason is that you already know what the text means, so none of your attention is going on the lexicon and all of it can go on the morphology. Every case ending you identify lands on a sentence whose sense you can already check. That is the cheapest grammar practice available to anyone who chants, and it is available on day one.

Expect it to be slow and expect some of it to surprise you. A phrase recited a thousand times turns out to be an absolutive plus a locative absolute, and the recitation had smoothed over a joint that the grammar makes visible.

### Stage 1 · The short Saṁyutta suttas

Now prose, and what follows is the shortest and most repetitive prose in the canon. The three suttas at **SN 22.15–17** are the place to start: *Yadanicca*, *Yaṁdukkha*, *Yadanattā*, seventy-seven, fifty-two and forty-six words long, and drawing 85.7%, 88.5% and 91.3% of their vocabulary from the commonest thousand forms. The last of those is among the most accessible texts in the four Nikāyas by this measure, and far from a curiosity: it is the *anicca–dukkha–anattā* argument in its barest form. The first of the three is read through in full at the close of Chapter 12, together with the two editorial abbreviations that open and close it, *sāvatthinidānaṁ* and *…pe…*, which all three use and which no rule of grammar explains.

Then **SN 22.14** and **SN 35.1**, and then work outward through the Khandhasaṁyutta (SN 22) and the Saḷāyatanasaṁyutta (SN 35). These *saṁyuttas* are built by permutation: one frame, filled with a different member of a short list each time. Having read four of them you have read the vocabulary of forty, and the fifth is where you start noticing the grammar instead of decoding it.

The best-known short suttas measure lower than the texts that open this stage. The *Sabba* (SN 35.23) draws 75.4% of its words from the commonest thousand and the *Kaccānagotta* (SN 12.15) 69.6%; the *Sīsapāvana* (SN 56.31), at 68.0%, would belong to the next stage. The first two are listed here anyway, at the foot of the stage, on the reasoning of Stage 0: a text whose teaching you already know costs less to read than its figure says. That is worth a few points of coverage.

Grammar assumed: Parts I to III, and the participles from Chapter 10. You will not need compounds yet, and so this stage sits where it does.

### Stage 2 · Where the formula gets long

The same repetitive structure carries on at greater length, and with the texts of this stage a reader first covers real ground in a sitting. **SN 22.59**, the *Anattalakkhaṇa*, is 474 words at 81.6% coverage and is the argument of Stage 1 worked out in full. **SN 45.8** analyses the eightfold path in 303 words: its coverage jumps from 78.9% at the commonest thousand to 93.4% at the commonest two thousand, the signature of a text that defines a technical vocabulary and then reuses it: learn the terms once and the rest is free.

Then **MN 63**, the simile of the arrow, which is 1,653 words long and has one of the lowest type/token ratios in this pathway, 0.159. It is the clearest demonstration in the canon that long and difficult are different properties.

Grammar assumed: add compounds, Chapter 13. From here on you will meet them constantly.

### Stage 3 · The Majjhima

The Majjhima's suttas are longer, and they have a structure and a narrative frame that reward reading them as wholes, not mining them. **MN 10**, the Satipaṭṭhāna, at 65.4% coverage and a type/token ratio of 0.211: long and formulaic, and the text you will most want to have read closely. **AN 3.65**, the Kesamutti (the "Kālāma Sutta"), at 62.1%. Then **MN 2**, at 61.4%, and **MN 8**, just under fifty per cent from the commonest thousand and worth the work; then **MN 21**, the simile of the saw, at 53.7%, whose difficulty is almost entirely simile vocabulary, not grammar.

The Vinaya enters here. Its narratives measure with the Majjhima: the first sermons in the Mahākhandhaka (Kd 1) at 65.0%, the going forth of Sāriputta and Moggallāna at 59.1%, the opening of the Vibhaṅga at Verañjā at 58.1%. The ranking behind those figures is drawn from the four Nikāyas alone, so the Vinaya's own vocabulary of robes, bowls, dwellings and procedure counts as rare even where the Vinaya uses it constantly. If anything, then, the figures are a little severe on it.

Grammar assumed: the whole of Parts V and VI. The case-syntax and clause-syntax chapters were written for this stage, and reading them against these suttas, not before them, is the better order.

### Stage 4 · Narrative, and then verse

The Dīgha last of the prose, because its difficulty is real and specific: **DN 2**, the Sāmaññaphala, is 7,090 words at 48.1% coverage, and **DN 16**, the Mahāparinibbāna, is 15,115 at 51.7%. Neither is grammatically harder than the Majjhima. Both are lexically much harder, and long enough that a reader still decoding will not finish. The longer Vinaya narratives belong beside them: Sudinna's story, which leads to the first rule of the Pātimokkha; Devadatta's, through to the schism; and the First Council.

Verse last of all, and by then it will be the second time you have met it. Go back to the chanting texts of Stage 0 and read them again with the metre chapter beside you; then the **Dhammapada**, then the **Theragāthā** and **Therīgāthā**, then the **Sutta Nipāta**, whose oldest strata are the hardest Pāli in the canon and the most rewarding to have earned.

### How to read, once you are reading

Four rules, and people break the first.

**Do not look up every word.** To stop at every unknown form is not to read, and reading builds the reader. Read the sentence, take what you get, and look up only what blocks it.

Read the same text more than once. The second pass through a sutta costs a fraction of the first and teaches more, because the vocabulary is no longer in the way. Here the low type/token texts pay off: reread SN 22.59 instead of moving to a new sutta of the same length.

**Read aloud.** Vowel length and consonant doubling are half the morphology, and they are invisible until pronounced. This is the same instruction as in the front matter and it matters more here, not less.

**Keep a translation shut but nearby.** Use it to check after you have committed to a reading, not to arrive at one. An answer read cold teaches a fact; an answer read after an attempt teaches the reason the fact was needed.

### The pathway at a glance

Each stage above names where to begin, and the tables below give the rest of the route, seventy-two texts in all. Stages 1 to 3 run from the most accessible text down. Stage 0 is chosen by what is chanted, not by the numbers, and Stage 4 runs from prose to verse.
